Chapter 1 : The Instruction of Narada

The latter part of the Shri Ganesha Purana begins here.

Salutations to Shri Ganesha. Salutations to the great Lord of the Ganas. Salutations to the lotus feet of the great Siddhi, great Buddhi, Lakshmi, Labha, and the king of the naked Bhairavas. Salutations to all the gurus of the Ganesha tradition.

The Sages said: O wise Suta, you have narrated the story beautifully. We have listened with devotion, yet we are not satisfied. Just as a person does not get satisfied by eating food day after day, please tell us another story so that we may find satisfaction.

Suta said: I have already narrated the Upasana Khanda; now I shall narrate the Krida Khanda to you. I will describe how Ganesha destroyed various demons and how He protected the sages, the twice-born, and the cows with great devotion. Listen with a steady mind.

The Sages said: The more you narrate this Purana, the more our desire to listen grows. Therefore, please narrate it fully so that all people may be immediately liberated from the ocean of worldly existence.

Suta said: I will tell you what was told by Brahma to the immensely radiant Vyasa, and by Bhrigu to Somakanta.

Vyasa said: O lotus-born Lord, please tell me the auspicious deeds of Ganesha—the forms He assumed and the actions He performed. O Brahman, out of your compassion, please speak at my request, for I am not satisfied even after hearing the Upasana Khanda. You should tell me about the various incarnations in which the mighty Vikata (Ganesha) destroyed various demons. Hearing about His childhood pastimes and various divine plays from your lotus-like mouth will bring peace to my mind.

Suta said: Hearing these various questions, the lotus-seated Brahma spoke to Vyasa, who was inquiring about the various divine stories.

Brahma said: You have asked well, O Vyasa, you who bring joy to the heart. A speaker’s affection grows when the listener is attentive. Because you are a person of virtuous fame, I have the strength to speak even that which is usually unspoken, just as a listener gives strength to the speaker. Now I will tell you all about the deeds of Ganesha. By taking various incarnations, the Lord removed the burden of the earth. He killed various demons, restored the gods to their positions, protected the virtuous, and was capable of uprooting the wicked. Listen, O sage, to the history of Ganesha, which was spoken by Vishnu and grants all desires upon hearing.

Vishnu said: In every age, Ganesha has a different name, a different vehicle, different actions, different qualities, and destroys different demons. In the Krita age, He is known as Vinayaka, riding a lion, having ten arms, radiant, large-bodied, the bestower of boons, and the controller of all. In the Treta age, He is known as Mayureshwara, riding a peacock, having six arms, the color of a lotus, and famous in the three worlds. In the Dvapara age, He is known as Gajanana, red-colored, riding a mouse, having four arms, and worshipped by gods and humans. In the Kali age, He is known as Dhumraketu, smoke-colored, riding a horse, having two arms, and he destroys the armies of the barbarians.

I will now tell you, O sage, which demons He killed. In the city of Angadesha, there was a brahmin named Raudraketu. He was a knower of the essence of all scriptures, a master of the Vedas and Vedangas, equal to all living beings, an observer of the Agnihotra sacrifice, and a worshipper of gods and cows. He was always devoted to the worship of the Lord and was an expert in all the Agamas. His wife, Sharada, was endowed with beauty and grace. There was no one like her among the Apsaras or the eight great heroines. Her face defeated the moon, which withered away in anxiety. She illuminated the earth with various divine ornaments, just as the sky is illuminated by strings of stars.

Once, the lotus-eyed Sharada became pregnant in the month of Sharada (Ashwin), which is beautifully adorned by the autumn moon. Her body was so radiant that nothing else could be perceived. She fulfilled all her desires. In the ninth month, she gave birth to twin sons who were extremely beautiful and brought joy to their hearts. Seeing them with long arms reaching to their knees and large eyes, their father said, "Today I am free from debt, and my penance has become fruitful. My lineage is blessed, and my birth is blessed, as I have seen these two sons."

After worshipping the best of brahmins and Ganesha with offerings, he performed the birth ceremonies and the auspicious rites with devotion. He fed the brahmins and gave many gifts, honoring his friends with clothes, jewels, and wealth. With the sound of various musical instruments and the distribution of sweets and betel leaves in every house, he was filled with joy at the birth of his sons. Just as a yogi who is intent on righteousness attains the best object, after ten days passed, he invited the best of brahmins who were experts in astrology and asked them, "O best of brahmins, what should be the names of these two? Please tell me after contemplating their past and future."

They meditated and said, "O brahmin, their names should be Devantaka and Narantaka, for these names are meaningful." Hearing their words, the brahmin, who was devoted to the twice-born, performed the naming ceremony according to the rites prescribed in his own branch of the Vedas. He fed all the brahmins and the townspeople.

The boys grew up like Mount Meru and Mount Mandara. Wherever they stepped, the head of Shesha in the netherworld would bow. The disc of the sun could be seen resting on their heads. They constantly showed wonders to their parents. Once, Narada suddenly arrived at the house of Raudraketu. Hearing of the fame of the children, he took them out of curiosity. Smelling their heads and placing them on his lap, he spoke to their parents.

Narada said: "I have come here after hearing the wonderful and auspicious fame of your sons. Even more wonderful things will happen to them in the future, O sage. It is your great fortune that you have obtained such sons." Seeing them, everyone felt joy; what to speak of their own kin? Hearing these auspicious words spoken by Narada, the parents bowed to the sage and said, "By your grace, may our sons have a long life. Please grant them your favor so that they may become powerful, world-renowned, omniscient, and punishers of their enemies."

Brahma said: Seeing the devotion of Raudraketu and Sharada, Narada initiated both boys into the five-syllabled great mantra. He told the sons, "Propitiate the destroyer of Cupid (Ganesha) with this." Placing his hand of protection on their heads, he instructed them in the practice. Having said this and bidding farewell to Raudraketu, the divine sage Narada disappeared. When the sage had departed, the boys joyfully said to their parents, "Please give us your permission to perform this practice."

Thus ends the first chapter, titled "The Instruction of Narada," in the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana.

Chapter 2 : The Obtaining of Boons by Narantaka and Devantaka

Vyasa said: O Brahmin, how did they perform their penance and where did they go? Please tell me all this in detail, as I am asking you with great respect.

Brahma said: You have asked well, O Vyasa. I will tell you everything now. Devantaka and Narantaka performed their penance. Having taken permission from their parents, they went to a dense forest where even the wind could not enter, filled with various trees and creepers. It was adorned with ponds and lakes, beautiful with flowers and foliage, and contained vast caves and various types of rocks.

Both of them stayed there and began great penance. Standing on one toe, they remained there with steady minds. Chanting the auspicious five-syllabled mantra (Panchakshari) taught by Narada, they meditated on Lord Shankara for a thousand years. For two thousand years, they remained without food, consuming only air. For another thousand years, they ate only dried leaves.

Thus, ten thousand years passed while they were chanting. As their intense penance increased, their radiance grew so bright that the sun’s rays became dim. Their bodies were covered in ash; they wore tiger skins and carried rosaries and elephant hides. At dawn, they worshipped Shambhu with flowers and leaves. In that forest, due to their wonderful radiance, even animals like lions and elephants, who were natural enemies, became devoid of enmity.

Pleased with their penance, the five-faced, three-eyed Lord appeared. He had ten arms, a beautiful body, and held a trident, wearing tiger and elephant skins. He carried the Ganga on his head, a damaru in his right hand, was adorned with snakes, wore a garland of skulls, rode a bull, had a blue throat, and his body shone with divine light. Adorned with various ornaments, white-bodied, and wearing the moon on his forehead, he appeared before them.

Seeing the Lord in such a form, they were filled with supreme joy. They danced and then prostrated before him with their whole bodies. With folded hands, they addressed the Lord of Lords: We are blessed, our parents are blessed, our birth and our eyes are blessed, and our penance is successful, for we have seen Maheshwara. You are beyond the reach of the Vedas, inaccessible, and beyond the scope of speech. Even the scriptures, the six sciences, and the mind are baffled by you. Even the thousand-faced Shesha is unable to praise you, nor are Sanaka and others. You are the creator, sustainer, and destroyer of the entire universe. You turn a pauper into a king and a king into a pauper. You turn a snake into a ring and a ring into a snake. You make the dead alive and the living dead. You make the poor wealthy and the wealthy poor.

Hearing their words, the husband of Uma said: Well done, well done. I have heard your nectar-like words with great joy. Pleased with your penance, I, riding my bull along with Uma, have come here. Ask for the boons you desire.

K (Vyasa) said: Hearing these words from the enemy of darkness (Shiva), Devantaka and Narantaka spoke with voices choked with joy: O Lord of Gods, Lord of all, Lord of the universe, if you are pleased, if you wish to grant us boons, and if you wish to show us grace, then grant us this: may we not meet death at the hands of gods, humans, kings, Yakshas, Rakshasas, ghosts, men, serpents, Gandharvas, Apsaras, or Kinnaras. May we not die by any weapon or missile, nor by wild or domestic animals, nor by planets, stars, spirits, Danavas, or Asuras. May we not die during the day or night, nor by insects or worms. Grant us the kingdom of the three worlds and devotion to your feet.

K (Vyasa) said: Hearing all their requested boons, the trident-wielder, the tree of life for his devotees, spoke to them.

Shiva said: Whatever you have desired, it shall be so, and not otherwise. You shall be fearless, immortal, and possess the kingdom of the three worlds. You shall be free from the fear of death. Saying this, Shambhu placed his lotus-like hand on their heads, granting them fearlessness.

Having obtained their desired boons, Shiva disappeared. Having received his permission, they circumambulated him, bowed, worshipped him, and then returned home. Seeing their parents, they were filled with great joy and bowed to them. Embracing their parents, they narrated their entire experience. The father, smelling their heads, spoke with joy: With your attainment of boons and the vision of Shankara, your birth and lineage are purified, and great glory has been earned. Hearing this news, my limbs have become cool, my sons. It is as if I have attained the supreme satisfaction of drinking nectar.

Then, they ate various auspicious foods served by their mother, along with their father and Brahmins well-versed in the Vedas and scriptures. Then, their mother worshipped them with various ornaments and jewels. Having received many blessings from the Brahmins, they bowed to them and sent them off, and both spent the night in happiness.

Thus ends the second chapter of the Krida Khanda of the Sri Ganesha Purana, describing the attainment of boons by Devantaka and Narantaka.

Chapter 3 : The Conquest of the Heavens

K said:
Then, rising in the morning, they meditated upon their gurus and paid them homage. Remembering and bowing to all the gods, they set out toward the southwest direction. After attending to the calls of nature, cleaning their teeth and tongues, bathing, and performing their morning prayers, they worshipped their chosen deities. Having worshipped the Brahmins with devotion, they gave them wealth and clothing. Seeing clarified butter (ghee) in a bronze vessel, they offered it to a Brahmin. They looked at a clear mirror along with a donation, and after dressing in their garments, they held a consultation.

The elder said: "I shall conquer the heavenly realms by the grace of the boons granted by the Great Lord. You, by that same grace, conquer the mortal world and the netherworld."

K said:
Having reached this decision and observing an auspicious day, one of them set out for the heavenly realm with the speed of the wind. Upon reaching Amaravati, he shattered the gardens and groves. Tying his waist-cloth firmly, the powerful one stood before Indra. A great clamor arose among the gods as they ran in panic, asking, "Who is this? Who is this? How did he arrive suddenly near Indra?" They shouted, "Strike him! Bind him firmly! Drive this man away!" But all those who ran toward him were repelled by him.

As he leaped and descended suddenly again and again, the great trees and the wombs of the celestial women fell due to his movements. The entire earth, with its mountains, forests, and mines, trembled. The radiance of his body made the best of the gods appear dark and pale. Just as a mortal who has overcome an illness suddenly becomes pale, so too did all the gods become pale upon seeing him. Even Indra became distressed and pale, O Sage. Some fled in the ten directions, while others prepared for battle. Some of the most cowardly and inferior gods sought refuge in him. Then, Indra mounted his four-tusked elephant, holding his thunderbolt (Vajra) in his hand.

He roared with a terrifying sound that made the three worlds tremble. He asked the prepared gods, "What are you looking at? Where has your valor gone, now that an Asura has arrived here?" Then they moved forward, ready for battle. Seeing the gods eager for conflict, Devantaka (the slayer of gods) said to Hari (Indra), "Why do you exert yourself, Shakra? Consider my boons."

Antaka (Death/Devantaka), terrified, fled. "I consider your thunderbolt to be like a blade of grass by the command of Shankara (Shiva). By my breath, the gods who move within your sight are all departing. Listen to my words, Shakra; surrender your positions to me peacefully. Stay wherever you wish. Otherwise, abandoning everything, you will meet your death in vain. Do you not know my name, Devantaka, O god?"

K said:
Hearing these words, the heart of Hari (Indra) shattered. A fierce fire emerged from Shakra's mouth, just as it happens when water falls into boiling oil. Then, the Great Indra, filled with anger, said to him, "How many demons like you have been slain by me? Now, out of pride from your boons, you have come, O lowest of Asuras. Surely, struck by my thunderbolt, you will fall to the earth. Your name itself implies that you are a compound word where the latter part is dominant (referring to his name 'Devantaka' meaning 'Slayer of Gods')." With that, Shakra struck him with his firm-fisted thunderbolt.

The thunderbolt shattered into a hundred pieces, like an unbaked clay pot. Devantaka stood in the battlefield with his hair unshaken, completely undisturbed. Then, he struck the best of the gods (Indra) on the back with his fist. Struck by that, he fell to the ground like a tree felled by a storm. The slayer of the demon Bala (Indra) realized his valor and fled, and the other pursued him with speed, like a lion chasing a herd of deer.

Opening his terrifying mouth, he said to the fleeing one, "Why do you flee now? Where has your boasting gone? Face me, Shakra, along with all the hosts of gods. A hero does not strike one who is fleeing and not facing him." He himself stood before them and began killing those gods; he struck them on the mouth and caused them to fall lifeless. He whirled one around and smashed him to the ground. He struck some with kicks and fists, and others with elbow blows. He grabbed some by the throat and dragged them to their death. He broke the knees of some, the arms of others, and threw the pairs of thighs of others far away.

Some gods fell face up, others face down. Some, unable to move, received blows to their faces. Some wondered with distressed minds, "Has the Lord of the World suddenly begun the dissolution?" All the gods were scattered like elephants harassed by a lion. Then, the victorious slayer of gods roared. Just as a flock of peacocks cries when the clouds thunder, so he roared. The Sun (Bhaskara) abandoned the sky and went far away. All the gods fled, abandoning their respective positions. Then, he himself stood in the position of Shakra with a fearless heart.

All the gods went to the excellent caves of the Golden Mountain (Meru), eating roots, tubers, and fruits, and spent their days in misery. Then, countless demons and Asuras arrived from the earth and the directions. They performed the coronation of the slayer of gods using various substances and water brought from various sacred places, accompanied by the chanting of mantras by various sages, and the sounds of conches, kettledrums, and drums. The demons said to their lord, "There has never been, nor will there ever be, anyone like you in the race of demons. Please give us your commands."

K said:
Thus, he alone conquered Shakra and the others. He ruled over the gods and protected Amaravati. Then, accompanied by crores of demons, he went to the realm of Brahma. Brahma also went to the place where the gods had previously gone. Leaving one leader there, he went to Vaikuntha. Sometimes, mounting Brahma's vehicle or Shakra's, he would travel. Along with Lakshmi, Hari (Vishnu) went to the Milk Ocean. Leaving the extremely powerful demon there, he pondered, "What have I not conquered? Tell me, demons, I shall go there." They replied, "There is nothing left; there is no domain of the gods anywhere else."

"Place various demons in the positions of the guardians of the world for the sake of the people. Fearless and without effort, protect Amaravati." Hearing the words of his ministers, he was filled with great joy. Devantaka carried out that advice exactly as it was spoken.

Thus ends the third chapter of the Krida Khanda in the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Conquest of the Heavenly Realm."

Chapter 4 : Narantaka's Victory

Vyasa said: I have heard the story of Devantaka from your mouth with devotion. Now, O Brahmin, tell me about the exploits of Narantaka.

Ka (Brahma) said: Listen attentively, O son of Satyavati (Vyasa), I shall now narrate it, seeing your great eagerness. Narantaka went to the earth accompanied by various groups of demons. He caused a great slaughter of kings and killed many of them. Seeing many kings killed, all others sought his refuge. Whatever army he saw, it fled in all ten directions. Just as ignorance is destroyed by knowledge and darkness by the sunrise, Narantaka brought the entire globe under his control. Those who sought his refuge were established in their positions as his subjects, and the sons of those who were killed were installed as tax-payers. Those kings who were ready to fight also became his servants. He placed his own favored demons in their positions. He ruled the earth surrounded by the ocean. The gods, demons, and Kinnaras were scorched by the burden of terror. Sages went to mountain caves, devoid of sacrifices and scriptural study. Due to the fault of abandoning the Vedas, they practiced the Shruti (Vedas) only in their minds. With minds overcome by terror, they did not perform penance.

Then Narantaka sent very powerful demons, skilled in various illusions, to conquer the Naga realm (underworld). They went in the form of Garuda and devoured the best of the serpents. Countless were eaten by them, so Shesha went forward. Taking pearls, jewels, and beautiful divine garments, along with the wives of the Nagas, he offered them all and made peace with the demons. He accepted the command issued by Narantaka and agreed to pay an annual tax. Even then, the demon (Narantaka) installed a prominent demon, along with many others, as the overseer of the underworld. Narantaka ordered him, who was stationed there, saying, "Whenever you see any disturbance from the serpents, inform us through a messenger; we will kill all the serpents." Having instructed him thus, all those demons went to where Narantaka was staying in the mortal world and told him the whole story. Narantaka always sent news of everything that happened in the mortal and nether worlds to Devantaka, along with all the gifts that were rare in the heavenly world. Devantaka then sent him whatever was rare on the earth. Thus, they both ruled the three worlds with great joy.

Somakanta said: How were these two killed, by whom, and in what form? Tell me by what weapon, missile, and incarnation they were destroyed.

Bhrigu said: When asked this same question, the four-faced one (Brahma) spoke to Vyasa. I shall tell you the same story with great affection.

Ka (Brahma) said: O Sage, listen with respect as I tell you how those two, who were invincible and proud of their boons, were killed, by which incarnation, and by which weapon and missile, and how the worlds were attained by Brahma and the gods were established in their own positions.

Thus ends the fourth chapter of the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Victory over the Earth and the Netherworld."

Chapter 5 : The Adityas Obtain Boons

Brahma said: My own mind-born son, Kashyapa, is highly intelligent, virtuous, righteous, ascetic, and a master of his senses. He is extremely compassionate in this world, a destroyer of sorrow and grief, and with eyes closed, he knows the present, past, and future. He is the creator and destroyer through his mind, a master of Vedanta, a knower of the essence of all scriptures, and he views a lump of clay, a stone, and gold as equal. His eldest and most beloved wife was Aditi, who was endowed with all auspicious signs and was incomparable in the three worlds. Her devotion to her husband and her inner radiance were such that they could reduce the three worlds to ashes, and even Shesha, with his thousand mouths, is unable to describe her virtues. The eight Nayikas always serve her to attain virtues, and she, the sinless one, gave birth to the hosts of gods, including Indra. She is the embodiment of the Primordial Nature (Mula Prakriti) and assumed such a form. Once, she spoke to her happy husband, who was also happy, saying, "O Lord, I wish to ask you something; please be kind and tell me. Without a husband, there is no refuge for women, O Lord."

Kashyapa said: You have spoken well, O virtuous one, who brings me joy and is sinless. Tell me what is on your mind, and I shall answer.

Aditi said: The gods, led by Indra, have become my sons. But the Supreme Soul, the Lord of Bliss, who is the highest of the high—when He becomes my son, only then will my mind be at peace. I wish to serve Him; tell me the means by which He may become my son, so that my mind may feel fulfilled.

The Sage said: Well spoken, O noble lady; your words are as satisfying as water to one thirsty or food to one hungry. Your words have brought me great joy. But how can the Supreme Soul, without merit, become one's son? Know the means for this and keep it steady in your heart. He who is beyond the reach of the Vedas, beyond Brahma and others, who is without qualities, without ego, without desire, and without duality; He who is not the subject of Maya, the dancer of Maya, and the one who deludes the deluded; He who is beyond Maya yet the foundation of Maya, beyond cause, the creator of the expansion of Maya, and the cause of cause and effect—how can He attain a physical form without spiritual practice, O dear one?

Aditi said: Whose meditation should I perform, and how should I perform the practice now? With which mantra should I do it? Tell me, O great sage.

Kashyapa said: Being asked thus, the sage instructed her in His name-mantra. It is the five-syllable mantra, ending in the fourth case, preceded by Om, ending with 'Namah', accompanied by meditation and the prescribed rituals. He explained the entire procedure of the preliminary rites to her. Then, O Brahma, she, being delighted, bowed down and worshipped her husband, Kashyapa, with respect. Having received his permission, she went to the forest for penance. Having bathed and wearing pure clothes, she meditated with a steady mind, performing the rituals for Vinayaka, the God of gods, according to the rules. In a place filled with trees and creepers, windless and undisturbed, Aditi, having controlled her senses and seated in an auspicious posture, chanted the supreme mantra, remembering Vinayaka. Desiring a direct vision of Him with a focused mind, she lived without food, consuming only air, devoted to chanting and meditation. By the power of her penance, all creatures became devoid of enmity. All the gods were troubled, wondering what she would accomplish. Thus, Aditi performed supreme penance for a hundred years. Seeing her various hardships and her patience in her feminine form, Vinayaka appeared.

A mass of radiance stood before her, with the brilliance of ten million suns. He had an elephant face, ten arms, and was adorned with earrings. His body was extremely beautiful, accompanied by Siddhi and Buddhi. He wore a garland of pearls, held an axe and a weapon born of the clouds, a golden waist-band, a mark of musk on his forehead, a serpent at his navel, and was adorned with divine garments. Seeing this great mass of light before her, Aditi trembled violently. Closing her eyes, she fainted and fell to the ground. She forgot her chanting and meditation and thought in her mind, "What has come before me? What is this wonder? Have I forgotten my chanting and meditation, or has the Supreme Lord come to grant me a boon, illuminating the directions with His radiance?" While she was thus bewildered, the Lord spoke to her.

Vinayaka said: O Goddess, I am He whom you meditate upon day and night in your mind. Seeing your dedication and severe penance, I have come to grant you a boon. Ask for the boons you desire in your heart; I shall grant them, being satisfied with this penance of yours, O virtuous one.

Kashyapa said: Hearing His words, Aditi became composed. With folded hands, she bowed down to Vinayaka in her misery and spoke to the Lord, who is always beyond the reach of the mind.

Aditi said: You alone create, sustain, and destroy the universe, O Lord of all. You are eternal, stainless, divine, without qualities, and without ego. You assume various forms, are eternal, attainable through yoga, and the fulfiller of all purposes. Now, O Vinayaka, grant me a boon in a gentle form. If you are pleased, O Lord of gods, and if a boon is to be granted to me, then become my son; then my purpose will be fulfilled. Then I shall serve you, and it will lead to the protection of the righteous and the destruction of the wicked, O Lord, and the fulfillment of the people.

Vinayaka said: I shall become your son, protect the righteous, destroy the thorns, fulfill all your desires, and satisfy you as well.

Kashyapa said: Having said this, the Lord of gods, Vinayaka, disappeared. Aditi went to Kashyapa and told him the whole story.

Aditi said: By your command, I went to the forest and performed great penance. The elephant-faced one came in a great form to grant me a boon. Seeing His form, I was afraid, and then I prayed to Vinayaka. He granted me various boons, O best of sages. Saying that He would become my son, Vinayaka disappeared. My task accomplished, I have returned to your hermitage by your power, O sage.

Kashyapa said: Hearing her words, which were like nectar, the chief of sages rejoiced with her. They both rejoiced with great love, as if satisfied by nectar.

Thus ends the fifth chapter of the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, describing the attainment of the boon by Aditi.

Chapter 6 : The Manifestation of Vinayaka

Bhrugu said: Then the Earth, oppressed by the heavy burden of wicked demons, assumed a different form and went to the lotus-seated Brahma. Distressed, she folded her hands and spoke to the creator.

The Earth said: I have become tarnished, miserable, and devoid of sacrifices and vows. O Creator, the gods, along with Indra and the sages, have been displaced from their positions. I am scorched by excessive burdens; to whom else can I turn for refuge? Provide a way for the destruction of the wicked, or else I shall sink into the netherworld, along with all my mountains and inhabitants.

Bhrugu said: Having heard these words of the Earth, Brahma spoke to her. He said, "I, along with all the guardians of the world, Indra, and the hosts of gods, and even the sages, have been deprived of our offerings and are deeply distressed. We have lost our stations, our mantras, and our righteous conduct. We all pray to the Lord of Lords, Vinayaka, who is the form of Brahman, formless, and the cause of all causes. When He assumes a form through divine will for the sake of the worlds, then there shall be peace for all, including you, O Earth."

Bhrugu said: Having said this, Brahma and the sages, with joyful hearts and folded hands, praised the Lord, who is both formless and with form. They prayed: Salutations to the Lord of all worlds, salutations to the abode of all worlds. Salutations to the creator of all worlds, salutations to the destroyer of all worlds. Salutations to the destroyer of the enemies of the gods, salutations to the protector of those who have taken refuge. Salutations to the one who nourishes His devotees, salutations to the one who is easily pleased by devotion. O formless one, eternal and beyond illusion, O Supreme Brahman, beyond the perishable and imperishable, devoid of qualities, compassionate to the distressed, salutations to You, O Lord. O healer of all, fulfiller of all desires, pure one, destroyer of all demons, eternal, truthful, benefactor, and impartial one, salutations to You everywhere.

Having praised the Lord of Lords, Vinayaka, in this way, all the sages and gods, overwhelmed and deeply sorrowful, spoke again. The entire world is in chaos, devoid of sacrifices and offerings. We have fled to the caves of Mount Meru, like wild animals in the forest. Therefore, O Sustainer of the World, destroy this great demon now. While they were all praying, a voice from the sky spoke: "The Lord will soon incarnate in the house of Kashyapa. He will perform wondrous deeds and restore your positions. He will bring about the destruction of the wicked and the protection of the virtuous."

Bhrugu said: Hearing these words, Brahma spoke to the Earth: "Be at peace, O Goddess Earth, for the voice from the sky is certain. All the gods will incarnate on the mortal plane for your sake, and Vinayaka, having incarnated, will remove this great burden."

Bhrugu said: Having been told this by Brahma, the Earth felt relieved and returned to her place with a peaceful mind. After a long time, Aditi conceived. Her radiance grew day by day, like the moon. In the ninth month, she gave birth to an excellent son. According to the words of Brahma, he became known as the son of Kashyapa. He had ten arms, great strength, and was adorned with ear-pendants. His forehead shone with musk, and his head was graced with a crown. He was endowed with Siddhi and Buddhi, adorned with a necklace of gems, his chest shone with the Chintamani jewel, and his lips were red like the Japa flower. With a prominent nose, beautiful eyebrows, a radiant forehead, and shining teeth, his bodily luster dispelled the darkness, and he was adorned in divine garments.

Seeing such a child, his parents were blinded by his radiance and could not look at him for a moment. Forcing their eyes open and seeing his excellent form, they both rejoiced. Then the child spoke to them: "Mother, because you prayed to me for a thousand years in the form you desired, and asked for me as your son, I have granted your various boons and have now come as your son. I shall remove the burden of the Earth and serve you both. I will restore the positions of Brahma and the others and destroy the wicked demons."

Bhrugu said: Hearing these words, they shed tears of joy, like two Cakravaka birds seeing the sun in the sky. They said to him, "We are blessed that the Supreme Lord Vinayaka has become our son. Our lineage, our ancestors, our birth, and our knowledge are all blessed. Since the Guru of the moving and non-moving, the witness of all, the formless one, the embodiment of eternal bliss and truth, has assumed the auspicious state of being our son—He in whom the entire universe is strung like beads on a thread, He who is all-pervading and all-knowing—you are undoubtedly He. Now, withdraw this supreme divine form and assume a natural form, and play like a child. When the time comes for a specific task, you will perform it."

Hearing this, he concealed his divine form. As a natural two-armed child, he cried on the ground, making the netherworld, the sky, and all directions resound. Hearing that sound, barren trees became pregnant with fruit, and the dry became lush. Indra rejoiced with the hosts of gods. The fear of demons vanished, and the Earth flourished. Kashyapa performed the birth rituals with the Brahmins. He fed him honey and ghee, touched him with mantras, and Aditi suckled the child while reciting mantras. After cutting the umbilical cord and washing him, she put the child to sleep. The sage gave many gifts to the Brahmins. On the seventh day, Aditi gave gifts to every household, and on the fifth day, she distributed sweets with great joy. His father named him Mahotkata on the eleventh day. The child grew quickly, like the moon in the bright fortnight. Because he was superior to all, he was known as Mahotkata.

Chapter 7 : The Liberation of the Demoness Viraja

Brahma said: Upon hearing of the birth of the child, the sages Vashistha, Vamadeva, and others set out with great eagerness to see him. They arrived at the hermitage of Kashyapa, who honored them by offering seats, water for washing their feet, and the traditional offerings of hospitality. With folded hands, Kashyapa said, "I am blessed, and my ancestors are fulfilled, for I have seen your lotus feet, which are worshipped even by Indra and Hari. May I know the reason for your visit, O sages?" Vashistha, the foremost among them, replied, "O Brahmin, we heard from Narada about your extraordinary son. We have come only to see him and you; there is no other purpose."

Kashyapa then brought the child for them to see. Vashistha, observing the child’s thirty-two auspicious marks, said, "This child, who will perform great deeds, is the Supreme Lord Vinayaka himself, descended in a glorious form. O great sage, he will face many obstacles, but they will all be destroyed; he must be protected at every moment." Seeing the child’s red feet, marked with the signs of a flag and a goad, the sages worshipped the son of Kashyapa. They prayed for him to relieve the burden of the earth, protect the righteous, and destroy the wicked demons. Having bowed to him, the sages departed as they had come, and the child became famous as the son of Kashyapa.

One day, when the sage had gone out to bathe, Aditi put her son to sleep and went to attend to household chores. At that time, a demoness named Viraja, who was terrifying in appearance, arrived. She had protruding teeth, a cavernous nose, feet that crushed mountains, long breasts, a lolling tongue, and a stature that rivaled the city gates. This demoness, who devoured everything, snatched the child and, being hungry, swallowed him instantly as if he were a ripe banana. She then flew into the sky, but needing to drink water, she descended to the earth. She drank a vast amount of water, but her belly became so bloated that she fell down. She suffered intense pain, writhing on the ground like a man bitten by a snake. Crying out, "Release me, release me!" and unable to move, she struck her chest and head in agony.

Meanwhile, the son of the sage grew inside her body. He tore through her abdomen and emerged, standing upon her chest. Just as a large fish tears through a net to get out, the child emerged, and the wicked demoness died instantly, crying out in pain. Her falling body crushed trees for five yojanas around. Because of this, she attained her own divine abode, for how can there be rebirth in the ocean of worldly existence for one who has been seen or touched by the Lord of the World, the ocean of mercy, who grants knowledge and liberation?

Aditi, having finished her chores, returned and, not seeing her child, wept in great sorrow. She searched every corner of the house and, finding him nowhere, collapsed on the ground, wailing. Hearing her loud cries, others also wept, and all the people were filled with fear and wonder. With a withered face and tear-filled eyes, she lamented, "Who has taken my child, who came to me like a wish-fulfilling tree? How could the boon of the Lord of the World be in vain? Who is the wicked one who has taken the treasure given by the Divine? How have I, who possessed the ocean of knowledge, become so foolish? How have I become poor after obtaining a mountain of gold?"

As she lamented, striking her chest and head, she walked a krosa outside the hermitage. There, she saw the demoness lying dead, and upon her chest, she saw the child, playing and smiling as if he had just awakened. Running to him, she picked him up, embraced him, and kissed him. Filled with joy, she said, "Great is my fortune that by divine grace, the child has been freed from the demoness." She returned to her hermitage with the child after bathing. When she told Kashyapa everything, he said, "My dear, what the sages, who know the past and future, said has come true. Through the power of his merit, the child survived even after being swallowed. He is protected." Kashyapa then performed rituals, gave many gifts, and conducted peace ceremonies. He instructed Aditi never to leave the child alone even for a moment.

Thus ends the seventh chapter of the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Liberation of the Demoness Viraja."

Chapter 8 : The Liberation of the Crocodile

Ka said: Hearing that the powerful demoness Viraja had been slain, two other mighty demons, Uddhata and Dhundhura, arrived at the hermitage of the great sage Kashyapa. Seeing the child in the form of a parrot, they spoke to Aditi. They demanded that she give up the child so they could play with him, but Aditi refused, saying that such birds could not be easily caught by a woman on the ground. If she tried to hold them, they would simply fly away. However, the child suddenly leaped up and caught them like a hawk. The demons struck him with their wings and beaks, but the child slammed them against the earth with great force.

Both demons lost their lives and reverted to their original forms. The earth trembled under the weight of their massive bodies, and their fallen forms, which sounded like crashing trees, stretched for a distance of two miles. Aditi took the child back, and the sage Kashyapa, seeing their vast bodies, cut them up and burned them using a pile of wood. He performed a grand ritual for the child's well-being, marveling at the infant's prowess. Deeply pleased, he said to Aditi, "These two demon lords, whom even Indra could not defeat, have been killed in play by this infant. Why did you worry? This child is protected by the Lord of the Universe; we do not know the extent of his longevity. He must be guarded with great care. How did this child manage to kill these mountain-like demons? How will he survive in this forest filled with such creatures?"

Ka said: After speaking, the couple bathed the child and, filled with wonder, went to sleep. In his fourth year, the child wandered from the hermitage to a lake filled with aquatic life, crocodiles, and fish. It was surrounded by various trees like Tamala, Sarala, Jambu, Mango, and Jackfruit, adorned with flowers and fruits, and inhabited by many birds and sages. The water was as clear as the mind of a virtuous person. On the day of Vyatipata and Somavati, Aditi went there to bathe. She sat the child down and waded into the water up to her neck. The child, trying to reach her, fell into the water and began to play, but a crocodile seized him.

The mother cried out for help from the middle of the water, but she could not reach the child. The crocodile dragged the child further and further away, away from the mother. The child told her, "Do not worry, mother; I will not be released by you. I will die with you, for I have no strength." Seeing the child submerged and the mother in great distress, the disciples tried to fly down to save him, but even they could not overcome the powerful crocodile. Then, the child displayed his true strength. With a mere flick, he threw the crocodile from the water onto the land, just as the wind might blow a ripe fruit or as a child might toss a stone.

The crocodile’s body, which was miles long and wondrous, lay shattered on the ground, lifeless and still. Aditi and her disciples were filled with joy. Suddenly, a divine being, a Gandharva, appeared in his celestial form and said, "O Gajanana, I was once a king of the Gandharvas. During my wedding, all the Gandharvas came to my house, and I worshipped them with great devotion, but I failed to worship the sage Bhrigu. The sage, angered by my negligence during the wedding rituals, cursed me to become a crocodile. When I begged for mercy, he said that when the son of Kashyapa, Gajanana, touches you, you will regain your original form."

"Now I recognize you, O Gajanana in the form of a child. You are the Lord of the worlds, the Creator, the Preserver, and the Destroyer. You are beyond qualities, ego-less, and the ultimate cause of all existence. You are the protector of devotees and the destroyer of the wicked through your various incarnations. You are all-pervading, complete, the Lord of countless universes, and beyond the reach of even the sages, though described by mind and speech." After praising, bowing, and worshipping the child-form Gajanana, the Gandharva circumambulated him repeatedly and departed.

Aditi took the child, caressed him, and fed him. With a heart full of wonder and joy, she returned to her home. She bowed to Kashyapa and recounted the entire story. Amazed, he said to her, "This is the Supreme Lord himself. He has assumed a human body for his divine play. He will perform many other wondrous deeds. Through his grace, things impossible for gods and demons alike will be seen. People seeking their own welfare should cultivate firm devotion to him."

Chapter 9 : The Dispelling of the Confusion of Haha and Huhu

Kashyapa said: I will tell you another story of the child-formed Gajanana, which destroys all sins; listen with a focused mind, O Sage. Once, the Gandharvas Haha, Huhu, and Tumburu, who were devoted to Lord Hari and skilled in playing the Veena, were traveling. They were adorned with the symbols of the conch, discus, mace, and lotus, wore garlands of Tulsi, were smeared with Gopi-chandan, and dressed in yellow garments. Wishing to go to Kailasa, they arrived at the hermitage of Kashyapa. They were honored and worshipped by him according to the scriptures.

Kashyapa, bowing to them, asked with ease, "By what merit have you attained this state that grants Dharma, Artha, Kama, and Moksha? Your penance, birth, parents, knowledge, and hermitage are all blessed. I do not know the purpose of your journey; please tell me." Hearing this, they replied, "Our meeting you while we were on our way to Kailasa has caused our sins to vanish and our birth to be fruitful. We have attained supreme success and rest. Please permit us to leave, as we are eager to see Lord Girisha."

Hearing their words, the sage said, "No one leaves here without eating and resting. First, take water for bathing and have a meal." The sages bathed and, being devoted to divine worship, worshipped Shiva, Parvati, Vishnu, Ganesha, and the Sun. While they were absorbed in meditation, the child Ganesha, having played outside with other children, came into the house and saw the five idols. He took them outside, threw them away, and went into the fire-room. He smeared himself with ash and hid. When the sages finished their meditation, they did not see the idols in front of them.

Astonished, they asked each other, "Which demon has taken our idols? Have Gandharvas, Rakshasas, or Yakshas come to take them? Or have they disappeared to test our devotion?" Filled with great anger, they went to ask Kashyapa, "How could the idols of those absorbed in meditation disappear from your house? Even animals with natural enmity play together in this hermitage; why are there thieves here?"

Hearing this, Kashyapa, also enraged, decided that they should not leave without the idols. He called his disciples and said, "No thief has ever dared to enter here. Tell me, who has become a thief now?" The disciples replied, "We are not thieves, master. Your own son is known to you. We do not wish to speak ill of him, but we cannot ignore the truth."

Hearing this, the sage took a staff and went searching for his son, finding him in the fire-room. He brought the child before them. Kashyapa, filled with fury, said, "Bring the idols back quickly, or you will die, my son!" The child, fearless, replied, "I have not taken them, father. Whatever oath you ask me to take, I will do." Saying this, the child wept out of fear of his father. He fell to the ground, crying and distressed. His mother arrived, and the child said to her, "If I have eaten the gods, look into my mouth." When he opened his mouth, she saw the entire universe within.

She was astonished and fainted, falling to the ground in fear. Then the Gandharvas and Kashyapa also saw it: Kailasa, Shiva, Vaikuntha, Vishnu, Brahma, Satyaloka, Indra, Amaravati, the earth with its mountains and forests, rivers, oceans, Yakshas, Rakshasas, serpents, trees, birds, the fourteen worlds, all the gods, the netherworlds, and the ten directions.

Then, the mother regained consciousness and happily gave him milk. Kashyapa bowed to him, seeing him as the form of the universe. He realized in his mind, "He is the Lord himself who has descended into my house, and I went to strike him." Kashyapa then said to the sages, "Eat. This child is of great power and cannot be struck by me. If you have the strength, you may punish him to retrieve your idols, as he possesses the form of the whole and the parts."

They replied, "We have not eaten anything in your house, neither grain nor roots, other than the five idols." As they spoke, they saw the child in five forms—Shiva, Vishnu, and the others. With steady minds, they bowed, worshipped, praised, and sang to him. For a moment they saw the child, then the five forms, then a terrifying form, and then the universal form. They offered him food and ate the six-flavored meal. Then, they praised the child with devotion.

They said: "The world, deluded by ignorance, wanders in the cycle of birth and death, turning away from its true nature due to diverse thoughts. Bound by various attachments and ruined by the fruits of its own actions, it ignores You, the one who is formless, without qualities, and the Supreme Lord. Others, devoted to Your lotus feet, abandon the fruits of their rituals and meditate on You within themselves. By knowledge, they burn away all impurities and the seeds of karma, entering You quickly just as all rivers enter the ocean. Those whose minds are attached to worship and attain great success often forget Your lotus feet due to being immersed in that success. They fall from their high state due to ignorance, drinking poison while ignoring the rare nectar. Therefore, whether our birth is auspicious or inauspicious, may Your memory, which grants auspiciousness and destroys sorrow, always remain. If, while wandering in the ocean of existence, we are to be liberated, it will only be by Your merciful glance. Salutations to You, the Soul of all. No one is capable of knowing Your incarnations—how, how many, or when. O Lord of Yogis, Lord of Maya, Lord of Qualities, O Infinite One, salutations to You, O Lord, salutations to You."

Kashyapa said: Having praised him thus, they went to Kailasa, the abode of Shiva, still astonished by the child's pastimes and worshipping the five idols.

Chapter 10 : The Determination of Various Names

Kashyapa said: Then, in the fifth year, the wise Kashyapa performed the auspicious thread ceremony (Upanayana) for the child according to the rites prescribed in the scriptures. At an auspicious moment and planetary alignment, the gods, demons, and demons were invited by groups of disciples, as were sages, Yakshas, Nagas, royal sages, Vaishyas, and Shudras, all bringing various gifts in their hands. Many musical instruments were played, and the gods themselves performed the worship of Ganesha and the auspicious chanting (Swastivachana). He performed the installation of the pavilion, the worship of the Matrikas, the prosperity ritual (Abhyudaya Shraddha), and the worship of the Brahmins. He gave clothes to friends according to their status, and they, in turn, presented clothes to him.

After the sacrificial fires (Homas), Kashyapa again honored the Brahmins. Holding a curtain across the altar, he quickly performed the fire installation and brought the child forward. Auspicious women and Brahmins showered him with unbroken rice grains. In the midst of this, five wicked demons, disguised as Brahmins, threw weapons at him, desiring to take his life. They were Vighata, Pingaksha, Vishala, Pingala, and Chapala, all wearing large sacred threads and adorned with rosaries. They carried water vessels and wore precious garments. From their weapons, the child’s body appeared to be shattered. Realizing they were wicked, the child enchanted some rice grains and threw them at the five. At that very moment, they lost their lives, reverted to their true forms, and fell to the earth, shattered across ten yojanas, just as mountains fall when struck by the thunderbolt of Indra.

There was a great uproar, and the directions were covered with dust. Everyone wondered how this child had killed five powerful, shape-shifting demons in an instant, and they did not know who he was. They questioned if the Supreme Lord had descended to relieve the burden of the earth. Seeing this, the great gods, led by Brahma, showered flowers from their celestial chariots. After the demons were dead, the Brahmins and Kashyapa proceeded with the initiation. They gave the child the sacred thread, the girdle, the loincloth, the staff, and the deerskin according to their mantras. Then, filling his hands with water and looking at the sun, Kashyapa completed the Homa and whispered the Savitri mantra to him.

First, his mother gave him alms, and then all those who had gathered gave him countless alms. After instructing him in various rules of purity and conduct, the sage again honored the Brahmins with clothes, gold, and cows, as this great danger had passed. Vashistha took him into the assembly to the presence of Brahma. Brahma took water from his water pot to sanctify him and gave him a lotus that was always in bloom, which he held in his hand. Brahma then named him Brahmanaspati. Brihaspati, after worshipping him, named him Bharabhuti. Kubera gave him a necklace of gems to wear around his neck and, after worshipping him, named him Surananda. The Lord of the Waters (Varuna) gave him a noose and named him Sarvapriya. While the assembly of gods listened, Shankara also gave him a trident and a drum, naming him Virupaksha. He also gave him a crescent moon and named him Bhalachandra.

The mother of Rama (Kaushalya) gave the boy an axe and joyfully named him Parashuhasta. Again, worshipping him, she gave him a lion as an excellent vehicle and named him Simhavahana. She instructed him, "O Vinayaka, destroy the wicked quickly." The Ocean, in the form of a Brahmin, gave him a garland of pearls and, after worshipping him, named him Maladhara. Shesha requested to be his seat, and he joyfully named himself Phanirajasana. Agni gave him the power of burning and the name Dhananjaya. Vayu gave him great strength and the name Prabhanjana. Thus, everyone gave him names according to their power.

No one, O sage, could explain the power of these names. However, Indra, deluded by pride, did not worship him, nor did he give him any auspicious gift. "I am the Lord of the gods, worshipped by the three worlds, elderly, an eater of nectar, while he is a small child. I am the one who rides the elephant, worshipped by Hari and the trident-bearer; why should I bow to the son of Kashyapa?" Just as a lion does not eat grass, or the ocean does not beg for a puddle of water, the wish-fulfilling tree does not ask for anything from others, as it gives everything itself. Understanding his state of mind, Kashyapa, being meditative, spoke words of wisdom for his benefit: "Where there are wonderful deeds and where there are mines of virtues, that person is worthy of salutation and worship, even if he is a Brahmin without qualities."

Thus ends the tenth chapter of the Krida Khanda of the Ganesha Purana, titled "The Determination of Various Names."

Chapter 11 : The Breaking of Indra's Pride

Kashyapa said: This Supreme Being has descended into my house. He possesses indescribable qualities, is devoid of the three gunas, and remains unattached. Whoever opposes Him shall fall from their position. O God, listen as I recount His miraculous deeds. The fierce demoness Viraja came to kill Him, but she was slain by this child and fell, spanning two yojanas. The demons Uddhata and Dhundhu, both extremely arrogant, assumed the form of boars to kill Him. He grabbed them by their wings, smashed them against a rock, and they fell dead upon the earth, visible to all. Similarly, the Gandharva Citra, who had taken the form of a crocodile in the water, attained a divine body merely by His touch. To purify the essence of Haha, Huhu, and Tumburu, He took away their five-fold existence and became five-fold Himself. In the presence of all of you, five demons were slain. Hearing these words of the sage, Indra, the slayer of Vritra, spoke.

Indra said: I have not seen the excellence of His qualities; how can He be worthy of such honor?

Kashyapa said: Then He commanded Vayu to take Him into the sky. At His command, Vayu blew with the force of the end of the world, shaking all the worlds and causing the mountains to tremble violently. The sages were terrified, trembling and thinking, "Is this the untimely dissolution of the world?" Vayu, possessing great power, arrived to take the boy, but the boy’s seat did not move, nor did even a hair on Him stir. When Vayu failed, the slayer of Paka (Indra) commanded Agni, "Burn this boy quickly; your power must be seen today." Accepting the command, the fire-god approached Him, resembling the fire of dissolution, as if to burn the three worlds. Seeing Him burning everything—trees, drying up oceans, and scorching all people—the son of Kashyapa swallowed the fire instantly, just as a patient swallows a medicinal pill. Seeing the fire swallowed, Indra’s eyes turned red with anger. He looked at the boy with all his thousand eyes and saw that the boy possessed more than a thousand eyes.

He saw Him with countless heads and crowns, infinite ears, infinite hands and feet, and infinite, immense prowess. He had the moon, sun, and fire as His eyes, His crests covered the sky, His feet reached the seven netherworlds, and His head supported the seven worlds. He was as radiant as countless suns, served by countless Indras, possessed of countless hairs, and every pore of His body contained various universes. Just as countless figs grow on a fig tree, or countless mosquitoes swarm around a fig, similarly, in every single pore of His body, there were countless universes. Seeing this, the bewildered Indra entered into one of them. Inside, he saw the three worlds with all their moving and non-moving beings, just as one sees a fruit within the leaf of a banana plant. Indra saw countless worlds there and, with a confused mind, wandered about, unable to find an exit. Then, with his pride broken, he bowed his head and prayed to the Lord of Gods, the Elephant-faced One.

Indra said: The son of Kashyapa, born to remove the burden of the earth, has an inconceivable glory; how can I describe it? O Lord of Gods, grant me an exit from this vast womb. I have wandered for twelve years without seeing the end. In Your womb, I saw the fourteen worlds, divided place by place, each within a single pore. I saw Your forms, both aggregate and individual, vast, gentle, and otherwise. I saw Your countless miraculous faces and eyes, which are difficult to behold and cause agitation to the world. They are filled with demons, gods, humans, Yakshas, Rakshasas, and Pisachas. O Creator of the Universe, withdraw this terrifying, immense form. By Your grace, I have regained my memory and lost my delusion, O Lord of all. With body, mind, intellect, and speech, I have taken refuge in You. O Lover of Your devotees, show me Your natural form.

Kashyapa said: As he prayed, he suddenly saw the Brahmachari (the boy) standing in the middle of the assembly. In the sight of all the worlds, Indra bowed down to Him with his whole body, his mind filled with great astonishment, shame, and joy. While all the gods listened, he praised the Brahmachari who was born in the sage’s house, peaceful and playing in human form.

Indra said: I do not know You, the Infinite Power, the Supreme Lord, the Soul of the Universe, the Seed of the Universe, the Lord of Qualities. You are the manifestation of the universe, worshipped by the universe, the Three Truths, the One who takes three forms, the cause of birth, protection, and destruction. I bow to You, the One, the Eternal, the embodiment of Truth, Consciousness, and Bliss, the Overseer of all, the Lord beyond cause, the cause of movement in the stationary and the moving, the fulfiller of desires, the All-pervading. I praise You, the Lord of all, the treasure of all knowledge, the Soul of all, the light of all wisdom, the One beyond all, beyond speech and mind, the abode of all, the All-knowing.

Kashyapa said: Having praised and bowed to Him, Indra worshipped Him and offered Him his own goad, the wish-fulfilling tree, and two maidservants, explicitly naming Him Vinayaka. Indra then made this name, which grants all success upon remembrance. The sky and the earth were filled with shouts of victory, sounds of "Namah," various musical instruments, the songs of Gandharvas, the dances of Apsaras, and showers of flowers. The rivers, which were flowing in reverse, began to flow in their auspicious, natural direction. The directions became clear, and pleasant breezes blew. Fires burned with flames turning to the right, and everything became peaceful. Then, the pleased One, the Sole Monarch, granted fearlessness to Indra. "O Indra, you shall have no fear anywhere on the battlefield. Recite this hymn with devotion during the three junctures of the day. Any other person who recites these three verses with devotion shall attain all their desires and be victorious everywhere." Then, Indra bowed, received the auspicious boon, and all, having paid their respects, returned to their respective abodes with joy.

Thus ends the eleventh chapter of the Childhood Deeds in the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Removal of Indra's Pride."

Chapter 12 : The Slaying of the Night-Roamers

Kritayuga said: Then, in his seventh year, Vinayaka, having bathed and performed his daily rituals, appeared with his head shining with a crown. He, mounted on a lion, held four weapons: the goad, the axe, the lotus, and the noose, which removes all fears. He wore golden earrings set with gems, a loincloth of deer skin, and held a water pot and sacred grass, dressed in the finest yellow silk. On his forehead, he wore a musk mark and a crescent moon of waning brilliance; he wore a garland of pearls around his neck and the lord of serpents around his waist.

He roared playfully, shaking the heavens so loudly that the chataka birds opened their mouths, mistaking his voice for the rumble of clouds. Great rivers overflowed, drenching the sky, and both Aditi and Kashyapa rejoiced, praising themselves as blessed ancestors. At that moment, the King of Kashi arrived at the hermitage. They all greeted each other with embraces and sat down after offering mutual respects. The sage fed the King delicious food of six flavors, and after the King had rested, the sage asked the reason for his visit, saying, "O King, by good fortune we have seen you today. Why did you not inform me of the news regarding my priest?"

Kritayuga said: Hearing the sage’s words, the excellent King replied, "Forgive my offense, O Brahmin, for my mind was troubled by state affairs. My son’s wedding is approaching, and I have come to invite you, O Lord. I am fulfilled by your sight. Please come quickly to perform the wedding, for without your presence, it would not be esteemed among the people. I have come myself to take you, O great sage."

The Sage said: "I will not come, O best of kings, as I am currently engaged in the four-month vow. If you wish, take my capable son instead."

The King said: "Command your son, O sage, and we shall depart together."

Hearing the King’s request, the sage spoke to his son, "At the King’s insistence, I send you, O Vinayaka. Though it is a great sorrow for me to be separated from you, accept this command upon your head." After bowing to his parents' feet, he went outside, where the King had provided a chariot. He bowed to them again and climbed into the chariot. Aditi approached and said to the King, "O King, this child of mine must be protected constantly. Calamities arise wherever my child is, so he must be protected with effort, like the pupil of an eye. Just as you take my son, bring him back in the same way." The King said "Om," bowed to Aditi, and left.

He traveled quickly with him in a chariot as swift as the wind. As they traveled along the path, they reached a great forest, the beautiful abode of the uncle of Narantaka, a powerful demon named Dhumraksha, the brother of Raudraketu. He had performed severe penance for ten thousand years, constantly worshipping the thousand-rayed sun with joy, desiring a supreme weapon that could destroy everything and wishing to bring the three worlds under his control. Hanging by his feet from a tree branch, facing downward, and consuming only smoke, he spent a long time until the infallible weapon was granted to him by the Sun. Vinayaka saw the weapon's brilliance, which filled the sky, and quickly flew up and grabbed it like Garuda seizing a serpent.

The noble King of Kashi was astonished and pondered the gain, the loss, and the life, thinking that nothing happens in the three worlds without divine will. He had never before seen such a sudden acquisition. To test the power of the weapon, he weighed it and threw it. It went upward, made a terrifying sound, and fell upon Dhumraksha, who was instantly split in two. The boulders and trees were crushed by the two halves, and the earth was covered for five hundred bow-lengths. Then his two famous sons, Jaghana and Manu, who were devoted to serving him, saw their father in that state. Filled with rage, they saw Vinayaka nearby and rushed toward him with mouths wide open like the god of death. "How have you killed our father, you boy?" the two enraged night-roamers shouted at the King of Kashi. "You were protected by my father from Narantaka before; how are you still alive, O King, after killing him?"

Kritayuga said: Hearing these words, the King trembled, overwhelmed with fear, and wondered why he had brought this son of Kashyapa, who stood there like a demon. "If Narantaka gets angry, he will take my kingdom by force. Who will protect me then?" He began to swear, "O night-roamers, I did not bring this child here for this purpose. I swear by the Brahmin and the Lord that this is the priest's son, brought here for the wedding. Do not create obstacles; take the boy away."

As he said this, the sage's son said to him, "How can you hand me over to the enemy after binding me? What answer will you give to Aditi or Kashyapa? If Kashyapa is angered, he will surely turn you to ash." As the sage's son spoke, the two demons rushed to eat him like cats after a mouse. The boy opened his mouth and made a terrifying sound. The three worlds trembled, and by the force of his breath, the two demons were blown away like straw in a gale. Within two moments, they fell in the city of Narantaka, like boulders blown by a great wind onto a mountain peak. Their falling bodies crushed houses, and there was a great outcry of "Alas! Alas!" as messengers ran about.

Hearing that the sons of Dhumraksha were dead, they looked carefully and, seeing them still alive, they revived them. The two demons told the messengers the whole story: the killing of their father by the son of Kashyapa, his fall due to the boy's breath, and that he was traveling in a chariot with the King of Kashi. Hearing this, the messengers told Narantaka. Hearing that his uncle had been killed, Narantaka, with eyes reddened by anger, looked at the night-roamers and ordered a thousand of them to go and bring the sage's son. "If the sage's son resists, kill him. And the King of Kashi's chariot, too—go quickly, demons!"

At the mere command, they went with the speed of the wind. They saw the son of Kashyapa and the King. Then Vinayaka made a terrifying, deep sound. The night-roamers fell; some lost their lives, some fled, some had their legs broken, some had their heads severed, some had their bellies torn by arrows, and others had their faces, eyes, thighs, and arms broken. Those who fled returned to Narantaka and told him everything that Vinayaka had done.

Chapter 13 : The Slaying of Vighanta and Dantura

The messengers said: "O Lord, after receiving your command, we went to Vinayaka, who was seated on a chariot. Everyone saw him then, and he appeared like the God of Death himself. O sinless one, all the demons were killed due to fear of him. By your grace, we survived and have returned to you. Through divine grace, he is like a lion among a herd of elephants; there is no man in the three worlds who can fight him."

K (the demon king) said: "Having heard these words, the killer of men spoke: 'Why do you speak out of foolishness? Where is the child and where is the killer of men? What can a moth do in the presence of the fire of dissolution? Can a digging mouse bring down Mount Meru?'"

He then ordered the demons to go to the city of Kashi and plunder it so that the King would become distressed. "If he is distressed, then the son of Kashyapa will also be affected. If not, both must be killed by all of you with all your might." He then gave them many precious jewels, exquisite garments, and various weapons. They bowed to him and went to the city of Kashi, spreading out across the horizon with their army.

The commander of the army ordered: "Whoever the demons see, let them be killed. Otherwise, they will be punished by me, and I will take their lives." Thus commanded, the demons went in all ten directions.

Vinayaka, accompanied by the King on his chariot, went to the city, which was decorated with various flags, banners, and garlands. With the sound of drums and various offerings, the ministers and citizens went to welcome Vinayaka. Everyone worshipped him with devotion using the sixteen offerings. The King then entered the city with everyone.

When he entered, all the women climbed onto the balconies. Some rushed out to see him, their ornaments and clothes in disarray. Ignoring their fathers, brothers, husbands, mothers, and friends, some even left their food plates half-eaten. One woman, restrained by all, meditated on Lord Vinayaka, closed her eyes with devotion, and gave up her life—it was a miracle.

Young maidens showered him with parched grain and flowers. The gods, residing in their celestial chariots, witnessed this grand festival. The Brahmins saw Vinayaka as the Supreme Soul; the Kshatriyas saw him as a great warrior eager for battle; the Vaishyas saw him as Rudra, the destroyer; and the Shudras saw him in the form of Hari and a king. As one sees a crystal in different colors—red, white, or yellow—so he appeared according to the inner state of each person. Just as one man is seen as a father, brother, or brother-in-law by different people, so Vinayaka appeared.

In the middle of the city, he saw two great demons, Vighanta and Dantura, and invited them to play with respect. The two demons, evil-minded, approached the child. When they tried to embrace him, he realized their wicked intentions. He embraced them and crushed them as easily as a flower in one's hand. When they were thrown to the ground, they appeared ten yojanas long. The King of Kashi and the people were amazed.

The gods in the sky showered flowers, and some shouted, "Well done! Well done!" accompanied by shouts of victory. The sages and gods praised Vinayaka, who was moving in the form of a human child as a divine play. Those who were unconquerable even by Indra and others were crushed by him.

Then the chariot moved forward, passing through the streets. Two other demons, Patanga and Vidhula, who were like storms and possessed great strength, came to kill him. The people were distressed, covered in dust; palaces and trees fell to the ground. Clothes flew in the sky like birds, and turbans fell from people's heads in all directions. There was a great uproar, and nothing could be understood. Even the chariot, which was ready to go to the sky, was stopped by them. People fell down as if they were newborn infants. Seeing them as extremely powerful, he grabbed them both by their hair. With one fist, the Lord whirled them around for a long time and threw them to the ground. The people saw that they were dead.

They speculated, "This child is surely the powerful son of Kashyapa, for he has killed these demons who were a yojana long. Such strength is not seen in anyone at this age." Seeing this power, the King of Kashi was also delighted. He jumped down from the chariot and bowed to Vinayaka, saying, "O Great Yogi, this is not the work of a mere child. How can those with physical eyes understand the knowledge of Brahma and others? You are the creator of the world, appearing in many forms for the sake of the earth. There is no limit to your incarnations, O Lord."

Then the chariot moved quickly to the King's palace. There, the child saw a demon in the form of a stone. He struck it with an axe, and it shattered into a hundred pieces. From it emerged a terrifying, huge, tawny-colored man with protruding teeth and a long beard. Then all the children and other people ran away. He struck him with his fist, and he fell to the ground.

The people, who had not understood the child to be the Lord in human form, were amazed. The King, with a happy heart, got down from the chariot, took the child, and quickly brought him inside. He seated him on his own throne made of jewels and gold. He worshipped him according to the rules with sixteen offerings, using precious clothes, ornaments, and divine fragrances. Filled with supreme joy, he praised and bowed to him. The King fed him and his friends various types of food, including six-flavored dishes and many delicacies. Then the King gave them various fruits and betel leaves adorned with gold and jewels.

Then, surrounded by the children, the King ate himself. After the child had performed his evening prayers, the King put him to sleep on a beautiful bed decorated with lamps and canopies. By his command, the King slept with his beloved. He placed four trusted guards to keep watch nearby.

Thus ends the thirteenth chapter of the Childhood Deeds in the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Killing of Vighanta and Dantura."

Chapter 14 : The Slaying of the Demoness Jrimbha

Then, rising at dawn, he performed his morning ablutions and rituals according to the prescribed methods. He performed the fire sacrifice (Homa), placed the sacred kusha grass and the auspicious black antelope skin, took up his staff, and played with the other children, that Vinayaka.

Hearing of his fame, a Brahmin named Dharmadatta, who was well-versed in the Vedas and scriptures, arrived at the palace of the King of Kashi, desiring to see the son of Kashyapa. Honored by the King, he asked the noble monarch, "Tell me, where is that mighty son of Kashyapa?" The people replied, "He is playing there with the children."

The Brahmin approached, took him by the hand, and said, "You are the son of my friend, and I have heard of your glory. I have come to take you to my home, without a doubt. Purify us with the dust of your feet and fulfill all our desires. You are the form of the Supreme Brahman, the Supreme Soul, the highest of the high. You have taken birth in the house of Kashyapa to relieve the burden of the earth. I know you in truth, O child, though you appear in human form for the sake of play."

Vinayaka replied, "Why have you come here? Why would I not come to your side at your mere command?" Saying this, he departed quickly, following Dharmadatta.

As he walked along the path, followed by the children who raised dust with their feet, two wicked demons named Kama and Krodha, who were like death to men, approached, fighting with each other to kill him. Like moths falling into a fire, these two evil-minded demons, in the form of wild boars, fell before the child. All the children fled in ten directions. Then Vinayaka, grabbing their feet with force, whirled them around many times and smashed them onto the ground. They fell to the earth, lifeless, and the people looked on. The three worlds trembled at the sound of their falling bodies. Dharmadatta gained faith in the reports of the people, and having witnessed this great deed with his own eyes, the great sage departed, leading the way with him.

He then saw a mad, powerful elephant, hidden in form, running here and there, ready to kill people. Great warriors fled in all directions, and there was a great uproar among the running crowd. The sky was covered with dust as if by mist over mountains. The powerful elephant went to attack the King’s own mad elephants. Those in the palace watched the terrible elephant battle. The elephant broke into the stables and saw the horses tied there. The horses broke their tethers and fled in all directions.

Dharmadatta, taking the child, tried to head home. But the boy, slapping his hands, forcibly climbed onto the elephant. Vinayaka repeatedly pierced the elephant's temple with a goad. A stream of blood fell to the ground, and the elephant let out a roar that terrified all the people. As it fell, it crushed countless houses and all their contents. The entire earth, with its mountains, forests, and mines, shook. When the darkness cleared, the people of the city arrived. Seeing the elephant dead, they were terrified, but they approached the powerful child and forcibly took him down.

Dharmadatta carried him home in a basket, fearing further disturbances. Once the situation was resolved, he gave wealth to the Brahmins and worshipped the child with various offerings—clothes, ornaments, flowers, and many kinds of food. He fed him according to the rites and presented him with Siddhi and Buddhi. For the sake of the offering (dakshina), Dharmadatta bowed down to the ground like a rod and said with a joyful heart and a voice choked with emotion, "My fortune has borne fruit, for I have seen your lotus feet. You are the Lord of the Universe, the Creator, the Witness, and the Guru of the world. You have come here at my request to deliver my ancestors."

While he was speaking, the sage seated him on his throne. Vinayaka, the Divine, was worshipped with great devotion. The child shone brilliantly, accompanied by Siddhi and Buddhi, just as the three-eyed Shiva shines with Ganga and Gauri.

Meanwhile, an evil woman named Jrumbha arrived. Clad in yellow garments, wearing beautiful bracelets and ornaments, she was the wicked-minded wife of the demon Dhumraksha. She spoke sweet words, "This is a great treasure. Such misfortunes arise, but they are destroyed by effort. How can the life of his parents be pleasant?" Saying this to the women, she addressed Vinayaka, "It is my great fortune, O Lord, that I have seen you. You are tired, O Lord of Gods, from destroying so many wicked ones. Let me massage your body with fragrant oil."

When he said "Om," she approached with poison in her hands, applying it to his feet. The people praised her, thinking her to be good, but she was wicked and served him with evil intent, just as she did her husband. Then, a great burning sensation arose in his body. Realizing her evil nature through his divine knowledge, he struck her on the head with a coconut. She fell to the ground, assuming her true form. A stream of blood flowed from her, spreading for two yojanas. Dharmadatta and the people were filled with wonder.

He worshipped him again and fed him various delicious foods seasoned with the six tastes. At this moment, the gods rained flowers. Women performed the waving of lights (nirajana), showered parched grain, and offered worship. Then the King of Kashi came to invite him. Placing him on a chariot amidst the sound of musical instruments, with Gandharvas singing and Apsaras dancing in front, the King led the unharmed Vinayaka to his palace.

As he went, the child appeared like Indra, mounted on a lion, holding weapons, and accompanied by many warriors. He shone with Siddhi and Buddhi, like Shiva with Ganga and Uma. Praised by many bards, he entered the royal palace.

Whoever listens to this childhood pastime of the Lord of the Universe will attain all their desires and will never be troubled by enemies.

Thus ends the fourteenth chapter of the Childhood Pastimes in the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Slaying of Jrumbha."

Chapter 15 : The Liberation of the City

Ka said: The next day, the King went to the assembly hall with his people, accompanied by Vinayaka and his ministers. He spoke to them all about the many virtues of the child, and having honored that virtuous and noble-minded boy, he said, "I went to Kashyapa to invite him, and for the success of the marriage, this son was brought here. Since he arrived, I have neither seen nor heard of such a group of amazing demons and calamities. First, he killed the demon king Dhumraksha, and then he slew Manu along with five hundred others. He killed Dantura, who had carried him into the sky, as well as Vighanta, the child-formed Vidhula of great strength, and Patanga, who had assumed the form of wind in the city. He also killed Kuta, who stood at the gate in the form of a stone, as well as Kama and Krodha, who had assumed the forms of donkeys, and the night-wanderer Kunda, who had taken the form of an elephant. Whatever other calamities remain, the child will destroy them.
