Now, a decision must be made with the Brahmins regarding the marriage, the installation of deities, the turmeric pavilion, and the requirements for the wedding, as advised by the experts in astrology."

Ka said: Having heard these words, the ministers said to the King, "O best of kings, as long as this child remains in the city, it is our firm belief that the marriage cannot take place. While he is here, great disasters will occur day after day. You should perform the marriage after a fortnight or a month." The King agreed, and everyone went to their homes. The King and Vinayaka ate and slept peacefully. Then, at midnight, when everyone was asleep, three demons named Jvalamukha, Vyaghramukha, and Daruna, wishing to avenge the demons previously killed, arrived. The first demon wanted to burn the entire city, and Daruna, in the form of wind, prepared to assist him. The tiger-faced one prepared to devour those who were fleeing. Having decided this, they roared with a great sound. The three worlds trembled, and everyone was filled with doubt, wondering if the dissolution of the universe or an enemy invasion had arrived.

The tiger-faced demon, with a huge body, placed his lower lip on the ground and his upper lip in the sky, with his tongue appearing like a river in the middle. He spewed flames from his mouth, burning the city in the sky. Just as the city was burned by Hanuman with his tail-fire, this wicked one burned the city with the fire from his mouth. Trees, gardens, and rows of palaces were consumed. There was a great uproar of voices and hands as people abandoned all their tasks and fled in all directions. The tiger-faced demon devoured anyone coming out of the city; that was his food, and the children were his snacks. Some fled with their clothes disheveled, others naked. Some women fled with only their bodices, others with only their lower garments. Thinking the tiger-faced one’s mouth was a cave, some entered it in their desire to live, and the demon ate them as they called out to others.

When this untimely dissolution arrived, the King of Kashi, abandoning his kingdom, his wife, his children, and all his possessions, carried Vinayaka on his shoulders and wandered from place to place. Tormented by great sorrow and exhausted by the heat, he thought, "Why did I bring this child who causes all these calamities? Through my foolishness, he has become the cause of all this misfortune and the destroyer of all my wealth. Without him, what would I say to Aditi and Kashyapa? He has already destroyed the previous great calamities, but now he remains silent, and I do not know the reason for this." While lamenting thus, he climbed a high mountain with him. Even there, the fire and the wind followed him. Servants poured water from earthen pots. The wives, abandoning their modesty, fled outside with their children. The King, overwhelmed, descended and came down. All the horses, mules, heroes, chariots, elephants, infantry, and citizens with their cattle fled the city. Vinayaka searched for the King of Kashi, and the King of Kashi searched for Vinayaka while the destruction of the people was occurring.

While all the people were in the midst of this, the tiger-faced demon did not close his mouth. As long as the divine Vinayaka, the great soul of infinite virtues, did not enter his mouth, the demon kept it open. When the sun rose, Vinayaka saw the tiger-faced demon, the burning city, and the mouth full of people. Seeing the fire nearby, he forcibly entered that mouth. Once he had entered, the demon closed his mouth. The mouth, resembling a cavern, was intended to consume everything, but the child grew immensely at that very moment. He tore apart the demon's body, and the sky echoed with a cracking sound like that of splitting bamboos. The demon was split in two and died instantly. He threw one piece into the sky, which was carried by the wind and fell in a distant land, crushing a dense forest. The other piece became a playhouse that brought joy to the child.

The people then rose, awakening others. Vinayaka then drank all the fire and crushed the demon with the strike of his feet, along with the fire-demon. Having thus killed those wicked ones by the power of his Yoga Maya, he brought all the dead back to life and restored the city as it was before. Vinayaka roared like a lion. All the people and the King praised him with joy: "O Lord of infinite power, O Master of virtues, obeisance to you, the destroyer of calamities, the creator of the universe. Obeisance to the container of the world, the destroyer of obstacles, the giver of knowledge, and the destroyer of ignorance. O Lord of the world, you have saved us from the untimely dissolution caused by the fire-demon, and you have protected us from the demons through your play. Who else has the strength to drink such a powerful fire? Who else could kill such a great demon with a face like the globe? Who else but you would dare to bring the dead back to life? You are the mother, O Lord, you are the father and the protector. Great is the fortune of us people that we have attained your presence."

Having said this, Vinayaka mounted a horse and a lion-chariot. All the heroes, numbering in the thousands, mounted their respective vehicles. They went to the palace with joy and performed their daily rituals. All the citizens, now free from illness, went to their own homes. As all the musical instruments played, the bards praised the King. The King of Kashi gave many gifts to the Brahmins, and they blessed him and worshipped the gods and Vinayaka. People from various places gave gifts to the Lord, and Vinayaka gave them to the Brahmins as he pleased. The King gave garments to the ministers and heroes. Having dismissed everyone and eaten, they both went to sleep happily. Whoever listens to this "Liberation of the City" with devotion attains all their desires and is never troubled by calamities.

Chapter 16 : The King's Return in the Childhood Pastimes

Kritayuga said: Listen, O Vyasa, I shall narrate a wondrous story, by hearing which with devotion, a human being attains supreme happiness.

Rising at dawn, the King performed his daily duties and went out. He visited the temple of Vinayaka, bowed to Him, and worshipped Him. He then prayed to the child Vinayaka for food. Vinayaka then gave him the remains of His meal, which was satisfying and consisted of various delicacies. The King saw there laddus, payasam (milk pudding), cakes, vatakas, rice with soup, curds, milk, honey, ghee, and many vessels of side dishes. He also saw necklaces of pearls and gems and various ornaments on His body, all priceless and new. Bowing again, the King asked Vinayaka, "O Lord of the Universe, by which devotee was this worship performed? I wish to know and see him by your grace."

Being asked thus by the King, Vinayaka said, "I shall tell you, O King, about the offering made by My devotee, the King of Kashi."

The Deity said: Listen, O King, I shall tell you briefly what you have asked. In the Dandaka forest, in a city named Amala, there lived a devotee named Bhrushundi, who knew the past, present, and future. Brahma, Shiva, and Vishnu, who are capable of creating, sustaining, and destroying the three worlds, always desire to see him. He is a master of his senses, and his sins are destroyed by constant meditation on Me. Long ago, when he was performing penance, a trunk (shunda) emerged from between his eyebrows. Thus, he became famous in the three worlds as Bhrushundi, and through his intense devotion, he attained a form similar to Mine in the mortal world. Today, on the Shukla Chaturthi, he worshipped Me with joy. Look, O King, at the ghee, milk, curds, and honey flowing from My body, and this leftover food you see is what he offered.

Kritayuga said: Then the King of Kashi said, "O Lord of Lords, Lord of the Universe, I wish to see him. How will he come here?"

Vinayaka said: Go today to his hermitage, O King. Worship him, bow to him, and invite him for the marriage. Request him with effort and convey My message: "O Sage, Vinayaka has come to my house and is highly satisfied with your worship and devotion. He is also longing to see you, which is why He has sent us. I also have a great desire to see you and have you worship Him. Please come and make my home and my wealth fruitful." When you say this, O King, and he hears My name, My devotee Bhrushundi will come immediately.

Kritayuga said: Being told this, the King worshipped Him and departed, carrying his bow and quiver, riding a swift horse. Crossing rivers, forests, and mountains, the King reached his hermitage and dismounted. Seeing the sage Bhrushundi, he bowed down. The sage honored him, and the King worshipped the sage with joy. At the sage's command, the King sat down and looked at the great forest; the hermitage was beautiful with lakes, trees, creepers, flowers, and fruits. Not even Kailasa, Vishnuloka, or Satyaloka were like it; it was resplendent with the chanting of Vedas, Shastras, music, and dance. Seeing the hermitage, with its sacrificial fires, ponds, and birds, the King was filled with joy. He prostrated like a stick and told the sage everything—the marriage, the worship, and the command of Vinayaka—and prayed for him to come to his house.

The Sage said: Who are you, O Great King, and who is this Vinayaka?

The King said: Know me as the King of Kashi, born in the solar dynasty. Vinayaka, who is your deity, is the son of Kashyapa. Desiring to feed Him, I heard of your glory and worshipped Him well on Chaturthi with various offerings. He showed me those offerings in my city, being satisfied, and said, "O King, that sage will come at My name." Therefore, I have come to invite you, O Great Sage, considering your sight rare for mortal eyes.

Kritayuga said: Hearing the King, Bhrushundi was filled with wonder and said, "Is this true or false? He who is beyond the reach of the minds of those who know the Vedas and Vedanta, how could He stay in your house? I doubt this. Thirty-three crore gods have come to see Him; how could He say 'I will go' at your word? If that God is staying in your house, O King, describe His form, and then I will go to your house."

The King said: Even Brahma is unable to count His infinite forms, O Brahmana. He has now descended into the house of Kashyapa, O Sage, and is famous in the three worlds by the name Vinayaka. He has a wondrous form of a seven-year-old boy and performs great, miraculous deeds. I shall describe the form in which the Lord of Lords dwells in my house as a Brahmachari. When He descended, He was a four-armed deity with divine radiance, wearing divine garlands, garments, and ornaments, holding divine weapons, and anointed with divine fragrances. When requested by Kashyapa, He became a natural child.

The Sage said: This is not the God who is the object of my meditation, O best of Kings. By whose power this trunk (shunda) grew on me, which is rare even for the divine sages, I will go wherever He calls me.

Kritayuga said: Hearing the sage's words, the King was filled with anxiety and said, "My fortune or misfortune has arrived. Hoping for you, I crossed terrible mountains and forests to reach here and have attained your great sight. I have no power to force you to come to my house, O Lord." Then the sage, out of compassion, placed his hand on the King's head. "Close your eyes, O King," he said, and the King did so. Opening his eyes, the King saw himself back in his own house in a moment by the sage's grace. He told Vinayaka the whole story—that the sage did not come despite his efforts, as he was not the one he meditated upon. Forcing a Brahmana would turn everything to ashes.

Thus ends the sixteenth chapter of the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The King's Return."

Chapter 17 : The Meeting of Bhrushundi and Gajanana

Ka said: Vinayaka, who is omniscient, asked the king, who is like a tiger among men, with a voice as deep as a cloud and with great respect: O King, tell me what you said to him and what he replied to you. After hearing everything, I will tell you my opinion.

The King said: I told him your words and prayed to him a lot. The sage asked, "Who are you, and who is this Vinayaka?" He said he would only come to see you if you appeared in your worshipable form. Being thus rejected by him, he sent me back to my house while I was lost in thought about the path. He placed his hand on my head, and I, who had my eyes closed, opened them in a moment. O God, I saw you standing before me in my own house. Remembering his hermitage, joy arose in my heart.

Hearing these words, Vinayaka laughed. Vinayaka said: O King, you are tired; go back to that hermitage. When he hears my name, Gajanana, the sage will come. As Vinayaka, who is the embodiment of all forms, was saying this, the King saw Bhrushundi standing in his own hermitage. Seeing the King again, the sage pondered in his heart: Why has the King returned, and what will he say? Then the King, having bowed down, said to that best of sages: O Brahmin, your master Gajanana is calling you. Upon hearing just the name, the sage rejoiced and fainted. His body became thrilled with ecstasy, and he shed tears of joy. He said, "If I had wings, I would have flown there."

With such eagerness, the Brahmin set out with the King. As Bhrushundi moved, the earth trembled, though the sage himself was steady. The earth, becoming face-to-face with him, trembled and said, "Protect me, O great sage; I am not afraid of you, but I cannot bear your weight." The best of Brahmins said, "I am going in haste to see my master, Gajanana." Having said this, he moved forward. Upon taking the third step, he revealed the city. Seeing the city, the King joyfully said to the best of sages: By your grace, I have reached this city quickly. O sage, the glory of those who perform penance and chant is difficult to know. Having said this, the King quickly led him to his house. After seating Bhrushundi on a golden throne, he worshipped him with great devotion using water for the feet, offerings, and a seat.

The Sage said: O King, I have been deceived; I do not see Gajanana here. Show him to me, or else I will curse you and return to my hermitage.

The King said: Gajanana is playing like a child among children. He looks just like a hero covered in dust. The sage saw him adorned with a trunk, shining like ten million suns, two-armed, and elephant-faced. He said to the King in anger: How can I bow to him? Shame on the great person who bows to someone so small. How can a lion, which tears apart the temples of elephants, eat grass?

Ka said: The child heard the sage's words. Being a divine being who assumes forms for his pastimes, he was filled with curiosity. Vinayaka said: O Bhrushundi, tell me now, what kind of master do you want?

The Sage said: One who wears divine garments, has ten arms, is adorned with a garland of pearls, is accompanied by Siddhi and Buddhi, and is decorated with earrings. One who has a trunk, large ears, is smeared with vermilion, has a great navel adorned with a serpent, and has tinkling anklets on his feet. One who is rich in the beauty of a great crown, has ten weapons in his hands, has one tusk, a moon on his forehead, is adorned with small bells, rides a peacock, and whose feet are worshipped by the host of gods.

Ka said: Hearing those words, the child, sitting in the lotus position, himself assumed that form, the creator, protector, and destroyer of the world. Seeing his worshipable Gajanana, Bhrushundi rolled on the ground with love, indifferent to everything else. He danced in supreme bliss, his body thrilled. Attaining a state of divine consciousness, he bowed down according to the rules and worshipped him with various offerings. The Supreme Lord rejoiced, embracing him with his ten arms. Seeing them both with tears in their eyes, the King also became emotional. The King said: The great merits of my previous births have borne fruit. Today I have seen the wonderful happiness born of devotion to God. Both bowed down and worshipped each other according to the rules. Sitting on the great throne, they spoke to each other.

Gajanana said: I knew your devotion, and the King also informed me of it. That is why I assumed this form adorned with ten arms. O sage, as other people meditate on me and worship me, I assume those forms and grant them their desires. He does not worship with faith in the world. The earth, oppressed by the burden of the wicked, went to the realm of truth and took refuge in Brahma, who then enlightened me. By the boon of the Sun, I was born as the son of Kashyapa. I will remove the burden of the earth, establish Indra and others in their positions, and destroy the demons in many ways. Seeing your intense penance, I became like this. After killing the destroyer of the gods along with his brother, I will return to my own abode.

The Sage said: Having seen your pair of feet, which bring happiness to all worlds, remove all suffering, are worshipped by gods, and grant all desires, O Lord, I have become fulfilled and purified. O Soul of the Universe, grant me a boon so that I may be satisfied. Whenever I meditate on you in this form, O remover of obstacles, may you be manifest before me, O ocean of compassion. May your name, "Asha-puraka" (Fulfiller of Hopes), become famous. Gajanana said: Whenever you desire, I will come to your side. This name, given by you out of devotion, will become famous.

Ka said: Hearing these two boons, the sage bowed down with joy. As the sage bowed, Gajanana spoke again, placing his hand on the head of that Brahmin: O Brahmin, whatever you desire will be fulfilled. And, O sage, you will always have my remembrance.

Ka said: Having said this, Vinayaka played in his child form. The sage also sat in the lotus position and entered into meditation. All the people were filled with wonder, seeing the pastimes of the Supreme Lord in his child form. The King of Kashi said: I am blessed on this earth; I have obtained the vision of him who is difficult to attain even for Brahma and others. Holding that child by the hand, he joyfully took him inside the house. He fed him delicious food and put him to sleep as before. Having received his command, the King himself slept as before.

Thus ends the seventeenth chapter of the Krida Khanda of the Sri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Meeting of Bhrushundi and Gajanana."

Chapter 18 : The Killing of the Deceptive Demon

Vyasa said: O Lord of the people, tell me what happened on the next day. I am never satisfied listening to these stories, so please narrate them to me.

K (the narrator) said: Listen carefully, O Brahmin, as I narrate to you briefly the divine and sin-destroying story performed by Vinayaka. When the sun rose, the King, after completing his daily rituals, went to his throne to play with the child.

At that time, a demon appeared in the guise of a Brahmin, an expert in astrology. He held a palm-leaf manuscript in his left hand and a rosary in his right. He wore a saffron garment, a large turban, and twelve marks of Gopi-chandan on his body. With a very long beard, he approached the King. As he drew near, the King stood up and bowed to him. The King offered him a seat, asked about his well-being, and inquired about the purpose of his visit. The King asked, "O best of Brahmins, what is your name? What is your knowledge and your penance? O ocean of mercy, please tell me the truth."

The demon, thus questioned, gave his blessings and spoke in order. "O Prince, my name is Hemajyotirvid. I have come from the Gandharva realm to live under your protection. I know the past, the present, and the future, and I can provide remedies for disturbances. I will answer any questions you ask, O sinless one. I have come because I know of the misfortunes that have occurred here and the evil omens that are yet to come. I desire to live under your protection, O best of kings."

The King asked, "O great sage, what is the cause of the misfortunes here? Tell me truly what is going to happen in the future. If I am convinced of your words, I will provide you with a residence and a livelihood."

The astrologer said, "As long as Vinayaka, the son of Kashyapa, remains in your house, obstacles will arise because he is the Lord of Obstacles. O Prince, leave him in the forest and go away. Then there will be no obstacles in your city or house. If he stays, the water will flood the entire city. If he is somehow destroyed, the mountains, moved by the wind, will crush the city; there is no doubt about this. How do you not know this? There were no such troubles before. A king should not trust a deceitful, greedy, and impure person. Those who are overly heroic and cunning, looking only for their own gain, destroy kings out of a desire for the kingdom. I have come to tell you this, knowing that you are about to lose your kingdom. A wise person must tell the King what is beneficial or harmful. Consider my words and do as you wish."

K said: Hearing these words, the King replied to him. The King said, "O sage, you have spoken of the past and the future, but to me, it seems as false as the illusions of the world. If I am convinced by your words, I will give you your reward. You have not recognized this child, O best of astrologers. If he wishes, he can create many universes, and he can create Brahma, Vishnu, and Shiva. If you are certain of your words, O best of Brahmins, then take him to the deep, terrible forest, leave him there, and come back. He has destroyed many powerful enemies who were hateful to the world. How could I not know that he will protect those who do not hate him? We are not capable of thinking ill of him. By him, the city and the kingdom have been saved from many calamities. He makes the powerless powerful and the powerful powerless; he makes the low high and the high low.

Hearing the King's words, the astrologer became red with anger. He began to speak to the King, looking down. "I have spoken for your benefit, but it seems like an evil to you. No one can ever transgress the creator's design. O King, show me this child and his features. I will tell you everything."

The King then called all the children. Vinayaka came forward, and the others followed him. They bowed to the astrologer and asked where he had come from. "You are an expert in palmistry and astrology, a knower of the past, present, and future. Tell me the fruit of my destiny."

K said: Hearing the child's words, the deceitful Brahmin pondered in his mind how he could succeed. He thought, "He has killed many powerful demons of various forms." Taking the child's hand, he began to predict his fortune. "In four days, you will fall into a well. If you somehow escape, you will drown in the ocean. If you survive that, you will be engulfed in darkness. If you survive that, a mountain will fall upon you. Two great beings of time will devour you while you are alive. These misfortunes will surely happen to you. I will tell you the remedy; do it to be fearless. Come with me for four days, and I will bring you back. I swear this by my feet."

Hearing these words, the child went inside slowly. He took a diamond ring from the King's hand and, holding it in his own hand, said to the Brahmin, "O astrologer, tell us quickly about a lost object. Who took it and when was it found? If you can tell me this, I will be convinced."

Asked thus by the child, the Brahmin, in the assembly of people, said with a smiling face, "If the ring is found, and if it is given to me, then I will tell you." When the child agreed, saying, "It is in your hand," the Brahmin was exposed. The child, with that enchanted ring, struck him in the heart in an instant.

His heart pierced by the ring, he fell to the ground like a mountain struck by a thunderbolt, shaking the earth violently. As his body fell, a portion of the city was crushed. The King of Kashi and all the people were astonished. All the gods rejoiced and showered flowers. The heavens and the earth were filled with the sounds of celestial music.

The people said, "How did he know this wicked demon was in the guise of a Brahmin? By divine grace, the King did not follow the words of that evil soul. This child should not be considered an ordinary being; he is the Lord who has descended to remove the burden of the earth, the ocean of mercy, born in the house of Kashyapa. How did the powerful demon die just by the strike of a ring?" Saying this, they all worshipped, bowed, and sang his praises.

The lotus-eyed King gave many gifts to the Brahmins, bards, and the poor. After honoring the citizens, he dismissed the assembly. The child went to play with the other children as if he were ignorant.

Thus ends the eighteenth chapter of the Childhood Pastimes in the Krida Khanda of the Ganesha Purana, titled "The Killing of the Deceptive Demon."

Chapter 19 : The Killing of Kupa and Kandara

K said: Hearing that the Brahmin who had assumed a human form had been killed by the boy, the great demon Narantaka sent the powerful demons Kupa and Kandara, who had obtained boons from Brahma, after giving them many garments and various jewels.

The demon said: Go at an auspicious time and kill that boy quickly. Take revenge for those who were killed by various means.

K said: Thus commanded by him, the two demons, Kupa and Kandara, set out with a four-fold army for a distance of two miles. Having dismissed the army, they went ahead, feeling very joyful. On the way, they devised a plan: "I shall become a well, and you shall become a boy. You should play and push him into me with effort. I, in the form of a frog inside the well, will devour him instantly. We shall then disappear after accomplishing this great task." Having decided this, they reached the great city of the King of Kashi. A great well appeared in the king's courtyard, and Kandara took the form of a boy and set his mind on playing with the other children.

The king saw the well full of water and rejoiced. As he looked inside, he saw a frog. It was making a sweet sound, was yellow in color, and looked terrifying. Kandara, who was among the boys, said to Vinayaka, "Come out, O mighty one, and see this beautiful well." Vinayaka, having come out, saw the well with the boys. Seeing that the well was very beautiful, he played with the children in various ways and in different places. At midday, while in the water, they splashed each other and made one another dive and emerge. They jumped into the water from a distance. After playing there, they prepared to go home.

Just then, Vinayaka saw his own reflection in the water. Knowing how to swim, he remained outside, watching everyone. Suddenly, the wicked Kandara pushed him into the water. Knowing the water was deep, he played around in it with ease. Like a submarine fire in the ocean of milk, he moved up and down in a moment. Assuming his illusory power, the Gana (Vinayaka) began to submerge. The child threw his hands and feet in many ways. The other boys were terrified, but Kandara forcibly grabbed him and took him to the bottom of the well. He forcibly submerged him, and Vinayaka said, "Release me, release me!" Vinayaka also said to him, "Release me, release me!"

Both were equal in strength, engaged in submerging and emerging, like the irresistible Hiranyakashipu and Lord Narasimha. When the other children realized they had been underwater for a long time, they cried out in distress, saying clearly that the boy was dead. Hearing such cries, the women, the elderly, and the children came running. The King of Kashi also cried out, asking what had happened. "How can the boy survive in this deep well?" Some said, "If he is pulled out quickly, there might be a way for him to live. I will give whatever is asked to the one who saves his life. Pull the boy out with speed and strength."

Hearing the king's words, three men went into the well, but they were drowned by the illusion created by the demons; no one else could enter. The king then grieved. The king said: "What sin have I committed that I am suffering such grief? How did the boy I brought for happiness become a source of sorrow? How will I show his face to his parents? How could this death happen? The boy had already overcome great dangers; I do not know the will of God as to how he fell into this state."

While everyone was grieving, the frog-like demon in the well stood with his mouth wide open. Just then, the boy arrived, pushed by Kandara. The demon, pushed by him, fell into the mouth of the wicked demon. The well devoured him like a large fish eating a small one. Thinking the boy was swallowed, the demon assumed his true form. The well also boasted, reaching the sky, "I have repaid the debt of all the demons." He rejoiced in his mind, considering the Lord to be powerless. But Kandara, filled with rage, tore open the demon's stomach. Emerging with speed, the boy disappeared in an instant. The well, saying, "My stomach is torn," bit Kandara in the throat in anger. Thus, due to mutual killing, both fell, completely distraught. The city was crushed by the trampling of their feet. Just as Sunda and Upasunda died by killing each other, so too did Kupa and Kandara die by killing each other. The king's servants dragged them out and left them outside the city.

Instantly, the well in the courtyard vanished, and Vinayaka was seen playing as before with all the children. The children, the king, and the people were astonished. Some said to each other, "Deceit always leads to destruction; there is no doubt about it. Just as a moth comes to destroy a lamp and is destroyed itself, so too is this. Even Time itself would not be able to move a hair of his. We have seen the multi-faceted power of this Avatar. Desire went to conquer the Lord and was reduced to ashes itself."

Hearing this, the King of Kashi said, "It is true, it is true." He gave gifts to the Brahmins and sent them off after honoring them. Having bowed to Vinayaka, the people went to their homes. Some embraced the boy who had been honored by the king. The gods showered flowers, and the people praised him.

K said: Thus, O Brahmin, the power of the wind, the nature of illusion, the fortune of men, and the conduct of demons cannot be understood. The virtues of the Supreme Lord's Avatar also cannot be fully grasped.

Thus ends the nineteenth chapter of the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Killing of Kupa and Kandara."

Chapter 20 : The Slaying of the Three Demons in the Childhood Pastimes

Vyasa said: O Brahmin, when did the marriage in the King of Kashi’s palace take place? O four-faced one, please tell me this in detail.

K said: One misfortune is destroyed, and another follows. While they were contemplating how to finish the task, three other demons—Andhaka, Ambhasura, and Tunga—all cruel in appearance, were stirred up. With the intent to kill the child, they began a battle, the sound of which caused even Brahma and the other gods to tremble in fear. Those who had crushed the guardians of the directions—what to speak of the gods? They were determined to destroy the son of Kashyapa, whom they had been seeking, in various ways. Every hero they sent met his end. They declared, "We will kill him ourselves; we will not return home without defeating him. Assuming the form of fire, we will burn the sage’s son."

Andhaka said, "I will cover the sky and the directions with darkness and create total darkness upon the earth." Then, they would not be able to see each other. Ambhasura said, "I will flood the entire earth with water so that there will be no movement anywhere." Tunga said, "I will become a towering mountain and crush that city, just as a winged mountain would." With darkness, water, fire, and a mountain surrounding everything, who could go anywhere? How would the child escape? Having made this firm resolution, the three roared loudly. The three worlds trembled at the sound of their roar, and the oceans, with agitated waters, abandoned their shores.

Then, Andhakasura covered the sun and stood there. In the great, terrible darkness, nothing could be perceived. People engaged in bathing, chanting, fire sacrifices, penance, Vedic recitations, marriages, initiations, singing of Puranas, and worship of gods and Brahmins were suddenly plunged into night. They wondered, "Has the Vindhya mountain blocked the sun? Or is this the impending dissolution or an eclipse?" The scholars spoke to the King sitting in his assembly. While they were debating, the cows returned to their sheds, and people lit lamps in their homes in the middle of the day. Women were astonished, wondering why the sun had set, and some cows were milked at the wrong time. Merchants and Brahmins performed their tasks with great difficulty using torches and lamps. Servants, lustful people, the lazy, and the sleepy were delighted.

After Andhaka did this, Ambhasura took the form of a cloud and rained down torrents of water as thick as elephant trunks. People could only see for a moment by the light of lightning. Fearing the water, everyone stayed inside their homes. Some palaces and walls of houses collapsed, and many people died inside and outside. Trees were uprooted by the gale, and many houses were scorched by lightning. The city, filled with all kinds of people and creatures, was flooded as if by the oceans.

K said: Realizing this was the demons' illusion and the impending dissolution, Vinayaka created a great illusion. He instantly manifested a banyan tree, adorned with creepers and bushes, stretching for a hundred yojanas, with matted roots and branches. He stood there in the form of a bird, touching the earth with his wings and the sky with his head. He illuminated the city, drank the vast water with his beak, and dried up the pools of water with his trunk like a wild elephant. People joyfully cried out, "The obstacle is gone, the water has vanished!" When the darkness cleared, they saw the dense banyan tree and the bird of wondrous form, such as had never been seen or heard of before. All the people, eager to stay under the banyan tree, went there. The King of Kashi arrived under the tree with his horses, elephants, chariots, camels, palanquins, men, women, and servants. By the power of Vinayaka, there was neither rain nor darkness there.

Everyone resumed their duties and occupations as before. The citizens engaged in their noble tasks as they had previously. Whether it was the Lord of the World or Ganesha who took the bird form to protect everyone, we do not know. By spreading his wings, he stopped the rain caused by the demons. He himself endured the rain, fireballs, and hailstones with ease. The people, having reasoned this way, remained calm. Eleven days passed while he stayed there. No one knew the deeds performed by Vinayaka.

Seeing Vinayaka, the child, playing among the group of children, the demons lost their strength and became motionless. Tunga roared repeatedly, making the directions resound. "Assuming the form of a mountain, I will kill the bird in an instant." Saying this, he instantly became a terrifying mountain, filled with lakes and rivers, five yojanas wide, illuminating the sky and directions with glowing divine herbs, adorned with various birds and hermitages. Seeing the winged mountain falling, the King of Birds flew up, circled, and blocked the water, causing the moving and non-moving world to spin with the wind of his wings. The peaks of the mountains fell to the earth here and there. The son of Kashyapa caught the mountain Tunga with his beak, just as one would catch a snake.

He flew in the sky with it, targeting both demons. With one foot, he trampled Andhaka, and with the other, Ambhasura. The great bird crossed the middle world. Exhausted by the excessive flying and scorched by the sun's rays, the three mountain-like demons fell dead to the earth. As they fell, they crushed forests and groves. People, including women and children, went near the city to see the bodies of the demons and were filled with great wonder. The fragments of their bodies looked like broken rocks. When the demons' illusion was dispelled, the people were amazed. They saw the banyan tree in its divine form and then never saw it again. When the tree vanished, the child abandoned the bird form.

Then the King of Kashi embraced the child Vinayaka. After inquiring about his well-being, the citizens and the King worshipped Vinayaka, the destroyer of obstacles, with great love. The people praised the son of Kashyapa with joy. "We do not know the divine, supreme illusion created by the demons, nor your power and qualities, before which even the Vedas are silenced. By your play, we have all been protected and saved from many troubles. The winds, calamities, and rains have all been destroyed. The sun shines brightly now that the darkness has vanished." The minds of all the people were filled with joy. The rivers had clear water, and everything was as it was before. They showered flowers upon the Lord of Lords, Vinayaka. They entered the city to the sound of various musical instruments and gave alms to the Brahmins, praying, "May the Lord be pleased with us." After performing the peace sacrifice, the King gave much wealth in the form of cows. Having dismissed everyone, the King dined with Vinayaka.

Thus ends the twentieth chapter of the Childhood Pastimes in the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Slaying of the Three Demons."

Chapter 21 : The Demoness Bhramari

Vyasa said: Listen, O Brahmin, to a great wonder that I shall relate to you, the story of Vinayaka, which destroys all the sins of mankind.

The head of Ambasura had reached his own abode. The bee-like demoness (Bhramari), who had been watching, informed his companion. Upon seeing the head of her grandson, she fell to the ground in a faint, having risen from her golden couch and reached the courtyard. Beating her chest with her hands, filled with grief, her ornaments falling off, she appeared like a lotus plant crushed by an elephant. With broken bracelets and torn garments, her slender form visible and then invisible, she rolled on the ground in agony. Though restrained by her companions, brothers, and kin, she regained consciousness after three hours and began to speak. Rising, she struck her head again with her hands.

Bhramari said: By whom has this earth been terrified, along with the city of the gods? The thousand heads of Shesha have been shaken by the mere movement of his eyebrows. By whom were those two, Devantaka and Narantaka, installed in their kingdoms? By whose mere hiss the heavens and the earth trembled violently—how has he fallen, where, and by whom was he killed? He, seeing whom even Time itself trembled—how did he meet his end? Thus she lamented, miserable as a cow deprived of her calf.

As she lamented, her companions stopped her, saying, "O friend, no one has ever seen death accompanying the dead. Through excessive grief, another grief is attained by the foolish. If you mourn your son out of affection, do what is beneficial for him. Send spies to gather information from all sides. Let his head be burned with mantras; in this way, do what is good for him. It is said by the great sages that the tears of loved ones falling upon the face of the deceased burn him; therefore, do not abandon this."

Hearing these words, she spoke to her friend in anger, "O fortunate one, let this head be kept carefully. I shall bring the head of Aditi's son along with him; I shall have him burned, so keep this one in oil."

That demoness, accompanied by the best of the demons, went to the royal city to slay Vinayaka, concealing her true form, like a moth approaching a fire. She arrived in the guise of Aditi, illuminating the ten directions, a repository of beauty, adorned with all ornaments. She had teeth like pomegranate seeds, lips like the Bimba fruit, a slender waist, lotus-like eyes, and breasts shining with strings of pearls. The warriors were bewildered seeing such a beauty entering the palace, and in their minds, they desired to embrace and kiss her. Others wondered if she were Rambha, Tilottama, Menaka, Ghritachi, a Naga, a Yakshi, or Shiva herself. Or perhaps Urvashi, Rati, or Aditi, the queen of Kashyapa.

Recognizing her as the queen, the queen of the palace bowed to Aditi with joy. She worshipped her with auspicious items, ornaments, and perfumes. The beautiful-limbed queen spoke with a voice choked with affection, "By great fortune, I have seen you, the mother of the gods. Only by the grace of Vinayaka could I have had your vision." As she spoke to the queen, the demoness, with tears in her throat, said:

Aditi said: Where has my child, who has been away for many days, been kept? Out of great longing, I have come here to see him. You know the nature of women, whose hearts are agitated by illusion. My body is scorched with grief; let me embrace that child. By touching his cool body, I shall attain supreme satisfaction.

Hearing these words, the queen, with respect and haste, sent messengers to search for the child. Others told the King of Kashi that the wife of Kashyapa had arrived. He bathed, came home, and was filled with joy upon seeing her. Bowing to her, the King said with folded hands, "My kingdom, birth, parents, wealth, vision, knowledge, and penance—all are blessed by seeing you, the mother of the gods, the creator of the world. I have no power to describe your virtues. This child of yours, Vinayaka, has prowess greater than Indra's. He has performed many deeds against the demons, and I have no power to describe the destruction of the demons he has caused. O Mother, rest for a moment; it is my great fortune that he has destroyed the demons who relied on the power of illusion. Your child lives happily here in his own form. Why have you come? If you have come, please stay for a moment. Once this marriage is over, I shall send you both."

She replied, "What are you saying, O King? Due to his separation, I have felt great sorrow and have found no happiness anywhere."

Vyasa said: As she was speaking, Vinayaka arrived. Sent by the boys, he said, "Your mother has come." She then embraced the child, and with tears of joy, she said, "You have stayed here for a long time, abandoning me, O cruel one. Distressed by your separation, I have come, leaving everything behind. Abandoning all hope of life, I performed penance for your sake. I have attained you after great hardship; the Lord knows those hardships. Without you, a moment for me became like a Yuga.

Speaking such deceptive words, the wicked-hearted woman, out of the strength of her false affection, held him at her waist. He, knowing her intentions, thought of her and, with hidden intent, asked, "What did you take from me before?" As he was saying this, she gave him a sweet. As he ate it, she gave him another filled with great poison. As he ate that, he forcefully demanded another sweet. The King of Kashi and his wife then prayed, "Rise, rise, Mother, and eat with the child."

As she tried to rise, she was held back by the child, whose body became as heavy as a mountain, and she became distressed. "Release me, release me," she cried, "I am your mother, O child. I have come after a long time to see you out of the bonds of affection. How are you trying to crush me like a mountain?" She pushed him with her hands, and then she fell into a deep sleep. Stretching her feet and hands, breathing heavily, she remained there. The King restrained her, who was distressed and burdened, "O virtuous mother, do not break his sleep."

Some, out of compassion, tried to lift the child, but no one could lift or move him. Others prayed to the lotus-eyed child, "Have mercy, O child, your mother will die." He then moved his feet, hands, and head. "What is this you are doing, child? What will you tell your father?" As the people were saying this, the demoness died. Her body, ten Yojanas long, covered the earth. Seeing her terrifying form, some fled, while others, curious, went to see her. The King and the people were astonished. "How did this child-slayer come in a deceptive form? She was recognized as a sage's wife, not a demoness."

"Her knowledge and power are truly wonderful," the people said, and they went near her with joy. Lifting the child, they said he was as light as a flower. "Remove this wicked one," they said, "for she was of a wicked nature. Whoever wishes ill for others meets his own end." The King of Kashi and the people, along with the sages and the guardians of the world, worshipped Vinayaka with devotion:

"You are the Lord of the gods, humans, serpents, and demons. You are the Lord of the Yakshas, Gandharvas, Brahmins, elephants, horses, chariots, and birds. You are the Lord of the past, present, and future, and of all senses and intellect. You are the Lord of joy, sorrow, grief, happiness, knowledge, and delusion. You are the Lord of wealth, actions, gain, and loss. You are the Lord of the heavens, the netherworlds, the earth, and the oceans. You are the Lord of the stars, planets, ghosts, and plants. You are the Lord of trees, rivers, men, women, and children. Salutations to you, the creator, sustainer, and destroyer. Salutations to you, the Lord of animals, the giver of true knowledge. Salutations to you in the form of Vishnu, Rudra, Brahma, and the Infinite. Salutations to you, the cause of liberation, the remover of obstacles. Salutations to you, the destroyer of the non-devotees, and the beloved of the devotees. You are the soul of the gods and the elements; you are the remover of the three types of suffering. Salutations to you, the destroyer of all calamities, the embodiment of divine play. Salutations to you, the indweller of all, the overseer of all. O Vinayaka, born from the womb of Aditi, salutations to you. Salutations to you, the son of Kashyapa, the embodiment of the Supreme Brahman. Salutations to you, who possess immeasurable illusion, who are the master of illusion, who bewilder through illusion, who remove immeasurable illusion, and who are the great refuge of illusion."

Having praised him thus, they all returned to their homes with joy, after the demoness had been cut into pieces and removed far from the city. Whoever reads this hymn, which destroys calamities at the three junctures of the day, will not face great calamities, obstacles, or fear of ghosts. Whoever reads this hymn at the three junctures of the day will attain all desires, and Vinayaka will always protect him, O sinless one.

Thus ends the twenty-first chapter of the Childhood Pastimes in the Krida Khanda of the Sri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Slaying of the Demoness."

Chapter 22 : The Conversation Between Shukla and Vidruma

King K said: The next day, the King, having completed his morning ablutions, pondered along with all the citizens, "This boy is neither a god nor a mere human. If he were not a god, how could he have destroyed the five great, invincible demons who were feared even by Indra and Hari? He must be a god, yet he plays here as a small child." Thinking thus, they all went to see the King. The King welcomed them and said, "Tell me what you wish, and it shall be fulfilled. Why have you all come here so early in the morning?"

They replied, "We have come to speak of a matter close to our hearts. You brought this Vinayaka, the son of a sage, here. We have never had the opportunity to serve him. You have worshipped him many times in your palace, and whatever goes into your house, you send it to us. Why, O King, has this Vinayaka not been sent to us?"

The King replied, "What you say is true. When people share what they have, it becomes nectar, but if they do not, it becomes poison. Whoever has devotion for him—whether they see him as a god or a human—should take him to their home, worship him, and feed him. People are of three types based on the qualities of Rajas, Sattva, and Tamas. The wicked test him, while the virtuous worship him. Some criticize him, while others praise him. A person acts according to their own nature. Just as sandalwood does not lose its fragrance even when rubbed, or as an onion retains its nature even when mixed with musk, so too does a person remain true to their nature. If you have pure, singular devotion for this son of the sage, take him, worship him, and offer him food. Do not take him for the sake of testing him. I cannot force people to take him and worship him. Seeing his immense power, my devotion for him has deepened. If he were not here, where would I be, and where would you be?"

Hearing the King’s words, the citizens said, "O King, whatever you say is true. Please fulfill our desire. We will take this child to our homes and worship him as he deserves." At that moment, the child, who is the witness of all and the Guru of the universe, knowing their inner thoughts, spoke.

Vinayaka said, "Why do you, who are so great, pray to me? I am but a child, a sage’s son. I do not know what fruit you will gain by spending your wealth on my worship. If there is a wedding, a sacred thread ceremony, a sacrifice, or any other festival, I will come to your homes when there is a feast. You are countless; how can I go to every house? Why would you milk a cow that is already dry? Why would one seek a petty tree when they have the wish-fulfilling tree? You are eager for something trivial."

King K said: Hearing Vinayaka’s words, the leaders of the guilds said, "Once this wedding is over, you will not stay for a moment. We have seen you through the King’s grace; do not let our devotion go in vain. You are the creator, sustainer, and destroyer of the universe. You are the indweller of all hearts, knowing all thoughts, capable of doing, undoing, or not doing anything. We have no need for rituals for you, who are pure consciousness and bliss, but do not make the scriptures that say 'God loves devotion' false."

Hearing their words, Vinayaka said, "If your devotion is such, then I will go by the King’s command." Hearing this, everyone went to their homes. Some prepared pavilions, others tents. They decorated with devotion, creating wonderful displays. They gathered precious vessels, fragrant sandalwood, perfumes, musk, exotic fruits, clothes, ornaments, and various delicacies mixed with the five nectars. They installed various idols adorned with pearl necklaces. Thus, in every house, everyone was eager to prepare.

At the edge of the city lived a Brahmin named Shukla, who knew the Vedas and Shastras. He was calm, self-controlled, forgiving, a performer of Vedic rites, devoted to Brahman, and loved guests. His wife, Vidruma, was a noble soul, desireless, full of wisdom, and beautiful in every limb. Though poor, she was devoted to knowledge and her husband. Their home was simple, yet bright like the starry sky. They had no gold, silver, or copper vessels. She wore bark garments, yet she shone with a brilliant inner light. Her husband, satisfied with her conduct, lived in devotion to knowledge, often remaining without food.

Once, having gone to beg for food and being turned away from every house because everyone was busy with the festival for Vinayaka, he returned home. He told his wife, "Listen, my wife, I was turned away from every house. They say Vinayaka, who has descended to remove the burden of the earth, will visit every home for worship. If he comes to our house, prepare for his worship."

She replied, "How will he come to our house, where we have no food? We have only wilted flowers, dried fruits, and roots. What use would he have for our home?" The sage replied, "He knows me as his devotee; he is the Lord of the helpless. This God loves devotion, not greed. He is pleased with water, a leaf, or a flower, not with wealth offered out of vanity."

Hearing this, Vidruma said, "If that is so, then offer whatever we have." She prepared a paste of eighteen types of grains and cooked rice. She placed a mat of Kusha grass in the well-cleaned courtyard. The sage, deep in meditation, performed the Vaishvadeva offering with great effort, waiting for the Lord.

Chapter 23 : The Bestowing of Boons upon Shukla

Kritayuga said: While the child was playing with other children, filled with curiosity, the King sat on his throne, watching the dance along with all the people. Just as he was watching the dancers, the two sages, Sanaka and Sanandana, arrived from the celestial realm. The entire assembly was illuminated by their radiance, as if by the sun and fire. Seeing them, the King immediately rose from his throne. He placed his head at their lotus feet and performed a full prostration. With folded hands, he said, "Today my lineage is blessed, as are my kingdom, my knowledge, my body, my wife, and my children." Having said this, he held their hands and seated them on his own throne. He worshipped them with the sixteen traditional offerings with great devotion and relieved their fatigue by massaging their feet. After bowing to them again and again, he began to speak.

The King said: "Sages who have renounced all worldly attachments and desires have no personal agenda. Yet, I seek your command. I am eager to make my penance, kingdom, lineage, and character meaningful."

The two sages replied: "He who has attained all desires, who is the embodiment of the Supreme Brahman, and who is the Lord incarnate in human form, is your son, born to Kashyapa. He performs many wonderful miracles in your home. Having heard of his exploits, we have come here to see him. Otherwise, what reason would we have to leave the abode of Indra? He who has the wish-fulfilling tree in his own home, what else would he seek from others? Let us bow to his lotus feet and return to our own abode, O King."

Hearing these words from the two sages, Vinayaka, who was playing nearby, left his game and came over, holding a modaka in his hand. He playfully held the modaka, which was made of five types of food. The King, smiling gently, showed him to the sages, saying, "This is the God for whose vision you were so eager. He has come; now do whatever you wish to do."

Seeing him, the two sages spoke to each other: "Surely, the great sage Kashyapa has been corrupted by becoming the father of this child, for he has completely abandoned his own conduct. He has no regard for the rules of purity and impurity, nor for what should or should not be eaten. Great sin will surely arise from seeing or touching him. How can he, having abandoned his own vows, reside in a Kshatriya's house? What good will come to us, who are established in our own dharma, by seeing him?"

The Lord, who was near the King, heard these words. Being a knower of speech, he spoke like Brihaspati himself: "Where has your knowledge gone, O sages who labor in vain? Why have you left heaven and come here like ordinary mortals?"

Hearing these words, the sages, completely deluded by the maya of the Lord, replied to the King, "Give us permission; we shall return to our own ashram." Then the King, driven by firm devotion, said to them, "Please stay and listen to my words." He prayed to them with devotion, asking them to bathe and eat before departing. Hearing his prayer, the sages replied, "We do not eat food from a king's house; therefore, we must leave." Having obtained his permission, they went to Manikarni.

Vinayaka, followed by all the children, playing with joy, bathed and went to Shukla's house, honoring his devotion. Just as a cow runs to her calf, or a mother to her crying child, or as Krishna went to Draupadi when she sought refuge—seeing the Lord, the couple fell at his feet. Shedding tears of joy and speaking with choked voices, they embraced the Lord with delight and danced, overwhelmed with happiness. They were so lost in bliss that they forgot their own bodies; their hair stood on end, and they shed streams of tears.

After a moment, regaining their senses, they bowed to the Lord of the Universe. Then the sage said to the Lord, "Your name, known in all three worlds as the Lord of the Poor and the destroyer of sins, is auspicious. Our birth has been made meaningful today by the sight of your feet. Leaving behind palaces, you have come to this humble hut. My lineage, my birth, my knowledge, my penance, and my life are blessed today."

The sage then worshipped him, seated on a mat of kusha grass. He washed the Lord's feet and placed the water on his head. He offered sandalwood paste, rice grains, flower garlands, incense, and lamps. He offered sprouts of durva grass, shami leaves, fragrant oil, and ketaki flowers. Placing a small amount of food and some forest fruits before him, he bowed down, feeling ashamed, thinking, "How can I offer such poor food to Him?"

Knowing his intent, the Lord of Lords, Vinayaka, said, "Mother, whatever food you have in your house, please give it to me. The poor food given with devotion is greater to me than nectar. Food given with arrogance and without faith becomes poison."

The sage's wife, filled with shyness, placed before him the dry rice and the vegetable dish she had prepared. Seeing the oil and the simple meal, the other children laughed. The Lord ate it with great respect, as if it were a feast. Taking water with every morsel, he ate with great vigor. Then the sage threw the entire pot of rice, and the water splashed in all directions; the child could not stop it.

Suddenly, the Lord assumed his ten-armed form and ate the rice with them. The people watched in astonishment, laughing and clapping their hands. "This is an unprecedented meal, cooked with water only up to the knees," they said, and the children ate it, finding it as delicious as pudding. Those who ate with him attained divinity, while those who had laughed with open teeth later felt remorse.

A great commotion arose in the city: "Where is Vinayaka?" Some said, "He is eating dry rice at Shukla's house." He assumed his ten-armed form and ate the rice with them. After eating, he gave them a dried myrobalan (amla) fruit. Vinayaka, the Soul of the Universe, used it to cleanse his mouth. Pleased, he said to Shukla, "O sinless one, I am pleased with your devotion. Ask for any boons you desire from me."

Shukla said: "You have come to eat dry food, leaving behind all the citizens. This itself is a great boon for me, that I have had your vision. Following your command, I ask for firm devotion to you. At the end, grant me liberation so that I may never return to this world. My mind no longer finds pleasure in worldly happiness, O Vinayaka."

Kritayuga said: Saying "So be it," the child returned to his two-armed form. He granted him boons—wealth, jewels, gold, excellent character, knowledge, and prosperity—far greater than what was in Indra's house. Having been granted permission, the Lord departed with the children.

Chapter 24 : The Awakening of Sanaka and Sananda

Ka said: In that city, all the prominent people looked for Him, having prepared various offerings for His meal. In every house, in the royal palace, and in the homes of all the ministers, the people searched for the son of Kashyapa. Everyone asked, "Has Vinayaka been seen?" But He, whom the Vedas describe as "not this, not this," how could He be within the reach of human eyes? People said, "He must not have been seen by us." Those who were at the edge of the city heard this from the people and learned that He had come to the house of Shukla. They went to that house, but not seeing Him, they asked, and were told that He had gone elsewhere.

That illusory child, eager to deceive the people, left the children behind, came through the door, and lay down to sleep. Some saw Him sleeping like a lion in a cave, while others, overcome with anger, criticized Him, saying, "Why has this small boy gone to the house of a poor man? If He is the Lord and knows the hearts of all, He should do what is pleasing to everyone." They lifted Him up, held His hand, and said, "Come to a noble refuge. Make our efforts fruitful, as we have prepared everything for You. The fatigue of our wandering has vanished upon seeing Your lotus feet. Come, come to our homes to eat with the groups of children."

He replied, "I have already eaten." The people asked Him again, "What could You have eaten in the house of a poor man, living on alms? You did not even drink water, so how could You have eaten, O Lord?" Ganesha said, "My stomach is full, and I have no strength to go."

Ka said: Then, realizing that He had eaten at Shukla's house, the people became hopeless, their intentions shattered, angry, miserable, and confused. The provisions gathered with much difficulty went to waste. Those wicked, hypocritical, and devotionless people consumed the food themselves. Those who were true devotees remained fasting, absorbed in meditation.

Knowing that everyone in the worlds was absorbed in meditation, He became one yet assumed many forms, clearly like the reflection of the sky in various pots, or like the sun seen in many water-filled vessels. Somewhere He was lying on a bed, somewhere He was absorbed in chanting; somewhere He was engaged in charity, and somewhere He was eager to eat. Somewhere He was teaching students the Vedas with their auxiliary parts and meanings; somewhere He was explaining scriptures, and somewhere He was reading them Himself.

In this way, He shone in many forms in many houses. Everyone believed, "He came to our house first." They offered Him oil, massage, bathing, worship, and food. Thus, everyone rejoiced, including the King of Kashi. Seeing the noble Vinayaka with supreme joy, they worshipped Him and fed the Brahmins and their friends. They partook of the five nectars, cooked food, side dishes, milk-rice, and everything else that was tasty, according to their preference.

While the Brahmins, gods, friends, and the King of Kashi were offering blessings, the King of Kashi, bewildered by the illusion of the sage's son who assumed many forms, asked, "It is clear that Vinayaka has gone to many houses to eat. Yet, He is also near me. Who is eating in every house? Somewhere Vinayaka goes to eat riding a palanquin, somewhere riding an elephant, and somewhere riding a horse."

While the child was busy being worshipped in all the worlds, those two (the sages) went to the city. They saw Vinayaka eating, but when they turned back, they met Him again. In whichever house they went, they saw Vinayaka. After finishing everything, He went elsewhere to beg. They saw Him lying on a divine bed, eating fruits, chewing betel leaf, or anointed with fragrances. Somewhere they saw Him watching the dance of courtesans, surrounded by groups of people; somewhere playing with dice or testing His intellectual strength. Somewhere they saw Him adorned with various ornaments and divine garments, reading or chanting, absorbed in meditation. Somewhere they saw Him being praised by devotees, or Himself praising the Lord of the world, or showing His divine power to devotees in many ways. Somewhere He was delighting His extremely beautiful wives, or treating an old man, or enjoying the best nectar. Somewhere they saw Him crawling in a courtyard, eating mud, or tired and drinking water after playing with balls.

Thus, hungry and tired, wandering from house to house and seeing that God everywhere, they said to each other, "There is a pure, clean Brahmin named Shukla in this city. Let us go to eat at his house." Saying this, they went there. Even there, they saw Vinayaka in the courtyard. "We should not eat anywhere else in the city; there is nothing left but Vinayaka." Saying this, they came outside and saw Vinayaka in front of them. Moving in all directions, they saw Him everywhere. In the east, in the west, all around, above and below, they saw the all-pervading Vinayaka.

They saw the form of Vinayaka as the form of the universe; they saw everything as Vinayaka. Then, filled with great wonder, they contemplated Shambhu and Vishnu, and in their hearts, they saw Him—that very Vinayaka. Closing their eyes, the best of sages saw Him within; opening their eyes, they saw Him in their own hearts. They saw Him adorned with a crown, earrings, pearl necklaces, garments, armlets, a golden waist-band, and rings. He was mounted on a lion, ten-armed, accompanied by Siddhi and Buddhi, auspicious, with the moon on His forehead, a musk mark, and adorned with serpents. He was radiant as ten million suns, the creator, sustainer, and destroyer of the world. Abandoning all duality, the knowers of truth bowed to His lotus feet. With supreme devotion, they praised the God of gods, Vinayaka. Through His grace, those two great sages attained enlightenment.

Chapter 25 : The Praise of Devotion in the Childhood Deeds of Ganesha

They said: You are the cause of all causes, transcending all causality. You are the embodiment of Brahman, the cause of the universe, all-pervading and supreme. O sinless one, You alone create this universe and You alone dissolve it. You are formless yet assume many forms, endowed with the power of various illusions. You are the five elements, the Yakshas, the Gandharvas, and the Rakshasas. Who is capable of praising You, who are the essence of all moving and non-moving things? The Vedas, unable to comprehend Your true form, declare "Neti, Neti" (not this, not this). We were deluded and unable to know Your supreme form. O Lord, we did not know the greatness of Your manifold forms, but now, by seeing Your feet, we have fulfilled our purpose. Through Your infinite power, You remove the burden of the earth by assuming various incarnations.

Ka said: Hearing their praise, the Lord, who was in the form of a playful child and master of illusion, spoke to them with affection. You shall become knowers of the truth and omniscient by My grace. Having granted this boon, the Lord disappeared right there. Then, those two, Sanaka and Sanandana, created an incomparable image of Vinayaka. They built a magnificent temple adorned with gold and jewels and established the deity there. They named the deity Varada Vinayaka and created a lake which they called Ganesha Kunda. Whoever bathes there and worships the boon-giving Vinayaka will attain all their desires, enjoy worldly pleasures, and be blessed with sons, grandsons, knowledge, long life, fame, wealth, grain, and eternal wisdom. In the end, they will undoubtedly attain the abode of Vinayaka.

Ka said: Where the gods, Gandharvas, Yakshas, and Apsaras, having seen and worshipped the Lord of gods, disappeared in an instant. Amazed, they both departed for the supreme heaven. The King of Kashi also arrived there with his ministers and subjects. He worshipped the Lord with great devotion using various substances, ornaments, fine garments, cooked food, fruits, gems, pearls, flowers, and numerous offerings, along with the Brahmins chanting mantras. He honored the Brahmins with clothes, ornaments, and gold. Having received their blessings, he returned to the city. Similarly, the citizens worshipped Vinayaka, honored the Brahmins, and received their blessings. Then the subjects worshipped Vinayaka according to their capacity. All of them, with joyful hearts, entered the city. Thus, I have told you everything about the deeds of Vinayaka. It is holy and destroys all sins; what else do you wish to hear?

The Sage said: I have heard the story of Shukla, who was devoted to begging for alms. Vinayaka went to the house of Shukla, who was devoid of any touch of material wealth. He took the food, which was softened with water, with His ten hands. Having eaten it, He removed his fatigue and destroyed his poverty. He gave Shukla a house better than the city of Indra, wealth equal to that of the Lord of Alaka, a form more beautiful than the God of Love, and knowledge greater than that of the four-faced Brahma. O Brahmin, what is the reason for this? Please explain it to me.

Ka said: You have asked well, O Vyasa, for the story of the Supreme Lord, when heard with devotion, purifies the questioner, the listeners, and the speaker. My mind is inclined to speak on what you have asked, O Lord. I will tell you properly about the deeds of Him who is attained through devotion. The Lord is omniscient, all-seeing, all-pervading, and knows the hearts of all. He is pleased only by devotion; sincerity is the cause of His satisfaction. He is satisfied with a mere leaf, water, or a beautiful flower offered with devotion, and in return, He gives Himself. Wealth, gold, silver, pearls, and various jewels offered with pride, indifference, or shame are all in vain. He was satisfied with a Shami leaf and granted the hunter a place in His own realm. Therefore, devotion is supreme, as it is attained effortlessly when one holds firm faith in the heart.

Thus ends the twenty-fifth chapter of the Krida Khanda in the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Praise of Devotion in the Childhood Deeds of Ganesha."

Chapter 26 : The Liberation of Bhima and the Demon

The Sage said: What deeds did the hunter perform, and how was the leaf offered? Tell me how he attained the state of Salokya (residing in the same realm as the Divine).

The Crow said: In the Vidarbha country, there was a city named Madisha, famous in the three worlds and comparable to the city of Kubera. In that city lived a hunter named Bhima, who sold meat. Although it is generally considered a fault to speak of others' failings, one must speak the truth when asked, without malice. Bhima was full of faults; he wandered the forest with his bow and arrows. Carrying a quiver full of shafts, a sword, and a bow, he lived by killing animals to support his family. He was merciless toward travelers and Brahmins in the desolate forest.

Once, a great festival began in that city. Early in the morning, the hunter went to the forest, greedy for wealth and wishing to sell much meat to support his family. He killed a herd of deer and, laden with meat, was about to enter the city when a great demon appeared. Attracted by the scent of a human, the demon approached him in an instant. He was named Pingaksha, with tawny eyes, terrifying to all, a devourer of humans and beasts, and as insatiable as fire. Seeing him, the hunter trembled and fell to the ground. His weapons dropped, and he covered his eyes in fear. Opening his eyes, he saw a Shami tree nearby and climbed it. The demon also climbed the tree. As they struggled, a branch shook, and a leaf, moved by the wind, fell upon the head of the Lord of the Ganas (Ganesha), who had been installed by Vamana.

The Lord was pleased by this, and both were clearly blessed. The Elephant-faced Lord, who had appeared to grant boons to Vamana—the son of Kashyapa who wished to conquer Bali—is extremely pleased by Durva grass and Shami leaves. Without a sprout of Durva, worship is fruitless for men; only with a Shami leaf does it become fruitful, and never otherwise. The Lord is not as pleased by sacrifices, charities, vows, penances, regulations, gold, or heaps of gems as He is by Durva and Shami leaves.

Pleased by these two, the God of Gods sent a magnificent chariot, accompanied by attendants in their true forms from His own realm. Upon seeing them, the mountains of sins of both the demon and the hunter were destroyed. By the grace of Ganesha, just as a mountain of straw is destroyed by a spark of fire, their sins vanished. Abandoning their bodies, they assumed divine forms, boarded the chariot, and were honored by the messengers with various garments, ornaments, and ointments. Accompanied by the sounds of various musical instruments and surrounded by celestial singers, they were taken before the Lord, where they bowed to Vinayaka. Freed from all sins, they attained His likeness and were established by Ganesha in His own abode for the duration of the Kalpa.

Thus, the Lord, who loves devotion, is pleased by a sincere heart. Just as He was satisfied by a handful of food from the Brahmin and granted Shukla a divine house like Alaka, so too is He pleased. Therefore, worship without Durva and Shami leaves is in vain. The Elephant-faced Lord is not as pleased by grand, ostentatious worship as He is by even a small offering made with sincere devotion.

Thus ends the twenty-sixth chapter of the Krida Khanda of the Sri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Liberation of Bhima and the Demon."

Chapter 27 : Samba's Evil Conduct

Vyasa said: Who was this hunter in his previous birth, and was he also a demon? What was his character and his conduct? Tell me this, O Narada. How did he perform the rituals for Vamana, O Lord? How was the boon granted to him by the one who has the form of an elephant-faced god? Why does the world of Ganesha exist separately elsewhere? Why is the Shami tree dear to him? Tell me all this quickly.

Narada said: Listen, I will tell you everything that you have asked, O Sage. In his previous birth, that hunter was a king who was well-versed in the Vedas. He was a speaker of all scriptures, endowed with all auspicious marks, a performer of sacrifices, humble, modest, and a worshipper of gods and guests. The number of his elephants, horses, chariots, and foot soldiers was uncountable. He was a donor, a hero, intelligent, and as prosperous as Indra. His chief ministers, Dhvaja and Shatrujit, were highly intelligent, and because of them, even the teacher of the gods, Brihaspati, was clearly surpassed in eloquence. They were capable of conquering the earth with the power of their weapons. He had a virtuous wife named Madanavati, who was devoted to her husband, righteous, beautiful, and knowledgeable in all things; there was no woman like her in the three worlds. They were compassionate toward the poor, the helpless, and the elderly, as well as toward guests. As they performed sacrifices, gave donations, and were devoted to worshipping the virtuous, the citizens and the people of the kingdom were pleased with their conduct. Even enemies acknowledge the six virtues approved by the wise.

Thus, being of pure fame, he ruled this earth for many years until, by destiny, he met the fate of time. When he was about to die, he performed the gift of a thousand cows according to the scriptures and gave away other great gifts as well. When he died, the entire city mourned the king. Because he had no son, his wife followed him in death. The ministers had all the funeral rites performed by Brahmins, and on the eleventh day, they gave various donations with devotion. After a month, they deliberated on who was worthy of the kingdom—someone born of the family, intelligent, endowed with strength, a hero, and of truthful valor. They decided to give the kingdom to Samba, the son of the powerful Durdharsha, who was his heir, and who had been instructed by the king. Having decided this, they asked all the citizens and Brahmins. At an auspicious moment and time, with various preparations, they had him anointed along with the Brahmins.

The Sage asked: How was he born from the field (Kshetraja), and what was his lineage, O Grandfather? Tell me about this Samba, for you are omniscient, O ocean of compassion.

Narada said: O Sage, Durdharsha had made many efforts for a son, but no good son was born to him, as he was afflicted by bad destiny. His wife, a woman whose mind was attached to a fisherman, gave birth to him at an auspicious time, and it was not known that he was born of a paramour. The ministers gave him all the royal insignia and handed over the entire kingdom, including the treasury and the subjects. They remained in the position of ministers as before. Having attained the kingdom, the son of Durdharsha became intoxicated with wealth. He was addicted to women, meat, and wine, lazy like an intoxicated elephant, always engaged in evil conduct, and turned away from the path of morality. He took all the wealth of the rich and exiled them. He quickly insulted the Brahmins and the virtuous. He drove them all out after seizing their livelihoods and families. Although the ministers advised him on policy, he did not accept their words. When the two compassionate ministers repeatedly advised him, he became angry and bound them in chains. He was a wicked son who ignored his parents, who were trying to teach him—the very parents through whose grace he had obtained a kingdom free of thorns. "O villain, how can you punish those who made you what you are?" Ignoring their words like a fool, he kept them bound in chains in the prison. A very wicked person named Dushtabuddhi became his constant companion. He made him his minister, giving him horses, gold, elephants, jewels, divine garments, and deer-skin coverings. He provided him with countless servants, drinks, food, and women, declaring, "Whoever does not follow his command, I will forcibly cut off his head." Having said this to everyone, Samba went to the inner chambers.

Thus ends the twenty-seventh chapter of the Childhood Sports in the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, describing the misrule of Samba.

Chapter 28 : The Previous History of Bhima

The King used to attend to state affairs himself, enjoyed the company of beautiful women, and would listen to reports about women in his kingdom, whether they were unmarried or married. He would forcibly bring them to enjoy himself, regardless of their crying or weeping, and being addicted to sensual pleasures, he did not even consider the distinctions of his own kin. Any woman with well-developed breasts who came into his sight, that lustful man would embrace, kiss, and even violate her. He would seize the wealth of others and their women by force. His minister was of a similar nature, deeply engrossed in wicked thoughts.

Engaged in such misconduct, these two merciless men would not spare even a maiden, a mother, or a sister in their lustful pursuits. They did not consider the killing of a Brahmana, a woman, or a child as a sin. Being perpetrators of such sinful deeds, they were like mountains of sin. Let alone the two wicked-minded King and minister, even a Brahmana or an ascetic would not go to their house to beg for alms. In the kingdom, no one even dared to utter their names.

Once, the two went into a dense forest for hunting. They killed herds of deer and flocks of birds in large numbers. While returning to the city on horseback, they happened to see a great place called Vainayaka, where the ancient and auspicious image of Vainayaka was situated. It was installed by Rama’s father (Dasharatha) while performing penance for the sake of a son. There, the Lord of Lords, Vinayaka, was realized through devotion. By meditating for many days with the ten-syllable mantra, the Lord granted him his desired boons. Later, it was firmly installed by the hand of Vashistha, and because it granted boons, Vashistha named it Varada. By the sight of this, the four goals of human life are attained, and there is no doubt that by remembering or worshipping it, one attains success.

Thus, by the words of Vashistha, that place became famous on earth. By worshipping, remembering, and offering prayers to Ganesha, the sons Rama, Lakshmana, Bharata, and Shatrughna were born, who became famous in the world, omniscient, and praised by heroes. Seeing that beautiful palace built by the King, the King and his minister got down from their horses. They worshipped the Lord, the remover of all sins, with leaves and flowers. After circumambulating the Lord, they left after a moment. This was the only great merit they had acquired by chance.

After ruling and committing sinful deeds, they died. Bound by the ropes of the messengers of Yama, they were taken to the presence of the Lord of Justice (Yama). Yama summoned Chitragupta and asked about their good and bad deeds. Chitragupta said, "O Son of the Sun, they have not even a trace of merit; their sins are countless." He then said to the messengers, "Bind them, beat them, and strike them with iron rods. Cast them into the Vichi-maya hell for thousands of years.
