Then Vinayaka, who knows all, released the rain-bearing weapon. Instantly, streams of water as thick as an elephant's trunk appeared, and the fire was extinguished. Enraged, the demon then unleashed the wind weapon, which moves with great speed and disperses rain. The earth trembled, and trees and mountains fell to the ground. By that weapon, the great clouds were destroyed in half a moment. Then, by the power of his mind, the god manifested mountains to counter the weapon. Mountains appeared everywhere, and the wind weapon became ineffective. When the wind weapon dissolved, the demon unleashed the terrible weapon of Rudra. As it was released, Vakratunda unleashed the Brahmastra to counter it, which reduced the demon's army to ashes.

They fought each other with weapons for many days. From the collision of their weapons, fire fell upon the earth. Seeing the people scorched by it, the demon, realizing Vinayaka's superiority, protected his own people with his hair and spoke to his ministers. He said, "I will go and eat, and then return to the battlefield to fight him with all my forces." Then the ministers, along with the entire army, fought Vinayaka. Vinayaka, feeling concerned due to being alone, instantly created fifty-six forms through his own power. They were adorned with various ornaments and garlands, all having divine arms and decorated with the moon. Some had four arms, some six, and some ten. Some were mounted on lions, some on bulls, and some had mice as their vehicles. All of them fought there, tearing apart the enemy soldiers. Some had their feet severed, some their heads, some their arms, thighs, shanks, and bellies broken. Some roared with shouts, while others fled for their lives. Some died while fighting face-to-face. Those who died went to heaven and enjoyed the company of celestial nymphs. Horses, chariots, elephants, camels, and many others fell, struck by various weapons, their bodies shattered and lifeless. Rivers of blood appeared, adorned with hair like moss. They were decorated with swords, fish-like daggers, elephant-like crocodiles, shield-like tortoises, lotus-like severed heads, foam-like fat, and mud-like flesh. With bows, weapons, bone-like banks, and skin-like muscles, they appeared beautiful to the hearts of the wise.

Having returned after eating, the demon saw the entire battlefield. Seeing his entire army slaughtered, the demon was filled with great sorrow. He remembered in his mind the words spoken by the trident-bearer (Shiva) at the time of granting the boon: "Victory will be yours through power." He wondered, "Could this boy be the manifestation of that power? The strength seen in him is the essence of the three worlds. Even Time could not defeat him, so what to say of others?" Thinking this, he fled because he was alone. Vakratunda, concerned, thought, "An enemy who is fleeing should not be killed. Moreover, as Shiva said, he cannot die at the hands of the gods." Therefore, he decided to assume his supreme Virat (cosmic) form. Then, in that cosmic form, he caught the demon with his hand. He stood on one foot to protect Kashi by force, and placed his other foot on the demon's head. He said to the demon, "O demon, you cannot die due to the boon of Shiva. Therefore, remain in this city, standing as still as a mountain. Be the guardian of this city, as you were before. You shall be a tormentor of the wicked, and I shall always be present with you." The demon, with great devotion, requested the same boon.

The demon said: "Remain exactly like this, keeping your foot steady upon me."

The Sage said: "Saying 'So be it,' Vinayaka remained in Kashi. Thus, having conquered the invincible Durasada, he made the earth auspicious. Showering him with flowers, bowing, and worshipping him, the gods and sages returned to their respective abodes. Those who worship him attain all their desires. Thus, Vinayaka's fifty-six forms are in Kashi. In the city named Tunda, the one-footed Vinayaka, having withdrawn his cosmic form, remains as the granter of all desires. Whoever listens to this excellent story with devotion attains all desires, reaches the state of Ganesha, and gains victory, health, and prosperity everywhere.

Thus ends the forty-second chapter of the Childhood Deeds in the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Victory over Durasada."

Chapter 43 : The Greatness of Dhundiraja

Gritsamada said: When that formidable demon was defeated by Ganesha, the guardians of the directions, the sages, the moon, the sun, Brihaspati, and Shukracharya praised the Lord of Lords, the destroyer of formidable enemies. They said, "The path established by the Shruti and Smriti has been restored, and the demon has been vanquished. You have established us, who were powerless, in this difficult situation. You alone create the universe, and you alone are the giver of fruits. You are equal to all beings and are not tainted by actions. The four varnas and all living creatures depend upon you. By searching for various meanings and engaging everyone in their respective duties, you are called Dhundhi (the Seeker) by the wise, as you are the creator. Let your name be Dhundhiraja. Your vision, worship, meditation, and remembrance are the means to achieve Dharma, Artha, Kama, and Moksha, and they grant sons and grandsons." Having said this, they worshipped him with Mandara flowers, Shami leaves, sprouts of Durva grass, green and white flowers, various cooked food offerings, and many kinds of fruits. They pleased him with heaps of jewels. Thus, all of them, being worshipped, remained as the seven-fold coverings. The fifty-six Vinayakas remained to protect Varanasi. One of them, with five faces, stands at the gate of Vishveshwara. Others, with different names, remain spread throughout Varanasi. With Vishveshwara established there, everyone remained in that very place. Brahma and all the sages performed their duties and all the people carried out their tasks as before, until the end of time. Thus, I have narrated everything that you asked regarding the incarnation of Dhundhiraja, which grants Dharma, desires, and happiness. Now, I will tell you about the departure of Divodasa.

Thus ends the forty-third chapter of the Krida Khanda in the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled the Description of the Praise of Dhundhiraja.

Total number of verses from the beginning: 6004 + 14 = 6018.

Chapter 44 : The Story of Divodasa

Kirti said: This incarnation of Dhundhiraja is famous as being born of Shakti, for the destruction of the difficult-to-conquer demons and for the protection of the three worlds. It is heard that he stood on one foot in Tunda City and on one foot in Varanasi, having conquered the demons. How can he be present in both places with both feet? O Brahman, you who know all, please dispel this doubt of mine.

The Sage said: What is impossible for Dhundhiraja, who is the form of the universe, the creator of the universe, the protector of the universe, the pervader of the universe, and the lord of the universe? He is the indweller of the universe, the supreme one, the destroyer of the universe, whose feet are the seven netherworlds and whose hair pervades the heavens. He has hands and feet everywhere, ears and eyes everywhere. He is beyond the reach of the mind, the Vedas, Brahma and other gods, and the yogis in essence; he is the form of air, earth, and water. In the pores of his skin, millions of universes wander like insects in the sky, stirred by the wind. O Mother, there is no room for doubt or debate regarding him.

For the Lord who has many forms and is all-powerful, he can do anything with the moving and non-moving world. By his will, O Queen, nectar can become poison, and poison can become nectar; what is it that cannot happen? The ways of the divine incarnation are described as wondrous. It is not possible for all the gods, even with Shesha, to determine how many incarnations there are, when they occur, or where. Therefore, abandon doubt and listen to the story I tell, by hearing which, O knower of all dharma, one is released from the accumulation of sins.

Long ago, there was a king named Divodasa, born in the Solar dynasty. He was generous, highly respected, and honored throughout the entire globe. He was as eloquent as Brihaspati, omniscient, always auspicious, learned in the Vedas, Shastras, and Puranas, and beloved by the wise. He possessed a body that could enchant women, yet he remained a master of his senses. He was always intent on doing good and averse to harming others. He was free from greed for others' wealth, was magnanimous, and valorous. Brahma, pleased by his penance, granted him an excellent kingdom.

When Varanasi was devoid of rain, the king, who was a benefactor of the world, was given the city. Because all the gods had been excluded, the wise king took charge. He himself became the Sun, Indra, Fire, Wind, and the Moon, and ruled the city righteously. Through his penance, rain fell, and people from here and there came to Avimukta and praised the king. His wife, named Sushila, was a devoted wife, righteous, charitable, and obedient to her husband's words.

The king had the learned scholars determine the conduct, behavior, and penance for the people, and he did not impose royal punishment. By the command of Shiva, all the winds followed the Mandara Mountain. There was no untimely death, no grief, and no suffering for anyone. The three types of calamities did not exist while he ruled the kingdom. The cries of distress ceased, and there was no great suffering caused by drought. Because of the rain, the crops of animals, birds, and humans flourished, and every living being became prosperous. The offerings of Svaha, Svadha, and Vashatkara were all performed as before. Those gods who had previously prayed to the king attained happiness. The king, whom all the gods praised as urged by the words of Brahma, also praised the gods greatly upon seeing them.

Thus ends the forty-fourth chapter of the Childhood Sports in the Play Section of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled The Story of Divodasa.

Chapter 45 : The Story of Divodasa

Kirti said: O Sage, why did all the gods go to Mount Mandara, and why did Shiva abandon the beautiful city of Varanasi? O divine sage, please tell me this, for I have a great doubt regarding this matter.

The Sage said: When a twelve-year drought occurred, destroying all stationary and moving beings, and the earth was devoid of Svaha, Svadha, and Vashatkara (sacrificial offerings), the gods, urged by the words of Brahma, prayed to Hara. They said: O Mahadeva, Lord of the world, abode of compassion, Shankara, Marichi is performing penance on Mount Mandara. O Lord, go to him to grant him a boon after his ten thousand years of penance.

The Sage said: Thus prayed to by the gods, Maheshvara, the abode of compassion, along with all the hosts of gods including Agni, Chandra, and Aryama, went to grant a boon to the great sage Marichi. Shiva saw that he was reduced to mere bones; had he not arrived at that moment, the sage would have given up his life. Pleased by his intense penance, the Lord of Gauri granted him his own form and, with his divine chariot and the sound of celestial instruments played by his attendants, suddenly took him to his own abode. Shiva then stayed on that excellent mountain along with all the gods.

Shiva could not find any flaw in King Divodasa, so he sent the gods to look for such a flaw with great care. Whichever god went to Kashi could not find any fault; instead, they established a Linga in their own name and stayed there. In the kingdom of Divodasa, all castes followed their own duties, and all twice-born men lived in their hermitages, observing the prescribed conduct. Students were devoted to their gurus, wives were chaste, and the people were righteous, charitable, and dedicated to vows and fasting. The ascetics performed their three daily ablutions, observed silence, and performed homa. The householders, free from sin, performed bathing, sandhya prayers, japa, homa, self-study, worship of deities, hospitality, and Vaishvadeva rituals with devotion.

Because of this, righteousness flourished, and there was excellent rainfall. The gods in heaven and the ancestors who consume Svadha were delighted. There were no barren women, no women without flowers, no widows, and no women who lost their children. There was neither drought nor excessive rain, and there was no fear from internal or external enemies. There were no parrots, locusts, or mice at any time. Thus, all crops were produced in abundance.

The King, the best of men, made a daily proclamation: "Whoever eats or lives in Kashi without seeing or bathing in the presence of Vishveshvara, Madhava, Dhundhi, Bhairava, Dandapani, Guha, Ganga, Manikarni, or Bhavani, shall be punished." There was not even a trace of sin there as long as the noble King ruled. Shiva, however, wished to take over the kingdom of Kashi but could not find a flaw. Distressed by his separation from Varanasi, he sent the eight Bhairavas to create obstacles. He told them to cause even the slightest sin in the King's realm so that he might take over the kingdom of Kashi.

O virtuous one, they went quickly after receiving the command from Sadashiva. Upon seeing Varanasi, they were rested by the act of bathing and, finding no sin in the King, they began to live in Kashi. Shiva became anxious when they did not return. He then sent the Adityas to find a flaw in the King, but they too, seeing the merit of the place, stayed in Kashi, filled with joy. Thinking that the city should not be abandoned and that Hara had no task there, he sent sixty-four Yoginis. They too, finding not even an atom of sin in Divodasa, stayed in Varanasi, worshipping the eternal Shiva.

Then he sent Durga, the destroyer of sorrows. She also, finding no sin, stayed outside the village. She pleased the Lord through meditation and fulfilled the desires of humans. Then Shiva quickly sent the Dikpalas (guardians of the directions). They went to Varanasi but could not see any minor sin; they established Lingas in their own names and lived there in joy.

Later, the sages were sent by the eager Shiva. They went quickly, urged by Shiva, and were happy. After going and performing the pilgrimage rituals, they stayed in Varanasi. While going to offer blessings, they observed the King's conduct. The King worshipped them all with devotion, offering wealth and clothes. They did not accept his wealth, nor did they see any minor sin in him. They established Lingas in their own names and performed supreme penance.

Then all the gods were sent by him for the accomplishment of the task, but they too did not see any evil conduct in the righteous King. Whoever I send to Kashi does not return. Then Rudra, filled with anxiety, could not reach a decision. He thought in his mind, "When will I see that city? Only when there is sin in the kingdom of Divodasa will I be able to attain Kashi; not otherwise. Without Dhundhi and Madhava, all the gods are useless; they do not return and stay there in Kashi, absorbed in meditation."

Thus ends the forty-fifth chapter of the childhood deeds in the story of Divodasa, in the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, describing the contemplation of Shiva.

Chapter 46 : Dhundiraja Assumes the Form of an Astrologer

The Sage said: Tormented by the separation from Avimukta (Varanasi), Shiva, being distressed, bowed to Dhundhi (Ganesha), who is the master of all purposes, and prayed to him.

Shiva said: You are the cause of the five elements and the cause of all causes. You are the embodiment of consciousness and bliss, the object of meditation for the whole world, and the ultimate reach of the Vedanta. You are the Pradhana (primordial matter) and the Purusha (supreme spirit), the one who divides the three Gunas. You are all-pervading, the treasure of the universe, and dedicated to the protection of the world. You take on various incarnations and are intent on removing the burdens of the earth. You are capable of protecting the gods and destroying the demons. You are the refuge for the twice-born, for those who follow righteousness, and for those who are distressed and seeking shelter. Since you have taken the form of a child out of your own free will, and you are the very embodiment of Brahman, to whom else should I go for refuge, being pained by the separation from Kashi?

Dhundhi said: You are the one who sends others, those who are experts in all knowledge. Why do you, O Sadashiva, who are all-seeing, act as if you are deluded?

Shiva said: Go to Avimukta right now, O Gajanana, to create obstacles for Divodasa and to ensure the fulfillment of my purpose. Delude the people with your Maya so that his merit may diminish.

Dhundhi said: I shall go quickly, O Mahadeva; you need not worry. I will accomplish your benefit and reveal my own city, having expelled Divodasa, who is the divider of the people's sins.

The Sage said: Having said this and bowed to Shiva, Parvati, and the six-faced Kartikeya, and having circumambulated Narada and his Guru, the Lord, who is the repository of all knowledge and arts, departed. He reached the holy Varanasi and saw its holiness. He immediately became an astrologer, having perceived the sins of the city. He had a golden radiance, a divine body, and was adorned with a garland of pearls. He wore golden earrings in both ears, possessed the thirty-two auspicious marks, and knew the past, present, and future. A belt adorned with great jewels was tied around his waist. He was dressed in yellow garments, anointed with divine fragrances, and his body was extremely beautiful, captivating the hearts of women. Even chaste women were attracted to this Brahmin. He would speak of all the questions they contemplated in their minds. Therefore, chaste women followed him to ask questions, leaving behind their children, husbands, brothers, and other loved ones. He spoke to the people about their dreams, which were shown to them by his own Maya. He granted many boons to all the people who served him. By the power of those boons, even severe leprosy was destroyed. Through his blessings, barren women became mothers. By looking at their hands, he would describe their great fortune and their deeds. He would instantly reveal what each person had consumed or was about to consume. The people in the city were amazed and filled with joy. They said, "Such a Brahmin, a knower of all and a repository of virtues, has never been seen before. There has never been one like him, nor will there be." Knowing his credibility, they worshipped him with wealth and jewels. He would instantly tell them what objects they held in their hands. He knew the intentions of the poor and the effortless who came to ask him questions. He would speak of their intentions within three days, saying, "You will become wealthy," and it would happen just so. He would tell them about lost or gone things, and everything would happen exactly as he said. As Dhundhi, in the form of a Brahmin, spoke in this way, the news reached the ears of the King within two or three months.

The people said: Even the King's wives, who were devoted to their husbands, had not worshipped any other deity until this time. Without their husband, they would not even look at anyone without his permission, nor would they contemplate whether they would have a son or a daughter. Though forbidden by their companions, they thought, "Dhundhi will come here." Having called him, they brought him into a secret place. Seating him on a beautiful seat, the King's wives worshipped him. Having washed his feet, overwhelmed by his sight, they rubbed musk and sandalwood on his body. One of them, sitting near him, offered him betel leaves herself. They asked him many questions, and he answered them all. When their faith was established, they were all amazed. Thus, they worshipped him daily, abandoning their household duties, and became entirely devoted to him. "Such a knower of the past, present, and future has never been seen before," they said, praising him as the best of all astrologers. When the King arose, they quickly sent him away. Abandoning their thoughts of their husband, they always thought only of him. Divodasa, too, having heard of him, called him and bowed down. Seating him on his own throne, he worshipped him with offerings and the like. He respectfully offered him cows, respectful offerings, wealth, and clothes. Whatever he asked him, he answered with joy. Forgetting his own deity, the King thought only of him. In private, he asked him many different kinds of questions. When his faith was established, he respectfully requested, "I will give you villages, grain, and wealth; stay here near me, for I have seen many miracles in you." Then Dhundhi, free from worldly attachments, said to the King, "I have left behind wives, sons, daughters, and homes to come to Varanasi. Neither villages, nor wealth, nor grain please me, as I have no desires. I will tell you one thing; you should keep it in your heart. On the seventeenth day from now, O King, some other person will come; whatever he says, you must do it without hesitation. It will be for your ultimate benefit, O King, there is no doubt. By this much, I have already obtained villages, grain, and wealth."

Thus ends the forty-sixth chapter, titled "The Appearance of the Astrologer," in the Childhood Deeds of the Sports Section of the Shri Ganesha Purana.

Chapter 47 : Divodasa's Abandonment of the Kingdom

The King said: O best of sages, I will certainly follow your instructions. If I act otherwise, may I be cursed by Lord Shiva.

Gritsamada said: In this way, the people of Kashi were all brought under his influence. Abandoning all their duties, they served only him. Revealing the past, the present, and the distant future, Lord Vishnu, in the form of a Buddhist, stayed outside the five-krosha boundary. Arriving there, he created delusion in all living beings through his divine illusion (Maya). Opposing the righteous path, he preached doctrines contrary to the Vedas and Smritis. By his own words, he brought all the great people under his control and respectfully disparaged the worship of idols. He asked, "Why is a physical image or a golden idol worshipped when the Supreme Soul resides within the heart of every foolish-minded person? Why cut down living trees or kill animals? Why give your wealth to others?"

He argued that when the body is destroyed, the five elements merge into the five elements, so one should happily enjoy cooked food with ghee. He taught that the body itself is the soul and should be worshipped with various enjoyments. Consequently, all the people abandoned their own paths. Even abandoning the Brahmins, they indulged in supreme pleasures. His wife, named Kamala, fulfilling their desires, stayed in Varanasi and deluded all the women. Day by day, she diligently taught them the wicked path. The intellects of the residents of the cities and villages became divided. They began to think that just as the soul does not differ in a person due to the face, nose, or hands, the soul does not differ due to these four types of bodies. Thus tempted, the women began to consort with other men.

The Brahmins became lax in their Agnihotras, sacrifices, vows, charity, oblations, and the worship of gods and Brahmins. They became doubtful about the slaughter of sacrificial animals and, worshipping each other at home, indulged in supreme pleasures. They performed Shradhas and vows for their own sake. Thus, righteousness declined, and sin increased.

Then the Buddhist quickly went to the palace of Divodasa. He was worshipped with great devotion by the King on a beautiful seat. Then, the expert in speech spoke to Divodasa.

The Buddhist said: Listen, O King, I speak for your benefit; hear it and act accordingly. This Varanasi was created by the power of the wielder of the trident (Shiva). Even during the dissolution, it is held on the tip of His trident for all beings. Only the virtuous who desire liberation at the time of death should stay here. The sinful are kept outside by Bhairava. The protection of the virtuous is ensured by Him. You have stayed here only as long as your merit lasted. You are no longer the King of Kashi; sin has spread in your kingdom, O King, which leads to hell.

The King said: You have spoken well to me; I will follow your words. What was said by the astrologer and Dhundhi (Ganesha) is indeed the truth. Tell me, who are you who have come here for my benefit? If it is possible to know, O Supreme Person.

Gritsamada said: Then, filled with compassion, He revealed His four-armed form to the King, holding the conch, discus, and mace. He displayed His form as both the collective and the individual, the embodiment of consciousness. The King, along with his ancestors, said to Him, "I am blessed. Having abandoned the kingdom earlier, I performed great penance. This supreme kingdom was given to me by Brahma by force. By seeing You, my cycle of birth and death has ended; there is no doubt about this. I seek only this from You; I have taken refuge in You."

The Sage said: Being addressed thus, the Supreme Lord spoke to the excellent King, "O best of kings, by the grace of Vishweshwara, you shall attain supreme liberation. Abandon the kingdom and be happy. Otherwise, Bhairava and Dandapani will force you out." Hearing these words, the King fell into deep contemplation. Reflecting on the cause, he understood everything. Bowing his head with devotion to the astrologer, Ganesha, and the illusory Hari (Vishnu) in the form of a Buddhist, he asked who they were.

Realizing this, the great Vishnu assumed His own form—the four-armed, all-pervading Lord holding the conch, discus, mace, and lotus, adorned with yellow garments, the refuge of all worlds. The King said, "I am blessed, and so are my ancestors, for I have seen the feet that grant liberation through the power of merit. Grant me supreme liberation and accept this kingdom." Vishnu said, "Shiva will grant you liberation."

Gritsamada said: Having said this to Divodasa, the great Lord of Yoga, Hari, disappeared and returned to His abode in the form of a Buddhist. He sent a messenger to Shiva for His arrival, saying, "In Divodasa's kingdom, I have caused much increase in unrighteousness. The kingdom has been abandoned by the King due to the astrologer and Ganesha. Come quickly, O Lord of the Universe, to Your own city of Varanasi."

Then the King, having abandoned the royal insignia, performed penance. He installed a famous Shiva Linga in a supreme palace, naming it after himself, which grants liberation to the righteous and fulfills the desires of those with worldly wishes, awaiting the vision of Shankara, the granter of boons.

Thus ends the forty-seventh chapter of the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Abandonment of Divodasa's Kingdom."

Chapter 48 : The Granting of Boons to Kirti

The Sage said: Knowing that the kingdom had been renounced, Dhundhi, the bestower of boons, assumed his own form and resided there for the sake of all beings. He sent a messenger to Shankara, requesting his swift arrival. Filled with great joy, the wise Lord Shiva mounted his bull and proceeded to the city of Varanasi, accompanied by his attendants amidst the sounds of divine instruments. After blessing Jaigishavya, who stood motionless like an anthill, he bowed to Dhundhi and spoke.

Shiva said: O Lord of the Universe, O embodiment of the world, you are the one who creates, sustains, and dissolves the universe. O Ganesha, you observe the good and evil deeds of beings and grant them various experiences accordingly. Even Brahma creates through your inspiration, and Hari protects while I perform the dissolution; you are the one who divides the functions of the three gunas. By your grace, Hari, the lotus-born Brahma, and I are able to perform our respective duties. Without you, the Vedic rituals would be in vain, and it is by your power that the enemies of the gods are destroyed. In ancient times, the infinite power of Prakriti worshipped you and slew the wicked Mahisha, protecting the entire world that trembled at the falling of mountains caused by your breath. You are immeasurable, the witness of all worlds, the imperishable, and the cause of all causes. All the Vedas reside in you, and even Shesha supports the earth through devotion to you. Those who bow to you, worship you, and remember you in their minds, fixing their devotion solely upon you, attain liberation and are never reborn. You possess infinite forms, feet, eyes, heads, ears, arms, and tongues. You are the essence of infinite knowledge, the cause of countless universes, and the supreme light. By your grace, I have finally seen this Avimukta (Varanasi) after long effort.

The Sage said: Having praised and worshipped him in this manner, Shankara made a request. My sorrow of separation has vanished upon reaching the city of Varanasi. Now, always protect my devotees and this city. Without your grace, no one can ever reside in Kashi. Only when one receives the mercy of Dandapani Bhairava and you, will I grant them the Taraka Brahma at the end of their lives; it cannot be otherwise. Whoever worships you on the fourth day of the month of Magha, when it falls on a Tuesday at moonrise, with cakes, sweets, and other offerings, you shall remove his distress. Whoever recites this hymn, you shall grant him all his desires and bestow wealth in many ways. Whoever rises in the morning and recites this hymn with devotion at the three junctures of the day, or even just once, shall attain both worldly enjoyment and liberation. Because you seek out and provide all things for people here, you shall be known in the three worlds as Dhundhi. This name, Dhundhi, shall be the giver of liberation and the destroyer of sins. Remembering it shall grant success in all undertakings.

The Sage said: Having said this, the great Lord Mahadeva installed the image of Dhundhi, made of Gandaki stone, in a beautifully constructed temple. Then, all the gods entered their respective abodes. Shankara granted liberation to his devotee Divodasa.

Gritsamada said: Thus, Divodasa was deluded by His own Maya. Vishnu, in the form of Buddha, worked for his welfare. I have told you all the power that was manifested by Dhundhi. Such is the influence of this deity, by whose boon the son was revived.

Ka said: Having thus spoken to her about the deeds of Dhundhi, Gritsamada took leave and went to his own hermitage. At that very moment, Kirti took her son and departed. She went to the temple of Dhundhi, which was filled with many men and women. Seeing the great festival being celebrated, Kirti bowed to him. She went to holy Varanasi, which grants liberation at the time of death. On the fourth day of the month of Magha, which was a Tuesday, she was filled with joy. The deity was worshipped by crowds of devotees skilled in dance and song, with cooked food and milk-pudding served in many golden vessels. He was adorned with various ornaments, divine garlands, garments, jewels, and pearls. Seeing the heaps of gold and jewels offered as dakshina, she became anxious, thinking that the face of the deity was not visible. She thought, "I am destitute, what can I offer him?" She worshipped him with great devotion using Durva grass, Sami leaves, and Mandara flowers she had gathered, and prayed for the prosperity of her son. Dhundhiraja was not as pleased by heaps of wealth as he was by the Sami, Mandara, and Durva. Then, all the devotees returned to their respective homes.

Both the mother and son remained in his presence. Pleased by their fasting and devotional attitude, the powerful Dhundhiraja appeared from within the image. They saw the form of the Great Lord as described by the sage—the radiant, divine form adorned with all ornaments and a crown. He had ten arms, beautiful eyes like lotus petals, and wore a magnificent necklace of priceless gems and pearls. Seeing this supreme form, which was clad in yellow garments and adorned with excellent tusks, they were immersed in an ocean of bliss. They forgot to perform worship or offer salutations. The Lord said, "Choose a boon, O virtuous one. I am well-pleased and will grant what is in your mind. I am not as pleased by pearls, jewels, or various types of wealth as I am by Sami leaves and Mandara flowers, O beautiful one."

Ka said: Hearing those words, Kirti was filled with supreme joy. Regaining her composure, she spoke to the elephant-faced Lord. "What can a woman do, O all-knowing one, O embodiment of all?" By his command, and having gained understanding through his proximity, she spoke.

Kirti said: You are partless, ego-less, without attributes, and the Lord of the world. You are full of bliss, supreme bliss, ancient, and beyond the beyond. You are the guardian of the directions, the sun, the moon, the rivers, and the oceans. You are the earth, the air, the sky, the fire, the water, and the space; you are the Gandharvas, the serpents, and the demons. You are the embodiment of all moving and non-moving things, the ocean of mercy for the helpless. You have now manifested in the form of Vinayaka and others. Today, my eyes are blessed, my birth is fruitful, and my husband and son are blessed. My ancestors, lineage, character, beauty, knowledge, and penance are all fulfilled. Through the merit of previous births, I have seen you, the swift bestower of grace. By your command, O Lord, your name was established for him. My co-wife gave him poison, but he was revived by the sage Gritsamada through your devotion, O Ruler of the Universe. The worship with Sami leaves was told as the way to attain you. Through the power of the sage, I have seen you, the remover of the three types of suffering. If you are pleased, O Lord, grant my son your devotion, supreme fame in the three worlds, detachment while in power, long life, all virtues, strength, glory, happiness, forgiveness, victory in all battles, and supreme love for the twice-born and the gods.

Dhundhi said: Whatever boons you have asked for, I have granted them, O sinless one. Your son shall perform a thousand sacrifices and live for a thousand years. He shall be calm, self-controlled, my devotee, and a ruler. He shall bring everything under his control through the four means. My meditation and the chanting of my name shall always be his. At the end, remembering me, he shall attain my own form.

Ka said: Having thus granted her boons, the satisfied Vinayaka gave her his own axe (parashu), and thus he became clearly known as Parshubahu (the one with the axe in his hand). The Lord himself gave this name and then disappeared.

Chapter 49 : Describing the Fruits of Shami and Mandara

The King said: Having thus obtained the boon, Kirti spent the remaining night. At dawn, she bathed, quickly worshipped the idol, and departed with her son. Filled with both joy and sorrow due to the separation from the Lord, she reached her city, which was decorated with various flags, banners, sprinklings of water, and incense. The King and the Queen, both chanting the name of Dhundhi, learned of their son’s arrival and went to meet him with the royal chariot, an army, and the sound of musical instruments. They brought him to the city, which was famous by the name Karnapura. The mother smelled her son’s head and embraced him with great affection. Overjoyed, she spoke in a voice choked with emotion, "O son, who grants quick grace, you have been weary for many days. Seeing you, I have attained supreme bliss, as if I have obtained nectar." The King then distributed clothes and gifts to all the people. Kirti and the King spoke to each other, and she narrated the entire sequence of events to the King. They embraced, kissed, laughed, and joked with each other, and in their great joy, they engaged in the playful battle of love, unashamed.

After a few days, knowing his son to be virtuous, humble, a knower of all duties, and skilled in the science of politics, the King made preparations for the coronation. He invited noble Brahmins, friends, and fellow kings and bestowed the kingdom upon his son. At an auspicious moment, when seven favorable planets were aligned, he performed the Abhyudayika Shraddha and the Swastivachana. He performed the coronation with water mixed with various substances, while the Vedas (Rig, Yajur, and Sama) were chanted and all musical instruments played. Having satisfied the Brahmins with gifts of wealth and jewels, the King himself sought permission to take the vow of forest-dwelling. Having asked for their consent, the King dismissed everyone and remained established in his own spiritual practice. Thereafter, Parashubahu ruled the earth. Through the ethics of the scriptures and his generosity, he earned fame and became renowned in the three worlds for his valor and strength. He wore the Mandara idol of Dhundhi around his neck and never performed worship without Shami leaves and Durva grass. He enjoyed various pleasures and many wives righteously. After begetting sons and giving away gifts for a thousand years, he ruled the kingdom, then handed it over to his son and departed for heaven. He stayed in heaven for many eons, possessing the form of Dhundhi.

The Sage said: Thus, the influence of the Shami tree has been described to you in brief, along with the merit of worshipping it in the context of the Mandara. Therefore, O Sage, you too should worship me with Shami and Mandara. A flower or leaf offered with devotion becomes like nectar. One who offers forbidden leaves or flowers goes to hell. Whatever deity is primary to a person, or whatever is dear to others, if a devotee offers it without distinction, he incurs no sin. By worshipping all five deities in this way, one attains devotion to them. The Sattvic devotee merges into the deity; the Rajasic devotee attains a similar form; both the Sattvic and Rajasic attain the proximity of the deity; and the Tamasic devotee attains the same world as the deity. The worshipper thus attains the fruit, for devotion in these three ways is never in vain.

Vyasa said: O Brahma, in which world does Vinayaka reside? I must clear this doubt of mine. Whom else can I ask, leaving you, who are all-knowing?

Brahma said: I have told this to Narada, and he told it to Mudgala, and he told the King of Kashi about the auspicious world of Vinayaka. It was created long ago by the power of the wish-fulfilling Shakti. Vinayaka himself named it his own world. The King of Kashi, while seated in his celestial chariot, saw it with his physical eyes. Having reached that place, neither man nor woman experiences sorrow or duality. One who dwells there attains the form of light and lives for a Brahma-Kalpa. He enjoys the ocean of sugarcane juice. During the time of the Great Dissolution, that which remains indestructible is the eternal resting place of that deity. Siddhi and Buddhi serve him, and the Sama Veda sings his praises. The Kalpavriksha grants whatever a human desires there. Through its influence, even unthought-of riches are obtained. I have described all the divine worlds in various ways, but the power of Ganesha’s own world is beyond my description. I have told you this in brief; what else do you wish to hear?

Chapter 50 : Description of the Ganesha Realm

The Sage said: O King, you must tell me how the King of Kashi attained that supreme abode through the instructions of Mudgala.

Ka said: In the course of a pilgrimage, the sage Mudgala visited that King. The King, having worshipped the omniscient sage, asked him.

The King asked: Which is the realm of Vinayaka, and how can I attain it?

The Sage said: The realm of Vinayaka is known by two names: Svananda-bhuvana and Nija-loka. When the demons Devantaka and Narantaka are born, Vinayaka will also incarnate in human form to slay them. In this world of men, he will act as a child, performing wonderful deeds and moving according to his own will, possessing great power. When he dwells in the house of a poor but devoted devotee named Shukla, having bestowed upon him the incomparable wealth of Indra, he will display his prowess after being brought there for marriage. O King of Kashi, O mighty-armed one, when he comes to your house, you will attain his own realm through your devotion to him.

Ka said: Having said this, the sage departed. The noble King of Kashi, engaging in devotion to Ganesha, began to wait for that time. After a long time, Vinayaka, the Lord who assumes forms for his pastimes, incarnated in the house of Kashyapa. He slew various demons, performed many pastimes, and instantly removed the poverty of Shukla. Then, the mighty Lord came to the palace of the King of Kashi and slew the powerful demons Devantaka and Narantaka. He then departed for Svananda-bhuvana, which is located in the midst of the Ocean of Milk.

The King of Kashi, pining for him, constantly thought of him. Seeing any man, he would think, "This is Vinayaka," and thus he perceived the entire world as identical to Him. Day and night, he remained immersed in that state. Sometimes the King would remain without food, sometimes he would eat as he pleased, sometimes he would laugh, and sometimes he would dance. He would sleep for a long time, seeing Vinayaka in his dreams. Seeing the King in this state, his ministers became deeply worried, fearing that if the people found out, foreign enemies might attack.

While the ministers were worrying in the palace, Mudgala, having left his meditation, came to the King. Having gained enlightenment through his sight, the King bowed to the sage. After offering him a seat and worshipping him according to the rites, the King, with folded hands, said: "My lineage and my birth are blessed. My parents and my eyes are blessed by your sight, O Sage, for by your grace, one attains the supreme abode that liberates men from the world. I have seen you, whom I have attained through my merit, after suffering greatly from the pain of separation."

The sage, being highly pleased, spoke to him: "I have never seen or heard of a soul as humble as you. I am satisfied with your worship; I shall grant you your desire."

Ka said: Pleased by these words, the King of Kashi said to him: "O Sage, I desire to attain Svananda-bhuvana with this very body and to remain there for a long time, not desiring worldly wealth." Mudgala, pleased by this request, replied: "O King, you shall attain the Svananda-bhuvana of Ganesha with this very physical body, never to return. After enjoying the wealth of this world for five thousand years, you shall attain the time of a Brahma-kalpa, and thereafter, you shall attain Svananda."

The King asked: "What is that realm like? What is its name? Tell me the truth, O Sage. By what merit is it attained? I have heard of all the other realms."

Mudgala said: "Listen, O King, to the glory I heard from the sage Kapila. The realms you have heard of are generally well-known, but the path to this realm is extremely profound, O King. It is called Divya-loka and also Nija-loka. Vinayaka resides there on the Kamadayini pedestal. It is five thousand yojanas in extent, O noble one. It shines on a ground of gold and jewels, illuminating all directions. This divine Svananda-bhuvana is situated in the middle of the Ocean of Sugarcane Juice. It is not attained by Vedas, charity, vows, sacrifices, chanting, or various austerities. It is attained only through the grace of Vinayaka and constant devotion. The Lord of Obstacles, who is both the collective and the individual, always resides there. With his feet, he pervades the seven netherworlds; with his ears, all directions; with his hair, the sky; and he rests in the Adhara-chakra. The virtuous meditate upon him in the fire-circle in the center of the earth, in the two-petaled lotus. One who is proficient in the Khechari-mudra can meditate upon him; no one else is capable. He is the brilliance of the thousand-petaled lotus of the universe. He is the form of light in the heart, in the twelve-petaled lotus, the ten-petaled navel-chakra, the six-petaled lotus in the linga, and the sixteen-petaled lotus in the throat. Everywhere, the Lord of Obstacles shines as the form of light, just as Brahma does in Satyaloka. Vaikuntha is the twelve-petaled lotus where Vishnu always resides. In the throat-lotus, he is the five-faced one, just as he is in Kailasa with his attendants. He has the sun, moon, and fire as his eyes, and the earth as his belly. He pervades the twenty-one heavens, and his hair is the medicinal herbs. The rivers and oceans are but a drop of his dharma. In him, the universes shine like dust particles. The thirty-three crore gods and the thousands of living beings shine within him like gnats in an Udumbara fruit.

O King, I shall briefly describe the structure of that realm. The lofty peak of Mount Meru touches the summit of Kailasa. It is a thousand yojanas of void, inaccessible to the greatest sages. There, a supreme power named Bhramika resides. By her consent, the bees and the white ones revolve. The Adhara-shakti resides in the lotus seat, and above her head is the Kamadayini, shining like gold. Above her head is a great pedestal, shining like ten million suns. She bears a fierce mass of matted hair and a face that illuminates the ten directions. It is ten thousand yojanas in extent and length. In the midst of that, which shines like countless suns, shines the Svananda-bhuvana, one yojana in size, where countless houses made of gold and jewels shine like elephant pearls. Through his grace, one attains it, free from sorrow and delusion. To its north shines the great Ocean of Sugarcane Juice. In its midst is the beautiful Padmini, and in it, the thousand-petaled lotus shines like the moon. On the bed in its center, made of gold and jewels, Vinayaka reclines, adorned with divine garments. Siddhi and Buddhi always massage his feet with joy and supreme devotion. The Samaveda, in its three-fold form, sings his praises. The scriptures, in their embodied forms, praise him, and all the Puranas describe his virtues. He appears there in the form of a child, adorned with his trunk, with soft limbs, the color of the rising sun, large eyes, and tusks. He is the self-ruler, wearing a crown, earrings, and a musk tilak. He wears divine garlands and garments and is anointed with divine fragrances. He wears a necklace of pearls and jewels. He possesses the brilliance of infinite suns and wears the crescent moon on his head. By merely remembering him, one is instantly freed from sin, just as men are by remembering the Ganga. The powers Tejasvati and Jvalini, shining like a thousand suns, always stand by his side. There is no cold, no old age, no fatigue, no sweat, no lethargy, no hunger, no thirst, and no sorrow there. Those who have attained it through great merit remain immersed in the ocean of bliss. The all-pervading knower of the universe, in the form of a child, resides there."

Mudgala said: "Thus, I have described to you the realm of Ganesha according to my understanding. Therefore, worship Him alone with undivided devotion, O Lord."

Ka said: Having said this, the sage Mudgala departed to his own abode. The King of Kashi became devoted to the worship of Ganesha. After ruling for five thousand years, he ascended a chariot with this very body and departed for that Svananda-dhama.

Thus ends the fiftieth chapter of the Krida-khanda in the Shri Ganesha Purana, entitled "Description of the Ganesha Realm."

Chapter 51 : The King of Kashi's Ascent to the Chariot

Vyasa said: How was the Lord of Kashi able to propitiate the King of Obstacles (Ganesha)? And how did he attain the supreme abode while still in his mortal body? Tell me this, O Lord, for I am never satisfied with hearing these stories.

The Lord (Ganesha) said: Listen carefully, O wise one, to this story that destroys all sins. I shall narrate this ancient history of how the King, having been liberated, reached the Swananda realm. Having learned of the glory of Ganesha from the ocean of wisdom, Mudgala, the King became filled with devotion in body, mind, and speech.

Day after day, for the pleasure of Vinayaka, he offered gifts of cows, elephants, chariots, horses, houses, grains, and ten other types of gifts with great devotion. By satisfying the Brahmins with sweet meals and wealth, he prayed daily for firm devotion to Ganesha. On the auspicious day of Magha Shukla Chaturthi, which fell on a Tuesday, he performed his morning ablutions, sandhya prayers, and daily rituals. After adorning himself with various ornaments and garments, he invited all the people, Brahmins, and friends, and worshipped them with various gems and clothes.

The King kept looking repeatedly for the arrival of the celestial chariot, and he kept stopping those who wished to leave the assembly. Then, Vinayaka, knowing the time had come for that great soul, sent two messengers with a brilliant chariot.

Ganesha said: O Pramoda and Amoda, go to the earth, to the King of Kashi, and bring my devotee here, seated in this excellent chariot.

The Lord said: Having received that command and bowing to Him, both messengers descended from the divine realm and approached the King of Kashi with the celestial chariot. The people were dazzled by its brilliance and thought it was the fire of dissolution; some thought it was a lightning strike in a clear sky, while others speculated that the sun disk was falling. Hearing the sound of bells, musical instruments, and the songs of Gandharvas and Apsaras, others realized that the chariot had arrived by the grace of Ganesha, descending into the courtyard of the King of Kashi, shining with splendor.

Pramoda and Amoda, wearing divine garments and ornaments, arrived near the King. They looked like Cupids, radiant with divine garlands and ointments. The King bowed to them, seated them on his own throne, and worshipped them. Before the King could ask them anything, the two messengers spoke in the hearing of all the people.

They said: Listen, O King, we shall tell you the auspicious words of Vinayaka. There is no devotee in the three worlds as dear to Him as you, O King. He meditates on you day and night and speaks of your virtues incessantly. You are blessed; your birth has been made fruitful by your meritorious deeds. By your sight, the great darkness of sin is quickly destroyed. We do not know what penance you performed in previous births. This Vinayaka, who is the Supreme Brahman in the form of a child, came to your house and displayed various pastimes. The glory of the Lord and the devotee of the Lord is beyond comprehension. Since Lord Vinayaka is always thinking of you, He has sent this divine chariot endowed with majesty for your sake. We have come to take you, as we are bound by His command, for the Lord told us to bring the King of Kashi quickly.

As all the people listened, the King heard their words. He became immersed in an ocean of nectar, shedding tears of joy repeatedly. Having regained his composure, he bowed to them and said: My birth, my parents, my devotion, and my wealth are all blessed. It is because of my good fortune that I have seen your auspicious feet, as you have come in the form of Vinayaka to take me to the supreme abode.

He spoke of the Supreme Brahman, the eternal, the formless, the bliss of consciousness, the cause of the creation, sustenance, and dissolution of the universe, which is beyond the reach of physical eyes, residing in the middle of the ocean of sugarcane juice. He praised the Lord who is smaller than the smallest and larger than the largest, who is everywhere yet unattached, who takes on forms and enjoys qualities, and who is ready to remove the burden of the earth. He said: Such great mercy has been shown to me, a pauper.

Having said this, he bowed to them, embraced everyone, and looked at them. He said to the messengers: Please board the chariot, and I shall follow you into this chariot which is rare for mortals. After bowing to the messengers, he placed his son in their hands and instructed him to rule the subjects with righteousness and strength. He said: This is what I have experienced, as told by Mudgala.

Having said this, he boarded the excellent chariot while still in his mortal body. As he sat there, worshipped by the messengers in many ways, his body became radiant like the sun. They worshipped him with divine fragrances, ornaments, and garments. They departed at the speed of the wind, showing the King the realm of ghosts, spirits, and demons—the place of those who performed evil deeds. There, the beings had distorted forms, with feet pointing upwards and heads downwards. Some had faces on their backs, others had heads for eyes, some had eyes on their chests or backs, with thin necks and large bellies. Just as particles of dust dance in a sunbeam, these miserable beings wandered in the sky in various forms.

The King asked: O great messengers, tell me, what is this realm?

Chapter 52 : The Description of Reaching Svananda Bhuvana

The messengers said: O best of kings, this is the realm of ghosts, spirits, and demons, where those who are given to slander, malice, and theft arrive. Beyond this, he saw the realm of the Asuras and Rakshasas, where beings of various forms, with matted hair standing upward, appeared terrifying. They had fearsome faces, lolling tongues, voices like thunder, and bodies like mountains. Seeing them resembling pits of fire, the king asked the messengers again, "Whose city is this? Tell me, for I am curious." They replied, "This is the realm of wicked Danavas and Rakshasas. Sinners who are devoid of Vedic and scriptural duties reside here."

Beyond that, he saw a realm frequented by Gandharvas, where houses were made of silver and gold, and the land was composed of gems and gold. Men and women there were skilled in dance, song, and the delights of love. Seeing that place of great happiness, the king asked again, "Whose city is this? Tell me." They replied, "This is the city of the Gandharvas. Those who are singers in the abodes of the gods and those who have earned great merit attain this excellent city. It is also the great city of the Siddhas, Charanas, Yakshas, and Guhyakas, reachable through various vows, charities, and the accumulation of merit."

Then the messengers showed the king the magnificent city of Indra, earned through a hundred horse sacrifices, and accessible through the giving of gold, gems, cows, chariots, and elephants, as well as through various holy pilgrimages. They showed him the beautiful and auspicious palace of Indra. Seeing it, the king said that what he had heard was exactly what he now saw. Then they showed him the auspicious realm of Agni, where many who performed Agnihotra sacrifices shone with the brilliance of fire.

Then they showed the king the realm of Yama, both auspicious and inauspicious, where both the virtuous and the sinful go. It appears as Dharma to the righteous and as a cruel place to the wicked. The virtuous enjoy all pleasures, while the wicked suffer in hells. Beings experience the fruits of their respective karmas in various ways; some are cooked in the Kumbhipaka hell, others are cut by leaf-shaped blades, struck with iron clubs, or pierced by thorns. The wicked are cast into the terrifying hells of Tamisra, Andhatamisra, the pit of worms, and the pit of pus. Seeing them, the king closed his eyes, saying, "Move on, move on."

Then they showed him the excellent realm of Kubera, which was more astonishing and superior even to the realm of Indra. It was filled with radiant houses of gems and gold, attained by the highly fortunate through the donation of gems, gold, and the Tula-dana (weighing oneself against gold) millions of times over. Then they showed him the excellent realm of Varuna, which is beyond the abode of Kubera, attained by donating food at holy places. There, beings with dry bodies live in happiness, enjoying the waters. Those who provide water for wells, ponds, and tanks are seen there.

Then they showed him the pleasant realm of Vayu, where those who offer fans, cool water, and camphor in the summer reside, moving about according to their own will. Then they showed him the solar realm, which is difficult to attain. Sages who are devoted to the five-fire penance and dedicated to the worship of the Sun, having fulfilled their desires and shining with solar radiance, live there undisturbed for a hundred Brahma-kalpas.

Then they showed him the lunar realm of the stars, attained by noble souls through the donation of pearls and gold and the performance of Soma sacrifices. Then they showed him the realm of Goloka. Those who duly donate thousands of cows to Brahmanas reside there for as many Kalpas as there are hairs on the bodies of those cows.

Then they showed him the eternal realm of Satyaloka. Those who are truthful, knowers of the Vedas, devoted to Vedic study, of good conduct, learned in the scriptures, readers of the Puranas, givers of food, performers of sacrifices, visitors to holy places, Brahmanas who do not accept gifts, those who help others, ascetics, and those devoted to charity go to Satyaloka. It is vast and expansive, where Brahma is served day and night by hosts of sages, royal sages, divine sages, Danavas, Gandharvas, and Apsaras, who praise him with devotion. Seeing the houses of Indra surrounding it, the king was amazed and said, "I am blessed and favored by the merciful Ganesha, for I have seen these heavenly realms that are so difficult to attain."

The messengers then showed him Vaikuntha, famous in the three worlds, where Vaishnavas devoted to the Lord go. It is the best of all realms, having no equal. Even Shesha, with his many mouths, or Brahma himself, is not capable of describing it. One who bathes in sacred waters, gives sesame seeds, donates cows, studies the Gita, and helps living beings quickly reaches the Vishnu-loka, which spans five thousand Yojanas. The temples there, created by Vishvakarma, shine with the light of suns and moons, radiant with gems, gold, and silver, where darkness cannot enter due to the brilliance of the gems. The slayer of demons (Vishnu) resides there with Lakshmi and his attendants. The king rejoiced upon seeing that accumulation of merit.

Then they showed him Kailasa, the abode of Shiva, on the beautiful peak of Mount Meru, which is ten thousand Yojanas wide. There, mansions shining like the sun and moon appear, and those who are virtuous, devoted to the chanting of the Panchakshara mantra and the Rudradhyaya, go there. Seeing this, the king said, "By the grace of Vinayaka, I have seen all the divine realms that grant merit to those who behold them."

Beyond that, there was no sun or moon for a thousand Yojanas. One travels by the light of a celestial chariot and sees nothing else. The king heard a sound like thunder, which was unbearable. He asked the messengers, "Whose sound is this? When shall I see the lotus feet of the Lord clearly?" He then saw before him the powerful Bhramari Shakti, shining like many suns, capable of consuming the universe, with her mouth wide open. Seeing her terrifying form, the king fainted.

The messengers comforted him and led him further. Then he saw the Adhara-Shakti, even more terrifying. Seeing the Bhramari Shakti stationed above his head, with fierce hair, hanging lips, and a long tongue, the king trembled. Comforted, he moved forward and saw the wish-granting Adhara-Shakti, spanning ten thousand Yojanas, by whose breath the mountains revolve. That city stands with a body harder than a diamond. The messengers said to the king, "Behold the Svananda-pattana, the city of your own bliss, for which you have been satisfied through meditation." It shone like ten million suns, with houses made of gems, gold, pearls, silver, and sun-stones. The floors were of blue gems, the people shone like fire, and there were ponds and lakes with clear, cool, blue water and golden banks. By drinking that water, one is freed from old age, hunger, and disease. There were pleasure gardens adorned with various trees and creepers, and divine herbs that shone like millions of suns. The moon there is without blemish, and the people are always joyful. The fragrance of the flower forests destroys the fatigue of those who wander there. Having reached the shore of the ocean of sugarcane juice, they are filled with joy.

Thus ends the fifty-second chapter of the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, describing the attainment of the Svananda-bhuvana.

Chapter 53 : Description of the Attainment of Svananda by the King of Kashi

Brahma said: The King drank the nectar of sugarcane juice, which is easily obtained by the accumulation of merit. In the midst of that place was a white lotus pond, containing a thousand-petaled lotus. In the center of that supreme lotus, which shone like a thousand suns, was a couch of the Lord, extremely beautiful, adorned with divine coverings, perfumed with divine incense, and decorated with various divine flowers and heaps of self-luminous gems.

Illuminating all directions and intermediate quarters with its radiance, Vinayaka lay there, being fanned by attendants. He shone like millions of moons, adorned with various ornaments and serpent jewelry. His trunk resembled the stalk of a banana tree, his tusks were beautiful, and he was covered in divine garments. He had an eye on his forehead and wore a crown, earrings, armlets, a signet ring, and a highly valuable waist-girdle.

Siddhi and Buddhi, the causes of success, were massaging his feet. He was fanned by white whisks representing Anima and Garima. Jwalini and Tejaswini, both radiant, stood on either side. His limbs were anointed with divine fragrance and adorned with divine garlands. Mahima and Prathima stood near his sight, holding a water vessel, a spittoon, and a golden umbrella studded with gems.

Then, the two messengers, holding the King by the hand, brought him before the Lord. They waited for an opportunity to speak, and the Lord became aware of them. The messengers said, "O Lord, this King has been brought by your command." Having bowed, they stood at a distance, awaiting his further orders.

When the Lord awoke, the King bowed with folded hands and said with great joy, "My lineage is blessed today. My parents, my birth, my knowledge, my penance, my vision, and my children are all blessed, for I have been shown your two lotus feet, which are worthy of meditation, O Brahman. I have not seen such splendor in Brahmaloka, Kailasa, or even the realm of Indra, nor anywhere else."

The messengers said, "Be seated," and they sat down. Then the King saw Vinayaka in the form of a two-armed child, by whom those demons, Devantaka and Narantaka, had been slain. He was extremely fair, with delicate limbs, resembling snow and camphor. The King could not speak, his throat choked with emotion, and he bathed the Lord's feet with rivers of tears of joy.

Recognizing his great devotion, the Lord of Obstacles lifted him up and embraced him, smelling his head like a father welcoming a son who had been away for a long time. The Lord also became choked with emotion, and both were filled with horripilation, immersed in an ocean of bliss like sages beyond the body.

Then the child-formed Vinayaka spoke: "O dear one, whatever I did while playing in your house in various ways, you have forgiven it all, whether it was auspicious or inauspicious. Many demons were slain, the burden of the earth was removed, the virtuous were protected, and the bridges of Dharma were established. By your command, I went to Kashyapa, the creator, and after bowing to him, I reached my own realm, attaining all this splendor. Thinking only of you, I found no rest anywhere. Knowing your original nature, I sent the great sage Mudgala to your house. Through his grace, you have attained this place, which is unattainable even by gods like Brahma and the great sages."

Kritayuga said: Having drunk the nectar of his words, the King bowed again and again. Gathering his courage, he praised him according to his ability.

The King said: "I bow to your lotus feet, O Lord, which are most worthy of meditation by Brahma and others for the sake of auspiciousness, marked with the lotus, noose, sword, and axe, and which remove the obstacles of your own devotees. You are worshipped by Vishnu, Shiva, and other gods for the fulfillment of various tasks, and by men; you are skilled in destroying obstacles and are the rain of nectar for those scorched by the world. I bow to your lotus-face, O Lord, which has three eyes, fire, sun, moon, and stars; by the sprinkling of the nectar of your compassionate glance, you have the power to eradicate the threefold suffering. I bow to your lotus-hands, O Lord, which have destroyed hosts of demons with various weapons, which grant fearlessness to devotees, and which are the support for crossing the well of worldly existence. You alone, as the essence of Sattva, sustain the universe; as the unborn, you create it by assuming Rajas; and as the foundation of Tamas, you destroy it. The entire moving and unmoving world is under your control, O Ganesha. Whoever worships you constantly with a pure heart, you run to him, overcoming all obstacles; he who keeps you in his control rests happily, just as a cow runs to her calf. Those who worship others, suffering and wandering in the cycle of the world, eventually attain your grace and, having attained human birth, worship you. No one can measure the dust of the earth, the stars in the sky, or the drops of rain, nor can Shesha or Brahma, even in thousands of years, measure your virtues and hosts. If you, who are formless, did not have a form, the method of worship would be in vain; how could there be the manifestation of qualities, the play of nature, or the enjoyment of the people? If there is good company, there is divine grace; if there is devotion to duty, there is remembrance of the Divine. Through your great grace, purity of heart, knowledge, and liberation are not difficult to attain."

Kritayuga said: Having heard this hymn, Vinayaka was pleased and told the King of Kashi to ask for a boon that was in his heart.

The King said: "The purpose of the boon is fulfilled by the very fact that I have arrived here. Let it be ordained that there shall be no more birth and death for me from now on."

Kritayuga said: Pleased by the hymn, the Lord said, "So be it," and established the King, who had attained a divine body, near him for the duration of a billion kalpas. O sage, I received this hymn from his grace. I told it to Narada, and Narada told it to Gautama. He too told it to all the sages. It grants the fruits of all desires, bestows knowledge and liberation, destroys obstacles, and is supreme and purifying. Whoever reads it attains devotion in the presence of Ganesha. One who reads it at the three junctures of the day desiring knowledge attains knowledge. One desiring a son attains sons; one desiring victory attains victory. Thus, everything you asked, O sage, has been told. What else do you wish to hear about the story of the King of Kashi?

Bhrigu said: Having heard the words of Brahma, Vyasa, free from doubt, asked again about another story in detail.

Vyasa said: How did he remove the poverty of the extremely poor Shukla with respect? Having eaten poor food in his house, what did the Lord do after meeting the King of Kashi, and what did the King of Kashi do? How were those two demons slain, how was the burden of the earth removed, and how was Dharma established? Tell me according to the rules.

Kritayuga said: I will tell you briefly the deeds of the child-formed Vinayaka, and what he did after removing the poverty of Shukla.

Thus ends the fifty-third chapter of the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Description of the Attainment of Svananda by the King of Kashi."

Chapter 54 : The Childhood Pastimes

After eating at Shukla’s house, the Lord rested for a moment in the courtyard. Meanwhile, the citizens, having prepared a meal for the son of the sage, came to the King of Kashi’s palace to invite him. The King of Kashi told them that the boy had gone off playing. The people then began to see Vinayaka in every house. Some said he had gone to Shukla’s house, and they began to criticize the Lord for visiting the home of a poor man like Shukla. The people remarked that just as a camel ignores tender leaves to eat thorns, the boy had left them, the wealthy, to visit the house of a pauper. They questioned the King’s judgment in calling the boy a Lord, saying that if he were truly divine, he would satisfy everyone’s heart. They wondered how the boy could eat such poor, meager food.

Some noble men among them, eager to bring him back, ran here and there asking passersby if they had seen Vinayaka. Following the directions of those they asked, they reached Shukla’s house and found Vinayaka sleeping. The righteous ones waited silently for him to wake, but the restless ones said, "Get up! Go and eat; the food will go stale. The people in the city are making a commotion for your sake. How can you, the son of Kashyapa, eat such wretched food in the house of this poor man who only begs for alms? He has no food to spare, and no one even drinks water in his house." Some criticized him with various words, while others praised him.

The Sage said: Hearing all these words, the son of Kashyapa spoke. The Lord said, "I have eaten the food offered with devotion to my heart's content. I am so full that I do not have the strength to walk even a single step, nor do I have any desire to eat even a morsel more."

K said: Realizing his stubbornness, the people became disappointed and wondered what to do with the child. Sensing the agitation of the city dwellers, the Lord, like the reflection of the sky in a pot, instantly manifested his infinite form. Just as the sun appears in many forms when reflected in many water pots, the Lord became present in every house, engaged in various sports. He was seen on staircases, on swings, on beds, and in palaces; he played, laughed, and ate with the children of every home. He studied scriptures in some places and taught them in others.

Seeing him in his own house along with the King of Kashi, the householder rejoiced, feeling blessed. Thus, Vinayaka was present in every home with the King. In some places, he was massaged with oil or bathed in pure water; in others, his feet were washed with great devotion. He was worshipped with sixteen offerings and fed with delicious food. In some homes, he was adorned with perfumes, musk, sandalwood, and flowers, and served by the wives of the householders, children, and servants. After eating to his satisfaction, he would sleep while being massaged, or spend time listening to songs, sacred stories, or the stories of Hari. In some places, he watched the dancing of women with the King. He was honored with various lamps, and in other places, he played with young children, holding a noose in his hand. He had the Puranas, Dharma Shastras, and Smritis read to him. Thus, eating in every house and fulfilling the desires of all, the Lord, who is as generous as the wish-fulfilling tree, the Chintamani, and the Kamadhenu, made his name "Deena-natha" (Protector of the Poor) truly meaningful.

Divine drums sounded, and flowers rained down in every house. Seeing this, the bewildered King of Kashi said to his people, "This child Vinayaka is standing right next to me. How can he be worshipped by everyone in every house? How can he be present in every house and here at the same time? I keep telling those who call me that I cannot go to eat at their houses without Vinayaka. I am eating the food here in the house with the son of Kashyapa." Yet, even as he spoke, he saw the boy outside. The people were astonished, saying, "The Lord is eating in various houses along with the King of Kashi." Hearing this, the King laughed and said, "How could he have gone to eat in many houses without me?" The King then went to two or three houses and saw both himself and the son of Kashyapa eating in every home.

Meanwhile, the great sages Sanaka and Sanandana arrived, having bathed and wandering in search of food. They saw the city filled with a grand festival, permeated by the presence of Vinayaka. Whichever house they entered, they saw Vinayaka, and as they remembered him, they stepped outside. They saw him eating, sleeping, playing, chanting, reading, and teaching. Seeing him everywhere, they said to each other, "There seems to be no place here for us to eat. Let us go to Shukla’s house; he is the one who performs hospitality with such devotion." Saying this, they went to that house and saw Kashyapa’s son there as well.

Hungry and with downcast faces, they left the city. Then, they beheld a supreme, clear, and auspicious form of Vinayaka. Closing and opening their eyes, looking down, up, and in all directions, they stood still in meditation. Opening their eyes for a moment, they saw the form of Vinayaka. Sanaka and the other sage looked at each other and then at the Lord they were meditating upon. The all-pervading, all-formed Lord appeared before them: ten-armed, riding a lion, adorned with Siddhi and Buddhi. He wore a radiant crown, earrings, and armlets, and was adorned with divine scents, garlands, a serpent, and the crescent moon. With a musk tilak on his forehead, divine garments, and the luster of teeth, he possessed a gentle radiance that was soothing to the virtuous but difficult for the wicked to behold.

Abandoning their confusion, the knowers of truth bowed to Vinayaka and, with joined palms, praised the great-minded Lord. They said, "You are the eternal, non-dual, supreme Brahman. We worship you with supreme devotion, the Lord who is the essence of the five elements, who dwells in all moving and non-moving things, and who is the form of everything. Millions of universes exist within the pores of your skin. How can you be praised, you who are beyond the reach of the Vedas? You have known our desires and fulfilled them, O Lord. We did not know your greatness, you who have taken the form of a child to remove the burden of the earth."

K said: As they were praising him, that form vanished. When they could no longer see him, they were freed from their fatigue. They built a magnificent temple and installed a beautiful image of Vinayaka on an auspicious day, amidst the sound of Vedic chants and musical instruments. They named him "Varada Vinayaka." The excellent lake there was also named after him and became famous. By bathing, donating, and worshipping Ganesha there, one attains all desires and is freed from past sins. All the sages, gods, and guardians of the directions arrived there, worshipped the Lord, and after offering prayers, departed.

The Sage said: Thus, those two sages came to see the power of the child-form, and having witnessed his glory, they were satisfied and returned to their own abode. Whoever listens to this auspicious story of the child with devotion attains all desires and, after death, becomes one with Brahman. They are never troubled by childhood afflictions and are victorious everywhere.

Chapter 55 : The Liberation of the Messengers

The Sage said: After they had departed, the great King of Kashi, overcome by confusion, mounted his horse and wandered from house to house. Not seeing Vinayaka, he wondered where he had gone to eat. He thought, "How could he leave me and go off to eat delicious food all by himself?" He asked in every house, "Where has that Vinayaka gone? He just came outside, eager to eat and play." The citizens replied, "Why do you ask us in vain, O King of Kashi? We have not seen him." Rejected by all the townspeople, the King became as exhausted as a destitute man with a family. Some said, "Vinayaka is playing at Shukla’s house."

Filled with joy, the King went to Shukla’s house and saw the Lord in the courtyard, sitting upon a bull. Seeing him mounted on the bull, looking like another three-eyed Shiva, the King bowed with great devotion and, with folded hands, said: "O Child, there is no virtue, wisdom, or affection in your conduct. How could you leave me and eat various delicious foods by yourself?"

The Sage said: Hearing these words, Vinayaka replied, "Wherever I have eaten, you have also eaten. You speak falsely, you are childish in your intellect. Ask all these people, and they will bear witness." Hearing Vinayaka’s words, the people said to the King, "You have just eaten in the presence of Vinayaka. Why do you speak falsely, O best of kings, even though you are an elder?" Then, regaining his senses, the King understood and said, "Your supreme Maya is unknowable, even to the yogis. You are blessed, for you are honored everywhere in every form."

The Sage said: With his body trembling with joy, the King entered into meditation, and then he perceived the form of Vinayaka within himself. Just as water poured into water becomes one with it, so too did the King, through the power of Maya, perceive the Lord. He then took him, seated on a palanquin, to his own palace amidst the sounds of various musical instruments, dances, and songs. The boy shone brilliantly, like Kama among the gods. Shukla, along with his wife, followed slowly with devotion. Looking back, the boy felt great shame, thinking, "How did I leave without giving him a boon or satisfying him?" Thinking this, he bestowed upon him supreme wealth—the wealth of Kubera, which is the best and causes wonder to all.

Shukla, having returned home, was poor and high-minded, wondering if the Lord was angry because he had eaten simple food. Not seeing his leaf-hut, he fell into great anxiety. Then, servants bathed Shukla with fragrant oils and adorned him with ornaments and golden garments, and they did the same for his wife, as if by force. Both were filled with great wonder as they beheld all the wealth: walls of gold and jewels, couches, and various seats. Everywhere were images made of pearls and gems, golden vessels, clothing, and coverings. Seeing all the various types of food and delicacies, they spoke to each other: "How has this humble dwelling become like the palace of Indra?" Then Shukla said to his wife, "Know, O fortunate one, that this is by the grace of Vinayaka. The great Lord does not appear before us, yet he satisfies us with even a small amount. He considers the much that he gives as very little, while he considers the little offered with devotion as very much. Therefore, whether out of fear, desire, affection, or enmity, he should be remembered, bowed to, praised, and worshipped for one’s own benefit."

The Sage said: Meanwhile, in the city, two messengers sent by Narantaka, named Shura and Chapala, had been hiding for a long time. Their roar made the three worlds tremble like a peepal leaf, and at the shaking of their heads, the gods and Indra himself would tremble. In prowess, strength, and sound, there were none equal to them in the three worlds. They said to each other, "This one born of a sage must be killed while in the palanquin, for he has killed the powerful demons who came before. We must take revenge for them." Assuming the form of lightning, they roared with a great sound. The assembly of heroes, their eyes blinded by that brilliance, cried out in great distress, "What is this? What is this?"

In the meantime, they reached near the palanquin. All the horses fled, leaving Vinayaka behind. Vinayaka seized both of the powerful ones with his hands and spun them around to throw them to the ground. Then, with a heart softened by compassion, he placed them on the earth. "One should only kill a person who is superior in prowess. What is the point for a man to kill a mosquito?" He then asked them, "Whose messengers are you? Speak."
