Hearing his words, they stood before him and said, "You are known as the ocean of compassion, the sun, and our father. You are the one who initiates, the giver of knowledge, and the giver of fearlessness. You are the giver of food, known as the five fathers in the three worlds. We are secret messengers sent by Narantaka. You, O God, have protected us who were causing you obstacles, out of your mercy. You are all-knowing and know the past, present, and future. Those who came to you with enmity were killed in a moment."

At this time, the citizens said to Vinayaka, "Why have you protected these two wicked ones who are a terror to the world? Doing a favor to them is like doing a disservice. Milk given to a snake only turns into poison."

The Sage said: The Lord then replied to them, "I have already given them protection; how can it be otherwise now?" Having said this, he released them. The King then said, "Having forgiven their countless offenses, you have released these enemies. Whether a creature lives or dies depends on your will." Having said this, the King of Kashi and Vinayaka reached the palace. Bowing to them, all the people returned to their own homes, praising the King and the multi-formed Vinayaka.

Chapter 56 : The Departure of Narantaka

The Sage said: The two messengers reached the beautiful assembly hall of Narantaka in the city of Raudrapura. It was adorned with a thousand pillars, decorated with jewels and pearls, filled with many brave warriors, and possessed many wonders, spanning a hundred yojanas in length and width. Narantaka was seated on a throne made of various gems, and his two ministers were seated on excellent seats nearby. Their heads reached the sky, and they were the best among the powerful. After bowing, the brave and restless messengers spoke of many virtues. A messenger must speak what is true and appropriate; otherwise, it is a fault of the messenger, which leads to the terrible wrath of the master.

Having heard the command of the master and considered their own welfare, we went to that city to create obstacles for Vinayaka. We stayed there in secret, waiting for the child. First, Vighanta and Dantura, in the form of children, went to him. Vinayaka embraced them and turned them into ash. Then Patanga and Vidhula, assuming the form of wind, went to lift the child but were destroyed by him. A powerful demon in the form of a stone was shattered into a hundred pieces. Two demons, Kama and Krodha, took the form of donkeys to kill the child on the road but were crushed on the ground. Then Kundiya and Tuvara, assuming the form of elephants, blocked the path, but their foreheads were split open, and they were killed in an instant by the young boy.

Afterward, Jrimbhini came to kill the boy at the house of Dharmadatta, but she was struck on the head with a coconut and met her death. Jvalasura, Vyaghratunda, and the third, Vidarana, came to destroy the child. The first set the city ablaze, the last helped him by taking the form of wind, and Vyaghratunda, tearing his mouth, devoured all the people. All three were killed by that boy through his infinite maya. Meghanama, a great demon, took the form of a Ganak (accountant) but was killed by a strike of a ring; it was as if it were a miracle. Kupa and Kandara, both deceitful, were killed by force. Ambasura, Andhaka, and Tunga came to destroy the child. One created darkness, another rained down torrents, and the third, Tunga-shikhara, fell upon the boy. All three were thrown far away in the form of giant birds. Then a demoness named Bhramara, with evil intent, came to devour the boy, but the Lord of Obstacles grabbed her by the chest and took her life.

Then we both assumed the form of lightning and went to the boy, but we were seized by his powerful hands. Out of mercy, he released us after asking about our intentions. Having told him everything, we returned to our master. Once, in that city, Vinayaka was invited to eat by a poor Brahmin. After eating broken rice with oil, the Lord went to eat in every house. Everywhere, he ate along with the King of Kashi, but the foolish King did not understand his actions. Master, such power has neither been seen nor heard of anywhere. Now, whatever is beneficial for you, let that be done properly. We know that there is no man in the three worlds who can defeat him.

The Sage said: Hearing their words, the destroyer of the gods (Narantaka) blazed with rage. He vomited fire from his mouth, which he had opened to consume the world. Narantaka, knitting his fierce brows, said: "A monkey, even if it can fly, does not bother a lion. A python, even if it is prone to swallowing, does not swallow the earth. My mind is filled with wonder at your words. A firefly shines only as long as the moon is not seen; the moon shines only as long as the sun is not seen. When Rahu is not seen, the sun shines. As long as I, the embodiment of Time, am not seen, this boy is considered great. Know that he and the King of Kashi have gone to heaven; he will go where those demons have gone."

The Sage said: Having said this, he stood up and roared, causing the directions to tremble. The entire earth shook, agitated by the sound of his roar. He then ordered all his heroes to get ready, saying, "I am going to the city of the King of Kashi for battle." As he spoke, the army arrived, fully equipped, peaceful yet terrifying, and adorned with four types of flags. The horizon was covered with the dust of the army; the sun was obscured, and nothing could be seen. Innumerable foot soldiers with heads red like vermilion ran about, engaging in mock battles. Others held swords, shields, tridents, clubs, and nooses.

Then the elephant division arrived, adorned with the sound of bells, their cheeks red with rut, and their tusks shining. It looked like a moving mountain range of various minerals, breaking the mountains with their roars and tusks. If the mahouts were not there, everything would have been destroyed, but the rut-water calmed the dust. The cavalry followed, with crowns on their heads, adorned with weapons, bows, and arrows. They filled the sky with their neighing. They were decorated with skeletons, armor, and tridents. Others held maces, hammers, and axes. The horses were decorated with various ornaments and yak-tail fans, swift as the mind or the wind, wishing to conquer the sky.

Then the great minister, a great warrior, set out on a chariot adorned with gold, silver, pearls, and gems, equipped with golden flags and decorated horses. It was filled with various weapons, bows, and arrows, a great golden wheel, and adorned with a network of small bells. He was dark-bodied, large, and decorated with heroic bracelets. He had shining earrings, a great crown, necklaces of pearls and gold, and was adorned with rings and a sword. He was anointed with saffron, musk, and camphor. He looked like a sick man swollen with disease due to his heroic fervor. Making the directions resound with the noise of various musical instruments, the heroes roared repeatedly, eager to defeat Vinayaka. Hearing that sound, the frightened gods fled to their caves. Thus, Narantaka went to the city of the King of Kashi.

Chapter 57 : The Subjugation of the King

The Sage said: When the lord of the Daityas departed, the men who had gone to the outer ramparts ran back and informed the King, who was eating, that Narantaka had arrived. They reported that the Daitya king had come accompanied by his fourfold army, and upon hearing the sounds of their musical instruments, the citizens of the city began to wail loudly. A great clamor arose among the people in the city, and they fled in all ten directions, desperate to save their lives. The King, abandoning his meal, rose up as if touched by a spark. He donned his battle attire and issued orders to the people. He commanded them to prepare for war and ordered the beating of the war drums. The King, powerful and armed with a sword, shield, quiver, and bow, donned his armor and worshipped Lord Vinayaka. Remembering Him and offering a prayer for victory, he mounted his horse.

Upon hearing the third signal, the soldiers rushed forward, fully equipped and hurrying with infantry, horses, chariots, and elephants. Those warriors, emboldened by the audacity to conquer the three worlds, were filled with great joy. The King stood on a platform in the eastern part of the city and distributed clothes and wealth to his ministers and all his warriors. He told them that this was the time to demonstrate their prowess. He said that through the grace of Vinayaka, they had no fear anywhere, yet the powerful Daitya had forcibly conquered the earth. He remarked that there is no certainty of victory and that men are subject to fate, and therefore, he did not consider his army to be even a fraction of what was needed. He questioned the comparison between the ocean and a water pot, or a firefly and the sun, noting that they had survived only because they were protected by the grace of the Lord and the prosperity of the kingdom. He acknowledged that many offenses had been committed, and many Daityas had already been slain by the power of Vinayaka. He suggested that since the Daitya had rejected peaceful overtures, they should consider what was beneficial.

The Sage said: Then the chief minister spoke to the King, who was overwhelmed with fear. He advised the King to approach the Daitya Narantaka with four wise men, stating that seeking refuge with the strong for the sake of one's own objectives is a virtuous act. He told the King to listen to the counsel of Brihaspati and follow it, for that would lead to safety. Brihaspati had said that one should overcome enemies through various means such as offering a daughter in marriage, dining together, giving gifts of clothing, peaceful negotiation, bowing down, showing affection, providing proof of loyalty, praising their glory, and using the testimony of trusted people. The Sage continued, saying that if the Daitya would spare the kingdom in exchange for worshipping Vinayaka, that seemed to him the best course of action for the King’s own welfare.

Then all the people praised the King, saying that the minister had spoken well for the sake of ending the enmity. While they were deliberating, the powerful Daityas surrounded the city with their armies like a swarm of locusts. Seeing the men trapped in the middle, the wicked ones set fire to the city. A great fire blazed in all directions. The sun was obscured by smoke, and nothing could be discerned; it was as if the end of the world had arrived, and the situation for the people was dire. Those who tried to flee the fire were captured by the enemy. Women and children clung to each other and kissed in despair. Chaste women, overwhelmed by shame, gave up their lives. Some climbed the city gates and ended their lives, while others perished by weapons, nooses, or poison. Others were seized by messengers and taken to their master. Narantaka, having finished his meal, sent them back to the city. Seeing this devastation, the King spoke again to his ministers, saying that if their women were being taken by wicked, evil-minded men before their very eyes, it would be a great disgrace, and therefore they must fight the Asuras.

Having said this, the King, emboldened by the spirit of battle, spurred his horse and, readying his bow, unleashed a rain of arrows. The sun was covered, and the Daityas fell into confusion. Just as clouds release rain, the King released a torrent of arrows from his bow. The Daitya hordes were struck and killed; some died, some were broken, and some were left crippled. Some had their bellies torn open, others had their arms severed, and some had their eyes or chests shattered. The ministers, along with the army, followed the King of Kashi, striking the enemy warriors with swords and axes. Struck by these blows, some fell to the ground, while the Daityas also struck them with great weapons on the battlefield. In the great darkness caused by the dust of the army, they struck both their own men and the enemy indiscriminately. They engaged in hand-to-hand combat, driven by the desire to conquer one another—those on horseback, on elephants, in chariots, and on foot. The infantry and those in chariots fought with weapons and arrows. The battle became chaotic and tumultuous.

The Daitya army was broken and turned to flee. The King of Kashi, victorious, roared like a lion. Filled with joy, he entered the midst of the army, slaying the best of the warriors, breaking through the terrifying ranks like an elephant in a battle frenzy. He killed hundreds and thousands of warriors in the middle of the army. Seeing him slaying them, the enemy soldiers surrounded him. Realizing he was the King, they all converged upon him. Enduring the great rain of their arrows, they forcefully captured him. Along with the two sons of the minister, the Daitya chiefs captured the King and raised a great shout, declaring that the King had been taken. Some of the King's soldiers were captured, some fled, some died, some were wounded, and some sought refuge. Like a great bull in a deep forest surrounded by powerful wolves, the messengers took the King and the minister's sons to Narantaka.

The entire city was burned by the relentless soldiers. Then Narantaka spoke to his best warriors, saying that the purpose for which they had come was accomplished. He said he had no further concern regarding the Sage's son. He declared that with the lord defeated, the army was defeated, and with the fortress conquered, the city was conquered; and with the King of Kashi defeated, the boy was undoubtedly defeated as well. He said that the King would now bring the boy to him for punishment. Having said this, he departed for the city amidst the sounds of musical instruments. With the King of Kashi in front, praised by bards, and distributing gifts to the bards and the Brahmins, he proceeded.

Thus ends the fifty-seventh chapter titled "The Subjugation of the King" in the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana.

Chapter 58 : The Destruction of Narantaka

The King said: As Narantaka was halfway through his journey, filled with joy, the news reached the Queen of Kashi that the King had been captured. She grieved intensely, and the remaining citizens wailed as loudly as fish in a dried-up pond. Upon hearing that her husband had been seized by enemies, the Queen fell to the ground, unconscious and pale, like a banana tree struck by a storm. Accompanied by her companions, she wept and spoke:

The Queen said: My husband, who plays like a lion, destroys elephant armies, and crushes his foes—how could he be forcibly captured by a jackal-like demon? Where has the strength of my husband gone, he who is powerful enough to defeat millions of demons and is a slayer of wild elephants? When will I see my husband again, and when will the angry Maheshwara (Shiva) intervene? To which god should I go for refuge to have him released quickly? The boon granted to this young Kashyapa was in vain, and the King acted foolishly by opposing the great ones based on a child's words. Who can defeat that great demon Narantaka, who has seized my husband? Why has this time of destruction arrived, leaving me hopeless? How have both I and the Earth come to face widowhood? Never has there been such an ocean of compassion and protector of the surrendered.

The Sage said: Hearing this lament, the powerful son of Kashyapa (Ganesha) roared with a sound that shattered the universe. The sky, the directions, and the entire Earth, with its mountains and forests, trembled at the echo. Birds fell dead, and people were bewildered. Seeing this success, Ganesha, with eyes agitated by anger, asked the Queen where the battle was taking place. Perceiving his intent, she manifested various armies. Thousands of fierce-faced warriors appeared—some with plow-like teeth, serpent tongues, mountain-like heads, and tearing mouths, capable of swallowing the Earth. They breathed fire, possessed hundreds of eyes, and were immensely powerful. Great elephants were hidden within their nostrils, and the sun and moon fell to the Earth due to their breath. Their matted hair swept the Earth, and their arms were a thousand yojanas long, with feet twice that size.

Their cruel leader approached Vinayaka (Ganesha) and asked, "O Lord, tell me what I must do. Give me food to satisfy my hunger first." Vinayaka replied, "Devour the great army protected by Narantaka. Kill him and bring me his head quickly. If that army does not satisfy you, I will provide other food." Having received this command and bowing to the son of Kashyapa, the being went toward Narantaka with a great roar. The soldiers of Narantaka, terrified by the sound and the sight of this being, fled in all ten directions. The being grabbed them all and threw them into his mouth. Dust rose, obscuring everything, and in the deep darkness, they could only see by the light of torches. Seeing this horrific being, some died of fear, while others were devoured alive.

Seeing the destruction of his entire army, Narantaka wondered if another Yama (God of Death) had arrived. As he spoke, he saw half his army already consumed. The being was destroying the army like a fire at the end of time, as Agastya had once swallowed the ocean. The demon soldiers screamed; some were eaten, others crushed underfoot, some killed by the wind of his breath, and others died of sheer terror. Warriors fell upon one another, crying out to Narantaka for protection, but the being devoured them instantly. His hunger was not satisfied by the countless victims.

Hearing the commotion, Narantaka prepared his bow. Kneeling on the ground, he pulled his bowstring and unleashed a rain of arrows. The sky turned dark with the arrows, and many of the King’s servants fell. The being swallowed the arrows shot by the demon, and countless arrows emerged from his pores, dripping with blood, yet he felt no pain. Narantaka then manifested divine weapons, but the being swallowed them all like a python. With his weapons exhausted and his strength depleted, the demon king Narantaka fell. The spirit of Time (Kala-Purusha) then clung to his back. Wherever the demon fled—to the sky, the earth, or into the depths of the ground—he saw the being behind him.

The spirit of Time grabbed Narantaka by the hair, just as Garuda catches a powerful serpent entering its hole. The being said, "Where will you go, Narantaka, now that you are seen by me? You became arrogant due to Shiva's boon, causing trouble for sages and slaughtering countless humans, you great villain. Vinayaka has descended to destroy you; abandon your ego and take refuge in him. Your sins will vanish upon seeing his lotus feet." Having said this, he brought the demon before Vinayaka.

The being bowed to Vinayaka and said, "I have devoured your entire army as commanded, O Lord. I have brought this demon to you after much struggle. Grant me a place to sleep to relieve my fatigue, and grant liberation to all these beings for their happiness." Hearing this, the Lord replied, "Enter my mouth and sleep as you wish." Hearing these words from the mouth of Vinayaka, the being entered his mouth and became one with him. Just as the fragrance born of the earth dissolves back into it, so too did he who was born of Vinayaka dissolve back into him.

Whoever listens to this excellent and great narrative with devotion will surely attain all their desires and attain liberation.

Chapter 59 : The Liberation of the King

The Sage said: How was that man created by the child Vinayaka, by whom the army was devoured and the demon Narantaka was brought? Tell me this clearly, for I have a great doubt regarding this.

The Crow said: That which is the attributeless Supreme Brahman appeared with attributes in the form of Vinayaka, embodying Brahma, Vishnu, and Shiva. To remove the burden of the earth, to destroy the wicked, and to protect one's own duty, He assumes infinite forms. He Himself creates, protects, and destroys the universe by His own power. He expects a cause and the supreme destiny from the people, and there should be no doubt about this in the Lord who is full of manifold illusions. By His will, this entire world revolves. Now, I will tell you about His illusion in detail.

Until the King, along with his minister's son, reached the city while held by Narantaka, that man devoured the army protected by the demon. At that moment, the man was placed into the mouth by Vinayaka. The King, the minister, and the prince—they all saw the entire universe within the belly of that God. They saw the earth with its seven continents, mountains, trees, rivers, oceans, ponds, and people. They saw the heavenly realm adorned with gods, Gandharvas, sages, Siddhas, Yakshas, demons, serpents, and celestial nymphs. They saw the seven netherworlds while wandering. Thus, with bewildered minds, they saw many universes within each fold of His belly. Being distressed, they sought refuge in the God of gods, Vinayaka. They prayed to Him in their minds: "O Ocean of Mercy, have pity on us who are bewildered and distressed."

Then, the Lord in the form of a child showed them the way. They emerged through the pores of His skin just as they had entered before. The King of Kashi saw his own city in prosperity and saw the child Vinayaka, the son of Kashyapa, playing. By His grace, having regained his wisdom, the King praised Him with great joy: "I have known Your supreme illusion while I was inside Your belly. You are the creator of gods, humans, directions, creations, oceans, rivers, and the abodes of Bali. Since there are crores of universes in the pores of Your skin, I, being bewildered, have seen them by Your grace, O Lord. I have seen many wonders of Yours, O Knower of the Universe. I had come for battle, and the army returned. It was defeated by Narantaka's army in an instant. Along with the minister's son, I rushed toward Narantaka. I was suddenly captured by him, and my army was defeated in a moment. My entire city was burnt, and I saw that Lord of Death. He also devoured his various armies in an instant. That man, having captured Narantaka, went to You. We all went into Your belly; there I saw You as the wicked one, O Lord. I saw everything there, including the Jambudvipa in detail. Thus, I saw the earth in detail within Your folds. Then, distressed, we sought refuge in You, O Lord of the World. By Your command, we emerged through the pores of Your skin. Again, I see You as a child and my own great palace. What is this wonder, O God? What is this web of illusion, O Lord? Who is that man, O God, by whom the great army was devoured? By whom was that demon captured and protected? And by whom was the pore of the skin shown for our exit? O God, remove this doubt of mine, for I am Your devotee."

The Crow said: When the King asked this, the wise Vinayaka placed His hand on the King's head out of compassion. The King of Kashi attained divine knowledge that very instant. He praised the God of gods, Vinayaka, with great devotion. The King said: "You are Brahma, Vishnu, Maheshwara, and the Sun. You are the earth, the air, the sky, the directions, and the trees. You are the Siddhas, Gandharvas, Yakshas, and Rakshasas along with the mountains. You are the sages, humans, and the entire moving and stationary world. You are everything, O Lord of gods, both sentient and insentient. By the merits of my past lives, I have seen You, O son of Kashyapa."

The Crow said: As the King was speaking, the Lord deluded him again. Then the King worshipped the auspicious Vinayaka. Then the citizens, filled with joy, came to see the King. Bowing repeatedly, they gave him clothes and ornaments. The King also distributed many clothes to them. Having dismissed all the people, he went to his mother. "By Your grace, Mother, I have seen His lotus feet. I was captured by the demon king and protected by the God of gods." Then the happy mother embraced her son who had returned after a long time. The minister and his son bowed to the Queen and said, "By the power of Your merit, we have seen His lotus feet. We were deluded by the illusions of Vinayaka and then liberated." Then the King went there and spoke to his wife and mother. When the people had departed, with a voice choked with joy, the King said: "I was in great distress and was captured by the demon in an instant. I was liberated by His illusion and brought back to my own city."

The Crow said: The city shone with the sounds of various musical instruments, many flags, and grand festivals. With all the people brought back by the power of Vinayaka, the princes, princesses, women, husbands, fathers, mothers, sons, and brothers embraced each other and rejoiced. They gave many gifts, ate, fed others, looked at each other, drank, and sang.

Thus ends the fifty-ninth chapter of the Krida Khanda of the Sri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Liberation of the King."

Chapter 60 : The Battle Between Vinayaka and Narantaka

The narrator said: Then Narantaka, observing the activities of Vinayaka, pondered with his intellect upon the great wonders he had witnessed. He thought, "By him, a cosmic man was created while the King of Kashi was held captive, and my entire army was devoured by him. The King, along with the minister's son, entered his stomach and was brought out unharmed, and the entire universe was revealed to the King. There is no doubt that from him I shall attain both worldly enjoyment and liberation; therefore, I shall either kill him, or he will kill me." Having reached this conclusion, he spoke to Vinayaka.

The demon said: "You have displayed this magic trick in many ways. I am not afraid of it, for Narantaka is a master of illusion himself. At his mere breath, great mountains fall. At the mere movement of his brow, the universe trembles violently. By the blow of his palm, even the earth would split in two. Being a child, how will you fight with one such as me? How can one who walks in front of a tiger find happiness?"

The narrator said: Hearing this arrogant speech from Narantaka, the Supreme Lord in the form of a child, Vinayaka, replied.

Vinayaka said: "Why do you boast, fool? When your entire army has been devoured, where has that power gone, for which you speak repeatedly? Heroes display their valor, not empty words. A lion is killed in an instant by a tiny scorpion. A powerful darkness is destroyed by a small lamp. He whose death is near becomes weak due to his own impending fate. A mad elephant is controlled by a small goad."

The narrator said: Hearing these words spoken by the Divine, the demon king trembled with great fear and roared loudly like a thundercloud. Terrifying others, causing them to weep with his cries, and shaking the earth, he ran in a great frenzy, desiring to kill Vinayaka. Just as a moth rushes in haste to destroy itself in the flame of a lamp, he ran with a twisted brow, vomiting fire from his mouth. Seeing him approaching, the King of Kashi suddenly prepared his bow, pulling the string to his ear. He spoke gently to the demon, "O shameless one, you have been released from bondage; do not throw away your life, O King of demons, for if you live, you will see auspicious days. Turn back and retreat, or else you will meet your death."

The narrator said: Hearing these words, the demon spoke, burning with rage, "My name is Narantaka (the destroyer of men) because I devour those like you. Now, seek refuge if you wish to live." The King again said to the death-seeking Narantaka, "At the time of destruction, O fool, the intellect becomes perverse. Friends turn into enemies in adverse times. You too have committed many sins due to the pride of your boon. Because of the power of that boon, you did not count the thunderbolt as anything. Now, He has descended to slay those like you. The son of Kashyapa has come to earth to remove its heavy burden. Your boon and your merit have become weak due to the accumulation of your sins."

Hearing this speech, the demon king trembled in his heart. He rushed at the King, grabbed his bow along with the arrows, and threw it with force to the ground, where it shattered into a hundred pieces. Narantaka then struck the King with a blow of his fist. He fell to the ground like a mountain struck by a thunderbolt. Seeing this, Vinayaka, the mighty one, ran forward with his goad in hand. He roared, shaking the entire sky and all the directions with his sounds. The whole earth trembled, and the directions seemed to be ablaze. With the brilliance of his axe, which blinded all vision, he struck the demon's head just as Indra strikes a massive mountain. Struck thus, the demon king fell to the earth, having fainted, his vital parts shattered as if by a stone.

In an instant, the demon rose, grabbed two mountains with his hands, and hurled them, desiring to kill Vinayaka. They were crushed into a hundred pieces by the axe-wielding Lord. Then, through his illusion, the demon king assumed many forms. Whatever form he quickly took, Vinayaka immediately shattered the pride of the great demon in that very form. Having countered weapons with weapons and missiles with missiles, they fought each other in a wrestling match. They struck foot against foot and hand against hand. Their knees clashed like blooming flame-of-the-forest flowers. They fell to the ground chest against chest. Rising again, the powerful ones struck each other with their elbows.

The demon, remembering Mahadeva, created trees. They ran back-to-back and forehead-to-forehead, both bleeding from their ankles. The demon hurled mountains and trees, desiring to kill Vinayaka, but Vinayaka severed them all before they could reach him. With the blows of his lotus, noose, and goad, and the firm action of his axe, he warded them off. As one was warded off, the demon released another. Seeing the King of Kashi go far away, thinking Vinayaka was dead, Vinayaka felt distress and pondered in his heart, "The demon has immeasurable strength; no way to victory is visible. When will the gods reach their homes, their enemies slain? Narantaka has become a destroyer even for the gods."

As Vinayaka thought this again and again, arrows like the staff of Death fell into his quiver. The Pinaka bow, made of gold, fell before him, illuminating the directions and sub-directions with its radiance. Seeing the bow with its shimmering luster, he rejoiced, considering the demon defeated and the gods' desire fulfilled. He took the arrows, let out a loud roar, lifted the Pinaka, and strung the bow. Placing his knees on the earth with his quivers on both sides, he pulled the string, and the sound of the bow made the three worlds tremble. Pulling two arrows with his left and right hands, he released them, aiming at the demon, while they emitted sparks of fire.

The two arrows suddenly struck the demon's arms, roaring, illuminating the sky, and destroying all life. They severed his arms like trees, just as Indra severs the peaks of a mountain with a thunderbolt. One massive arm fell at the gate of Narantaka, and the other fell at the gate of his father, crushing many creatures. Vinayaka saw that he still had two arms left. The demon ran toward him again, like Death with his mouth wide open. He threw many trees with his hands and feet, casting a great shower of trees at Vinayaka. There was great darkness, and nothing could be perceived. Then, seeing the demon's strength, Vinayaka spoke.

Thus ends the sixtieth chapter of the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, describing the battle between Shri Ganesha and Narantaka.

Chapter 61 : The Subjugation of the Demon and the Vision of the Cosmic Form

Vinayaka said, "I have never seen anyone as powerful as Bali before. Now, witness my prowess and the actions of a child." Having said this, he drew an arrow from his quiver, pulled the bowstring to his ear, and released it at Narantaka. The arrow illuminated the sky, knocking down trees and swaying like a flower as it struck the demon. The arrow severed the demon's feet, and he fell. As the demon’s torso collapsed, Vinayaka roared. The severed feet wandered through the sky and fell into the house, crushing many people of Devantaka. The half-bodied demon, with a terrifying face, rushed forward with great force, as if swallowing the three worlds. By his own magic, his feet reappeared, and the demon king approached Vinayaka and said, "The prowess you showed me by severing my body—I shall now sever your limbs, witness my power as well."

Hearing this, Vinayaka roared again and unleashed a fierce shower of arrows upon the great demon. The powerful demon devoured the countless arrows. Then, Vinayaka empowered a great arrow—fiery-tipped, golden-feathered, and roaring terrifically—and after drawing the bowstring to his ear, he released it at the demon's head. It traveled with the speed of the wind, splitting mountains, scattering flocks of birds, and felling trees. Hearing its thunderous sound, the demon's army fled in all ten directions. The arrow struck the demon's neck like a thunderbolt hitting a mountain peak. The impact sent his head flying into the sky like a bird, and it fell, wailing, into his father's house. Yet, another head appeared, just as before. Enraged, the demon roared loudly, causing the gods, birds, and all people to tremble. He unleashed a fierce shower of arrows back at Vinayaka, who skillfully cut them all in half. The battle of mountains and arrows raged day and night.

Both the god and the powerful demon grew weary. The demon became deeply anxious and burned with intensity. He then grabbed a battle-axe wreathed in flames; as he swung it, the earth shook. The gods in their celestial chariots, who had come to watch the battle, trembled, and Shesha in the netherworld grew fearful. Vinayaka released an arrow and instantly severed the demon's head, but another beautiful head, adorned with a crown and earrings, appeared again. Vinayaka, in anger, struck it down again and again, but every time it was severed, another appeared. Having cut off hundreds of thousands of heads, the Lord pondered the reason for the demon's immortality. Through his divine illusion, he suddenly deluded the demon, who was arrogant and powerful due to Shiva's boon.

The demon became confused, losing track of himself and his surroundings. Moments seemed like nights or days; heaven, earth, and the netherworld blurred; states of wakefulness, deep sleep, and dreams merged. He could not distinguish between Vinayaka, gods, women, men, spirits, demons, friends, foes, parents, or the living and the dead. He fell into deep contemplation, thinking, "The boons granted to me by Shiva, the wielder of the trident, are now coming to an end; perhaps my death is near."

As he was thinking this, he saw before him Vinayaka in his Cosmic Form (Virata-rupa). His head reached the heavens, his feet covered the netherworld, the directions were his ears, and the trees were his body hair. The universe revolved in his pores, and the oceans were but drops of sweat on him. On the tips of his nails shone the thirty-three crores of gods, and within his belly appeared the fourteen worlds. Vinayaka then crushed the demon instantly with the tip of his big toe, just as one would crush a bedbug. The gods and sages, filled with devotion and joy, showered flowers and repeatedly cried out in victory.

When the Cosmic Form vanished, the Earth approached the Lord and said, "O Lord of Gods, half of my burden has been removed. Please always ensure my complete relief." After she disappeared, the King of Kashi worshipped Vinayaka and, bowing with humility, said, "O Lord, I have witnessed a miracle beyond the reach of mind and speech. You have descended to remove the burden of the earth, and you have accomplished it. You have slain the one who was invincible even to the thirty-three crores of gods. It is through great merit that I have seen your Cosmic Form, O Lord of Gods."

The King continued, and all the citizens said, "You are blessed, O Lord; you have bewildered us all with your child-like play. You have done all this to spread your glory." They worshipped him with great devotion and prayed, "Grant us your devotion, O Lord, and never leave us." The King and the people then gave many offerings, and the Brahmins prayed for continued victory. They exchanged gifts, and thus concluded the sixty-first chapter of the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, describing the subjugation of the demon and the vision of the Cosmic Form.

Chapter 62 : The Siege of the City

The narrator said: Sharada, the wife of the Brahmana Raudraketu, was sitting in the courtyard with her companions, filled with curiosity. Suddenly, they saw the head of Narantaka lying in the courtyard, adorned with ear-rings. It made the entire city resound like a mountain peak, dark and covered in frost, resembling a withered lotus. Terrified, Raudraketu and the grief-stricken Sharada looked at it and began to weep bitterly. They rolled on the ground, beating their chests, and both fell into a swoon, losing consciousness for a moment. The mother lamented, placing the head on her heart, and cried out in agony like a cow that has lost its calf.

Sharada said: Your body was filled with the glory of a hero, eager for battle. You have said nothing to me; speak now. Where has your body gone, that you have arrived only as a head? How did you come alone, and where is your great army? Where have your clouds gone, those bearing umbrellas and whisks? Seeing whom, noble women would wither with the fire of separation—how have you become withered like the sun following the sunset? What offense have I or your affectionate father committed against you? Why do you not speak, wiping away our tears? You, who were accustomed to sleeping on a bed covered with precious spreads, where are you sleeping now? My mind is deeply distressed. Without you, my son, we cannot bear to show our faces.

The narrator said: Hearing Sharada’s grief, Raudraketu also lamented, saying, "Without telling us the story, where have you gone, my dear son? Every day you would tell us all that had happened and what was to come; why do you not speak now? If you have gone to heaven after fighting face-to-face and being purified by weapons, why did you leave us without asking? How did you make your parents both your children and your enemies, when you took the sword from the servant's hand into your own? When you did that, the earth with its mountains, forests, and heavens trembled. I do not know by what turn of fate you were brought down to the ground. Destiny is powerful in this world; human effort is meaningless. Where has the ornament of my lineage and the world gone? You, who were the time of death for the enemy's elephants, how have you become split in two like a tree struck by the wind? You were the sun of valor, the fire that burns the cotton of enemies."

The narrator said: After Raudraketu and Sharada had expressed their grief in various ways, they took the head and went to Devantaka. Rising quickly from his throne, Devantaka saw them in that state. Seeing the head, he embraced his younger brother’s neck and wept. The demons of his retinue, who were like Yama, the god of death, also wept loudly upon seeing Narantaka’s head. Taking the head from his mother’s hands and holding it to his heart, he cried like an osprey, pained by brotherly love.

Devantaka said: We ate together, drank together, played, slept, and rose together. We suffered and prayed together; where have you gone, leaving me behind? Seeing whom, mortals and gods would flee in all ten directions—how were you killed, and by what wicked and powerful being? You, who had conquered the earth and the netherworld, how have you been killed? Why have you gone to heaven leaving me, you who were so fond of your brother? Hearing the twang of your bowstring, the moving and unmoving world would tremble. Countless kings stood at your door with humility; how have you left your parents and gone to a beautiful woman?

Hearing this lamentation, the heroes of the world arrived. They forcibly restrained him, taking him by the hands. The people said: "O King, heroes do not lament when a hero is killed in battle. They go and destroy the one who killed him. Death is born with the body of every creature; there is no doubt it will come even after a hundred years. Whether it is your own or another's, what is the point of lamenting?" Hearing the words of the people, the demon Devantaka, knowing the nature of duty, became composed and said to his parents: "Do not grieve. I am going to devour my enemy. Tell me where he lives, the one who killed my brother. Having killed him, I will return happily, or I will go to my brother in death. By the mere knitting of my brows, the three worlds tremble. When I am agitated, the parents of the world will tremble."

The narrator said: Hearing Devantaka’s words, both parents were comforted, though they were still submerged in an ocean of grief. They explained the matter in detail to their eldest son: "The King of Kashi, a very righteous man, was holding a grand wedding celebration. Everyone was invited, including the son of Kashyapa. He is the powerful one known as Vinayaka, who was but a child. On the way, he killed your brother, my son Dhumraksha. To retaliate, we sent our best warriors, five thousand night-rangers. All of them were destroyed by that seven-year-old son of a sage. To retaliate for them, Narantaka went himself, equipped with many weapons and a four-fold army. Not even one returned, and suddenly we saw his head. Having mourned, we have brought that head here now."

The narrator said: Hearing these words, Devantaka trembled, his eyes turning red. He stood up as if to swallow the three worlds. In his pride and delusion, he spoke quickly to his father: "I will destroy Time, the killer of all. I will turn the earth upside down. With a glance of my anger, I will reduce the universe to ashes in an instant." Saying this, he let out a roar, as if shaking the earth, and summoned all the demons under the command of the gods. Bowing to his parents, Devantaka said: "I will go now and bring that sage's son quickly. Along with him, I will offer his head to my brother." Having said this, they all flew up suddenly. Devantaka and his countless followers, swift as birds, reached the city of Kashi and surrounded it from all sides. There was a great uproar, and the directions were covered in dust. The sun was obscured, and the citizens cried out in terror. "It has only been two or three days since Narantaka was killed; how has this destruction come again to take the people? Who is this with a fierce body, terrible to behold, capable of consuming time or the three worlds? The demons' trenches have been filled, and the demon chiefs sent out have all been killed by him; there is no way to go." While the citizens were in this state, messengers arrived to inform the King of Kashi of the arrival of Devantaka.

Thus ends the sixty-second chapter of the Krida Khanda in the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Siege of the City."

Chapter 63 : Shuka's Journey to a Distant Land

The messengers said: Devantaka, who is extremely fierce, has arrived surrounded by terrifying demons. They are countless, diverse, cause fear of death, and their heads reach the sky. We were bewildered by the sight of them and have fled to you. The city is besieged, O King; do what must be done.

Ka said: Hearing this report from the messengers, the King trembled. Overcome with deep anxiety, he went to the child Vinayaka. Seeing him playing among other children, he told him the entire situation.

The King said: Salutations to you, O Supreme Brahman, who has assumed a human form for your pastimes. Salutations to you, O Guru of all moving and non-moving beings, who performs various divine plays. We have been saved many times by you in your child form. Now, O Lord, protect us from this Devantaka.

Ka said: Having been thus prayed to by King Kashi, the child assumed a vast form, mounted a lion, and took up his bow. Accompanied by Siddhi and Buddhi, he roared, making the mountain caves resound. Blinding the sun with his radiance and emitting sparks of fire from his mouth, he held a bow, sword, and axe in his hands. Vinayaka went out of the city through the sky, shaking the hearts of the demons with his shouts. He saw the wicked army and the intolerant Devantaka. The countless demons were wandering about like locusts. Seeing the diverse army, the Lord of Obstacles said to Siddhi, "This army cannot be defeated by me alone. Create various forces of your own to conquer these demons. Do this quickly."

Ka said: Then Siddhi, after bowing to the lotus feet of the Supreme Lord, went to fight Devantaka and roared. Her roar was terrifying to all creatures and demons. The serpent Shesha, the sky, the mountains, and the clusters of trees all shook from the echoes of that great roar. She remembered the eight Siddhis, and they arrived filled with joy. First came Anima, then Garima, followed by Mahima and Laghima. Then Prapti, Prakamya, and Vashitva arrived. After that, Ishitva was attained, and from them, an army was formed, complete with elephants, horses, chariots, and foot soldiers, adorned with various weapons. Just as rivers flow into the ocean from all sides during the rainy season, so did the army, eager for battle, move in all ten directions.

With the sound of countless instruments and the loud cries of heroes, they looked like the God of Death, eager to consume the earth. Seeing this, Devantaka thought to himself, "I will defeat this child in a moment," and came forward to fight. "Where did such an army suddenly emerge from? The strange power I see is created by the child's illusion. I will die or kill; there is no glory in living otherwise." As the demon king spoke, his commanders said, "We will fight with the armies; you protect the rear, and victory will be ours." Drinking in their nectar-like words, a pleased Devantaka said, "Well said, great heroes! Go to battle, and I shall follow. May there be victory for you, who perform virtuous deeds, through my command."

Receiving his blessing and bowing to the slayer of demons, the demon Kardama went forth in a chariot-shaped formation. Deerghadanta went in the invincible Chakra-vyuha. The chief heroes, created by Garima, formed the Veeramohana formation. Talajangha happily went into the formation created by Prathima. Yakshma went into the formation created by Mahima. The great Ghantasura went into the formation created by Prapti. Raktakesha, with his forces, went into the formation created by Prakamya. Kalantaka went into the supreme formation created by Vashitva, and the powerful and invincible one went into the formation created by Ishitva.

It is impossible to describe their power in words. The eight demons, possessing great strength, fought in eight formations. They struck each other, being extremely difficult to defeat, releasing streams of arrows like clouds releasing rain. The warriors severed heads with various weapons. The earth became difficult to traverse due to the fallen heroes, elephants, and horses. It was strewn with severed thighs, knees, and hands. Some used shields to strike the feet of others. Some flew like mountains to crush the heroes. In the darkness of dust, no one could distinguish friend from foe. Then, the demons struck by the gods suddenly fell to the ground. Both the gods and the demons made terrifying sounds. The headless trunks fought there, and the hosts of Apsaras rejoiced. They shone with the clusters of weapons like blossoming Kimshuka flowers.

Shukra, using his knowledge, revived the dead demons. The eight formations, along with the remaining powerful demons, became anxious. They all told Ishitva about Shukra's actions. Due to her anger, a Kritya (a magical entity) emerged. Commanded by her glance, she went to the private parts of Bhrigu's son (Shukra), hid there, and later abandoned him in the Barbarian region. That is why wise men call that region Barbarian. Then the gods rejoiced and fought with strength. The demons, being struck, fled. Some sought refuge, crying, "Protect us, protect us!" They spoke to the gods and Gandharvas who were created by Anima and the others. Sometimes the demons were victorious, and sometimes the hosts of gods. They remained engaged in battle, each desiring the victory of the other. A fierce duel took place until the battle ceased.

Thus ends the sixty-third chapter of the Childhood Exploits in the Sports Section of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Abandonment of Shukra."

Chapter 64 : The Defeat of the Eight Siddhis

Ka said: As long as the demon Kalantaka and the Siddhi named Prakamya were engaged in their duel, Kalantaka remained victorious. Seeing this, Vashitva came to her aid with great speed and, with a swift movement of his hand, dropped a mountain peak onto the demon's head. Struck by this, Kalantaka suddenly fell to the ground, and seeing him bloodied and split in two, a great cry of lamentation arose among the demon army. Then, the demon lords Musala, Bhalla, Mahima, and four others advanced with great respect to fight. They struck Prakamya with various weapons, and countless gods were killed, falling like broken trees, while rivers of blood flowed in the thousands.

Then Ishita, Vashitva, and Vibhuti arrived and assisted Prakamya in the battle. They dropped four mountains upon the four demons, crushing them, and those demons departed to the difficult-to-attain heaven. Anima, grabbing Kardama by his hair, forcibly struck him to the ground, where he shattered into a hundred pieces. Blood flowed from his mouth as he rolled and gave up his life. Mahima, Laghima, and Garima then felled the demons Yakshasura, Talajangha, and Dirghadanta. Then the invincible demon Ghantasura, with his red hair, arrived with all his powerful, panting troops. Seeing the army struck by them, the Siddhis, with their sharp intellect, struck them firmly on the forehead with their fists, causing them to fall to the ground and shatter into a hundred pieces.

Then all the Siddhis roared, having achieved victory through their own power, and declared that Vinayaka was triumphant. Other minor demons were also destroyed by them. However, all the great demon warriors then destroyed the army of the Siddhis. A great clamor arose between the two armies, with shouts of "Kill! Strike! Bind! Be alert!" Fire emerged from the clashing of weapons, and when weapons broke, the warriors fought with their fists in anger. The battle continued uncontrollably, a mutual destruction that became tumultuous until the sun set behind the western mountain.

The directions were enveloped in great darkness, and then they struck each other with divine herbs. For three terrible days and nights, the battle continued without pause, and rivers of blood flowed in all ten directions, carrying the bodies of heroes. The battlefield was filled with shields like tortoises, swords like fish, heads like lotuses, elephants like crocodiles, and corpses like logs, with hair acting as moss. It was a sight that terrified the cowardly and brought great joy to the heroes.

As the gods, who were always powerful, were winning, Devantaka became anxious and thought to himself, "By what power have all the gods been defeated? Where is that ordinary child-illusion that fascinates people? I shall now destroy the eight Siddhis and the entire army. I will seize the child Vinayaka and take him to my abode." Saying this, with sword in hand, he filled the directions with his roar. He charged at the enemy army, killing everyone with his sword. The gods, fainting from fear, fell to the ground. Some gave up their lives while remembering Lord Vinayaka, some became rivers of blood, and some went to heaven. Others lost their lives just by looking at the demon. The army of the gods was broken and fled in all ten directions.

The demon, sword in hand, struck them from behind. Seeing this, Garima dropped a mountain covered in trees onto the demon's body, but he cut it into a hundred pieces with his sword. Then the eight Siddhis, agitated, rained down many mountains, but he swiftly cut them with the speed of his sword strikes. Mahima flew up, landed on his shoulder, and snatched the sword from the demon's hand, then vanished. He then forcibly threw the sword at the demon's head. It shattered into a hundred pieces, but miraculously, the demon's head did not break. The demon then rained down a volley of arrows, breaking the backs of the Siddhis. He shot ten, five, or seven arrows at each of them, causing them confusion until they fell to the ground.

Then the gods fought while the eight Siddhis were fallen. After a moment, the Siddhis regained their composure and went to Lord Vinayaka. Understanding the situation, they spoke to Vinayaka, "How can there be any consideration where no intelligence is seen? The Siddhis have been defeated. Give me permission, and I will go to fight that demon to test his prowess."

Thus ends the sixty-fourth chapter of the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Defeat of the Eight Siddhis."

Chapter 65 : The Victory of Buddhi

Ka said: Hearing the words of Buddhi, the God (Vinayaka) was filled with joy and spoke to her.

The God said: Go and fight the demon, kill him, and attain glory. Having said this, Vinayaka gave her beautiful garments. She bowed to the God and went towards the demon for battle. At the sound of her roar, the three worlds trembled. From her mouth emerged a mighty power. She was matted-haired, hideous-faced, and eager to devour the world. From her wide eyes, she emitted masses of flames. Burning the demon army, she advanced and they fled. Upon seeing her, some demons lost their lives. Others wondered where to go and where they might find safety. Some cried, "Run, run, O destroyer of gods, we are dead!"

Hearing this uproar, Devantaka went forward. Having quickly prepared his bow, he shot arrows like venomous snakes. Displaying his dexterity, he showered her body with them, covering the sun with the network of arrows from above. She opened her vast mouth and swallowed the arrows like lotus stalks. All the quivers of that demon became empty. She was not satisfied, just as a rakshasa is never satisfied with humans. Seeing Devantaka depleted of power, she approached the army. She devoured those demons, struck some with her hands, threw some into her throat, and crushed others. Countless crores of demons were eaten, crushed, or killed. Crushing some with the kicks of her feet, she approached Devantaka.

She said to him then: Enter my yoni, where those demons lie like those who have returned to their mother's womb. Those who were eaten and died have been digested in my belly. Fearing this, he fled quickly, overwhelmed by the stench of urine and feces. Wherever he hid, she followed him. He wandered through the heavens, the netherworlds, and the ten directions. Then, grabbing him by his hair, she threw him into her yoni. With her, Buddhi then approached Vinayaka.

She bowed, leading her forward, her eyes swirling with intoxication, her breasts striking trees on both sides as she passed. Flowing like a dense cloud of arrows, moving slowly, the God Vinayaka saw her in that terrifying form and dismissed her. When she was dismissed, the demon fell from her yoni onto the earth. Because he was foul-smelling, he was cast out by the messengers. Regaining consciousness, he bathed and silently went home, ashamed, head bowed, lost in thought, withered, and deeply sorrowful.

Buddhi, having been presented, bowed to Vinayaka. Seeing her, some laughed, some were afraid, some withered, and some fell. Then she quickly said to him: The demon army has been devoured. That Devantaka has been cast into my anus and placed there by me. Now, O God, treasure of compassion, tell me my place of residence.

The God said: The demon has cheated and gone to his own home, O destroyer of demons. I have realized your valor is greater than that of Indra and the others. Enter my own mouth and find rest there. I shall deal with him; you need not worry.

Ka said: Hearing those words and seeing his mouth, she entered it. She slept with great joy, like a child in its mother's lap, within the belly of the God of Gods, who dwells in all worlds.

Thus ends the sixty-fifth chapter named The Victory of Buddhi in the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana.

Chapter 66 : The Defeat of Siddhi

Ka said: Sharada and Raudraketu saw their son, Devantaka, alone at night. They embraced him and spoke. He was covering his face, feeling deeply ashamed and distressed, trembling like a tree shaken by the wind, and unable to speak.

The father asked: Why do you remain silent like a mute about your condition? I will strive to obtain for you whatever you desire, even if it is rare in the three worlds.

Ka said: Having heard his father’s nectar-like words, the son, with a focused mind, spoke to his father in the presence of his mother without hesitation.

The son said: Following your command, I went to fight Vinayaka. I destroyed countless gods with arrows resembling venomous snakes. Rivers of blood flowed, and the army of the gods was broken. Then, a great sorceress appeared—she had a mouth like a cave, touched the sky, possessed terrifying hair, feet like the underworld, and breasts like mountains. She came to protect the army of the gods. I struck her with my sword, but she felt nothing. She then threw my entire army into her cavernous womb. She devoured all my weapons and arrows, cast me into her womb, and then went to Lord Vinayaka. Because of the slipperiness of that womb, I slipped and fell to the earth. In the great darkness, I was not noticed, so I escaped and returned home. After bathing in the river, I remained here, ashamed and downcast.

Ka said: Hearing the words of his son, Raudraketu spoke to him. I will tell you a remedy; you should not worry. After contemplating for a moment, he gave him a great mantra. The father spoke the Aghora mantra with its seed syllable again, instructing him to meditate on Shiva, worship Him, and perform the best rituals. He advised him to perform the homa (fire sacrifice), tarpana (libation), and feed Brahmins, each to the extent of one-tenth of the ritual. When Shiva is pleased, a horse will emerge from the sacrificial pit. Mount it and go to battle; you will surely achieve victory.

Ka said: Hearing his father’s words, the son spoke to him with joy. The instruction has been given well; please tell me the procedure. Then, having sent the people away, they went inside their house. Both wore red garments, were smeared with red sandalwood paste, and worshipped Shiva with red flowers. They performed the ritual for many days with great devotion. When the ritual was completed, they prepared a sacrificial pit with great care. It was hexagonal, endowed with proper characteristics, and joined with a yoni-shaped girdle. Having established the fire and the vessels according to the rules, they sat on a seat of five corpses and offered oblations. They cut pieces of flesh from their own knees and offered them with devotion. The fire became heated with the blood, ghee, and flesh. When the one-tenth homa was completed, he worshipped the fire deity. He immediately performed a sacrifice by cutting off his son's head, offered it as the final oblation, and then dismissed the fire. By the grace of Ishvara, his son appeared as he was before. He fed the Brahmins according to the rules after the libations.

Then, when the night passed and the sun rose, he saw a black horse with smooth limbs and great strength. It had the speed of the mind and shook the three worlds with its neighing. He worshipped it with great devotion and performed the ritual of waving lights. Adorned with auspicious ornaments of gems and pearls, he bowed to the Brahmins and embraced his father. Receiving their blessings, the destroyer of gods mounted the horse and took the remaining army, causing the earth to tremble. The army shone with weapons, armor, tridents, bows, and arrows. With them, the destroyer of gods reached the battlefield.

As all the soldiers cried out, the army of the gods became terrified. The sky was filled with dust, and nothing could be discerned. The demon, the destroyer of armies, had returned. The followers of the Siddhi army fled, feeling great distress. Seeing him arrive at the battlefield, the Siddhi army rose up, making the quarters resound with neighing and lion-like roars. Then they struck each other with weapons. They challenged each other, saying, "Endure this blow," and struck with force. Placing their knees on the ground, they drew their bows to the ears and fought each other with arrows like venomous snakes. Some fought using shields, while others remembered previous blows and struck back. Remembering past enmities, they made their enemies look hideous. Some, endowed with heroic splendor, grabbed their enemies by the hair. They knocked them to the ground with kicks and blows. Some, in a frenzy, struck each other on the head.

Seeing his demon army broken, the destroyer of gods sent his horse toward the army created by Siddhi. Hearing the neighing of the horse, some gods fainted and fell to the ground, while others were crushed by the horse's hooves. Others were killed by the trident or the sword. He struck down the gods with a multitude of arrows. As his entire army was being slaughtered, the Siddhis fled and went to Vinayaka.

Thus ends the sixty-sixth chapter of the Krida Khanda in the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Defeat of Siddhi."

Chapter 67 : The Battle of Weapons

Ka said: Knowing this, he felt great astonishment in his heart. Then Vinayaka, mounted on a lion, became eager for battle out of anger. With his own roar, he made the sky and the directions resound, shaking the minds of all beings and even the mountains. He moved with great speed toward Devantaka. Then the demon Devantaka laughed and spoke to him.

The Demon said: How have you come here, with dry palate, unable to digest even butter? O child, you should not stay for battle; go and drink your mother's milk. Born of Kashyapa and Aditi, why have you come, O fool? Why do you wish to fight with Devantaka today, O child? Time itself fears seeing me; you wish to die in vain. Because of your very delicate body, you will become but a single morsel.

Ka said: Hearing the demon's words, his eyes reddened with anger. Vinayaka laughed and said, emitting fire from his mouth.

The God said: You are intoxicated by drinking liquor or perhaps you are delirious. You speak incoherently and without logic due to your foolish nature. A small fire, fanned by the wind, burns everything. I shall destroy you, prompted by your own words, O lowest of demons; you do not understand. Now abandon that arrogance; your end is near. I have descended for your sake, I who am the eternal Brahman. Because of the boon from Sthanu (Shiva), you have caused suffering to all, but you do not realize, O mindless one, that the limit of that boon has been reached. The sin that has been generated by the suffering of the three worlds is yours, O evil-minded one. Enough of your boasting today; show your own prowess. Who would show his face to you after emerging from the power of Shiva? Endure and strike today if you desire battle. You desire your tomorrow's death today out of foolishness.

Ka said: Having spoken thus to the demon, the Lord prepared his bow. Wishing to grant him the fate of Narantaka, Vinayaka made the three worlds tremble with the twang of his bowstring. He pulled the string to his ear and released an arrow at the demon. The demon shattered that arrow into hundreds of pieces, and it fell to the earth. Then the demon, having readied his bow, released arrows. At the sound of his bow, the mountains and directions wailed. With a roar, the Lord of Obstacles caused those arrows to fall. Vinayaka again released many arrows at the demon lord. With one arrow, the God knocked his crown to the ground, with another his ear-rings, and with two he pierced his arms. Then, with an arrow to the forehead, the demon became filled with rage. Gnashing his teeth and widening his eyes, he released many other arrows. Covering the sky and the quarters, he showered them upon Vinayaka. Vinayaka, with a single arrow, neutralized them in mid-air. He himself instantly created a pavilion made of arrows, O Lord. In the terrible darkness, they fought each other. With a shower of arrows, they destroyed the other's shower of arrows, both filled with anger.

Then the demon, having chanted a great mantra eight hundred times, empowered an arrow with the Varunastra (Weapon of Varuna). He released it, and from it, millions of elephants appeared, four-tusked and mountain-like, capable of crushing Meru and Mandara. Rivers of rut flowed from them on all sides, and by their mere trumpeting, the three worlds resounded. Just as clouds roar during the rains, O great sage, those elephants incessantly destroyed the divine armies. The elephants pursued those who ran in the ten directions, killing the heroes with their feet, trunks, and tusk-tips. Seeing the slaughter of his army, the Lord released the lion-weapon. Then lions appeared by the hundreds and thousands. At the sound of their roar, the elephants fell to the ground. The lions tore apart the temples of the elephants. With the roars of lions, the trumpeting of elephants, and the cries of the demons, the three worlds trembled, and all the gods were amazed. The lions leaped repeatedly and fell upon the elephants' heads. Thus, all those elephants were killed by the swarm of lions, appearing like mountains struck by Indra's thunderbolt. Then the lions went forth, devouring the demons in the ten directions.

Seeing his entire army destroyed, the slayer of gods became anxious. This child, though young, appears powerful, this son of Kashyapa. Now I shall surely show him the abode of Yama. Having said this, the demon lord again chanted a mantra over an arrow. He fixed the tiger-weapon onto his bow. Pulling the bow to his ear, he released it at the divine army. That arrow went swiftly, making the sky and directions resound, and by the wind of its feathers, the groups of trees fell broken. Then many swarms of tigers appeared. Those tigers devoured the lions, and then those lions vanished.

Thus ends the sixty-seventh chapter named The War of Weapons in the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana.

Chapter 68 : The Battle of Weapons

Ka said: Then the Daitya (Devantaka) respectfully chanted mantras over two arrows, one with the Nidrastra (sleep-inducing weapon) and the other with the Gandharvastra (illusion-inducing weapon). Placing his left knee forward, he drew the bowstring and released them. At the sound of these arrows, the three worlds suddenly trembled. One fell upon the army and the other near the gods. Then, Vinayaka saw Gandharvas and Apsaras playing lutes and drums, and performing wonderful dances before him. He did not realize that his weapons had fallen from his hands. Bewitched by the sweet sounds, he did not know what to do. All the soldiers, deluded by the Nidrastra, fell asleep. Just as children sleep restlessly at the onset of night, all the women also fell asleep, devoid of shame.

Seeing this, Devantaka rejoiced and roared. He then set up many camps around the great army of the gods. He created a triangular pit, performed the necessary ground rituals, and brought hundreds of pots of blood with great effort. After bathing, he sat in the Padmasana posture and respectfully performed rituals amidst various spirits. Having killed hundreds of Daityas, the Daitya placed a large heap of meat and, having established the fire according to the scriptures, performed the Abhichara (black magic) ritual. Naked, he offered the meat into the fire while chanting mantras. After offering a thousand oblations, followed by the final sacrifice (Purnahuti) in the pit, he saw a goddess who was tormented by hunger. He gave her the pots of human flesh to drink. Realizing she was still unsatisfied, he offered her other spirits. Then, she emerged, terrifying, with hair like the sky, large hollow eyes, a hideous face, and a reddish complexion. She spoke, greatly pleased, causing the ten directions to resound: "I am satisfied with your blood and meat; you have nothing to fear anywhere." The Daitya then worshipped her with devotion, offering sixteen types of services and bowing down. Then, adorned with divine garments and various ornaments, he sat on the lap of the goddess and roared powerfully. Flying up, he remained in the sky with her. He appeared radiant, holding a bow and arrows, and various other weapons. The son of Raudraketu, overjoyed, said to her: "I was a restless child, the son of Kashyapa, but what is there for me to do now? I will destroy all the armies before you."

As the Daitya king was saying this, the King of Kashi heard his words. He then respectfully awakened Vinayaka. The King of Kashi said: "O knower of the past, present, and future, why do you not perceive this illusion? Why are you caught in this Gandharva illusion created by the Daitya? It is like the demonic magic created by Devantaka through black magic. It will destroy your army; be vigilant."

Ka said: Hearing the King's words, the Lord became vigilant. Realizing through his divine vision that everything was an illusion, he drew two arrows and empowered them with mantras. Vinayaka released those swift arrows, the Ghantastra (bell weapon) and the Khagastra (bird weapon), which caused a great stir. Roaring like clouds, with golden feathers and the speed of the wind, they suddenly covered the sun's disc. A great bell sound arose, deluding everyone. Hearing the sound of the bell, all the soldiers stood up. The Siddhas and all the warriors fought, weapons in hand. The other arrow, the Khagastra, was released into his army. Then, birds of various forms and great power appeared. By the wind of their wings, the Gandharva illusion vanished, just as darkness disappears at the sunrise of the sun's charioteer. Then, all those birds devoured the Daitya soldiers. Some were killed by the impact of wings, some were wounded by beaks, and some Daityas fell dead out of sheer fear. A great cry of distress arose among the Daitya army.

Then, Devantaka, in anger, sent the Khadgastra (sword weapon). Hearing its sound, the ocean of the army was agitated. Countless swords emerged from the celestial elephants. Due to the collision of the two armies, fire broke out. The Daityas, scorched by the fire, fell to the ground. Some birds were killed, some were burnt by the fire, and others disappeared, while the directions shone brightly. The light of the countless swords blinded the eyes of the army. Struck by the shower of swords, the gods fell to the ground. Some had their arms severed, others their heads or hands; some were struck in the stomach, knees, or backs. They fell among the heaps of dead gods, lifeless. All the armies, created by the eight Siddhis, were thus destroyed. Rivers of blood flowed in the ten directions, carrying the spirits.

Seeing the wonderful prowess of the Daitya, Vinayaka drew a strong arrow, empowered it with the Vajrastra (thunderbolt weapon), and released it into the Daitya army with a great cry. Mountains and trees fell to the ground at its sound. Due to the fire of that weapon, the directions were set ablaze. Suddenly, it covered the sun's disc, shining brightly. Some birds were killed and burnt by its radiance. It instantly shattered the sword weapon into thousands of pieces and burnt the Daitya army with many thunderbolts. Thousands were killed by a single strike of the thunderbolt. Wherever the Daitya went, it followed. It crushed heads, feet, hands, shoulders, and thighs. It even pursued the Daityas who had gone underground and struck them down. Thus, all the Daityas were killed by thousands of sharp thunderbolts. Then, all those thunderbolts went towards Devantaka.

He also took an arrow and empowered it with effort. Combining it with the Raudrastra (fierce weapon), he drew his bow and released the auspicious arrow marked with Shiva's name upon the enemy army. It made the sky, the directions, and the sub-directions resound. Vomiting sparks of fire in all directions, resembling the fire of dissolution, it caused the earth and the heavens to flee in fear in the ten directions. A great uproar arose in Vinayaka's army. When that arrow fell, a terrifying being emerged, with a hideous face, as if consuming the three worlds. He was matted-haired, long-armed, long-footed, and large-bellied. He supported the earth, moved in the sky, had a tongue like a mountain, and was terrifying. That great being devoured the Vajrastra in an instant. Wishing to kill Vinayaka, he approached him in an instant. Then, Vinayaka quickly employed the Brahmastra. Empowering a swift arrow with a hundred mantras, the god released it suddenly by drawing the bow to his ear. The three worlds trembled at its loud sound. The directions were burnt by sparks, and nothing could be discerned. Then, another equally terrifying being emerged. Both of them fought in the sky, each desiring victory over the other. Those two mighty ones engaged in various types of wrestling. In an instant, both disappeared, and were seen nowhere.

Thus ends the sixty-eighth chapter, titled "The Battle of Weapons," in the Krida Khanda of the glorious Ganesha Purana.

Chapter 69 : The Battle Between Gods and Demons

K said: Then Devantaka, greatly astonished, pondered thus: "The more illusion I create to stop him, the more this boy displays his valor. When will he meet his end, and when shall I be able to rest, my labors finished?" Thinking thus, he strung his bow, invoked a terrible arrow, and released it at Vinayaka. That arrow created an endless rain of shafts upon the Lord, and he also fashioned a terrifying power capable of consuming the three worlds. The Lord of Obstacles saw the demon chief in his presence, raining down many sharp volleys of arrows. Then the eight Siddhis quickly flew up, seized that power with great force, and brought it to Vinayaka. As it was being brought, it slipped from their hands and fled. Enraged, the demon lord seized them by their hair. The demon then struck Anima firmly with his fist; struck by the blow, she fell down unconscious. Then Laghima, Garima, and Vashima struck the great demon with kicks, but the demon quickly caught them by their feet one by one and dashed them against the earth, causing them to escape. Then Prakamya and Bhuti struck him with force, and the demon fell to the ground, vomiting fire from his mouth. Recovering his senses in a moment, he mounted his sturdy horse and, swift as the wind, struck Vinayaka with his weapons. The Lord was slightly stunned by the blow of the sharp sword, but within a moment, he became alert and rushed at the demon leader. Just as Vishnu rushed at Hiranyakashipu or the Lord of Sachi at Vritra, the Lord shone, bearing a lotus, a noose, and a goad in his four hands, glowing with supreme heroic splendor. Roaring like a cloud, the Lord struck the demon chief. Despite being struck by the four weapons with great speed, the demon did not budge. Vinayaka was filled with great astonishment to see his weapons go in vain. He considered the demon's body to be harder than a thunderbolt. Then the Lord took up the great weapon of the demon Dhumraksha—the thunderbolt that had come from the solar orb—and with it, he struck the demon, shattering it into a hundred pieces. Not even a hair of the demon moved, which was also a great wonder. Then they struck each other with various weapons. On his head, back, heart, and arms, the fire generated by the clash of their weapons burned the earth, yet neither was afraid and they continued the battle. Even when midnight was enveloped in darkness, they did not cease. Then they fought each other by artificial light. Then Raudraketu created an illusion to bewilder the gods. He made Aditi appear beautiful and placed her in the demon's hand—lotus-eyed, with full breasts, her forehead reddened with saffron, wearing loose necklaces and beautiful bracelets, adorned with divine garments, a wave of loveliness, and wearing a shimmering golden bodice. Seeing Vinayaka, she wept while in the demon's grasp. She cried out to him, "Run! Run! I am terrified; why are you just watching?" As she spoke, the demon forcibly tore her bodice and pulled at her garments with a mind filled with lust. She cried out loudly to the Lord, "Where has your heroism gone? Out of fear of public shame, release me quickly, for you have no affection!" Seeing this, Vinayaka, with a choked throat and filled with rage, forgot all reason, and his weapons fell from his hands. He lamented, "This is my mother; how did she fall into his hands? Cursed be the life of one whose mother has reached such a miserable state! The mother of the gods has met with such a plight!" Seeing the Lord of the Ganas lamenting, the King of Kashi and the people in the city also lamented greatly. Then Devantaka mocked the Lord in many ways: "Fie on your birth and your valor today! Why do you not give up your life? You are shameless to show your face in the world now. I shall sever her head from her body right before you, wretch!"

K said: Hearing these cruel words, the Lord Vinayaka pondered in his mind, "He speaks the truth. What is the use of living? Should I take poison, use a noose, or strike my belly with weapons to cause my death?" While he was thinking thus, filled with grief and sorrow, Vinayaka heard a voice from the sky.

The Voice said: "This is an illusion created by the demon of evil mind, O Lord. Be alert in battle and slay your enemy." Then, realizing it was an illusion, he became alert. The wise Lord rejoiced and prepared to slay the demon. Remembering the boon given to that wicked soul by Shambhu—that all weapons and missiles would be useless against him except at dawn—and knowing this boon, he went out to fight at dawn. The demon also saw Vinayaka before him at the end of the battle, his eyes reddened, shining with a crown and glowing earrings, radiant with the luster of his tusks, adorned with strings of pearls, wearing divine garments, the nectar-like Lord, the all-pervading one whose lotus-like face touched the sky. Seeing that form, Devantaka was terrified and bewildered. "Is he half human or half elephant?" Thus ends the sixty-ninth chapter of the Krida Khanda in the Shri Ganesha Purana, entitled "The Display of Illusion."

Chapter 70 : The Slaying of Devantaka and Entry into the City

King Kashi said: While the destroyer of gods was speaking thus in fear and confusion, the Lord held him in His lap like a small child. Ganesha, through His own divine power, assumed a beautiful lotus posture and told the demon king to remember his own auspicious boon. The demon grabbed Ganesha’s tusk with both hands and swung his body back and forth in the sky repeatedly. Just as the destroyer of gods fell to the ground having broken the tusk, the Lord skillfully caught His own tusk. With that tusk, He struck the destroyer of gods on the head and roared with a great sound, making the directions and sub-directions resound. Shaking the entire earth and all the netherworlds, He instantly shattered the demon’s body into a hundred pieces with the blow of His tusk. A rain of blood fell from the sky like a downpour from clouds, and all the inhabitants of the earth considered it a great calamity. The light that had been within the demon’s body entered Vinayaka as all the gods watched, having witnessed the battle. His body fell to the earth, covering three yojanas, crushing groups of trees and mountains along with their vegetation.
