Seeing his fate, the soldiers fled in all ten directions, and many perished due to the impact of his falling body.

The gods, having returned to their own abodes, showered flowers, and divine drums sounded along with the beating of royal drums. The directions became clear, pleasant winds blew, and the lights of the worlds and the fires became serene. Rivers that had been flowing in reverse began to flow in their proper courses. Then, Indra and the other gods, along with the sages, worshipped Him. They praised the Lord of Lords, Vinayaka, with supreme devotion, saying, "O Lord, we have been liberated from the bondage created by the destroyer of gods. Because You have performed a task like that of Upendra (Vishnu), You shall become famous in the world by the name Upendra. We shall now dwell in our respective domains without fear, and the offerings of Svaha, Svadha, and Vashat will be performed in every home." Having said this, they bowed to the Lord, circumambulated Him, and with His permission, departed to their own abodes, filled with joy. The sages, having given the name Hrishikesha to the Lord, bowed and returned to their own hermitages, overflowing with happiness.

Then, all the kings worshipped and bowed to Vinayaka, saying, "The earth has been uplifted by You. Because You were burdened by the weight of the demon, You are the supporter of the earth." Having said this, they went to their respective cities by His command. Thereafter, the King of Kashi looked at Vinayaka, who was in the form of a child, riding a lion and playing with other children. Seeing the King, the child embraced him with great affection, and both, filled with joy, shed tears from their eyes. Then the King said to the Lord, "My great fortune has arisen. The Supreme Eternal Brahman, who is inaccessible even to Brahma and others, has become visible to me today due to the fruition of my past merits. He who is the cause of all causes of the universe, yet devoid of them, who is known through Vedanta, the true light, and the radiance of all lights, who has many forms yet is formless—He is playing in my house in the form of a child, removing the burden of the earth in a charming manner."

King Kashi said: Hearing this praise, the Lord of Obstacles wiped away his tears and said, "I will never go far away from you for even a moment." Then the King worshipped Him with numerous offerings. Accompanied by the sounds of musical instruments, the chants of bards, and soldiers rejoicing over the death of the destroyer of gods, the King returned to his city while praising Vinayaka in His child form. Having dismissed all the people, given them garments, and honored some with betel leaves, he entered his beautiful palace with a heart full of joy, keeping Vinayaka in the lead.

Thus ends the seventieth chapter of the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "Entry into the City."

Chapter 71 : The Return to Vinayaka’s Ashram

Kritayuga said: The next day, O Brahman, the King went to his throne, summoned his ministers, brave warriors, elders, friends, and the twice-born, and after paying them respects, he spoke what was in his heart. The King said: I had invited Vinayaka for my son’s wedding. Many obstacles arose, but they were all removed by him. I told Aditi that I would soon bring her auspicious son back, but many days have passed since then. Now that the three worlds are at peace, let us consider his marriage. The ministers said: You have spoken well, O King; delay causes obstacles. Since this marriage task has begun, let it be completed now. By the grace of Vinayaka, the whole world is free from distress. Let wedding invitations be sent to friends living far away, and let messengers be appointed to arrange all necessary provisions in the villages.

Kritayuga said: Hearing the words of the ministers, the people and the courtiers said it was well-spoken, and the King rejoiced. He immediately consulted the astrologers to determine the auspicious wedding day. He sent friends and messengers to invite the guests and had the supplies arranged by trusted messengers according to tradition. Then, the King of Magadha also arrived with his daughter, and all the friends gathered from various directions. They exchanged many gifts with respect and performed the installation of the deities, followed by grand festivities. Having performed the wedding with all its rituals and parts with great effort, they pleased the Brahmins and all the people with wealth and gifts. After bidding farewell to all the friends, the King adorned Vinayaka with various jewels and fine garments. He anointed him and fed him delicious food along with his relatives. Then, the best of men, accompanied by him, mounted a chariot and set off for the excellent Kashyapa Ashram amidst the sound of musical instruments.

Upon hearing this, all the citizens left their duties and rushed out. They abandoned their meals, studies, sleep, and tasks. Without even dressing properly, they hurried out of the city. Thousands of children surrounded Vinayaka, crying, "How can you leave us? How have you become so heartless? Why didn't you tell us earlier that you were leaving? How can you go to your own ashram without eating at our homes?" Some children held his lotus feet while weeping, and others, out of affection, embraced him and held his lotus-like hands. Similarly, the women of the city, both young and old, came to see him, having forgotten their own adornments. Just as rivers flow into the ocean during the rainy season, and as swans flock to a group of lotuses, or as the wind rushes toward the Lord, they all came. Filled with great affection, they said, "Why are you leaving, Vinayaka? Why have you suddenly abandoned your affection and become so heartless?"

Kritayuga said: Seeing them tired, running, stumbling, and falling, he descended from the chariot along with the King. He said to them all, "O people, wait for a moment." Having come out of the city with the King, he said, "I now request you all not to abandon your compassion for me. Please forgive any offenses I may have committed in your homes. Do not break our bond if you wish to see me." Hearing these words, all the citizens were filled with sorrow. Their bodies bristled with emotion, and they spoke with choked voices. The people said: "You are the father of the entire world, and there is no deception in you. A father never abandons his children, even if they have committed faults. The moon never becomes hot, nor does gold turn blue. Why have you become so heartless, and why are you so eager to leave? If you had not come here in the first place, this grief would not have occurred. Why are you stealing our hearts as you leave? What shall we do now that our hearts are taken away? Just as aquatic creatures cannot live when the water is gone, what use is our body to us once the soul has departed? What remedy is there if the sugarcane itself begins to drip poison? Therefore, take us with you to wherever you are going, for we are your servants."

Hearing their words, he spoke with a choked voice, shedding many tears, to all the women, elders, and children. The God said: "You should never feel sorrow, for there is no separation from me anywhere. I am the indweller of all, devoid of any decay, and the embodiment of bliss. Your minds will not find peace merely by thinking of me. Instead, create my image out of clay and worship it in every home. Whenever you are in trouble, O people, remember me. I will grant you my presence and destroy your distress."

Kritayuga said: Hearing this, all the citizens rejoiced. They bowed down, circumambulated him, and praised him with shouts of victory. Having received his permission, all the citizens returned to their respective homes. Vinayaka also quickly mounted his chariot and proceeded forward. Accompanied by the King of Kashi and longing to see his mother, he looked back at the city's children. Seeing them weeping, he told them again, "I will return, so go now. Do not worry; I tell you the truth and never speak a lie." Having turned them back, he reached his ashram in a moment.

Chapter 72 : Vinayaka's Return to His Own Realm

Ka said: Vinayaka had already been mentioned by the messenger. He arrived accompanied by the King of Kashi, who was seated in his chariot, to meet Aditi. Upon his departure, she had run after him in distress, and in her haste, the end of her garment had fallen. Seeing her, the son of the sage quickly descended from the chariot, approached his mother, and embraced her with joy. He shed tears of happiness, and she, having seen him after a long time, also wept. For a moment, they became one, like milk mixed with milk. Out of affection and with a peaceful mind, Aditi joyfully offered him her breast. Wiping her eyes, she said, "You have been away for many days, and you must be exhausted."

Then the King bowed to her and spoke in a choked voice. The King said, "Many days have passed since I took the divine Vinayaka away. Therefore, Mother, you should not be angry with me. O beloved of the sage, I was unable to bear the separation from him. Just as there is no satisfaction in nectar and no indifference in the ocean, his presence in our home increases our love day by day. Through him, many calamities in our city were averted; countless demons were slain, and the gods were made joyful. Great fame was achieved, and the foundations of righteousness were established. He performed feats of valor that even Indra and the others could not accomplish."

Ka said: Thus, he narrated the entire account with reverence. Then Aditi, having circled a bowl of curd around the boy to ward off the evil eye, set it aside. The King then took the boy to the hermitage. Kashyapa, who had come out, saw his son and the King. Both bowed to him with folded hands. The sage embraced them both, smelled their heads with joy, and with a choked throat, holding Vinayaka in his lap, he said, "King of Kashi, it was not appropriate for you to delay in bringing the boy back. You said you would bring him back quickly, so how did this happen? O King, my limbs, which were scorched by the separation from him, have now attained coolness through the merit of seeing him."

Ka said: Hearing the sage's words, the King of Kashi, having sat on a beautiful seat after receiving permission from the sage, said, "O Lord of Sages, it is through his own Maya that this truth has manifested to me. I have realized that the Lord is truly the master of my house. This Lord, having fulfilled his purpose and being subject to his own will, has returned after completing the marriage of my son."

Ka said: He then informed them of his deeds, such as the slaying of the demons. Hearing of the prowess and the many virtues of their son, both the sage and his wife rejoiced. Then they all partook of a meal consisting of six flavors with reverence. After bestowing blessings upon him, the sage sent the King away. The King bowed to them, circumambulated them, and departed. Having received their permission, he left, his eyes filled with tears of sorrow. Remembering his virtues and with a heart overflowing with affection, he quickly traveled to the city amidst the sound of musical instruments.

All the citizens and children, desiring to see Vinayaka, came and saw him there, but then became sorrowful. Seeing the King of Kashi, they returned to their respective homes. The next day, all the citizens asked the King joyfully, "You said the Lord would come, so why has he not arrived, O King? Why did you leave him behind with such a hard heart?"

The King said, "I prayed to the Lord intensely, and he, the beloved of the sage, told me: 'Install my image and worship me, all of you. The One who dwells in the hearts of all is never separated from you.' Therefore, they had a beautiful metal image made—four-armed, three-eyed, adorned with all ornaments, with winnowing-basket ears, an elephant face, and beautiful in every limb. They installed it with reverence under the name Dhundhiraja. Learned Brahmins and priests, expert in the Vedas and scriptures, built a magnificent temple and worshipped him daily. Whatever desire one has when worshipping Vinayaka, the Lord, being worshipped with devotion, grants it. Thus, Vinayaka shone in many forms."

When Vishveshvara (Shiva) returned to his city with all the gods, and Divodasa remained happily in Avimukta (Kashi), Vinayaka said to the sage Kashyapa and his mother, "I became your son because I was worshipped through penance in the past. The burden of the earth has been properly removed; the two mighty demons, the tormentors of the three worlds, the wicked Devantaka and Narantaka, have been slain. The gods and the virtuous have been protected and established. Now, I shall return to my eternal abode."

Ka said: Hearing these words, both were distressed, and with choked throats, they asked, "O Lord, when will we see you again?" Then he said to his mother, "Mother, you will see me in the temple of Bhavani; this is my true and beloved promise." As he spoke these words, the Lord vanished. Distressed, they had an image made and a beautiful temple built, naming it Vinayaka with great devotion. In that image, the Lord, who is all-pervading and takes many forms, reveals himself whenever he is meditated upon.

Gritsamada said: Thus, I have narrated to you the auspicious story of the divine Vinayaka, which grants all success upon hearing it. It is blessed, brings fame and longevity, destroys all calamities, grants all desires, and washes away all sins. I will tell you again how he descended in the house of Shiva, known as Mayureshwara, for the destruction of the demon Sindhu, and the many wonderful deeds he performed from his childhood.

Thus ends the seventy-second chapter of the Krida Khanda in the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Narrative of Vinayaka."

Chapter 73 : The Description of the Origin of Sindhu

Vyasa said: O Brahman, how did the one known as Mayureshwara descend into the abode of Shiva? For what purpose did he come, and what were his deeds? How did the name Mayureshwara come to be associated with Vinayaka? Tell me all of this, for I am never satisfied with hearing your words.

Kavi said: In the Treta Yuga, there was a great demon named Sindhu. He was killed by the powerful Lord who descended into the abode of Shiva. In this regard, they cite an ancient story, a dialogue between Shaunaka and King Chakrapani. In the Maithila region, in the beautiful city of Gandaki, there lived a king named Chakrapani, who was like another Vishnu incarnate. His virtues could never be described even by Shesha. In brilliance, he eclipsed the sun; in beauty, he surpassed Manmatha (Cupid); and in intellect, strength, and valor, he surpassed Brihaspati and Skanda. By his power, he brought the entire earth under his control in an instant and engaged all other kings in his service. There was no counting the horses, elephants, infantry, and chariots he possessed on this earth. Lakshmi herself was permanently visible in his home, and he constantly illuminated the directions with jewels, gold, and pearls. His ministers were the highly intelligent Samba and Subodhana, who served their master’s cause by treating their own lives as mere blades of grass. His beautiful wife was named Ugra, who possessed a lovely smile. When her moon-like face shone during the day, the lilies would bloom, and the radiance of her many ornaments dispelled all darkness. All chaste women went to her to learn the virtues of devotion to one's husband. Thus, the king was honored by the wise and was always devoted to Vishnu. He was fond of listening to the Puranas and was dedicated to the scriptures. However, being without offspring, he suffered day and night. Whatever children were born to him died instantly. He performed many vows, charities, and sacrifices.

Then, at one point, the king, feeling detached in his royal assembly, summoned his ministers and citizens along with his wife. He said to them all, "I shall now abandon my scepter, my treasury, and my kingdom. What is the use of a kingdom to one who is childless and devoid of heaven? If the deeds I performed for the sake of a son were done with the intention of offering them to the Lord, then both of us might attain liberation and the exhaustion of our past karma. My life has passed in vain; I shall now go to the forest with joy. You all, who are obedient to my commands, take charge of the state. I will go to the forest to perform penance for the benefit of all. If I attain my desire, I shall return to my city. Give me your permission now." Hearing the king’s words, everyone was filled with sorrow. Shedding tears, they spoke to the noble king.

The citizens said: You are our mother and our father; how have you become so heartless? Why are you abandoning us without any fault of our own, O Lord? Without you, our birth is in vain, just as a child is without a mother. We will also follow you wherever you go, O Lord.

Kavi said: While the people were speaking thus, the sage Shaunaka arrived. He was like another Agni, a tiger among sages, the speaker of the Vedas, Vedangas, and scriptures, renowned in the three worlds, worshipped by Indra and the gods, and a knower of the past, present, and future. Seeing him, the king rose from his seat and bowed. He offered him a seat and worshipped him with great joy. After the sage had eaten and rested, and after his feet had been massaged, Chakrapani asked, "What merit of mine has borne fruit today, that I have been blessed with your sight, which destroys all sins and grants all desires to men, yet is difficult for those who perform evil deeds to attain?" Then the sage, satisfied with the king’s great devotion, humility, and self-control, spoke to the noble King Chakrapani.

The sage said: Do not worry, O King, and do not abandon your kingdom. You shall surely have a son; there is no doubt about my words. I have never spoken a lie, even in jest.

Kavi said: Hearing these words, the noble king rejoiced. He offered the sage ornaments made of gold and jewels, and precious garments, but the sage did not accept them. The sage said again, "O King, we wear bark garments, we are free from desire for all worldly enjoyments, and we are devoted to the welfare of all beings. We are capable of creation and destruction, we are oceans of compassion, we delight in the company of the virtuous, and we look upon a lump of clay and gold as equal. Lakshmi never dwells among the learned. Therefore, I will not accept this gold or these fine clothes. I came to you in the course of a pilgrimage, and many days have passed while visiting you."

Kavi said: The couple bowed to the sage Shaunaka and asked him for a method capable of producing offspring. Considering all vows, penances, sacrifices, and charities to be in vain, the sage then told them of the Solar Vow, which grants all desires to men, destroys the sins of many births, and bestows sons and grandsons.

The sage said: The vow should be performed for one month, starting on a Sunday. After performing the Shraddha ceremony for ancestors and worshipping the Mother Goddess, one should worship the Lord of the Ganas (Ganesha) and have the Brahmins recite blessings. One should place a golden image of the sun in a golden vessel and worship it with sixteen offerings, filled with devotion. Use red sandalwood mixed with rice, flowers, red jewels, and various fruits. One should pray to the Supreme Lord with twelve offerings, prostrations, circumambulations, hymns, and prayers. Then, one should perform or have performed one hundred thousand prostrations, and feed one hundred thousand Brahmins daily with great joy. One should give one cow daily to a learned Brahmin with a family. The king should remain celibate with his wife, and with compassion, give food to the poor, the blind, and the needy. At the end of the month, offer all the materials to a Brahmin. O King, when this vow is performed, by my grace, you will have a son who will be famous, pure, and devoted to the Sun.

Kavi said: Having instructed them in this vow, Shaunaka disappeared. Then the king, with his wife, performed the vow exactly as directed, with a humble mind and devotion to the Sun. He fed the Brahmins as he wished, up to the number of one hundred thousand. He remained fasting with his wife for a month. He gave cows and performed prostrations, and had the Brahmins do the same. He chanted the Sun mantra daily and remembered His name constantly. Then, one night, his wife saw in a dream the Sun God, appearing in the form of her own husband. Tormented by the fire of desire, she longed for Him. She said, her body burning, "Desire torments me greatly. Give me the season (union), O husband, or else I shall die." He, knowing her to be barren and seeing her desire, granted her the union in the dream, appearing in the form of her husband. When she awoke, she roused her husband and said, "O Brahman, why did you grant me the union while you were observing the vow?" He replied, "O beautiful one, I did not; I was exhausted from fasting and devoted to the Sun." Then the chaste woman said, "I know no one else. I was meditating on the Sun in your form while observing the vow. I was burned by the internal fire of desire." Then Chakrapani spoke to his beloved wife.

The King said: The Sun God is pleased, O beloved, by the prostrations, the feeding of Brahmins, the giving of cows, the fasting, and the chanting. He has granted us success, and you shall have a son.

Kavi said: As the pregnancy grew day by day, her heat increased. She, near death, used sandalwood and vetiver, and applied camphor to her body, but the heat did not subside. Not even the breeze from wet cloths helped. Then, with her companions, she went to the bank of the river Sindhu and abandoned the great fetus there. Exhausted, she returned with her companions to her palace, informed her husband that she had abandoned it, and returned to her household duties.

Chapter 74 : The Granting of Boons

Kritayuga said: After the fetus was abandoned by her into the ocean, a child of immense strength was born. He was radiant, with a fierce face, a broad forehead, and three eyes. He had matted locks of reddish hair, held a discus in his hand, and carried a trident. At his very first cry, the three worlds trembled. With arms reaching down to his knees, he sought to conquer the three worlds. O best of sages, the ocean was agitated by him, and the sea creatures were terrified. As the child lay there, he began to dry up the ocean.

The Ocean said: I shall take this son to the king’s palace so that I may live in peace.

Kritayuga said: Saying this, the ocean brought the child to the king’s palace and said, "Because this fetus was unbearable in your womb, your wife abandoned it." Placing the child before the couple, the Brahmin (the ocean in disguise) said, "A fierce child has been born, terrifying to all worlds. His first cry shook the three worlds, and he is so intense that he cannot be looked upon; that is why I have brought him here." Having said this, the ocean vanished. The queen then took the child in her arms and held him to her waist. She lovingly gave him her breast, and both parents were filled with joy at the sight of their son, just as a long-time practitioner of yoga feels upon attaining the nectar of Brahman.

The king then summoned the Brahmins and friends. After consulting with astrologers, the queen and the king named him Sindhu (Ocean) and performed the birth rites according to custom. Overjoyed, the king gave many gifts and various garments to the Brahmins. He had the city decorated with flags, and as all musical instruments played, there was joy in every house. As the people departed, the parents, out of affection, named him Raktanga (Red-limbed). The ministers said, "Since he is the son of the fierce one and possesses a fierce form, he shall be known as Ugrekshana (Fierce-eyed)." The citizens called him Vipra-prasadana (He who pleases the Brahmins). The citizens then gave various gifts to the king, who immediately distributed them.

The child grew like the moon in the waxing fortnight. Just as fire grows in an instant with the wind, the child grew by his own radiance, touching the sky. While playing, the child would uproot many trees near the house with his left hand and cast them down. While playing in the forest, he crushed mountains and trees. Once, he even leaped up and caught the moon-deer for a moment. When a female elephant blocked his path to the water, he struck her on the temple with his fist, and she fell. Seeing his miraculous deeds, the people were astonished, and his parents rejoiced, realizing he was superhuman.

Having grown up, Sindhu, the mighty one, said to his father, "O King, I shall go to perform penance. I feel that my life is passing in vain if I do not conquer the heavenly, earthly, and nether worlds." Hearing this, his parents spoke to him. A father always prays for the excellence of his son, and a mother prays for him through vows and offerings. Having received their permission and bowing to them, he went to the forest.

There, wandering about, he saw a pond filled with aquatic plants and, feeling joyful, decided to stay in that secluded place. Standing on one toe with his arms raised, remembering the sun, and chanting the mantra taught by Shukra, he remained motionless like a mountain. Placing his left foot on his right knee and folding his hands over his heart, he meditated on the sun. Enduring cold, wind, heat, and rain, he stood firm, eating only air, until his body was covered by an anthill. Even when only his bones remained, he continued to chant the great mantra. Two thousand years passed in this manner.

The radiance emanating from Sindhu’s body began to heat the sun. Seeing such intense penance, the Sun God appeared before him. Extremely pleased, the Sun said, "I am pleased with your penance. Ask for a boon that is in your heart, and I shall grant it for the duration of your life." Hearing the words spoken by the Sun himself, Sindhu regained his bodily form and saw the Lord Bhaskara before him. Bowing to his lotus feet with folded hands, he said, "Salutations to the Lord of the distressed, the witness of all. Salutations to the Lord of the gods, the embodiment of Brahma, Vishnu, and Shiva. Salutations to the one worshipped by the world, the cause of the universe. Salutations to the source of rain and the cause of crop growth. Salutations to the Supreme Brahman, the cause of creation, sustenance, and dissolution. Salutations to the one beyond qualities, the Guru, the one who stirs the qualities, the all-knowing, the giver of knowledge, the Lord of all. My birth, my lineage, my father, and my mother are blessed because I have seen you. If you are a granter of boons, O Sun, grant me immortality in all respects. By your grace, may I conquer all the gods in battle. May I have no death from the current host of gods."

Hearing his boons, the Sun was satisfied and said to his devotee, who was emaciated from his penance: "There shall be no fear for you from divine beings, humans, animals, or serpents, neither by day nor by night, nor at dawn or twilight. By my word, O prince, you shall not die. Take this vessel of nectar. As long as this remains in your throat, you shall not die. Whoever removes it from you, from him shall come your death. Only the Lord who descends, shaking the heavens with the tips of his hair, and on whose thumbnail the millions of universes reside, only that Lord shall slay you. Apart from him, you have no fear. By the grace of my boon, everything shall be as grass to you. The kingdom of the three worlds is granted to you; have no doubt about this."

Kritayuga said: Having granted these various boons, the Sun vanished. Sindhu, filled with joy, went to his own palace. His mother and father smelled his head and were filled with happiness. They said to their son, "We were devoid of food due to your absence and have become emaciated with worry. Look at our condition, son." Bowing to their feet, the son said, full of joy, "The Sun is pleased with me and has given me the lordship of the three worlds. I shall achieve it by his boon; you need not worry."

Chapter 75 : The Defeat of the Gods

Kala said: Knowing his son to be intelligent and proud of the boons granted by the Sun, he gave him the entire kingdom, along with its lands, treasury, and military forces. The father then retired to the forest, longing for the realization of his own soul. Having been coronated by his father, the son set his mind to ruling the kingdom. He summoned the heads of the guilds, the ministers, and the officials, honored them with garments and other gifts, and established them in their respective duties. By the beating of drums, he proclaimed the penalty for violating his commands.

He then swiftly ordered his warriors to conquer the directions. With his terrifying form and brilliance, he shamed even the Sun. The warriors marched forward, their faces fierce and reddened, wearing various weapons and covered in dust that obscured the sun. Then came the elephants, adorned in various colors, with four tusks each, moving in great chains and shaking the earth. Mounted by great commanders, they looked like moving mountains, draped in multicolored banners, eager to crush the guardians of the directions.

With the loud ringing of bells echoing through the quarters, the cavalry followed, adorned with various ornaments. Surrounded by countless quivers, bows, and arrays of weapons, the demon Sindhu shone as he rode upon his horse. With a necklace of pearls gleaming on his neck, bow and arrows in his hands, and holding a sword and shield, he moved, crushing trees and mountains in his path. Whichever city the mighty demon targeted, his warriors would seize its ruler and bring him before their master. They would install their own leader’s emblem and seal there, and those who sought refuge were accepted as servants. By making them pay tribute, he protected them under his rule by force. Thus, he brought everyone under his control, and millions of demons followed him.

Shumbha, Nishumbha, Vritra, Prachanda, Kala, Kadambasura, Shambara, and Kamalasura were there. Kolasura said to him, "Just as the King of the Tripura demons conquered the three worlds and gave us authority, do you also grant us authority after conquering the three worlds with your might. Since the powerful demon who was killed by Shiva today, you have been seen as the most formidable. Even Death cannot match your prowess. We wish to carry out your commands and serve you, O mighty one." Hearing these words, the demon king Sindhu was pleased and gave them horses, elephants, garments, and ornaments.

Sindhu said: "I shall be truly powerful when I conquer Amaravati, the realms of Hara, Vishnu, and Brahma, and the seven netherworlds." Kala said: As all the demon lords shouted "Well done! Well done!", they roared and rejoiced, causing the universe to tremble. "O King, our itch for battle has not been satisfied by fighting the gods; therefore, they will find no peace." Saying this, they swiftly ascended to heaven. They besieged the city of Shakra, just as Brahmins surround a wealthy man, while others entered the center, plundering the heaps of jewels.

There was a great uproar among the gods who were roaring there. Then, Hari, who was in the midst of the assembly, heard from a messenger about the arrival of the demons and the siege of his city. Quickly mounting Airavata, with his thunderbolt in hand and accompanied by the immortals, the Lord of the Gods came to fight that demon. Some gods said, "This one is not capable of fighting Hari. Without the Lord of Lakshmi, no one seems his equal." While they were still speaking, the mighty demon struck Shakra and his divine host with a shower of arrows. Some of the gods fled in terror.

Then the great Indra ran forward, roaring in anger, his hand raised with the thunderbolt, ready to devour all the demon hosts and destroy his enemies. He struck the demon king on the head with his firm thunderbolt. He fell into a deep swoon but rose again after a moment. He said to Hari, "Go to your own abode; do not meet your end. By the blow of my fist, even Time will meet its death. What account do you hold?" Hari did not heed those words. Enraged, the great demon struck with his fist, piercing the flank of Airavata, causing a flood of blood to pour forth. Then the demon flew up, seized the four-tusked elephant, and threw the king of elephants down. Hari was astonished. He held Hari by the foot and shook him, but Hari escaped from his grip in a subtle form. Then, abandoning the elephant Airavata, Hari went with the other gods to seek refuge.

With Indra and the gods having fled, the demon Sindhu sat upon the throne of Indra, surrounded by the host of demons. He distributed all the positions of the gods to the demons. Then Shumbha and the other demons stood there, untroubled, bowing to the demon lord and praising his superior strength, while the heavens resounded with the noise of various musical instruments.

Thus ends the seventy-fifth chapter of the Krida Khanda in the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Defeat of the Gods."

Chapter 76 : The Battle Between Gods and Demons

Brahma said: Hari, who was seated comfortably in Vaikuntha, was approached by the gods. Having bowed to Hari, they stated their purpose. Indra said: O Govinda, do you not know of the calamity caused by the demon Sindhu? The wicked ones have invaded our Amaravati. We fought against him with all our might, but the demon could not be defeated; therefore, I have come to you for refuge. We have no other path but you; you are always our refuge. O Lord of the world, destroy him and restore our positions.

Brahma said: Hearing the words of Hari, Vishnu was filled with concern and wonder. He said to him: Do not fear; I shall destroy the demon in an instant. Having said this, Hrishikesha mounted his vehicle, Garuda. At the moment of his flight, the three worlds trembled. Trees fell to the ground along with flocks of birds. Adorned with a crown, earrings, and a garland of forest flowers, his chest shining with the brilliance of the Kaustubha gem and marked with a bright musk tilaka, and holding the conch, discus, mace, and lotus in his hands, he departed for Amaravati.

Seeing the gods arrive with Garuda as their vehicle, the demons, armed with various weapons, came forward to fight. The demon Sindhu, holding a bow and discus, equipped with quivers, and mounted on a horse, approached in great rage, eager for battle. Then Kubera, Varuna, Vayu, Agni, Purandara (Indra), Soma, Mitra, Bhauma, the Ashvins, and Madana also arrived.

A duel ensued with the demon Sindhu standing at the forefront. Prachanda fought Varuna, and the King of Yakshas (Kubera) fought Kamala. Indra fought Vritra, and Nishumbha fought Pavana (Vayu). Shumbha fought the powerful Vishnu. Agni fought Chanda and Munda, Soma fought fiercely, Bhauma fought Kadamba, and Shambara fought Madana. The Ashvins fought the demon Kala. All the soldiers struck each other's vital parts repeatedly with weapons and missiles. Some fought with the playfulness of wrestlers, intoxicated by the battle, while others fell due to weapon strikes. Some died, some were dying, and others, maimed, leaped about. Victory and defeat shifted from one side to the other.

Then Vritrasura struck Indra with a fist, and they struck each other's heads with great force. They struck hand with hand, foot with foot, and chest with chest; then he struck him with the thunderbolt. Vritra struck Indra, who fell unconscious to the ground. As the god fled, vomiting blood from his mouth, he fell to the earth, deeply unconscious. Upon regaining consciousness, Indra struck Vritra with a fist as hard as a thunderbolt. Vritra struck Indra again, and he fell to the ground unconscious. As he fled forward, vomiting blood, the demons, eager to fight, gathered from all directions. Seeing their prowess, Maghava (Indra) disappeared.

As the gods fought their duels, their pride was broken, and they began to flee. When the gods were defeated, Hari sent forth Garuda, illuminating the directions with the brilliance of his discus and his own radiance. He struck the ranks of demons with the edge of his discus. Some demons had their faces broken, others their necks severed. Some were torn into hundreds of pieces, their knees, thighs, and arms shattered. Some sought refuge, but Janardana did not kill them. All the powerful demons who were struck by Hari attained liberation. Rivers of fat and flesh flowed there instantly.

Then Hari made everything resound with the blast of his conch. As Govinda triumphed, all the demons like Shumbha and others came to fight him directly, abandoning their duels. The Lord assumed a cosmic form and seized those four demons. Just as a man throws a stone with a sling, he threw Chanda, Munda, Nishumbha, and Shumbha. Having whirled them around, he threw them far away into the middle of the great river. Overcoming their unconsciousness, they returned to the army.

Hari, undisturbed, struck Prachanda in the heart, Vritra in the back with a fist, and Kala and Kamala as well. He struck Bhaumasura on the head with his discus and pierced Kolasura in the heart with his mace. Then Sindhu approached quickly, making a great clamor, causing the ends of the directions to resound, and said: O Hari, your strength has been shown. Behold my prowess as well; you cannot escape. No enemy of mine has ever left my sight alive. O knower of the past, present, and future, did you not consider before? One whose voice makes the three worlds tremble, how have you come before him like a firefly before the sun?

Then the gods said to the boasting Sindhu demon: Heroes do not chatter; they demonstrate their prowess. If it were not for the command of Hari, you would have been shattered into a hundred pieces.

Thus ends the seventy-sixth chapter of the Krida Khanda in the Shri Ganesha Purana, describing the battle between the gods and the demons.

Chapter 77 : Sindhu's Unprecedented Deeds

The King said: Hearing these words, the demon spewed sparks of fire in his rage. Just as a lion attacks a herd of elephants, he rushed toward the gods. With a blow of his fist, he struck Indra, who fell to the ground like a tree felled by a storm. The king of demons then struck Kubera on the forehead, Varuna on the jaw, and Yama on the back with the force of his discus. He struck Agni on the palate and Manmatha with a kick, while he crushed Shanaishchara (Saturn) with his foot. He whirled Soma and Bhauma around and threw them forcefully to the ground, and struck Sanaka and Sanandana on their backs. The Ashvins and Narada fled in fear, and seeing his prowess, all the other gods also ran away. Some fell unconscious, and the demon struck Madhava (Vishnu) with his discus, causing the weapon to fall to the earth. Madhava struck the demon king on the head with his mace, but the demon dodged it and threw his own mace, which shattered into a hundred pieces. 

Recognizing the demon's valor, Madhusudana said to him, "O demon, ask for a boon that is in your heart. I have never seen such prowess in any other being." The demon king, filled with great joy, replied, "If you are pleased, O Lord of gods, and if a boon is to be granted to me, then stay with your family in my city of Gandaki forever. I ask for no other boon." Great Vishnu replied, "I shall stay in your city. Since I have given my word, I am now under your control." 

The demon king then installed other demon lords in the realms of Satyaloka, Kailasa, and Vaikuntha, and he himself resided in the realm of Indra. Having installed others there, he went to his own city of Gandaki, accompanied by Rama (Lakshmi), amidst the sounds of drums and trumpets. Bards praised him, saying there was no man like him, for he had conquered Vishnu and brought many gods to his home. The citizens saw Hari, Varuna, Kubera, and other chief gods near him, and then they went to their own homes. Hari then said to the demon, "Dwell happily in the city of Gandaki with the immortals," and the demon did so. He stationed other demons all around at a distance. 

The gods then asked Hari, "O Lord with the Garuda banner, what have you done? Why do you remain filled with joy, having abandoned your own prowess? How did we end up in prison, and how did we come to the mortal world? O Lord of the universe, how will this indulgence end?" Hari replied to them all, "Time is difficult to overcome. Everything happens, wanes, or grows through time. Therefore, wait for time; time will consume him." 

Having thus conquered the three worlds with his great strength and valor, the demon told the entire story to his parents with joy, and they gave him their blessings, recognizing his manliness. Then, the evil-minded demon proclaimed on earth that anyone who worshipped gods, Brahmins, or cows should be killed or brought before him. He ordered that wherever an image was found, it should be broken and thrown into the water. "Let them make my image and worship it in every house," he commanded. His messengers spread this word everywhere. They broke the temple idols and threw them into deep water, and with great reverence, they installed images of Sindhu instead. 

The demons went to their master and said, "O Lord, the images of Ganesha, Hara, Hari, the Sun, and Rama have all been broken and thrown into the deep water. We have installed your images there for worship, and having done so, we have come to you." Thus, all righteousness was severed. He put an end to sacrifices, charity, offerings to the ancestors, and sacred chants. The worship of gods, Brahmins, and teachers ceased everywhere. Sages went to Mount Meru, and some fell from their grace. Thus, the demons and rakshasas became powerful in the three worlds, while the virtuous and the gods were destroyed or perished. Now, listen to how the gods attained liberation from Sindhu. 

Thus ends the seventy-seventh chapter of the Krida Khanda in the Shri Ganesha Purana, describing the tyranny of Sindhu.

Chapter 78 : The Granting of Boons to the Gods

Sage Ka said: All the gods were restrained by the powerful Sindhu. Anxious, they began to contemplate the means to kill him.

Hari said: A decision regarding any task should be made after knowing the consensus of all. Therefore, let everyone state their opinion today.

Sage Ka said: Hearing these words of Indra, the gods, led by Brahma, said: The Lord is the creator of all; He will bring about our welfare. Let the method by which the Lord is pleased be determined. Once He is pleased, He will defeat this demon and restore our positions to us.

Then, the foremost speaker, Brihaspati, said: The Lord is immediately pleased by even a small offering. He is the Guru of the moving and non-moving, the destroyer of demons, and should be prayed to by all.

The gods asked: O Master of Speech, tell us which deity should be prayed to, so that we may please Him and regain our positions.

The Guru replied: He who creates, sustains, and destroys the universe in His three-fold form; who is without seed yet the source of all seeds; who is beyond the reach of all words; who is eternal, Brahman-embodied, light-formed, and attainable through scriptures; who is without beginning, middle, or end; who is devoid of qualities yet free from suffering; who is many-formed yet one-formed—all people attain their desired success in every task by taking His name. When worshipped with devotion, He, Vinayaka, removes all distress.

Therefore, let everyone worship Him, and He will grant you success. O gods, the dark fortnight of the month of Magha has begun. The fourth day (Chaturthi) of this fortnight, which coincides with Tuesday, is dear to the Remover of Obstacles. He will manifest Himself and restore your positions to you. He will surely destroy Sindhu; have no doubt about this. He will grant whatever anyone desires.

The gods said: O Guru, your words are well-spoken, by which we are satisfied. You are the helmsman for us who are being carried away by a great current.

Sage Ka said: Indra, Varuna, Kubera, Vishnu, the Guru (Brihaspati), Mars, the Moon, Yama, Agni, Vayu, and others bowed to Him. Taking the five nectars, incense, garlands, Shami leaves, Durva grass, sprouts, forest fruits, various types of clay, and non-sugary offerings, they went to the Gandaki River. They built a large pavilion, cutting many trees, and made it shady and cool with creepers and banana stalks. After bathing and performing their daily rituals, they created beautiful images.

The images were mounted on a lion, ten-armed, adorned with ten weapons, having the trunk of an elephant, decorated with various ornaments, accompanied by Siddhi and Buddhi on their sides, radiant with crowns and earrings, wearing yellow garments, and adorned with serpent ornaments. Placing them in the center of the pavilion according to the prescribed rites, they worshipped them with supreme devotion using the sixteen offerings. They worshipped with the five nectars, pure water, garments, incense, lamps, various food offerings, fruits, and auspicious waving of lights (Aarati).

Having worshipped in this way, they chanted mantras to please the Sun. When the Sun set, they performed their evening prayers and praised the Lord.

The gods said: O Lord of the poor, Ocean of mercy, dwelling in the lotus of the yogis' hearts, salutations to You who are without beginning or middle. Salutations to the illuminator of the world, the reflection of consciousness, attainable through knowledge. Salutations to You who dwell in the minds of sages and destroy the demons. O Lord of the three worlds, beyond qualities, yet the cause of the agitation of qualities, salutations to You. O Lord who sustains the three worlds, omnipresent, salutations to You. Salutations to You who are beyond Maya, the fulfiller of devotees' desires, whose eyes are the Moon, Sun, and Fire, the supporter of the world. Salutations to You of immeasurable power, the wearer of the moon on Your head, white as the moon, pure, and the creator of pure knowledge.

Sage Ka said: As the gods were praising Him, a mass of light appeared before them. All the gods were dazzled and amazed. Then, by His grace, the Lord appeared with a gentle radiance. The gods saw Vinayaka mounted on a lion, holding ten weapons, with ten arms, wearing a divine crown, beautiful with various ornaments, adorned with strings of pearls, smeared with divine fragrances, His belly bound by a serpent, His feet jingling with small bells, and radiant with a musk tilak.

Seeing the Lord, the gods bowed and said: This is the very Vinayaka we contemplated upon following the Guru's words. We have seen Him directly, the Lord who is beyond speech and mind. Our birth is blessed, our vision is blessed, and our penance and charity are blessed, O gods.

Then the Lord said to the gods: I am satisfied with your praises, your worship, your devotion, and your Sankashti vow. This hymn will become famous as the remover of distress. O gods, whoever recites this daily with focus will be respected by Me. Upon seeing Him, all Yakshas and Rakshasas will perish. One will enjoy various pleasures and finally attain the state of liberation.

Listen to My words again, you who are oppressed by the demon Sindhu, who have taken refuge in Me, and are deprived of sacrifices and Vedas. You are restricted in the Gandaki region, devoid of Swadha and Swaha. To kill him, I will take another avatar. I will manifest in the house of Girija, and I will become famous by the name Mayureshwara. When I kill Sindhu, you will undoubtedly regain your positions and stations, for in the Krita Yuga, O gods, I am Vinayaka, mounted on a lion, ten-armed, and radiant. In the Treta Yuga, I will be six-armed, moon-colored, and ride a peacock. In the Dvapara Yuga, I will be named Mayureshwara, red-colored, four-armed, and ride a mouse. Then, when the Kali Yuga arrives, I will be dark-colored, made of stone, and known as Dhumraketu. I will fulfill your desires very soon, O gods.

Sage Ka said: Having announced this, the Lord disappeared right there. The gods were filled with joy, having decided on their course of action. Whoever hears, causes to hear, or recites this supreme narrative with supreme devotion, meditating on Lord Vinayaka, attains all desires and becomes Brahman-embodied after death.

Chapter 79 : The Bestowal of the Gauri Mantra

Kumara said: Knowing that the gods had been defeated by Sindhu, Shambhu left His abode and, surrounded by His seven-crore Ganas, arrived at the Trisandhya region. The great sages, led by Gautama, were living there in fear of Sindhu, having abandoned the recitation of the Vedas and the performance of Svaha, Svadha, and Vashatkara rituals. Seeing the three-eyed Lord, they surrounded Him from all sides, just as the best of Brahmins approach a holy place or children approach their mother. Having bowed to Him and worshipped Him, they said, "We are truly blessed. Our birth and our spiritual practices have become fruitful because of this vision of Hara. He who is beyond reach has come within our sight; He has arrived in the Dandaka forest. Our sins have dissolved, and our merits have borne great fruit. Now, there will be no more sorrow for us, just as darkness is never seen when the sun has risen."

Then the great Lord said to all those eminent sages:

Shiva said: Sindhu has conquered the three worlds and suppressed the gods. My mind is troubled because of him, even regarding some of the sages. I could find no rest anywhere, and that is why I have come here. I am greatly pleased and joyful to see you all. Now, grant me a place to stay with my attendants. Your vision, which destroys all sins, has been attained by me through great merit. Here, I shall perform the supreme meditation of the Lord of the Universe.

Kumara said: Hearing these words of the trident-wielder, all the great sages replied, "O Lord, who could be the provider of a place for the Lord of all? What desire can the wish-fulfilling tree have, and by whom can it be fulfilled? Will the thirst of the ocean of milk be quenched by a single leaf? The earth itself is Your form, and it is always under Your control. You are the Lord of all worlds; do as You wish. We shall show You, O Lord of the gods, a beautiful hermitage, filled with various trees and creepers, with charming ponds and lotus-filled waters. It is rich with aquatic creatures and birds, provides dense shade, is vast, and is endowed with sweet roots, fruits, and soft grass. If You wish, O three-eyed Lord, dwell here and protect us all."

Kumara said: Great Shiva dwelt there, accompanied by Ganga and the Ganas. He considered the hermitage of the sage to be even greater than Kailasa. Under His protection, all the sages, led by Gautama, performed various austerities and became free from all distress. Shiva also began His penance, supported by Ganga and Gauri. Then, the Ganas gathered the necessary materials for the great Lord. At one point, Gauri asked Sadashiva:

"You are the creator, the destroyer, and the protector of the universe, O Shankara. You are the eight-fold form and the formless one; You are the Lord of all and the source of all feelings. You are the most supreme, the granter of all desires to everyone. You are the knower of all meanings and the giver of fruits for all eight types of actions. Tell me, who is greater than You, whom You are meditating upon? The gods, sages, Nagas, Yakshas, humans, and demons—the entire universe exists by His power. Who is greater than You? You are worshipped by thirty-three crore gods and by those who have attained perfection."

Kumara said: Hearing these words, Shiva spoke:

Shiva said: You have asked well, O sinless Goddess; I am pleased with your words. Listen attentively, O Goddess, as I explain in detail. O Queen of the gods, even now you do not know whom I meditate upon. I shall describe His true nature to satisfy your desire, for the benefit of the worlds and for the salvation of the soul from the cycle of rebirth. He is the Lord who dwells hidden within all beings, the creator of the universe. He is the one with infinite heads, infinite glory, infinite feet, and is self-effulgent. He has infinite ears and eyes, infinite names, and is beyond all qualities. He is the Lord of infinite forms, the creator of the Vedas, and the giver of all meanings. He is of infinite power, the soul of the universe, incomparable, and ancient. There is no comparison for Him in the ocean, the moon, the sky, or even in Narayana, the Bharata, the Vedas, or the sage Vyasa. Just as infinite living beings arise from Him like clouds, or sparks arise from fire, He is the Lord who bestows the three qualities upon Brahma, Vishnu, and Shiva. He commands them in the acts of creation, preservation, and destruction. Therefore, He is known as Gunesha (the Lord of Qualities) due to the division of these qualities. I constantly meditate upon this Supreme Soul, the Lord who is beyond the beyond, the all-pervading Gunesha, who is the form of the Supreme Brahman.

Gauri said: I am pleased with Your words, but how can I have conviction? How can I behold that Gunesha and worship Him? Tell me the way, O Lord, if You are pleased with me, O Shankara.

Kumara said: Hearing her words, Maheshwara spoke again:

Shiva said: O Goddess, as long as you do not worship Him through penance with single-minded devotion, how can that Lord become manifest to you?

Gauri said: How should I perform this penance, and by what means, O Lord? If You are pleased with me, O Lord of gods, tell me the truth, O sinless one.

Kumara said: Understanding her earnestness, Hara told Girija the way by which Gunesha, the beloved of qualities, would be pleased. Shiva then gave her the single-syllable mantra. "Perform penance for twelve years, and then the Lord will be pleased. He will undoubtedly grant you a vision of Gunesha." Having bowed to Girisha with a joyful heart, Gauri went to perform penance, intent on the meditation of the mantra, to the north of Jirnapur, a beautiful and pleasant place.

Thus ends the seventy-ninth chapter of the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Bestowal of the Gauri Mantra."

Chapter 80 : The Vision of Ganesha

The King said: Seeing that beautiful forest filled with flowers and water, she sat in the lotus posture, fixing her gaze on the tip of her nose. Devoted to the meditation of Ganesha, facing east, and intent on chanting the single-syllable mantra, she remained as still as a dried tree. She consumed neither fruit, nor water, nor roots, nor leaves, nor tubers, nor air; she remained motionless, having attained the supreme state of liberation.

Twelve years passed in this manner. Then, the Lord Gunesha (Lord of the Gunas), the beloved of the virtuous, appeared out of compassion, being pleased. He wore a crown and earrings, possessed ten arms, held a trident, had the moon on his forehead, and was adorned with a conch, a discus, and a garland of pearls. He held a rosary, a lotus, and a musk tilak. In his center was the face of Narayana, on the right the face of Shiva, and on the left the face of Brahma. He was the supreme Lord, seated in the lotus posture, shaded by the hood of Shesha, and as bright as jasmine and camphor.

He said to the Mother of the World, "I am He whom you meditate upon day and night as Gunesha. I have come to grant you a vision. Seeing your steadfastness, devotion, and severe penance, I shall reveal my true form to you, O beautiful one. Among the thirty-three crore gods, there is none greater than me. Because I am the Lord of the three Gunas, they know me as Gunesha. I possess three bodies according to the Gunas, and I am satisfied with your penance. Ask of me any boon that is in your mind. I will grant even the unattainable in these three worlds, O Great Goddess."

Hearing these words, her voice choked with joy. Gauri opened her eyes and saw the Lord before her. She bowed to Gunesha, the Lord of the three Gunas, who possesses three forms. She said, "My birth, my penance, my mantra, and even my Lord Shiva have become truly blessed today by seeing your lotus feet. Today, I have attained the supreme success because I have seen you directly. I desire no other boon, and I shall not transgress your word. Therefore, take birth as my son and be the source of my joy. May I have your constant vision, service, and worship."

Hearing her words, Gunesha was filled with joy and said to Girija, "I shall become your son. I will fulfill your desires and those of the worlds." Having said this, Gunesha vanished in an instant. When the Goddess did not see Gunesha, she thought, "Did I see him in a dream, or was it a blissful moment?" By the instruction of Ishwara, she had seen the God who grants all desires. Unable to bear his separation, Shiva (Parvati) made an image of Gunesha and installed it with reverence. She had a beautiful four-doored temple built and named it "Girijatmaja." She declared that this place would be known as a place of success for humans, and those who perform rituals here will undoubtedly attain success.

Having granted this boon, she worshipped him according to the rites, performed circumambulation and salutations, worshipped the Brahmins, gave them gifts, and received their blessings. She then went to the region of the three twilights and saw Hara (Shiva) absorbed in yoga. She placed her lotus-like head at his lotus feet and recounted her story: "Having received your command, O Lord, I performed penance for twelve years, chanting the mantra and subsisting on air. Then, Gunesha was pleased, and knowing my inner state, he said, 'O daughter of the mountain, I shall take birth in your womb. I will fulfill your desires and those of the gods.' Having said this, the Lord vanished in an instant. Then, I, being filled with joy, made an image, built a temple, installed him there, and have come to you."

The King said: Hearing these words of his beloved, Hara was delighted. With blossoming eyes, he said, "You are blessed, O daughter of the mountain. You have seen Gunesha directly, and he will take birth in your home. He will slay the great demons, remove the burden of the earth, and restore the gods like Indra to their positions. O Goddess, you must not forget these words of mine."

The King said: Having said this, Shiva embraced Shiva (Parvati). Both shed tears of joy, their bodies thrilled, and they remained in supreme bliss.

Chapter 81 : The Manifestation of Ganesha

The narrator said: Then, Parvati, filled with joy, went to her companions and narrated the entire incident to them. They too became joyful upon hearing it. From that moment on, the Goddess became absorbed in the thought of Ganesha. She would look at every child, thinking, "Could this be Ganesha?" She would run to pick them up, only to be stopped by their mothers. She found no rest anywhere, as she was completely intent on meditating upon Ganesha. She chanted His name daily and perceived the entire world as being filled with Him. She asked her companions, "When will Ganesha come?"

Parvati said: "My father once told me of a sacred vow to attain the Lord, which involves the worship of an earthen Ganesha according to the prescribed rites. By the power of that vow, I attained my beloved Shankara. I shall perform it again now to attain Ganesha."

Then, on the fourth day of the bright fortnight of Bhadrapada, she joyfully made an image and worshipped the elephant-faced Lord with sixteen offerings. She performed the worship with meditation, the five nectars, various garments, sacred threads, sandalwood paste, incense, and lamps, all according to the rules. She offered various flowers, food offerings, diverse fruits, betel leaves with areca nuts and cloves, durva grass, shami leaves, numerous monetary offerings, artistic lamps, mantra-flowers, and hymns. With circumambulations, prayers, and the worship of Brahmins, the earthen image became pleased and attained consciousness.

At that very moment, its radiance surpassed that of ten million suns, the fire of universal dissolution, and the light of countless moons. Dazzled by the brilliance, Gauri fainted and fell to the ground. After a moment, she regained consciousness and addressed the Lord of the Universe: "What have I done wrong in the worship of the Lord? I wonder if some sorcery has been cast upon me; I do not know. Why has my effort become fruitless, O Lord, Ocean of Mercy?"

The narrator said: Hearing her words, the Lord assumed a gentle radiance. She became joyful upon seeing a child standing before her—one with countless faces, eyes, feet, and heads; with countless crowns; with eyes of sun, moon, and fire; wearing a necklace of pearls and gems, shining brilliantly. He held various weapons in His hands and was beautiful in every limb. Seeing this pure and wondrous form, Parvati closed her beautiful eyes and pondered. Her body trembled, and she did not know what to do. She thought, "What is this that I have seen? Is it an ill omen or a wonder?" Opening her eyes, the daughter of the mountain, possessing divine vision, saw Him. She recognized Him as the one Shiva had described and spoke.

Parvati said: "Deluded by Your maya, I did not recognize You, the all-pervading Lord." She then asked Him, "Who are You? Where have You come from? Why have You come? If You are Ganesha, tell me."

The narrator said: Hearing her words, that Great Being spoke, causing all the directions and sub-directions to resound with His voice.

The Lord said: "I am that Ganesha, the Supreme Being, whom you meditate upon day and night with devotion, and I have descended into your home. I am the One beyond the beyond, which is why I told you when you sought a boon that I would become your son. Listen to what I must do: I must serve both of you, destroy the demon Sindhu, and after granting the gods their positions, I shall return to My own abode."

The narrator said: Drinking in the nectar of His words, the daughter of the mountain was pleased and said, "This is my great fortune, the fruit of my supreme penance. The Lord of countless millions of universes, the embodiment of consciousness and bliss, by whom all things are created—the five elements, Brahma, Shiva, Hari, Chandra, Indra, the sun, the stars, Gandharvas, Yakshas, sages, trees, mountains, birds, the fourteen worlds, and all that is moving and stationary, sentient and insentient—has become my son. This is a divine play! Now, O Lord, I pray to You, please assume a human form so that I may cherish and serve You with supreme devotion."

The narrator said: While she was speaking, she saw Him before her—six-armed, beautiful as the moon, adorned with three eyes, with a lovely nose, beautiful eyebrows, a face, and a broad chest. His lotus feet were marked with the signs of a flag, a goad, and a lotus. He shone like ten million crystals and ten million moons. He appeared as a child with hair like golden stars. As He let out His first cry, the earth trembled. Due to the sound of His voice, the places of the people lost their boundaries. The trees, though dry, suddenly became lush with leaves. The cows gave abundant milk, and the three worlds rejoiced. Divine drums sounded, and a shower of flowers fell. Seeing the child, the daughter of the mountain joyfully took Him in her arms and bathed Him with warm milk out of affection. She, filled with supreme joy, suckled Him at her breasts. Shiva also rejoiced greatly at the sight of that supreme child.

Thus ends the eighty-first chapter of the Krida Khanda in the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Manifestation of Ganesha."

Chapter 82 : The Naming of Gunesha

Vyasa said: Then, the attendants of Shiva informed Shambhu of the wonderful news that Shiva, along with her son, had appeared. They said, "To see the child, please return to your own abode." Hearing the words of the attendants, the Lord of Girija was delighted. Upon arriving and seeing the child, who was wonderful, moon-like, radiant as a crystal mountain, white as a jasmine flower, and possessed of kohl-lined eyes, he was filled with great astonishment and spoke.

Shiva said: This child is not an ordinary being; he is beginningless, birthless, and deathless. He possesses a divine form, is self-luminous, and transcends the gunas. He is pure consciousness, the Lord of all living beings, and the Lord of the universe. He is the one meditated upon by sages, the foundation of all, the embodiment of Brahman, and the bestower of all things.

Vyasa said: Having said this, Shankara took the child in his hands. After caressing him and placing him in his heart—though he is always present in the hearts of all—Shiva, the destroyer of inauspiciousness, spoke again to Girija. "The Supreme Soul, who transcends the gunas, has come to you as a son. O Devi, through your supreme devotion, you have directly beheld the Lord."

Vyasa said: Then, having summoned the Brahmins and worshipped the Lord of the Ganas, they performed the auspicious rites and the worship of the Matrikas. After performing the Abhyudayika Shraddha and feeding the child ghee and honey, Girija blessed the child, touched the earth, and offered her breast to him. After worshipping Gautama and other sages, they gave many gifts. Then, permitted by them, the great sages spoke the name. "This Lord of all, who is beginningless, has taken the form of a child. He is the creator of all worlds and the Guru of all moving and non-moving beings. He is the master of Maya, beyond the reach of the Vedas and Vedanta. Having descended at this auspicious moment, he will manifest his glory. He was born on the fourth day of the bright fortnight of Bhadrapada, on a Wednesday, under the Vayu constellation, in the Simha Lagna, with five auspicious planets in alignment. He will be glorious, valorous, a performer of wonderful deeds, and will grant great happiness to his devotees. On the eleventh day, O Shambhu, give him a beautiful name."

Vyasa said: Having said this, all the sages returned to their hermitages. Hara placed the child on Girija’s beautiful bed, left the worlds, and went outside, filled with joy. Then Shesha arrived there and provided the shade of his hoods. The clouds made the earth dust-free and blew pleasantly. A breeze scented with Malati flowers blew, the Apsaras danced, the Gandharvas sang, and the Charanas praised the Lord. Then, all the sages made a beautiful image of the child out of clay, created a beautiful pavilion decorated with banana flowers, and worshipped it with great devotion while sitting in the Padmasana posture. They continuously chanted, "Gunesha, Gunesha." They fasted day and night and remained awake at night. After feeding the Brahmins according to their capacity, they broke their fast. In this way, they fed the Brahmins with respect for ten days, and in every house, they performed great festivities to please the Lord.

On the eleventh day, all the sages, having been invited, arrived to name the son of Shiva. Honored by Him, they performed the naming ceremony. Because he is the Lord of all, they named him "Gunesha," the remover of all obstacles. Shiva told the sages, "You have chosen a perfect name. I grant the boon that he shall be worshipped at the beginning of all undertakings." Having worshipped the sages and given them gifts, and having received permission from Shambhu, they returned to their respective hermitages. The Devi, having gone inside the house, worshipped the deities herself. From that day on, that date became known as the day that grants the boons of Gunesha.

On that fourth day, a great festival should be held for one's own prosperity. One should make a clay image and worship it according to the prescribed rites. After creating a beautiful pavilion, one should fast and keep vigil at night. One should worship the Lord with modakas, cakes, laddus, and payasam. On the next day, one should feed twenty-one Brahmins according to the rites and give them dakshina according to one's capacity. After bowing to them, one should then eat. A person who does not worship the clay Ganesha on this day becomes overwhelmed by obstacles and afflicted by various diseases. One should never look at such a person, just as one would avoid a fallen person. If one happens to see him, one should remember Ganesha. It is impossible to fully describe the glory of the fourth day.

I have described the deeds of Gunesha according to my understanding. The secret spies of Sindhu, who were stationed in the abode of Shankara, went and reported this news to Sindhu, who was seated and surrounded by many warriors, while Apsaras danced and Gandharvas sang.

The spies said: In the southern Dandakaranya, in the area known as Trisandhya, we went and saw the Lord named Shiva. We saw many hermitages of eighty-eight thousand sages. One day, Shiva gave birth to a six-armed child, a treasury of beauty, incomparable in the three worlds, and radiant with light. After ten days of great festivities, the great sages and Shankara named him Gunesha. We stood there like deaf men due to the sound of the musical instruments. If we go to kill him, his seven crores of attendants—each of whose strength is terrifying and capable of uprooting mountains—will come and kill us instantly. Who will report this news?

Vyasa said: As the spies were saying this, a voice from the sky was heard: "O Sindhu, the one who will kill you has been born somewhere; be careful."

Sindhu said: "Who is this who speaks such wicked words? Let him be killed!"

Vyasa said: Some of the demons flew up and fell, while others ran away. Meanwhile, the demon fell to the ground in a deep faint, lost in thought, his face withered, spiritless, and devoid of luster. After a moment, regaining consciousness, he spoke with faltering words: "Who has appeared as a thorn to me, the thorn of the three worlds? How can an elephant kill a lion, or a mosquito kill an elephant? I have conquered the thirty-three crore gods in a moment. Why would my death occur? The voice from the sky must be false. Even if it is true, I will go and devour that enemy." Having said this, he sat on his throne after half a moment. All the heroes, having heard the news, said, "You are the death of death itself; how can your death occur? If it is so, we will kill him. Whether he resides in heaven, on earth, in the netherworld, or in the sky, give us the command, and we will go." The demon then spoke to them. Having drunk the nectar of the words of his friends and servants, he said, "I am pleased. Go quickly to where my enemy is. Kill him through various illusions and report back to me."

Vyasa said: Having received this command, the countless demons departed. They reached the Trisandhya region and stayed there in secret, wondering how to kill the son of Gauri through illusion.

Chapter 83 : The Slaying of Gridhrasura

Kritayuga said: O Brahmin, the child grew day by day like the waxing moon. Hearing of the auspicious birth of Gauri’s son, the Himalayas set out to see him. Taking with him ornaments adorned with precious gems, he arrived at the courtyard. Seeing the child, he hurried toward his daughter. Gauri, filled with joy, embraced her son whom she had not seen for a long time. She shed tears of happiness, and the child also spoke to her.

Gauri said: Why have you become so heartless? You never sent word about yourself, nor did you come to see me, O cruel one.

Himavan said: You speak the truth, Gauri; my life resides in my throat. That is why, longing to see you, I have come, O beloved of Hara. Just as a cow’s heart is fixed on her calf, so is my heart fixed on you.

Kritayuga said: Hearing these words, she offered him an auspicious seat. Having sat down, the king of mountains spoke again to his daughter, saying that he had heard from Narada about her son, who was truly wonderful. He had come to see the auspicious remover of all obstacles.

Kritayuga said: Parvati took the child and placed him on her father’s lap. After adorning him with ornaments, the mountain king spoke of his greatness.

He said: This child is endowed with great Maya; he will make this earth free of thorns, just as the moon cools the earth with its rays. He will establish all the gods in their rightful places. Great sages will serve his feet. All things, both moving and stationary, are his forms. He alone is the one to be meditated upon by Brahma and others; he is the thousand-eyed, the thousand-footed, the cause of all causes. He is the thousand-faced, the infinite form, existing as both the collective and the individual. Never before in the three worlds has such a beautiful child been seen. I have attained oneness with him upon seeing his lotus feet, just as milk poured into water becomes one with it in an instant. Take care of him, O auspicious one; he is an ancient treasure.

Kritayuga said: The mountain king then placed a crown on the child’s head, armlets on his arms, a lotus on his chest, jeweled earrings in his ears, a waistband on his waist, and precious tiny bells on his feet. Having given these ornaments, the mountain king gave him the auspicious name Heramba, the remover of great obstacles and the giver of fearlessness to his devotees. Having eaten and received permission to leave, the king of mountains returned to his own abode.

One day, the child was playing in his own home. A demon named Gridhrasura, who had the form of a vulture and possessed great strength and prowess, arrived. The wind from his wings caused mountain ranges to crumble into dust. With his footfalls, he crushed boulders and trees. Covering the child with the shadow of his wings, he flew in the sky. The dust from his wings blinded everyone, and his loud cries made them deaf. Then, the vulture king grabbed the child with his beak and flew far into the sky, just as Garuda carries a serpent. The wicked demon wandered in the sky, unaware of the child’s power.

Then, the daughter of the mountain looked for her son, wondering where he had gone. Not seeing him in the courtyard, she became overwhelmed with grief. She cried out, asking which wicked, evil-souled being had taken her son. Sitting down, she saw her son in the sky in the vulture’s beak. She fainted and fell to the ground, crying, "Run! Run!" She lamented that her life would end without her son, and she questioned why the Lord of the universe had become so cruel toward her. She recalled how she had performed penance for twelve years without food, and how the ocean of compassion had descended to her home. She wept that the treasure she had received was now gone due to her own misfortune and accumulation of sins. With the child gone, all her ultimate happiness had vanished.

Kritayuga said: As she lamented, her companions also grieved. Then, they comforted her with wisdom and logic, saying that she should not grieve for a child who is beginningless and endless. They reminded her that the self-effulgent one had descended due to her many austerities, and asked her where a clay image could ever possess consciousness. They told her to be steady, for she would see her auspicious son in a moment, as the child is the destroyer of death itself and would return through his own resolve.

While the companions were speaking, the powerful child grabbed the vulture’s beak with his own fist so tightly that no breath could escape. Suffocated, the vulture died along with the child and fell to the earth like a mountain struck by a thunderbolt. The vulture’s body, ten yojanas wide, crushed houses as it fell, creating a great spectacle. As trees were broken, flocks of birds flew in all directions. Seeing the child, the companions said that the son of Girija had fallen with his body unharmed.

Kritayuga said: Hearing these words, she hurried and took the child. Gauri held him to her heart and attained supreme joy. Filled with great wonder, she gave many gifts in charity. She prayed to the Brahmins that such a great calamity might not happen again, and after bowing to them, she entered the palace. She wondered how a small, delicate child could have killed such a powerful demon who was ten yojanas wide. She remarked that if this was his strength now, what would he do in the future? Saying this to her companion, Gauri happily gave him breast milk. She realized that he should not be considered small, for just as a spark of fire can burn a large pile of wood, so had the demon been killed by him. Then, the Ganas threw the pieces of the demon’s body far away. Whoever hears this story will never be overcome by demons.

Thus ends the eighty-third chapter of the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Killing of Gridhrasura."

Chapter 84 : The Killing of Balasura

Kritayuga said: In the second month, Gauri anointed the child in the evening, placed him in a cradle, and sang to him with great joy. Meanwhile, two demons, Kshema and Kushala, arrived in the guise of mice, desiring to kill the child. Armed with sharp claws and teeth, they entered the palace, fighting each other near the cradle. Their shrieks deafened the ears, and their heavy footsteps churned the earth like a plowed field.

Parvati grabbed a stick to frighten them, but they flew up and fell upon the child while fighting. The impact on his chest awakened the divine child. Parvati cried out in fear, and the child, in his infant state, moved his hands in terror. By that touch, the two demons fell dead, vomiting blood from their mouths. Seeing this, Parvati was bewildered. The Ganas carried the remaining life-force of the demons outside, where they attained liberation through the touch of Ganesha’s hands. Their dead bodies, ten yojanas long, caused a great commotion. As they fell, they destroyed ashrams, trees, mountains, and dwellings. The Ganas cut the bodies into pieces and removed them. Parvati, trembling with fear, took her child and affectionately nursed him.

The wives of the sages arrived and said to Parvati, "O Gauri, you possess great merit, for which this obstacle has been destroyed. A two-month-old infant has killed these great demons; there will be no one like him in the three worlds. O Mother, you must be careful, for other deceitful demons will surely come to take the child."

Kritayuga said: Having said this, the goddesses departed, and the Ganas told Parvati, "O Shiva, this child will kill demons day by day. We shall keep them away for some time." The sages then performed protective rites for the child. Parvati gave them gifts and bowed to them.

In the third month, while everyone was busy with their duties, Parvati fell asleep with the child on a cot at noon. The attendants were asleep or occupied elsewhere. A demon named Krura, in the form of a cat, entered the palace. Seeing Parvati asleep, he thought, "I shall take the child while she sleeps." He climbed the cot, but as he lifted the child, he fell beneath the cot, and the child cried out. Parvati awoke, saw the cat with the child, and screamed in terror. She fainted, but as the cat bit the child’s throat, the child grabbed him. With his hands and feet, the child threw the demon against the ceiling, causing him to fall dead outside like a ripe fruit falling from a tree in a storm. Parvati, overwhelmed with grief at the foul smell of the blood, covered her nose. She took the child and nursed him. The attendants arrived, and seeing the child’s prowess, they touched him with wonder, believing him to be immortal. The cat’s body grew to twelve yojanas, crushing many attendants. The Ganas were astonished at the child's power, realizing that even those who are invincible to Indra are easily killed by him.

In the fourth month, the wives of the sages arrived with various auspicious items. Parvati welcomed them, and they played together.
