Among them, a great demon named Balasura, in the form of a child, arrived to play. He began to wrestle with Ganesha. They grappled, and the demon struck Ganesha’s head with his feet. Parvati, seeing them, asked whose child was hitting hers, but thinking it was just child's play, she ignored it. The two children rolled on the ground, pulling each other's hair and laughing. Ganesha, knowing he was a demon, played along. Suddenly, Balasura grabbed Ganesha’s throat with great force. Ganesha retaliated, and the demon, gasping for breath, turned pale. The women cried out in fear, thinking the child would be killed. Parvati begged the child to let go, fearing the sages' curse, but before she could finish, the demon’s life departed through his eyes like a bubble.

The demon’s body, ten yojanas long, fell, crushing great trees. The women fled in fear, taking their children away. Parvati took her child, nursed him, and had the sages perform peace rites. She gave gifts and received blessings. She realized that no one could harm those protected by the Lord. The Ganas threw the demon’s body far away and returned to their duties. Thus ends the eighty-fourth chapter of the Krida Khanda of the Ganesha Purana.

Chapter 85 : Protection Through the Ganesha Kavacha

Ka said: Having heard from the mouth of the sage about the great strength of the son of Girija, Marichi went to see him when the fifth month arrived. He was a knower of the past, present, and future, who viewed a lump of clay, a stone, and gold as equal. He was a knower of the essence of the Vedas and Shastras, and had abandoned all confusion regarding himself and others. He was capable of creating, protecting, and destroying the world through his mind. Seeing him, Parvati, along with her companions, fell at his feet. Having seated him on a throne, she washed his feet with devotion. After drinking that sacred water, she joyfully sprinkled it inside her home. She worshipped him with the sixteen offerings, considering him to be the Lord himself. Pleased, Parvati said, "It is indeed a rare fortune to have your presence."

The Sage said: O Goddess, I am self-absorbed and do not go anywhere. Yet, your son suddenly became the object of my meditation. I have come here desiring to see him, as well as Shiva.

Ka said: Hearing those words of the sage about her son, Gauri, with a heart full of affection, placed him on his lap. She said to him, "My great fortune has been fulfilled by your visit. I have attained supreme merit, and auspicious things will happen in the future."

The Sage said: O Gauri, this son of yours appears to me as the very form of Brahman. He is the one whom the great sages, who are devoted to the piercing of the six chakras and established in Yoga, worship while subsisting only on air. He is the one who, through the division of the three qualities, assumes three forms and performs three actions. By whose existence even Shesha carries the earth on his head. He is the atomic form and the gross form, the creator of the twenty-seven elements. He is the form of the entire conscious and unconscious universe, the performer of all actions. In the first age (Krita Yuga), Vinayaka is ten-armed and mounted on a lion. In the second (Treta Yuga), he is six-armed, rides a peacock, and shines like the moon; he is known as Mayuresha, the destroyer of various demons. In the third age (Dvapara Yuga), the Lord is red-colored and four-armed. In the Kali Yuga, he is known as Gajanana, smoke-colored, two-armed, and the slayer of all demons, known as Dhumaketu. O Gauri, always protect him from the demons.

Gauri said: This child is very restless and kills many demons. I do not know what he will do in the future, O best of sages. The demons are of various kinds, wicked, cruel, and enemies of the virtuous and the gods. Therefore, you should tie something around his neck for his protection.

The Sage said: One should meditate on Vinayaka riding a lion with ten arms in the first age; in the Treta Yuga, on him as the six-armed one riding a peacock, who grants success; in the Dvapara Yuga, on Gajanana, the four-armed, red-bodied Lord; and in the fourth (Kali) age, on the two-armed, white-bodied one who is always the grantor of all desires.

May Vinayaka, the Supreme Soul, the highest of the high, protect his crest. May the extremely beautiful-bodied one, the great and mighty one, protect his head. May Kashyapa protect his forehead, and Mahodara his eyebrows. May Bhalachandra protect his eyes, and the elephant-faced one his lips. May Ganakrida protect his tongue, and the son of Girija his chin. May Vinayaka protect his speech, and Durmukha protect his teeth. May Pashapani protect his ears, and the granter of desired objects his nose. May Ganesha protect his face, and the Lord Ganjaya protect his throat. May Gaja-skandha protect his shoulders, and Vighna-vinashana his breasts. May Gananatha protect his heart, and the great Heramba his abdomen. May Dharadhara protect his sides, and the auspicious Vighnahara his back. May the mighty Vakratunda always protect his private parts. May Ganakrida protect his knees and shanks, and the one with the form of the auspicious circle protect his thighs. May the single-tusked one of great intellect always protect his feet and ankles. May the one who is quickly pleased protect his arms, and the fulfiller of hopes his hands. May the lotus-handed destroyer of enemies protect his fingers and nails. May Mayuresha, who pervades the universe, always protect all his limbs. May Dhumaketu always protect any place not mentioned.

May Amoda protect his front, and Pramoda protect his back. May Buddhisha protect the east, and Siddhidaya the southeast. May the son of Uma protect the south, and Ganesha the southwest. May the remover of obstacles protect the west, and Gajakarnaka the northwest. May the lord of wealth protect the north, and the son of Ishana the northeast. May the single-tusked one protect him by day, and the remover of obstacles by night and at twilight. May the holder of the noose and goad protect him from demons, asuras, spirits, planets, ghosts, and vampires. May he protect his Rajas, Sattva, and Tamas qualities, his memory, knowledge, righteousness, wealth, modesty, fame, compassion, and lineage. May Mayuresha, the bearer of all weapons, always protect his body, wealth, grain, home, wife, children, and friends. May Kapila protect his goats and sheep, and Vikata protect his cows and horses.

The wise person who writes this on birch bark and wears it around his neck will have no fear from yakshas, demons, or ghosts. One who chants this at the three twilights becomes as strong as diamond. One who reads this at the time of travel will achieve his goal without obstacles. One who reads this at the time of battle will surely attain victory. In acts of killing, driving away, attracting, paralyzing, or deluding, one who reads this seven times for twenty-one days will undoubtedly attain the respective fruit. One who reads it twenty-one times for the same number of days will be immediately released if imprisoned or sentenced to death by a king. One who reads this three times when meeting a king will bring the king under his control and win over his court and subjects. This Ganesha Kavacham was spoken by Mudgala to Kashyapa, and then to the great sage Mandavya. It is the protector of all and the fulfiller of all desires. He graciously told me this Kavacham, which grants all success. It should not be given to the faithless, but only to those who are devoted and auspicious. By this, his protection is ensured, and he will never be hindered by demons, asuras, spirits, or giants.

Thus ends the eighty-fifth chapter named Ganesha Kavacham in the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana.

Chapter 86 : Description of the Earth-Sitting Ceremony

Ka said: Then, Gauri bowed to him and took her son from his lap. Having received permission, she went to her own abode, feeling very joyful. He also, having received permission, bowed to Shiva and went to his hermitage. Then, on an auspicious day and at a favorable time, Gauri summoned Gautama and other sages and made them sit down. She bowed to the Brahmins and offered them her own salutations. All the hosts of gods, as well as the Ganas, Gandharvas, and Apsaras, arrived there carrying various gifts. Gauri asked those leading Brahmins to deliberate and perform the ceremony of seating her son.

All the sages said that since it was Thursday, the thirteenth day of the lunar fortnight, and the Revati (Paushna) constellation, the great festival should be held. Then, Gritsamada quickly placed a collection of various gems on the ground and immediately constructed a platform made of jewels. After performing Ganesha worship and the recitation of auspicious prayers, they seated the son of Shiva on that platform, which was covered with divine garments. The women, with great respect, performed the waving of lights (Nirajana) around him as if he were a husband. He appeared radiant, surrounded by the luster of gems and adorned with various ornaments.

All of them, filled with devotion, gave gifts to the son of Shiva while divine music played. Then, she honored the Brahmins with gems, clothes, and gold. They showered him with blessed rice (Akshata) and offered their blessings. Bhavani (Gauri) honored the wives of the sages and the maidens. They said to Girija, "You are blessed; there is no one like you anywhere, nor is there a son like this, adorned with all auspicious marks."

Ka said: While all the women were speaking thus, a great villain named Vyomasura, who was skilled in various illusions and had no equal in strength in the three worlds, arrived. He entered the large tree standing at the gate, which was covered with many branches and was impenetrable even by hundreds of elephants or the winds at the end of the world. Vyoma entered it through his magic, just as a wicked spirit enters a body. He shook that tree as the wind does at the end of the world. Fearing the tree would fall, all the sages who had gathered were frightened and asked, "Why is this great tree shaking without any wind?"

Just then, they heard a cracking sound coming from the tree. The women and all the Ganas fled. Girija, confused and forgetting her child, also ran away. At that moment, the tree fell upon the infant. Struck by his hands, it fell to the ground shattered into a hundred pieces, just as a betel nut is crushed when struck by a heavy object on a stone. The branches with their leaves scattered in the sky. Some sages' hermitages were broken by the falling branches. As the people fled, some branches with leaves fell upon them.

Then, the wicked Vyomasura fell down dead, vomiting blood from his gaping mouth, and he was shattered into a hundred pieces. Then, all the women and the sages returned and saw the child sitting there undisturbed, as calm as Mount Sumeru. They were all astonished that a five-month-old child, through his great strength and prowess, had playfully shattered the tree into a hundred pieces with his hands. Gauri, hearing the commotion, ran toward her son. The sages told her, "Your son has killed the demon." She then saw the child, who resembled the Himalayas. She picked him up, held him to her waist, and joyfully gave him milk. She remembered the words of Marichi and praised Maheshwara, thinking, "He whom the Lord protects, whoever tries to kill him, meets his own destruction like a moth in a flame."

All the sages and women went to their respective homes. Some Ganas also left after seeing the child unharmed. They said, "The mother is blessed; your son is protected from the demons. Only the wicked meet their end; the virtuous never suffer anywhere." Then, Shiva (Gauri) gave them sugar and permission to leave. They all went to their own homes after seeing him. Whoever listens to this holy narrative with devotion will never face any obstacles and will be fearless everywhere.

Thus ends the eighty-sixth chapter of the Krida Khanda in the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "Description of the Earth-Sitting Ceremony."

Chapter 87 : The Slaying of Kamathasura
The Narrator said:

The sister of Vyomasura was deeply distressed by her brother's death. On the day of the new moon, at nightfall, overcome with hunger, she devoured hundreds of mighty buffaloes. She was terrible to behold—with gaping jaws, a long tongue, a flattened nose, and a cavernous mouth. She had teeth like ploughshares, eyes like wells, ears that covered her hearing organs, long breasts, and a fearsome form. Her arms were long, her navel was sunken, and her skin was rough like that of an iguana. The people named her Shatamahisha, meaning "one who devours a hundred buffaloes," for she truly lived up to that name.

Driven by love for her kin, she set out to slay the killer of her brother. Transforming herself into a most enchanting form, she became exquisitely beautiful in every limb. Adorned with various ornaments and appearing as a sixteen-year-old maiden with a radiant complexion, she assumed all sixteen kinds of amorous adornments and captivated others with her sidelong glances. With evil intent, she proceeded to the excellent abode of Girija.

She bowed first to Parvati and spoke with great reverence: "I am blessed, I have accomplished my purpose, I have attained the desired fruit, for you are the mother of all, the embodiment of all gods, the auspicious one. You are meditated upon by all, the bestower of all accomplishments, and the cause of all causes. You are skilled in enchanting the world, and by my good fortune, I have come into your presence."

The Narrator said:

Hearing her words, Girija spoke to her: "Arise, arise—may all be well with you. I am pleased by your visit. Whose daughter are you? Where have you come from, and for what purpose, O lovely woman? I shall fulfill your desire; speak the truth before me."

Shatamahisha said:

"I have been suffering long from the pain of separation and have come to you for refuge. It is heard that the lord of Amaravati has been forsaken by the demons. He has disappeared somewhere, O Girija, along with all the hosts of gods. But you are fortunate, O Goddess, for you do not experience separation from Hara. All other sorrows may be borne, but the sorrow of separation is unbearable."

Parvati said:

"This child of mine, O fortunate one, will forcibly slay the wicked and liberate the gods. That time is approaching; let us wait for a little while. For this very purpose, Ganesha has taken on a body in my abode—to relieve the burden of the earth and to protect the sages."

The Narrator said:

Having spoken thus, she worshipped her, washing her feet first. With her friends, she offered her saffron and other substances and served her food with great respect. After eating, she slept on Shiva's bed and spoke to Parvati's friends: "Bring Girija's child to me, O friends."

All the trusting friends placed the child in her hands. She caressed him with affection, kissing his face and embracing him. But the all-knowing son of Shiva, realizing her murderous intent and knowing she was a demoness, placed his hands over her ears. Then he placed upon her nose a weight as heavy as a great mountain and brought down his body upon her limbs, causing her breath to be obstructed.

Distressed, she violently kicked the child and cried out, "Release me, release me!" to those who had come to witness the spectacle. "How can you kill a woman, O wicked one, who has done no wrong and is like Parvati herself?"

The Narrator said:

Hearing her cries, Parvati came with her friends to free her son but could not do so. She said to him: "Do not kill her; release her quickly. She is Shakra's beloved queen."

Hearing his mother's words, the child threw her far away. She fell and, by a trick, immediately rose again. Her ears, nose, and hands were seen bleeding. From the blows of the child's limbs, she gave up her life in an instant.

Seeing her lifeless, Parvati was filled with grief and affection. She said, "This fickle child has earned ill repute." Hearing that Shachi had been killed, Indra wondered what he would do upon coming there. He went forward and saw that demoness, inauspicious even for Shiva, ten yojanas in length, with the earth and trees wounded. He abandoned his grief and rejoiced, praising his own son.

Taking her flawless son, Parvati went to her abode. The child amazed everyone, and they were astonished at his youthful valor. Parvati was filled with wonder at his prowess. Her friends looked at the demoness with severed ears and nose and said, "How is she so light, and how is he so strong, this child?" They laughed, slapping their thighs, and spoke among themselves.

Then the attendants threw the demoness far away. With the defeat of the evil one, Gauri gave gifts with devotion. Then, in the seventh month, when the night had deepened, when people were asleep, when darkness had enveloped the ten directions, when the wild animals and frogs were crying in the forest, when Parvati was sleeping on her divine bed, and when her friends and attendants were also asleep—at that very moment, a wicked demon named Kamathasura covered Parvati's courtyard with his own body. His touch could instantly shatter even the firmness of a thunderbolt. His body was extremely cool, like the moon on the full-moon day.

When the sun rose, people walked about on his back. Some bathed and stood chanting prayers, while others fell asleep. Some slipped and fell due to his extremely soft body. Young girls sprinkled water and made decorative patterns with cow dung. Then Girija went out with the young girls. She put her son to sleep on an excellent bed and, placing her friends there, went to serve Shiva.

Understanding this, Kamatha moved his back. "Why is the earth trembling? What calamity will befall us?" said the attendants standing there, their minds filled with fear. Those who had been sleeping woke up and asked, "What has happened?"

At that very moment, the all-knowing child, turning over, placed his belly upon the demon's back with great force. He deposited the weight of all fourteen worlds upon him. With his breath and life-force obstructed, the demon tried to move his limbs. Immeasurable blood flowed from his body. He tried to fly up but could not.

Then all the attendants who were there, happily residing, went to see what calamity had occurred and whether the earth was shaking. The friends and all the young girls went to serve Parvati and said, "Arise, O Mother! Your child has been taken away or perhaps is dead!"

Parvati hurried and ran outside. She saw her son motionless and tried to lift him. Finding him as heavy as the weight of the earth, she stood bewildered. Then the child suddenly crushed him, and the demon died. She then beheld the demon—a tortoise ten yojanas long, with its mouth opened wide, vomiting copious blood. It instantly crushed various hermitages and trees. Then she took the child and joyfully gave him her breast to suckle.

"The ways of the demons, who create various illusions, are not to be understood. By the grace of Trilochana, I have regained my son, for he who is mightier than death has slain Kamathasura." The friends and attendants came and asked about the child's well-being. "All is well," Gauri told them joyfully. Then the attendants cut the demon into pieces and threw him far away. The hosts of gods showered flowers upon him.

Whoever hears or recites this supreme narrative shall be freed from all calamities and shall attain all desires.

Thus ends the eighty-seventh chapter, entitled "The Slaying of Shatamahisha and Kamathasura," in the Krida Khanda of the Ganesha Purana.

Chapter 88 : The Slaying of the Cot-Demon (Manchakasura)

Vyasa said: O Brahmin, I have heard wonderful stories from your lotus-like mouth, which wash away all sins and were performed by the Lord of Virtues (Ganesha). Yet, my desire to hear more does not fade, O knower of all. Therefore, please tell me again, with devotion, the stories of the child.

Brahma said: Listen attentively, O Brahmin, to the stories of the Lord of Virtues again, for they destroy sins and grant righteousness and liberation. In the eighth month, during the heat of summer at midday, Goddess Girija (Parvati), feeling oppressed by the heat in her home, went to the flower garden. There, the trees were filled with Ashoka, sandalwood, jackfruit, and Bhibhitaka, along with Malati, Champaka, Jati, tamarind, and neem trees. Parijata trees provided dense shade, and there was a beautiful, cool lake adorned with lotuses.

There, on a beautiful cot brought by her companions, Girija lay down with her child, her mind free from fear. As her companions fanned the child with palm-leaf fans, the child fell asleep. At that moment, a powerful demon named Talpasura, who could take the form of wind, entered the cot. He hid inside it and carried it away into the sky. Terrified by the sound, the companions fell to the ground, and Shiva (Parvati), sitting on the cot, cried out in great distress.

While in the sky, she lamented, "Run, O Shankara, run! Why have you forsaken me? Why do you ignore me? This powerful demon is carrying me away through the sky; how can you allow your beloved to be taken by another? Because of this son, I have suffered many sorrows. Is my death destined along with my son because of this demon? If he had not been born, why would I be in such bondage?"

Ka (Brahma) said: Hearing this lamentation, the great-minded child of Uma roared like thunder, shaking the directions. The earth, along with its oceans and forests, trembled. Seeing the Ganas and Pramathas grieving because they could not see her, the child kicked the cot. All beings were terrified, and the sky seemed to shatter into a hundred pieces. The cot broke into fragments and fell to the ground. Talpasura became visible, and the child seized him. With one hand, he held his mother playfully, just as a Chataka bird catches a drop of water falling from a cloud. He placed her on his shoulder and held her hair with his left hand.

Then, the child suddenly threw the demon to the ground with great force. He sat on the demon's body in a lotus posture, like a yogi. The demon fell far away, crushed by the weight, and died, vomiting blood from his mouth. People saw the demon, who was twenty-one yojanas long, crushing trees, rocks, and hermitages as he fell. The Ganas and companions saw the boy and said, "O Gauri, your child is playing happily and is very powerful."

Parvati, in her confusion, did not realize the child was in the sky or on the ground. The sages said to her, "O virtuous one, be attentive. The demon has been killed by him; open your eyes and look at your auspicious child, Shiva."

Ka (Brahma) said: Awakened by their words, she saw the demon and the child. Gauri said, "I saw something strange; how was this hidden by the child? This demon, twenty-one yojanas long, has been killed by him. My child, who gives me life, has repaid the debt of motherhood." Saying this, she took the child and performed rituals for peace, gave donations, and breastfed him. Seeing this wonder, they were filled with joy.

Meanwhile, a terrible demon named Dundubhi, who sounded like a war drum and whose palm-strikes could turn the earth into dust, made a loud noise. His body, touching the sky, covered the sun. He called out to Gauri's child in the form of a Gana-boy, "Come here, let us play." The child went to him. The demon took the child on his lap and gave him a poisonous fruit, saying, "Eat this fruit quickly, brought from a distant land; it removes birth, death, and old age." Knowing the demon's evil intent, the child took the fruit, ate it, and fearlessly asked for another.

Ka (Brahma) said: By the will of Shiva, the nectar became poison, and the poison became nectar. The child climbed onto his chest and stood up. Placing his feet on the demon's knees, he grabbed his hair with his hands and pulled his beard, swinging playfully. He danced on the demon's knees, his weight pressing down on the earth. The demon, pained, said, "Get down from my knees! My body is crushed by your weight and dancing. Let go, let go! I will go to my abode, O son of Girija."

Ka (Brahma) said: Though requested, the child did not let go. The demon, his body already broken, stood up by force. The child suddenly pulled his head, and the head came off into the child's hands. The earth was instantly covered in blood. The son of Girija played, holding the lotus-like head in his hand. His body smeared with blood, he showed himself to his mother. She went and saw her son in that state. She threw the head far away, wiped him with a cloth, bathed him, and joyfully breastfed him.

Then the companions and Ganas asked if the child was safe. He replied that the child was perfectly safe through the devotion to Shiva and the protection of Marichi. Then they cast the evil demon far away. Filled with joy, the Ganas and companions went to their homes, and Parvati, happy, entered her house with her child.

Chapter 89 : The Killing of Shalabhasura

Kumara said: Then, in the tenth month, when the sun had moved three muhurtas, the son of Shiva, while crawling slightly, began to play. Sometimes he moved his feet, sometimes he spoke in indistinct sounds, and sometimes he rolled on the ground, danced, or cried for his mother. Seeing Shiva, he would suddenly follow her, and seeing such a child, Parvati was filled with joy.

At that moment, a demon named Ajagara was seen. He was emitting sparks of fire from his eyes, his body was as hard as a thunderbolt, his tongue was lolling, and his massive body seemed to swallow the great mountains. He sat at the base of a tree, flicking his two tongues repeatedly. The child, in his playful nature, crawled toward him and caught him. The demon quickly pulled the child into his mouth. As the child entered, the air-eating demon closed his lips. Not seeing the child in the courtyard, Gauri became distraught. She lamented, wondering where the child had gone or who had taken him, and she struck her head in extreme grief.

Meanwhile, the ganas consoled her, saying, "O Mother, do not be distressed. The power of the child is known to be far greater than any other." Inside the demon's belly, the child grew and blocked the demon's breath, shaking him until the tip of his tail moved. The demon's life-force escaped through his eyes along with blood. The son of Ambika tore through the demon's body and emerged. He played, his limbs stained with blood, looking like a blossoming flame-of-the-forest flower. Seeing the child licking his own fingers, the ganas shouted loudly to the daughter of the mountain, telling her that the child had emerged after killing the wicked Ajagara demon.

Kumara said: After the killing, Shiva quickly took the child, bathed him, caressed him, and gave him her breast. She wondered where the child had been for a moment, as it felt like an age to her. Then she saw the Ajagara, stretched out for a hundred yojanas, with a terrifying face, vomiting blood, and having fallen after crushing trees and houses.

Gauri asked: "How many such demons and rakshasas might be in this place? How many have met their end due to the prowess of this child?" Then, all the ganas threw the great demon far away.

In the eleventh month, Shiva went to the doorway. She and her companions watched the many playful antics of the child. He would kiss someone's face or make them kiss him. He would cover someone's eyes from behind, or forcefully drink milk from another mother. He would cover his face with a cloth to frighten others, or pull at people's hair, noses, and ornaments. He would speak faltering words to those he saw.

At that time, the wicked demon Shalabhasura arrived. He was as large as a mountain, his head reached the sky, he was dark like a cloud, his eyes were like hibiscus flowers, and his peaks touched the clouds. He approached the child, appearing as if he would swallow the three worlds. The child ran to catch him wherever he went, and for a long time, they both moved with great speed. Then, the child became tired and, in anger, ran toward him and suddenly caught the powerful demon Shalabhasura. He flapped the demon’s wings just as one would a hawk in hand. Shalabhasura tried to hurt the child with kicks, staring at him with cruel, wide eyes. The child released him and caught him again and again. He showed him to his mother and dashed him against the ground.

Shiva, filled with compassion, said to the child, "Do not kill, O child; release the living being." But the child forcefully threw him against the rock again. The demon shattered into a hundred pieces and fell to the ground, roaring, causing the sky to shake and trees and houses to collapse. He vomited blood from his mouth, spanning ten yojanas, crushing hermitages and trees. Then the mother took the child, settled him, and gave him milk and food. Seeing the demon dead in such a way, the companions praised the daughter of the mountain, saying, "Well done, well done!" Then Girija and her companions entered the house, filled with joy. The ganas, following her orders, threw the demon far away, and the gods showered flowers upon the child.

Thus ends the eighty-ninth chapter of the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Killing of Ajagarasura and Shalabhasura."

Chapter 90 : The Killing of Avijaya

Kumara said: Then, in the twelfth month, the daughter of the mountain (Parvati) sat with her companions, holding her wonderful son, who was beautifully adorned. He shone like ten million suns and was decorated with ten million ornaments. At that time, there was a great festival as she watched him dance. Seeing the desire of Hara (Shiva) and the other children, the son of Girija began to dance to the sound of "thethi-theyi." Hearing that sound, Hara came to dance, and seeing Shiva dancing, the Lord of Sri (Vishnu) also began to dance.

Then Indra and the other gods came there to dance. Just as the child danced, they also danced in the same way. They displayed the same expressions that he displayed. Humans, animals, trees, Yakshas, Rakshasas, serpents, sages, Manus, kings, the fourteen worlds, those in the twenty-one heavens, and all sentient and insentient beings danced on the earth, following the rhythm of the child due to his influence. The wives of the sages and the wives of the gods danced along with the daughter of the mountain.

The directions and sub-directions were filled with the jingling of anklets, the sound of small bells, and the thumping of feet. O sage, the sky and the mountains became resonant. The earth, Shesha, the sun, the moon, and the stars all trembled. Then, realizing the intensity of the dance, Gauri forbade him, but the child did not heed Gauri's words.

A great demon named Nupura, who had a massive black body, feet in the netherworld, and a head touching the sky, entered the child's anklet in a subtle form. The child immediately bound him with his Maya, just as one binds a rutting elephant with chains. The daughter of the mountain took the child onto her lap again. Feeling the weight of the child, which was equal to the weight of the earth, Gauri said, "Why are you so heavy? You were born just now; get off my lap, O child, for my life will depart due to your weight."

Kumara said: Hearing his mother's words, the child, seeing her in great pain, forcibly climbed off her lap and ran on his two feet. Then the demon emerged and wandered in the sky like a bird. Darkness covered the entire earth as he obscured the sun. Suddenly, he fell to the earth and shattered into a hundred pieces. Then the sages and gods, filled with supreme joy, praised that child-form, the imperishable Supreme Soul.

The divine sages said: We do not know your forms, O God, nor your greatness. Your various playful illusions destroy the demons. The demon who obscured the sun was destroyed by your feet. After wandering in the sky, he fell and shattered into a hundred pieces. Seeing your dance, everyone began to dance.

Kumara said: Then they all worshipped that child, who is the embodiment of the universe. When the dance festival concluded, Gauri sent away Gautama and the others. Taking the child, Parvati caressed him and gave him milk. After sending away all the women, she entered her own palace.

Then, once again, accompanied by the sons of the sages, he went outside the house to play and sported playfully. They engaged in wrestling in many ways, pairing off and throwing each other down by their hair. They struck head against head, knee against knee, elbow against elbow, and foot against foot. They climbed on each other's backs and shoulders, and struck each other's stomachs and heads with kicks. Some worshipped the child with sandalwood, flowers, and rice grains. Some took him to their hermitages and fed him food. Some bowed to his feet and gave him auspicious garlands.

A demon named Avijaya, sent by Sindhu, appeared in the form of a ram. Seeing him, even the wielder of the staff (Yama) would be terrified. That powerful demon, with sharp-edged claws, struck the others with his strength. He bound everyone he defeated on his arms. He was large-eyed, had huge horns, and could shatter great mountains. With a blow of his tail, he destroyed living beings, and with a blow of his horns, he uprooted trees and mountains. He followed humans and killed them by force. He went to kill the son of Parvati.

As he ran from behind to kill him, the child caught him by his horns with his hands. He climbed onto his back like a child on the back of a horse. Then some of the sages' sons grabbed his tail and pulled him. Some struck the great demon with sticks and clubs. He ignored them and quickly struck them with the tip of his tail. They fell to the ground, pained by the blow of the tail. Seeing his strength, the child descended and caught him. He whirled him around for a long time and slammed him onto his back. The demon's body shattered into a thousand pieces and he died instantly. The three worlds were terrified by his final cry. He vomited blood from his mouth, stretching his distorted face.

Then the children told the sages that this invincible great demon had been killed by the child who had come to play with them.

Kumara said: Seeing the great demon killed and her son, the Mother was filled with great wonder and took him to her palace. She waved cooked rice around the child and purified him with water. She discarded the remains far from the house and then gave him milk. The Ganas threw the pieces of the demon far away. The sages, their wives, the children of the sages, and the women praised Gauri's son. The gods showered flowers, the Brahmins gave blessings, and the Apsaras danced.

Chapter 91 : The Killing of Vatsa and Shaila Asuras

The narrator said: Then, in his second year, the child played with countless other children in a garden filled with various trees and creepers. He ate fruits like dates and jackfruit, and after knocking down many more, he gave them to his friends with great strength. A wicked and cunning demon named Kata, a master of deceit, knowing the time of their thirst, mixed poison into the pond with the intent to kill them all. The mere touch of this water would kill any living being instantly, and birds touched by the vapors rising from it would fall dead to the ground. Kata went there and stood by, waiting to witness the destruction.

Then, the children, unaware, went to the pond to drink. They all drank from their cupped hands. They picked up the dead fish and went outside, but then they themselves fell dead. The village guard announced their deaths in the town. Hearing this, all the townspeople came there crying loudly, beating their chests with their palms and their heads with stones. There was a great wail from men, women, and children alike. Hearing their cries, the child of Shiva, by the mere glance of his eyes, brought all those town children back to life. With a fierce look, he caused the demon Kata to fall dead upon the ground. The townspeople saw the garden, which had been five yojanas wide, crushed by the demon, and some of them fled in terror. He revived the aquatic creatures and made the pond free of poison. Everyone took their respective children and went home. Some praised the child of Shiva, while the wicked atheists criticized him, though others said that by his association, all misfortunes are destroyed.

Then, in the third year, at dawn, the son of the mountain-born Goddess slipped away, and the sons of the sages followed him. Due to their separation for a day and night, they embraced each other. They said, "O Lord, we see you in our dreams every night. A night without seeing you feels like an age. Now, seeing you, we are all filled with joy. Let us play happily today, forming pairs." They divided into two groups and played, throwing mud balls at each other, making the heavens resound, fighting with joy, and pulling one another.

While playing, they went far away to the ashram of a sage. They enjoyed themselves in a lake that was a yojana in length and width, splashing water on each other in pairs as before, diving, climbing on shoulders, and forcibly submerging one another. Then, a demon in the form of a fish began to play among them, striking the water with its tail. Because the water became murky and turbulent, they went to the shore again and again. Seeing the son of the mountain-born Goddess on the shore, the fish approached him. It opened its mouth, grabbed his feet, and dragged him into the deep water while he cried out, "Run! Run!"

The great demon kept him submerged in the water for a long time. Seeing the son of the mountain-born Goddess drowned, the sons of the sages wept, wailing and covering their faces with their hands. Some went to the beautiful lake, but looking for him, they could not find him. Fearing the wrath of Bhavani, some children fled, while others went and reported that the child had been drowned. They said, "The fish took the child while we were all playing and left us behind."

The narrator said: Hearing those words, Gauri fainted and fell to the ground. After a moment, regaining consciousness, she went out weeping, her hair disheveled and her garments falling. Stumbling, sighing, and falling to the earth, she was followed by her friends who quickly left their tasks. She reached the lake shore quickly with her friends. The sages, hearing the news, also went to the lake. Some dived into the deep water but could not find the son of Uma. Some told the mother, "Our children are drowned in the water." They could not see the son inside the belly of the fish. Hearing this, Gauri began to weep again.

Uma said: "My life will depart without that all-beautiful one, whom I obtained with great penance, the powerful Lord, the teacher of the moving and unmoving, who is beyond Maya, the master of Maya, the Lord of infinite millions of universes, the origin of the world." She struck her forehead repeatedly with her hand. Seeing her in such a state, the sages wept.

Then, the compassionate Lord, hearing her pitiful words, himself took the form of a fish, and the two fought each other. By their impact, the aquatic creatures died and fell to the shore. They bit each other with their fangs and struck with their tails. They struck belly against belly and back against back. After fighting fiercely for a long time with great energy, the Lord of Gunas forcibly struck the fish-form in the mouth. Its jaw was broken, its pride shattered, and its eyes destroyed. It fled into the water, and the son of Uma followed it. Wherever it went, he followed. Seeing it hiding somewhere, he struck it with his tail. The Lord of Gunas caught its tail in his mouth and brought it outside. He crushed it with his own weight and cast it aside, lifeless. It bled from its mouth and roared loudly. By that great sound, the three worlds trembled. He emerged from the water and embraced Parvati.

Gauri, immersed in joy, happily gave him her breast to feed. "Where did you go with the children without asking me? Whatever obstacle comes to you is destroyed by fate. By the protection of Marichi and the grace of the Lord of the world, why do you act so restlessly? How am I to protect you? My life would depart upon your separation, my dear child."

The narrator said: Then the group of sages said, "We were miserable without you, but now, O Lord, we have attained joy again by seeing you." The sages, their friends, and the women worshipped him with various offerings. After bowing to the Lord of Gunas, they all prayed to him, "We are yours, O Lord of Gods; you should not abandon us." Then Uma took the child and went to her excellent abode. Afterward, the ganas, friends, and sages were filled with joy.

On the way, there was a demon named Shaila, a great warrior, who destroyed all enemies, had a body as hard as a crocodile, and was immune to thunderbolts. At his mere sound, mountains would shatter in an instant. He stood suddenly, blocking the path, touching the sky. His head was two yojanas high, and his lower body was twelve yojanas. Where he stood, lakes, ponds, trees, and creepers shone, and lions, tigers, elephants, yakshas, and rakshasas played. All of them were distressed and said, "What is this obstacle that has arisen?" Gauri stood nearby, and all the others stood there too.

Then the sages said, "When will we see the women? When will the valor of the Lord of Gunas be shown here? Our time for homa and ancestral offerings will go in vain." Gauri told them all, "You should not feel distressed. I am also worried, as is Shankara."

The narrator said: Hearing those words, the wise Lord of Gunas immediately assumed a cosmic form and blew him away with a single breath. Even the sages, deluded, could not see that form. By the force of his breath, that mountain-like demon spun in the sky. All were filled with great wonder and praised him. Like a leaf caught in a gale, he fell to the ground. The mountain-demon shattered into a thousand pieces and crushed the trees again. Then the sages said, "Well done, well done, Lord of Gunas! Our misfortune has been removed, and we shall live happily in our ashrams, devoted to our respective duties by your grace."

The narrator said: Having said this, they bowed, worshipped him, and departed. Parvati took him and entered her own palace. The friends and the wives of the sages asked about her and went to their own homes. Any mortal who hears this will attain happiness everywhere, along with longevity, health, wealth, and victory in all endeavors.

Chapter 92 : The Vision of the Universal Form

Kailasa said: Once, in the morning, after bathing, Girija (Parvati) was in a hurry. While her four-year-old son was sleeping, she began to worship the Shiva Linga. She held the excellent earthen image in her left hand. The boy woke up, began to cry, and stubbornly demanded milk. The mother forbade him, saying, "Wait a moment, wait a moment." In his anger, he struck her hand, causing the image to fall. Enraged, she struck him back. The boy, in turn, bit her finger severely. She cried out, "Let go, let go, my life is leaving me!" The boy released her finger and ran far away. Blood flowed from her finger onto the ground, just as milk flows from a tree when it is struck hard.

She took a stick and went to beat her son. As she chased him, she caught sight of him in his Shiva form. He had five faces, ten arms, three eyes, and was adorned with a serpent. He wore a trident, a damaru, ash, and a garland of skulls. He was clad in elephant and tiger skins, with the moon on his forehead. Seeing this, the embarrassed Shiva (Parvati) dropped the stick and hung her head. She could not bring herself to go to the city or the temple. Realizing her state, he returned to his child form. He began playing among the sages' sons, appearing as a sage's child. Girija searched for him but could not find him among them. She asked the sages' sons, "Where has my boy gone? He bit my finger and ran away, the naughty child." They replied, "Your child went in that direction."

Girija ran forward, her hair disheveled, exhausted, her clothes slipping, running here and there. Realizing her fatigue, he returned to his original form. She reached him, held him firmly in her arms, tied him with her garment, and happily went home. She said in anger, "You have come into my hands now, you restless one, I will punish you severely! Or I will tell Shambhu, and he will beat you." Hearing his mother's words, he rolled on the ground. Realizing his bonds were loose, he ran away again. She ran behind him, deeply distressed.

Meanwhile, a wicked demon named Kardamasura, disguised as a Brahmin, appeared, holding a garland in his right hand and a water-filled kamandalu, his body smeared with ash, wearing sun-colored garments, and full of illusion. He said to the boy, "Why are you running? I will protect you from her, have no doubt. I will take you where your mother will never find you, and there will be no fear of time or anything else." The boy, acting afraid, said, "Do as you say, so that my mother does not see me; I have come to you for refuge." As he was speaking, the boy, in his child-like nature, went with the wicked Kardamasura.

Seeing the footprints of his lotus feet on the ground, Shiva (Parvati) followed. She saw the Brahmin ahead and asked him about the boy, "Swami, have you seen my son passing this way? Look at these footprints. Where has he hidden, O Brahmin?" The Brahmin, seeing her grief, said, "Mother, what need have you for a son? Remain indifferent, with a mind focused on the Lord. I have not seen your son anywhere, O daughter of the mountain. Did you make him dependent on you, O Devi?"

As he spoke, seeing the sorrowful Parvati, the Lord of Gunas appeared from within the boy. She said, "Seeing my son, why do you speak lies? He was seen right near you, hidden by you. The righteous never speak untruths even when their lives are at stake." Hearing her words, the giant demon, whose head touched the sky, took the boy and started to leave. Shiva followed behind, crying for her son. Ganesha then assumed a form even larger than the demon's. Seeing his mother's repeated grief, he tore the demon's body into hundreds of pieces with his feet. As the body fell, it crushed the trees. Having destroyed the demon, he stood before his mother.

Ganesha, the sages, the gods, and their wives arrived. They said to Parvati, "How many obstacles come his way! But they all perish through your merit, O Queen of the Gods." Having worshipped the boy, Shiva took him on her hip and went home with them, her heart full of joy, just as Garuda takes the nectar after defeating the gods.

Shiva set the boy down in her courtyard. He rolled on the ground, repeatedly yawning and stretching his lotus-like mouth. Wondering what had happened, Shiva ran to him. Inside his mouth, she saw the entire universe—the universal form. She saw the earth with its seven islands, cities, villages, forests, and mines. She saw Brahma, the sun, the serpent, Vishnu, mountains, and oceans. She saw Gandharvas, Yakshas, Rakshasas, sages, birds, rivers, ponds, the eight Manus, and the Vasus. She saw the moon, sun, fire, stars, the animate and inanimate, the seven netherworlds, and the twenty-one heavens.

Seeing the three worlds, Girija fainted. She closed her eyes, bewildered, and remained unconscious for two muhurtas. She remembered Shiva in her mind and regained her composure. She saw the boy standing before her as he was before. Pleased by his grace, she praised him: "You are the Supreme Soul, the Guru of the moving and unmoving. You are the embodiment of consciousness and bliss, eternal, the essence of both the eternal and the transient. In your womb, I saw the fourteen worlds. I saw all the moving and unmoving; I cannot describe it. I was bewildered and fell to the ground, but regained consciousness by remembering Shiva. I thought you were an ordinary child."

As she was praising him, he manifested his illusion again. Shiva caressed him, took him on her hip, and gave him milk. Girija entered the house and became busy with her chores. The sages and their wives returned to their respective homes. Whoever hears this story is freed from all sins.

Chapter 93 : The Killing of Cañcala

When the fifth year began, the young sons of the sages approached the house of Guneśa (Ganesha) at dawn. They called out to Shiva, saying, "Why is he still sleeping in the morning? Wake him up, O friend." Shiva replied, "Sleep has not come to you because you are always engrossed in play, day and night. Why do you ask me to wake him?" The boys replied, "Mother, we are never angry at your words. Are we not your children, and are you not our mother? Our minds are always fixed on your child. We see him constantly, and without him, we find no peace." Hearing their words, the child Guneśa stood up, came outside, and greeted them.

Together, they went outside, holding hands, and began to play, dividing into teams and engaging in various mock battles. They struck each other with their heads, chests, and arms, and played with water, dust, balls, fists, mud, and cow dung, while also pulling and pushing each other. They made a great noise, blowing horns and pipes. In this play, the demon-like boys were winning, and the god-like boys were losing.

While they were fighting, a great demon named Khaḍga arrived in the form of a massive bull that touched the sky. With the wind from his tail, he knocked down trees. With sharp teeth and a lolling tongue, he trampled the directions with his feet. He let out a roar that shook the earth and charged at Guneśa. Seeing him, the sage-boys fled in terror, some shouting to Guneśa to run. Hearing their cries, Guneśa suddenly assumed a massive form, flew up, and struck the demon on the head with a fist blow as powerful as a thunderbolt. The demon’s heart shattered, he vomited blood, let out a terrifying cry, and fell to the ground. After struggling for a moment, he died. His body, eighteen yojanas long, crushed many trees, and the dust raised by his fall covered the sky. Seeing him dead, the boys praised Guneśa, amazed that he had killed such a great demon with his small, heroic stature. They then resumed their play as before.

Then, another demon, possessing great strength and the ability to assume a shadow form, approached. With a kick, he shook the earth. He entered Guneśa’s shadow and made him fall. The other boys, not seeing the invisible, powerful sorcerer, were distressed and ran to Guneśa, asking why he had fallen. Guneśa, realizing through meditation that a demon had entered his shadow, picked up a large boulder and threw it onto the demon’s belly. The demon was crushed to dust. At the moment of his death, he assumed a massive form, destroying trees and mountains, and his blood and fat turned the earth red, like the flowers of spring.

After killing him, Guneśa continued to play. Then, a great demon named Cañcala, who had the shoulders of a bull, the face of a boar, and the belly of an elephant, arrived in the form of a child. He joined the boys and displayed various tricks. He would grab a boy’s foot and drag him, or hold a boy’s hands and strike his head. They played a game with a ball, throwing it into the sky and jumping on each other’s backs to catch it. During this, the demon Cañcala caught the ball, and when Guneśa jumped on his back to retrieve it, the demon complained about the weight. After playing for a while, Guneśa jumped onto the demon’s back with great force. Cañcala, now carrying Guneśa, flew into the sky. The other boys on the ground were distressed and watched him go. Guneśa, realizing the demon’s nature, suddenly made his body as heavy as the Himalayas. Unable to bear the weight, the demon fell and begged for mercy, saying, "Remove this great weight, or my life will depart. Have mercy on the lowly who seek your refuge." As the demon fainted, Guneśa picked him up, whirled him around like Garuda with a snake, and threw him into a distant land.

Chapter 94 : The Ninety-Fourth Chapter

K said: Then the sons of the sages, who were waiting, departed. The sky and the quarters were filled with the sounds of their victory cries. They played there again with various dances and songs. While the children were thus enjoying themselves, half the day had passed. Their parents, not seeing them, took sticks in their hands and went towards the children in the village. Finding Gunesh in their midst, dancing and making them dance, they scolded him.

The parents said: Without you, our children do not even bathe or eat, nor do they attend to their studies, conduct, or Brahminical duties. Then, having lost their anger, they looked at each other and said: "Why did we think of beating him? If we tell Shiva and his consort, what will they do? Many powerful demons have been killed by this child alone. We shall go to another land out of fear of you, O Gunesh."

K said: As the people were saying this, Gunesh vanished and appeared in a distant land. The children saw this, left their parents, and ran after Gunesh. Playing, they quickly went to the excellent hermitage of Gautama. Seeing the sage in meditation and his wife cooking, Gunesh went to the middle of the area and took the vessel of food. He divided the food and gave it to them, requesting with respect: "Because of my company, you have all been hungry for a long time. Eat at your leisure, and then we shall play again."

Ahalya was immediately angry: "How did he touch my food? The Vaishvadeva ritual has not been performed, nor has the offering been made, you mischievous children!" Gautama, the best of sages, stopped his meditation. He stood up and saw all the children eating the food due to hunger, and he spoke to Gunesh.

Gautama said: Having attained the status of a son to great parents, how can you act so unjustly? Having heard of your wonderful deeds in the past, we consider you the Supreme Lord, the form of the Supreme Brahman. Why do you act like a child, O Gunesh?

K said: The sage took him by the hand and went to the house of Parvati. Holding both the child and the food vessel, he went to his own abode and said to the mother: "This child is always doing me injustice. I have come to tell you today; what should be done, O Gauri? If you think he should go far away, tell me."

K said: Hearing the words spoken by the sage, Gauri became filled with anger. She beat him with a stick, while fire seemed to emanate from her eyes. Bowing to the sage, Gauri said in anger: "O Sage, I am also terrified of him since his birth. How many obstacles created by demons must be endured? Even the wives of the sages do not come here. This one is wicked in every way and causes division among the sons of the sages. Because he is my son, people do not curse him."

Having tied the child's hands and feet in front of Shiva, she placed him inside the house and locked the door firmly. Saying "Do not do this, do not do this," Gautama went back to his hermitage. Then all the children began to grieve for Gunesh, wondering how and when they would see him again. While they were saying this, he appeared in their midst in an instant.

He was seen at his mother's waist, and the house was also visible. They said to her, "Gauri, your son has come out from inside the house." She looked and saw Gunesh tied up inside the house, while she saw the children outside. Distraught, Shiva called out to him for breastfeeding, but the children prevented her, as Shiva was inside the house. Opening the door, she released the child and joyfully gave him milk.

Gautama went to his hermitage and began the worship of the deities. He saw all the gods satisfied, as if they were in the form of Gunesh, and food scattered before them. Astonished, he thought in his mind: "I have acted with a foolish mind. I told Uma everything and showed her the food vessel. She beat and bound him, the Unmanifest, the cause of the manifest. He is the Supreme Lord, and when he is satisfied, one gains great merit. He came and ate with the sons of the sages. Deluded by his Maya, I did not recognize him."

K said: Ahalya, with a wondrous mind, cooked again. He also sat in meditation and completed his daily duties.

Thus ends the ninety-fourth chapter, titled "The Taking of Gautama's Food," in the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana.

Chapter 95 : The Slaying of Vrikasura

Vyasa said:
When the sixth year began, the child (Ganesha) went outside. He played in many ways with the children of Uma. Wishing to see him, Vishvakarma went to the house. Parvati came out and honored him greatly. She seated him on a beautiful throne and worshipped him with respect. After washing his feet, she offered him sandalwood paste and betel leaves. Seeing her respect, Vishvakarma was pleased. He praised Shiva (Parvati) with supreme devotion, bowing down with joy.

Vishvakarma said:
I bow to you, O Goddess of the Universe, whose form is the universe itself, who embodies Brahma, Indra, Rudra, Aryama, and Vishnu. The universe is woven by you, O infinite power, you who are praised by the gods led by Brahma. You create the universe with Rajas, protect it with Sattva, and destroy it with Tamas; this threefold quality is your eternal form. By you, the hosts of demons have been destroyed, and the Brahmarshis have attained liberation through the power of knowledge. You are the incomparable power of Vishnu, the cause of all, the supreme Maya. You are the supreme power behind both the existent and the non-existent; you create the moving and non-moving universe. Deluding all the worlds and the lords of gods, you grant them enjoyment according to their deeds. Those who surrender to you have no fear of death or demons at any time. You are Lakshmi, and you are the inauspiciousness for the wicked; you are the form of a woman. You are the form of knowledge in the three worlds, and you are the radiance of the moon and the sun. Those who take refuge in you, the support of the world, never face even a trace of adversity, O Mother. You are the Goddess of the Universe; you are this universe, and you nourish it in the form of water. You are without beginning, middle, or end, and you are inaccessible to the lords of Vishnu, Shiva, and the gods. Through your grace, devotees worship you and reside in heaven in a state of bliss. You destroy those who lack devotion when you are angry; I have surrendered to you, O Mother. Blessed are these eyes of mine, my knowledge, my birth, and my lineage. My clan is blessed because I have seen your feet, O Mother of the World.

Vyasa said:
Praised in this way, the Mother of the World offered him a boon. He chose supreme devotion, and she said, "So be it." Whoever reads this hymn will attain all their desires, victory everywhere, nourishment, knowledge, longevity, happiness, and auspiciousness.

Shiva said:
O Vishvakarma of great intellect, you are wise in every way. Everything that was heard through the words of Shiva has been seen in you. The gods, oppressed by the ocean (Sindhu), are all in prison. Shiva has come here because Kailasa was taken. In this Dandaka forest, no one else is seen. You have come at the right time; it has been a long time since we met.

Vishvakarma said:
What is the wonder, O Mother of the World, if a child comes to his mother? I am a great devotee, wishing for knowledge, and I have come to see the Guru, Shiva. O Mother, I have heard of the wonderful glory of your son. I have come to see him, eager for the sight of both of you.

Vyasa said:
As they were speaking, Vinayaka arrived. He shone like countless moons, his body reddish like Rajas, with a pleasant face, surrounded by a group of children. He shone in their midst like Vishnu among the winds. Seeing him, Vishvakarma bowed down with folded hands. Knowing him to be the son of Girija, he praised the Supreme Soul.

Vishvakarma said:
I bow to the Supreme Soul, whose form is Truth, Consciousness, and Bliss. The Guru of the moving and non-moving, the cause of all causes. The Lord of qualities and beyond qualities, the creator, sustainer, and destroyer. The all-pervading Lord, whose form is both manifest and unmanifest. Inaccessible to all gods, dwelling in the lotus heart of sages. The Lord of success and intelligence, the giver of success to many devotees, the all-pervading one. The consumer of the desires of the non-devotees, shining like a thousand suns. Endowed with many powers, the crusher of demons and giants. Beginningless, imperishable, peaceful, free from old age and death. The three-bodied, the root of the three Vedas, the embodiment of Brahma, Vishnu, and Shiva.

Vyasa said:
Hearing this praise, the Lord of qualities was satisfied. He seated him on his own throne and worshipped him with respect. After washing his feet, he offered sandalwood, rice, flowers, incense, lamps, and food, and then spoke.

Gunesha said:
O Vishvakarma, you have come desiring to see me. What excellent gift have you brought for my joy?

Vishvakarma said:
What can be given for the satisfaction of one who is full of his own bliss, who fulfills the desires of others, who is desireless, who is the creator of all, who is all-powerful, who sees gold and stones as equal, who is the wish-fulfilling tree, who is capable of doing, not doing, or doing otherwise, who is independent, satisfied, and exists in his own power—what can be given by a mortal who is dependent, powerless, and poor? I have brought something according to my own ability.

Vyasa said:
Having said this, Vishvakarma placed before him a goad, a lotus, an axe, and a noose, shining like a thousand suns. The Lord of qualities took them, which were sharp and destroyers of all enemies. He asked Vishvakarma, "O creator of the universe, from where have these been brought for my pleasure? Tell me now, O sinless one."

Vishvakarma said:
I had a daughter named Sanjna, of beautiful form. Seeing her face, the moon-marked one (the Sun) was suddenly ashamed. Rama, Shachi, Savitri, Sharada, Arundhati, Rati—there is no one equal to her in the three worlds, O Lord of qualities. She was given by me to you, the three-fold one, for the three Vedas. When the three worlds arrived, there was a great festival for eight days and nights. Seeing her, the gods stumbled and left with their heads bowed in shame. Savita (the Sun) then took her and went to his own excellent abode. Scorched by his radiance, my daughter became thin. She then created a shadow (Chhaya) from her own power. Having told her everything, she immediately went to my house. After some time, the shadow was discovered by the Sun. Realizing she was not Sanjna, Aryama (the Sun) immediately went to my house. Then, frightened, Sanjna told me, "Father, do not tell the Sun, I cannot bear his radiance." Then, scolded by her father, she went outside. Taking the form of a mare, she lived in the forest in secret. Then, Vishvakarma, not seeing her anywhere in the house, told Aryama, "I do not know where she has gone, but I will tell you the remedy. If your radiance is reduced a little, she will appear; then you can enjoy with her."

The Sun said:
You should do as you think best.

Vyasa said:
Vishvakarma then placed him on a machine and filed him down. He reduced his radiance, and he became somewhat gentle. Aryama went to where Sanjna was hidden. Becoming a horse, he enjoyed with her, and she gave birth to the Ashvins. Taking her, Aryama went to his own world with joy.

Vishvakarma said:
O Lord of qualities, with the remaining radiance, I have quickly fashioned these weapons for you, O Lord of the World. They are very sharp, always victorious, and four have been given to you; the discus and mace were given to Hari, and the trident was given to Shambhu to destroy all enemies.

Gunesha said:
You have done well, Vishvakarma. These weapons are auspicious, given for the destruction of demons and for the benefit of others by the virtuous.

Vyasa said:
Having said this, he quickly took the weapons and brandished them. Then the earth, mountains, and forests trembled. He shone with those weapons, which were like ten million suns. Vishvakarma bowed and went to his own place by his command. The son of Uma again played, surrounded by the children of the sages. Then came a great and most wicked demon named Vrika. Terrifying in appearance, intoxicated, powerful, as if swallowing everything. Shaking the earth with the strike of his tail, he had teeth like a plow. Seeing the terrifying demon, the sons of the sages fled. He (Ganesha) took the weapons and struck Vrika. By the mere strike of the goad, the demon fell to the ground. Vomiting blood, he assumed his own form, crushing trees, destroying living beings, and spanning ten yojanas. After the sun set, he went home with the children. The children told Uma, "Today, this demon named Vrika was killed by him with a strike of the goad, and he was ten yojanas long." The daughter of the mountain said in anger, "Go to your own homes." Hearing her words, the children went to their homes.

Thus ends the ninety-fifth chapter named the Killing of Vrikasura in the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana.

Chapter 96 : The Dispute Between Gauri and Aditi

Kautsa said: Then, one day, the daughter of the mountain (Parvati) spoke to the pleased Shankara.

Shiva said: O Goddess, the child has now entered his seventh year. Let his sacred thread ceremony (upanayana) be performed with great festivities at an auspicious time.

Shiva said: You have spoken well, O virtuous one. Knowing my heart's desire, I shall perform his thread ceremony according to the prescribed rites.

Kautsa said: Having said this, the Lord called Gautama, and after deliberating on an auspicious day, he prepared all the necessary materials. He had a vast pavilion constructed and invited all the groups of sages. Having welcomed and honored them, Hara, following their guidance, joyfully performed the thread ceremony according to the rites.

He offered gifts to Shiva, the child, and the eighty-eight thousand sages. He worshipped them according to the rules and bestowed upon them various treasures. He did the same for the thirty-three crore gods and the Yakshas.

Amidst the sound of musical instruments, with Kinnaras singing and groups of dancers performing, while all the worlds watched the grand festival, Sadashiva distributed gifts and donations with great joy. After installing the deities, he fed everyone.

In the morning, after bathing the boy and completing the tonsure ceremony, he had him bathed again by four Brahmins and fed him. Between the altars, behind a magnificent curtain, he performed the auspicious rites with sages who were experts in mantras.

At that moment, two demons named Kritanta and Kala arrived, assuming the forms of powerful, intoxicated elephants, dripping with ichor. They had sharp tusks, long trunks, and heads that touched the sky, terrifying the people with their trumpeting and having foreheads red like vermilion.

The earth trembled under their footsteps. Fighting with their tusks, they covered the sky with dust. They reached the entrance of the assembly hall, which was occupied by Indra, and began to shatter the pillars with their tusks.

The king of elephants, Airavata, fell to the ground, wounded and unconscious, bleeding profusely. After a moment, regaining consciousness, he was attacked by the two elephants, who struck him with their tusks. Then, the two mad elephants entered the middle of the assembly hall.

They began to strike the pavilion with their trunks. Hearing the commotion, all the people stood up. Wherever the elephants went, the gods and sages fled in all directions out of fear.

The Ganas said: They informed Shiva that this obstacle had arisen. The assembly, including Indra and the sages, was devastated by the fear of the elephants.

Then Gauri fled to the house with her companions. The child, seeing the two powerful elephants, roared like thunder and quickly grabbed them by their trunks with both his hands. The elephants began to trumpet.

Whirling them around, he struck one elephant against the other. Both were shattered into hundreds of pieces and fell to the ground. The earth shook, and the trees fell, crushed to the ground. Then the Ganas quickly threw the pieces far away.

The mother came quickly and took the boy to her side. All the gods and sages, who had fled, returned. The companion of Shiva said that the two demons had been quickly killed by the child. Then the gods, led by Indra, and the sages gathered together.

They all said to Gunesha (Ganesha), "O Lord, source of all virtues, these two demons, who were destroyers of all life and had assumed the forms of elephants through deception, have been killed by you in play, O Lord, though they were very powerful and full of maya." Having said this, Indra, the sages, and the gods entered the assembly hall.

When everyone was seated, the Apsaras danced. Then musical instruments were played in the presence of Brahma and Rudra. Exchanging signals, they tied the waistband on the child. They performed the sacred thread ceremony, the wearing of the deerskin, and the offering of oblations into the fire according to the rites, followed by the recitation of the Savitri mantra. Then the mother gave him alms, garments, and ornaments.

Shiva gave him an upper garment, pearls, sweets, and food, as well as a trident and the moon. He gave him the name Bhalachandra (Moon-browed). Hari gave him a discus and named the great soul Shulapani (Trident-bearer) and Shuchishka. Then Purandara (Indra), after worshipping him, gave him a Chintamani (wish-fulfilling gem) for his neck and named him Chintamani, the auspicious bestower of all desires. Brahma, having worshipped him, gave him a lotus and established his name as Vidhata in the assembly.

Then all the gods, having worshipped the Lord of Virtues, gave him various names according to their own wishes. Aditi and Kashyapa then worshipped him with devotion. The Lord revealed his auspicious original form to them—Bhalachandra, ten-armed, adorned with a crown, wearing divine garments and fragrances, decorated with divine ornaments, riding a lion, and bound by a serpent.

Seeing this, Aditi was filled with joy and embraced him with great affection. Her body thrilled, her voice choked with love, she was filled with supreme wonder, her eyes streaming with tears, and she was immersed in supreme bliss. Kashyapa also shed his bodily consciousness at that moment. Out of love for their son, they said, "O child, we were emaciated by your separation, but now we are nourished by your sight. We are now helpless, O son, devoted only to your feet."

Gajanana said: "Mother, I promised to give you my vision one day. I have given it now, so you should not grieve. There is never any separation from me, for I am the indweller of all."

Kautsa said: While they were speaking, Parvati arrived. Seeing their affection for the child, she said with great joy, "O Aditi, give me my son, whom you have held for a long time."

"He is not your son, O beautiful one, look carefully," Aditi replied. She looked again and saw him as her own son, the Lord.

Aditi said: "O Gauri, look yourself, my son is standing before me." She looked again and saw him as her own son, Gunesha.

Aditi claimed him as hers, and Parvati claimed him as hers. As they were arguing, the gods, filled with wonder, said: "He is the beginningless and endless Lord, the creator, sustainer, and destroyer of the universe. He has infinite forms, infinite glory, and possesses many powers. Whose son could he be? Both are deluded by his maya."

They said: "Whoever he belongs to, let him be given into her hands."

Kautsa said: Then the gods, looking at the Lord who takes many forms, were amazed. One called him the Creator, another called him the four-armed Vishnu, and another called him the three-eyed Varuna. They said, "It is impossible for us to determine. You both must decide through wisdom who this supreme being belongs to."

Then Gauri quickly took the Lord as her own son. She gave him milk with affection, and Aditi became indifferent. "If this child were mine, why would he go to another? I was attached to another's son in vain due to delusion." The sages and Kashyapa worshipped the Lord of Virtues. Having bowed and taken leave, they went to their respective homes. Bhavani went home, taking her son with joy. Then everyone else departed to their own abodes.

Chapter 97 : The Binding of the Birds 

The Sage said: O Lord, Creator of the Universe, you have previously described to me the glory of the deity Vinayaka, known as Mayureshwara, by the name Gunesha. How did he receive that name, and what great deeds did he perform? Please tell me all of this now.

The Sage (K) said: I shall narrate to you in full how the deity Vinayaka received the name Mayureshwara and the deeds he performed. Once, Shesha was in his palace in the netherworld (Patala), surrounded by Vasuki and other serpents. Then, Kadru arrived, radiant and beautiful, wearing a lovely garment adorned with pearls. Seeing their mother, Shesha and the others bowed to her, saying, "Mother, it has been a long time since we saw you. We have been longing for your presence, yet you have been so stern." They seated her on a throne and worshipped her with great devotion. Shesha then asked her, "Mother, you are the beloved wife of Kashyapa, the creator and sustainer of all. Even the gods like Brahma do not fully know your true nature, and you possess the power to curse or bless. We are your sons, capable of consuming the three worlds. Why have you come here, and for what purpose?"

Kadru replied: No one comes without a purpose, my son. Listen carefully. My co-wife, Vinata, the mother of the birds, once refused to see me. When I went to her house, she offered me no seat, no hospitality, and no welcome. Remembering an old enmity, she ordered her son Jatayu to attack me. He tore my clothes and humiliated me, saying, "You were once a slave to my mother; you deserve no respect." He threatened to take my life if I did not leave. O King of Serpents, hearing these words, I was filled with grief and despair. I have come to you for help. If you have devotion for me, you must destroy my co-wife so that my heart may find peace.

Hearing this, Shesha became enraged, like a fire fueled by ghee. He vowed to take revenge for the insult to his mother. He and Vasuki set out for Vinata’s hermitage. Vasuki said, "I will go myself with millions of serpents and bring Vinata here." He arrived at the hermitage, and Vinata, seeing the countless cruel serpents surrounding her, asked, "Why have you come to bind me? I have committed no offense. My son’s power is known to all serpents; release me, or he will destroy you."

Seeing their arrogance, she remembered Garuda. He arrived immediately with the birds—Shyena, Sampati, and Jatayu—whose wingbeats shook the three worlds. The serpents began spitting venom, and a fierce battle ensued. The birds were eventually captured, but Garuda arrived, shaking the earth and causing trees and mountains to fall. The serpents fled from his presence. Vinata was released, but Vasuki, enraged, spewed poison. Garuda struck him down to the earth with his wings. Vinata escaped to safety. Garuda, seeing Vasuki’s power, assumed a subtle form to protect Vinata, but Vasuki, in his fury, dragged the birds into a cave in the netherworld and sealed it with a stone.

Vinata, grieving for her captured sons, went to Kashyapa and told him everything: how she had been insulted, how the birds had tried to save her, and how they were now imprisoned in the netherworld. She lamented that she could not live without them. Kashyapa comforted her, saying, "Do not grieve, noble one. I will grant you a boon; you shall have another son. When this child, whose egg cannot be pierced even by a thunderbolt, breaks it with his feet while playing, he will be your son. He will be a powerful Nilakantha (Blue-throated one). At the sound of his name, the serpents will be defeated. Gunesha will ride upon him and relieve the burden of the earth, and your sons will be freed from their bondage."

Having said this, the sage granted her the boon in private. Vinata went to a secluded forest and, in time, laid an egg that could not be broken by any weapon. She placed it in an earthen pot, wrapped in bark, and waited.

Chapter 98 : The Granting of the Boon to Shikhandi

The narrator said: Thus, when the seventh year had passed and the eighth began, Guneshwara (Ganesha), having bathed at dawn, sat chanting the four Vedas. Adorned with a musk tilak and various ornaments, dressed in divine garments, and perfumed with celestial scents and garlands, he shone brilliantly. Then, the sons of the sages arrived, and they were illuminated by his radiance just as clouds are lit by the dawn. Seeing them, Guneshwara felt a desire to teach them; he placed his hand on the heads of all the children.

Immediately, the Vedas manifested in the children, who were three, four, or five years old. Without any physical effort, the sound of the Vedas was heard as if by divine voice. All of them recited the four Vedas perfectly. Even the animals stopped eating, their mouths full of grass, intent on listening to the Vedic chants. When the two Vedas were completed, they began the chanting of the Samaveda. Deer, lions, tigers, snakes, and birds, all captivated by the music, shed tears of joy. Eighty-eight thousand sages, absorbed in listening, remained as still as if they were asleep at night. The Pramathas and other divine attendants, along with Shiva, were filled with joy, and the moon situated on Shiva’s forehead began to drip nectar. Due to this divine influence, the very skeletons of the dead appeared as living men. The Gandharvas and Kinnaras were humbled by this music. Women, tormented by the god of love, became peaceful and performed the ritual of waving lights (arati).

Suddenly, a demon of strange form arrived there. His roar shattered the caves of Mount Mandara. He had teeth like a plow, a nose like a well, eyes like a pond, and he was a destroyer of enemies. He wandered about in the form of a beast, possessing five eyes, four horns, four feet, and four ears. He had three faces and two tails, and he laughed loudly upon seeing the Lord. Guneshwara said to the children, "Look at this curiosity." The children exclaimed that it was truly wonderful. Then the demon danced loudly, flying up and falling to the earth. He appeared and disappeared, staying for a moment and then vanishing. Guneshwara told the children, "Catch him, catch him!"

Saying this, Guneshwara chased the demon with speed. The great demon fled, and the children followed behind. They all reached a great forest where even the wind could not enter, and where lions, tigers, elephants, boars, and monkeys roared. The demon, the best of his kind, flew away to catch them, but then went to a far-off land. Ganesha, the elephant-headed Lord, became frustrated. With eyes reddened by anger, he released a powerful noose. The earth and sky trembled, and the clouds began to swirl. Stars fell from the sky to the earth, and the directions echoed with the sound. The noose struck the demon and returned to Guneshwara in an instant.

Choked for breath, the demon fell to the ground, rolling his feet, arms, and face. His life force departed through his eyes while the children watched. Having fallen, he assumed a form twenty-four yojanas long. Some children dragged him here and there with the noose still around his neck. Other children threw wood into his mouth and dust over him. Some even urinated and defecated on his head. The children, hungry, went to Guneshwara and ate mangoes, some even climbing onto the demon’s head. Those on the ground hit those above with fruits.
