While they were eating the fallen fruits, they saw Vinata.

She was running toward the children, covering her egg. Seeing them fleeing, Vinata followed behind, striking beasts and trees with the wind of her wings. Guneshwara, seeing this, hid in the hollow of a tree. He saw an egg, whiter than the moon’s orb. Guneshwara suddenly grabbed it with his hand. It cracked, and a bird with a blue neck, long wings, and large eyes emerged, vomiting sparks of fire from its mouth. It flapped both wings, shaking the surface of the earth. The oceans crossed their boundaries due to its sound. The sun’s orbit shook, and the children fled. The bird followed them, striking them with its wings.

Seeing their distress, Guneshwara, wishing to fight, grabbed the cruel bird by its wing, and a battle ensued. The bird struck with its wings and beak, and Guneshwara, with reddened eyes, struck him with his fist. Seeing the bird’s great strength, Guneshwara struck him with his four weapons, and they fell to the ground. Then, the bird flew up, quickly carrying away the children. Guneshwara released them and mounted the bird. Having brought the bird under his control, Guneshwara rode it. Then the children came, and Vinata also arrived.

She praised the Supreme Lord Guneshwara with her mind: "You are the Creator, Brahma, through Rajas; the Protector, Vishnu, through Sattva; and the Destroyer, Shankara, through Tamas. Neither the gods nor the sages know the truth of your manifest form. Who can know the unmanifest, the Guru of all moving and non-moving beings?" Having praised him thus, she bowed down with devotion and said, "Know me as Vinata, the wife of Sage Kashyapa. This Shikhandi (the bird) will be your servant. The sage had told me that whoever breaks this egg will be its master and will surely free my sons. Waiting for this, I have long seen your lotus feet. Jatayu, Shyena, and Sampati were taken by the sons of Kadru. O Lord of the Universe, free them and show me my sons quickly."

Ganesha said: "Do not worry, Mother; I will show you your sons."

The narrator said: Having said this to Vinata, Guneshwara, filled with great joy, said to the bird (Shikhandi), "Ask for a boon from me."

The peacock (Shikhandi) said: "If you are pleased with me and if a boon is to be granted, then let your name be known in the world preceded by mine. Grant me this, O Lord of all, and grant me firm devotion to you."

The Lord said: "Well said, well said! You have spoken with a selfless heart. My name shall be known in the three worlds as Mayureshwara (Lord of the Peacock), and may your devotion to me be firm."

The narrator said: Hearing this, Vinata went to her hermitage. Mayureshwara, mounted on Shikhandi, went to his home. Accompanied by the sage-children who were repeatedly chanting "Mayureshwara, Mayureshwara, Mayureshwara," he adorned the directions. Bowing to his mother, he told her the whole story. Describing Mayureshwara, the sons of the sages went to their homes. Thus, the Lord Guneshwara attained the name Mayureshwara.

Chapter 99 : The Play of Mayuresha

K said: In his ninth year, the Lord of Qualities performed a wondrous feat. Adorned with four weapons and various ornaments, decorated with musk, and wearing divine garments, the young boy mounted his peacock to go out and play. Radiant as the full moon, he was praised with shouts of victory. Some children bowed to him, while others carried his umbrella and flag. Some, celebrating the festival of seeing him, followed him as he went to play at a lake five yojanas wide.

The lake was deep, filled with crocodiles, fish, tortoises, and frogs, surrounded by creepers and trees, and inhabited by flocks of birds. Some children flew into the water, while others went in slowly. Seeing a large mango tree on the bank laden with fruit, the Lord of the Peacock climbed it, desiring to eat the fruit. When the children threw mangoes at him, he dodged them, and they ran away. Some children broke branches and fell into the water. While they were playing, a demon in the form of a horse arrived. His footfalls crushed mountains, and his neighing made the three worlds tremble. With the movement of his tail, he killed living beings and struck the trees. When the mango tree shook from his sound, the children fell. Some fled, some were injured on their heads, and some fell into the water.

When the Lord of Qualities fell into the deep water and remained submerged for two muhurtas, the sages' children wept. They wondered what they would tell Uma and how they could face her. They feared that Shankara, in his rage, would reduce them to ashes. They lamented, "We cannot enter the deep water to save him. He is our mother, father, protector, brother, savior, and friend."

K said: As the children grieved, the Lord of the Peacock caught the demon by the ears in the water and pulled him with his mighty strength. He climbed onto the demon and submerged him repeatedly with his weight. The demon, vomiting water from his eyes and mouth, his ears and breath filled with water, let out a great cry and gave up his life. Holding him with one hand and swinging him out of the water, the Lord of the Peacock triumphed, and the children rejoiced and danced. Seeing the great demon lying in a hundred pieces on the ground, they praised the Lord of the Peacock for his immense strength and valor. They said, "We thought you were dead and were weeping, but here you are, having killed the demon."

Then they entered the water and began to splash each other. With one mind, they all splashed the Lord of Ganesha. Just as clouds shower the earth and mountains during the rainy season, he was showered by them. When his six arms were not enough, he manifested countless arms and threw water at the children. Seeing this wonder, they asked each other, "How did he, who has six arms, become adorned with infinite arms? We are mere children with two hands, and you, O Lord of the World, possess infinite arms and power." They splashed him again in their excitement. The Lord of Ganesha, assuming infinite forms, showered them all back, appearing before each one of them with his six arms and his own radiance.

Then, once again, he stood on his peacock, adorned with his four weapons. The children folded their hands and bowed to the Lord of Qualities. Seeing the entire universe in his face—all the heavens, Gandharvas, Yakshas, Rakshasas, rivers, and oceans—and the entire moving and unmoving world, including gods, demons, and humans, they were filled with fear and prayed to him.

The children said: We do not know ourselves, nor others, nor anything at all. Please, O Lord of All, show mercy and take on a single form.

K said: Hearing their prayer, the Lord became as he was before. Meanwhile, Naga maidens came there to play. Seeing the Lord, they were overcome with shyness and, like deer, looked at him with bashful eyes. They were beautiful and adorned with all kinds of jewelry. Seeing the Lord of the Peacock, they were agitated by the fire of desire. They said to each other, "If he were our husband, our birth, life, and youth would be fulfilled." With courage, they asked him, "Where have you come from? Seeing your face, our hearts are agitated. O best of men, please calm our hearts with your touch."

The Lord said: I am the son of Shiva, clearly known as Mayuresha. I was submerged by the strong sons of the sages, and by chance, I saw your lotus feet.

They said: You should stay in our home for a moment and rest.

The Lord said: Parvati will suffer greatly due to my absence. Therefore, I will not come to your place. You may go, O Naga maidens.

K said: As he said this, they took him to their home. When the sages' children did not see him, they were filled with grief again. They lamented, "How has the merciful Lord of Qualities become so hard? The moon, which pours nectar, does not become hot. A father does not abandon his children even if they commit a thousand faults. Where have you gone? Without you, our lives will depart."

Some fell to the ground, some struck their foreheads. Some went to their ashrams, and on the way, seeing his lotus feet, they bowed and wept. They saw the demon Bhagasura, whose hair caused stars to fall to the ground when he shook his head. His feet were a hundred yojanas long, and he lay on the path with his mouth open. The children, lost in meditation on Mayuresha and grieving, walked into his stomach as rivers flow into the ocean. Confused, they spoke to each other, "Where has Mayuresha gone? Where are we going? We do not know the direction, and our homes are not visible. He has taken our minds, the lords of our senses. Without our minds, how will we have knowledge? Where are our mothers, brothers, fathers, and the Lord of Qualities?"

As the children spoke, the Lord of the Peacock, who fulfills all desires, appeared before them, adorned with his four weapons.

The Lord of Qualities said: Do not grieve. Knowing your suffering, I have come quickly. You children are inside the stomach of Bhagasura and do not realize it.

K said: The Lord of Qualities put the sages' children to sleep with his divine illusion. He grew inside the demon's body like a dwarf and tore the body apart, splitting it into two pieces. Then, as the sun set and the children did not return, their mothers and fathers were filled with anxiety and grief. They said to each other, "Where has the mighty son of Shiva taken the children? Has he died along with them? If he were alive, he would have returned by evening, hungry." Some mothers even gave up their lives. Some told Uma the whole story. Some went into the forest and wandered through the woods and mountains. Not finding the children, they returned home, distressed, and the mothers, fathers, and siblings raised a great clamor.

Hearing their cries, the merciful Lord of the Peacock assumed the form of each child, with their respective ornaments, clothes, character, qualities, and limbs, and entered their homes. Having taken the form and age of each, he appeared auspicious. The mothers, rising quickly, took their children and, filled with supreme joy, gave them milk with love. Then the fathers and mothers saw the children and asked, "Where were you? Why did you leave at dawn? You have not bathed, nor eaten, nor consumed anything else."

The Lord said: You should not leave the company of Mayuresha now.

K said: Having instructed the children thus, he embraced them and they ate happily. Shiva also saw that Mayuresha had returned. She embraced him and asked, "What did you eat in the forest? Because of the grief of your separation, I have not eaten anything. Drink from my breasts, which are full of milk, and take your meal." The Lord of Qualities then did everything as his mother requested.

Thus ends the ninety-ninth chapter of the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Journey of Mayuresha to Patala."

Chapter 100 : The Slaying of Bhagasura

Ka said: The Lord Mayuresha, the master of the universe, who possesses many forms and a beautiful body, was taken by the Naga maidens to their home for play. He was worshipped with great elaboration, bathed with fragrant oils and warm water, adorned with divine garments, ornaments, and sandalwood paste, and offered incense, lamps, food, fruits, betel leaves, and gold.

Folding their hands, the maidens said: "We are most blessed, O Lord, for we have seen the feet that even Brahma and the others long for. The Naga realm is most fortunate, and our lives are truly meaningful. Our minds are immersed in a pool of bliss, and all our suffering has vanished. Whatever you desire, O Lord, please accept it here. Stay for a few days before you depart."

Mayuresha said: "I will fulfill your wishes, but Girija (Parvati) awaits me. Distressed by my absence, she does not eat anything. Whose children are you? If I could see your father..."

Ka said: Then the Naga maidens said, "We are the daughters of Vasuki. Brahma, the sages, and the gods always visit his home. O Lord, the flames of his poison burn the three worlds."

Ka said: Having said this, the maidens led Him to their father, who was seated on a radiant throne of jewels, surrounded by serpents. He shone like a million suns, adorned with a garland of gems, and the rays from the jewels on his hoods illuminated all directions. Seeing Vasuki, who was very proud and powerful, the Lord quickly flew up and snatched the jewel from his hood, which had kept the underworld from being in darkness.

He shook his head, causing the seven mountains, the seven oceans, the heavens, the underworlds, and the nether regions to tremble. Holding Vasuki with one hand in his play, Mayuresha, the one adorned with serpents, tied him around his neck. He became famous in the heavenly realm and roared with joy. Hearing that roar, the three worlds were agitated.

Then the serpent hosts told Shesha to bring Vasuki back. Filled with anger, Shesha expanded all his hoods and began to burn the three worlds by releasing his poisonous fire. He shouted, "Who is capable of defeating my brother Vasuki?" Driven by rage, he rushed forward like a blazing forest fire, quickly reached Mayuresha, and cried, "Stop, stop!"

Then the Naga clans quickly followed Shesha. Seeing the serpent host, the Lord stood still. He placed his hand on the head of the peacock (his mount) to prepare for battle with the serpents. Shesha approached, bowing, yet looking as if he were about to swallow the Lord in his great wonder. The Lord flapped his wings, and the wind from them whirled the serpents around. He devoured some, crushed others, and killed some of the most powerful serpents. Seeing this, some serpents died, while others were paralyzed with fear.

Witnessing his prowess, Shesha released a breath, and the peacock fell to the ground, fainted. Then Shesha, as if burning with rage, went toward Mayuresha. Seeing the three worlds enveloped in poisonous fire, the Lord of qualities assumed his cosmic form and climbed onto Shesha's hoods. Flying in his child-form, he roared like a cloud. He danced, clapping his hands and crushing the serpent with his feet. Burdened by the weight of infinite universes, Shesha wondered how he, who carries a single universe, could bear such a load. The Lord tied Shesha around his waist like a child playing with a rope.

Then all the serpents approached, wishing to fight. With a mere roar, the Lord of obstacles caused them all to fall. He tied some to his head and others to his ears. Exhausted, Shesha then praised the Lord of qualities: "Even Brahma and the sages do not know your true form. You alone create, protect, and destroy the universe. You are the performer of many incarnations and the destroyer of various demons. You are the witness of all, dwelling within everything. You are the cause of all, the cause of causes. Please forgive us for our ignorance and pride in wanting to fight you."

Having brought Sampati, Jatayu, and the hawk, the King of Serpents offered them, bowed, and became silent. They also bowed and said, "O Lord of the helpless, by your grace, we are freed from the bonds of the serpents. Salutations to you, O Supreme Lord."

Ka said: Having said this, Mayuresha embraced his brother, the peacock, with joy, his voice choked with emotion. They asked about their mother's well-being, and she replied that she was safe. Then the Lord of the Ganas mounted the peacock and, accompanied by the women, traveled from the underworld to the earth. On the way, he saw Bhagasura, the devourer of children. Raising his shining axe, which resembled the sun and moon, the Lord struck his neck and killed the beast.

His head rolled around like a mountain peak struck by a thunderbolt. Then the children who had been inside him emerged, deluded by Yoga Maya. They all cried out, "Where is Mayuresha?" Whether sitting, playing, sleeping, waking, or eating, they meditated only on him. Emerging as if from the womb, they saw the Lord of qualities. Seeing him, they wept with affection and embraced him with joy.

They said: "Leaving us in the demon's belly, where did you go? By remembering you, we survived in his belly."

Mayuresha said: "I am all-pervading, present everywhere, the knower of all, the Lord of the universe. I never left you; you should not worry."

Ka said: Then Mayuresha went on, surrounded by the children. Some ran ahead, making various sounds; some carried umbrellas, others held staffs and whisks. Seeing the dust and the banner, the sages came out and saw Mayuresha mounted on the peacock, surrounded by children. The sages were amazed and said to each other, "Who are these children in this house, and who are these others near him?"

They all looked at them and saw them as divine forms. Through contemplation, they realized they were all the form of the Supreme Brahman. Immersed in a pool of bliss, they forgot themselves and others. They looked at them as if bewildered, not seeing them again. Deluded by Maya, they all saw their own children. One approached his father and began to study as before; another approached his mother and happily drank milk. One embraced his mother or father. Another, having been hit by his brother, cried as if from the pain of the blow. Uma (Parvati) also saw her son, embraced him, and happily gave him her breast. She asked in anger, "Why were you gone for so long?" Taking Mayuresha by the hand, Girija went home. All the sages went to their respective hermitages with the children.

Thus ends the hundredth chapter of the Krida Khanda in the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Killing of Bhagasura."

Chapter 101 : The Destruction of the Demon Army

Kritayuga said: Then, in the tenth year, Lord Shiva was sitting comfortably with Girija (Parvati) on his left, accompanied by his seven crore Ganas. Seeing the child Mayuresha playing in the courtyard, all the sages, led by Gautama, approached and bowed to him. With enlightened minds, they said to the radiant Mahadeva, "O Shiva, as long as you remain here with Mayuresha, countless demons gather. We are being oppressed by them; please go elsewhere, or if you command, we shall go elsewhere."

Shiva replied, "In your company, I have spent nine years happily, and Mayuresha has overcome all those obstacles. What is the point of us staying here if you are leaving? I myself shall go to another peaceful place."

Kritayuga said: Hearing these words of Shambhu, the sages bowed to him, shouted "Victory to Lord Mayuresha," and returned to their own places. Shiva, accompanied by his Ganas and Ambika, mounted his bull. With Mayuresha riding his peacock in front, he set out joyfully, filling the world with the sound of various musical instruments. All the sages followed Mayuresha and Shiva, along with their wives and children, who spoke with affection. The sky was covered with dust, and nothing could be seen. Shiva turned and headed towards the south.

On the way, they saw the demon Kamalasura approaching with an army of twelve Akshauhinis, his clothes covered in dust. Elephants, chariots, horses, and infantry moved in front; the brilliance of their weapons obscured the sun. There was a great clamor from the elephants, horses, chariots, and soldiers, mixed with the clashing sounds of various weapons. Shiva’s scouts, who had gone ahead, saw the great demon—a relative of Shankhasura, adorned with various ornaments and carrying many weapons—and returned to inform Shiva. The earth trembled under the demon's footsteps, causing the mountains to shake. The sages who were in front fell down in fear and said, "O great Mayuresha, how will you protect us all?"

Kritayuga said: Hearing their words, the Lord of Ganas replied, "O best of sages, do not worry while Shiva is present." Mayuresha then bowed to Hara and said, "O Lord, this demon named Kamalasura is accompanied by a great army. If I have your blessing, I will go to battle immediately."

Shiva replied, "You have spoken well, my son, words that gladden the heart. This demon leader has an army of twelve Akshauhinis. How can you go alone, even with the seven crore Ganas? Go, achieve victory quickly, and slay this powerful enemy."

The Lord (Mayuresha) said, "By your grace, I believe I could burn the three worlds. How many demons have not been killed? Do you not know? What is this demon worth? I shall return victorious."

Kritayuga said: Hearing these words, Shambhu embraced him, placed his auspicious hand on his head, and gave him his trident. Shiva gave the command to his son, who was accompanied by the Ganas. Mayuresha, the destroyer of obstacles, eager for battle, set out with the Pramathas, making the ten directions resound with his roars and shouts of victory. Hara, accompanied by Shiva, also followed on his bull to watch.

Seeing the infinite demon army, Mayuresha created a vast army from his own body, and then the battle began. Those warriors were like the end of time, ready to consume the universe; they were larger than Mount Meru and their shouts shook the peaks. They struck each other in the fierce battle, and the sky was covered with dust, creating great darkness. The demons were astonished, wondering, "How did he, who was alone, become many? He must be the Supreme Being who has descended to the abode of Shiva to remove the burden of the earth."

Suddenly, the Lord assumed a long, vast form. Covered in divine illusion, the powerful Lord appeared with ten arms, wielding ten weapons. Mayuresha and the demon fought brilliantly, both skilled in various weapons and combat. The Ganas and the demon soldiers fought chaotically, striking each other with fists, teeth, and weapons. Some had their heads broken, others their faces, and some fell in the battle with their knees and thighs severed. Roaring in the darkness created by the dust, they struck each other according to their own names. Headless trunks fought both friends and foes. The demon army was broken and followed their leader. Rivers of blood flowed, carrying countless dead.

The demon said, "Indra and the other guardians of the world, and the gods led by Brahma, have all been defeated by me. Who else dares to fight? Who else will be able to turn the earth upside down?"

Kritayuga said: Saying this, he shook the world with the movement of his weapons. With eyes reddened by anger, the demon king killed all of Mayuresha's Ganas, regardless of their vehicles or weapons, cutting them down in the battle. Seeing his prowess, Mayuresha moved forward, roaring towards the sky, and struck down ten thousand enemies. Countless demons were killed and attained the rare state of liberation.

Thus ends the one hundred and first chapter of the Krida Khanda in the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Destruction of the Demon Army."

Chapter 102 : The Battle with Kamalasura

The Sage said: When his army was destroyed in many ways, the demon king mounted his horse. With sword in hand and filled with rage, he went to fight Mayuresha. Seeing him, Mayuresha mounted his own peacock. Seeing the demon king approaching rapidly, Mayuresha respectfully chanted the mantra taught by Bhrigu a hundred times and released the serpent weapon. The directions blazed with its brilliance. The soldiers of the divine army were surrounded by serpent troops; some died, some fled, and others fell to the ground. The Lord praised that demon of great strength and valor, saying, "You fight well, O king of demons; no one like you has been seen before." Saying this, the Lord released the Garuda weapon. Then the serpents fled, and the soldiers rose up.

The Lord released the discus weapon, which destroyed the demon army. By it, some demons were killed, some had their limbs and heads severed, and others fell with broken knees, shins, and ankles. They roared, "Run, run! O father, O mother, O son!" Those who saw Mayuresha attained liberation and gave up their lives. Thus, his army was destroyed, and the demon rushed forward in anger. Sword in hand and roaring with rage, he rushed toward the elephant-faced Lord. Seeing such an enemy, the Lord took up his axe. He went toward the demon with great speed and released the axe. It burned the sky, the directions, and the flocks of birds with its brilliance. It severed the demon's hand and his sword into a hundred pieces instantly. The demon suddenly grabbed his bow, strung it, and shot many arrows. Piercing them with force, he covered the soldiers with countless arrows. He roared like thunder, filling the sky and all directions.

With the strike of his arrows, the powerful Lord warded off the cage of arrows. In the darkness created by the arrows, no one could recognize anyone. Mayuresha then quickly caused the demon's horse to fall to the ground. Standing in the sky, the demon said to Gananayaka, "His horse has been felled; watch my great wonder. I will kill your peacock while you watch, O Lord of virtues." He threw the arrows from his quiver onto the ground, pulled his bow to his ear, and showered volleys of arrows. Killing all the soldiers in Mayuresha's army, he rained down arrows like clouds in the rainy season. Many gods had their limbs severed, and some gave up their lives. As the heroes were perishing, Mayuresha, filled with rage, abandoned the peacock, which had been pierced by Kamalasura's arrows. He took the noose in his hand, weighed it respectfully, and roared, making the sky resound. He released it, filled with radiance. It struck down the demon troops and burned the enemy soldiers. It fell upon the neck of the leader of the demon army and choked his breath. The demon assumed another form.

He covered the solar disc with his head instantly. In the deep darkness, nothing could be perceived. Standing face to face, filled with rage, he said to Mayuresha, "Why do you fight with me, boy? Go and drink your mother's milk. Amuse yourself with children, or else you will die before me. The three worlds tremble at my cry alone. When I place my foot on the ground, even Shesha moves below. By the blow of my fist, great mountains have been pulverized. With the tip of my nail, I will send your head to the netherworld."

The Sage said: Hearing the words of the foolish demon, the elephant-faced Lord said, "Why do you boast before me like a ghost or a drunkard? One who reviles gods and Brahmins never attains victory. If my anger were to arise, it would burn the three worlds. I fight full of joy only to make your fame known in the world; otherwise, with a single roar, you would have been taken to the abode of Yama."

The Sage said: Hearing those words, the demon, consumed by the fire of anger, made the intermediate directions and the main directions resound with the echo of his roar. At that moment, the pregnant women fell down, and some of the Pramatha ganas fainted and fell to the ground. Kamalasura pulled his bow to his ear and released a fierce shower of arrows once again upon Mayuresha and his army.

Chapter 103 : The Killing of Kamalasura

The King said: Seeing the prowess of that demon, the Lord (Mayuresha) became swift. He approached the demon and struck him with a shower of arrows. The demon also warded them off with his own swift arrows. Then, the Lord Mayuresha, the foremost of the virtuous, was pleased. He showed him His infinite universal form. Kamalasura saw Mayuresha in all ten directions. Astonished, he closed his eyes and saw Him within his heart. Then, as the king of demons tried to flee, the Lord caught him by his crest and said, "Stay and fight, O king of demons, and remember those words."

The demon again saw Him standing alone before him. He ran to fight, roaring with great strength. The Lord, wielding ten weapons, fought him then. The Lord of Obstacles pierced his body with the strike of a weapon. Wherever a drop of his blood fell, another demon was born, having the same form and strength, and thus countless demons appeared. They struck Him with various weapons and showers of arrows. Then, the Lord Mayuresha turned back, burning with rage.

At that time, Siddhi and Buddhi, along with three and a half million forces, arrived, and a great battle ensued. They asked the Lord of the Ganas, "Give us food to satisfy our hunger." The Lord told them, "Consume these many demons born from the blood of the demon." Upon being told this, they, along with the host of spirits, quickly devoured all those demons. The Lord struck the demon Kamalasura with His sword, but again, hundreds of demons were born from his blood. They devoured those too, drinking the blood from all sides.

Then, Mayuresha, weary, lifted His trident with speed and threw it at the demon. It went forth, burning the directions, crushing mountains, making the sky resound, and causing the stars to fall. It fell with speed and split the demon's body in two. His head fell on the southern bank of the Bhima River. The Lord of Virtues stayed on the northern bank of the Krishna River. Everyone was pleased and hailed Him with shouts of victory. "Victory to Lord Mayuresha, the destroyer of the wicked!"

Then, the Lord of Gauri (Shiva) arrived there, surrounded by the Ganas, accompanied by sages like Gautama and by Parvati. As all instruments played, a shower of flowers fell from the sky. Parvati embraced Him and joyfully offered Him milk. The sages praised Mayuresha, the Lord of Gods: "O heart of the beloved of Kamala, O increaser of joy in the heart, O destroyer of Kamalasura, O Lord whose feet are served by Kamala, O giver of wealth, O Lord worshipped by Brahma, O cool one like a lotus pond, O Lord whose lotus feet are marked with the lotus, O Lord whose hands are marked with the lotus, O ornament of the relatives of Kamala, O giver of wealth to devotees, O destroyer of the enemies of the son of Kamala, O beautiful one, O Lord adorned with the garland of the gems of the father of Kamala, O preventer of the arrows of Kamalasura with the lotus, O Lord whose lotus-like hands conquer the lotus buds, O Lord whose eyes resemble the lotus bud in the hand of the Lord of Kamala, O joy of the lotus of all hearts, O destroyer of all sins, O Lord whose lotus-like hand holds the goad, O destroyer of obstacles, victory to You! You have slain the sinner, the enemy who caused fear to Indra and others. He who was impenetrable even by the thunderbolt and discus, and who was a terror even to the sages, has been slain by You."

Having praised and worshipped Him thus, the great sages led by Gautama, and Girisha (Shiva) also, embraced Him with His ten arms and worshipped Him. Then, the sages said to Mayuresha and Shankara, "Stay here forever, accompanied by all the hosts of gods, fulfilling the tasks of devotees and warding off all accumulations of obstacles."

The King said: Being thus requested by them, the two Lords, accompanied by all the hosts of gods, fulfilling the desires of devotees and removing the accumulations of obstacles, stayed there—Mayuresha and Shankara, the benefactors of the world. Vishwakarma then built a beautiful temple there, with countless spires and doors, filled with countless wonders. A beautiful city was established there, inhabited by all beings. The great sages named it Mayureshapura. The groups of sages performed penance there, adorned with various ornaments. Hara also performed penance, accompanied by Girija and the Ganas. The twice-born (Brahmins) performed His meditation, worship, and remembrance. Mayuresha again played like a child, as He did before.

Chapter 104 : The Vision of the Universal Form

Brahma said: O best of the twice-born, I went in a hidden form to see Him. There, I beheld that Lord, the Supreme Being, accompanied by Uma. He was bathing, sitting, and chanting the eternal Supreme Brahman. I praised that Lord, known as Mayuresha. I bow to Mayuresha, the ancient Purusha, the Lord who playfully performs various acts, the master of Maya, who is difficult to comprehend. I bow to Mayuresha, who is beyond the beyond, pure bliss, immutable, residing in the heart, beyond the Gunas, and yet the embodiment of the Gunas. I bow to Mayuresha, the Lord who creates, sustains, and dissolves the universe by His own will, and who removes all obstacles. I bow to Mayuresha, who is praised day and night by hosts of gods like Indra, who is both manifest and unmanifest, being and non-being. I bow to Mayuresha, who destroys various demons, assumes various forms, and carries various weapons out of devotion. I bow to Mayuresha, the Lord who is the embodiment of all power, who assumes all forms, the all-pervading one, and the teacher of all knowledge. I bow to Mayuresha, the son of Parvati, the one who increases the joy of Shambhu, and who always brings joy to His devotees. I bow to Mayuresha, who is meditated upon by sages, praised by sages, and the fulfiller of the desires of sages, who is both the collective and the individual. I bow to Mayuresha, who destroys all ignorance, bestows all knowledge, is pure, and is the embodiment of truth and knowledge. I bow to Mayuresha, the Lord of millions of universes, the Lord of the world, the infinite, and the all-pervading Vishnu.

By seeing You, I became purified and filled with supreme bliss. I was struck with great wonder at the killing of that demon. Kamala-asura, who had forcibly conquered Indra, Yama, the gods, and the guardians of the directions, who fought with thousands of hundreds of warriors and possessed countless bodies, was killed in battle. He fell in three parts in three places. I worshipped him with auspicious water from all holy rivers. I bathed him with water from my Kamandalu, which destroys sins. I adorned him with divine garments, divine fragrances, and auspicious divine flowers. Placing a garland of forest flowers around his neck, I gave him the name Vanamali, which became famous in the worlds as the bringer of all auspiciousness. After completing the worship, I circumambulated him. At that moment, by the touch of my feet, he was instantly pressed down. My Kamandalu, filled with the water of various holy rivers, was placed there. As I tried to carry that water, the all-pervading Lord spoke to me.

Mayuresha said: This hymn composed by Brahma destroys all sins, grants all desires to men, and removes all calamities. It brings liberation to those in prison within seven days. It removes physical and mental ailments and grants both worldly enjoyment and liberation, O Creator. Remain steady, O Virinchi (Brahma). This river, which is the source of all holiness, will be known in the world by the name Kamandalu-bhava. By seeing it, verbal sins are destroyed; by touching it, mental sins are destroyed; and by bathing in it, all physical sins are destroyed. Constant service to it will grant liberation.

Brahma said: Having granted this boon, the great sages bathed there. Along with their wives and families, the seven-crore hosts and I, the sages, performed penance there with Him. Then, deluded by His Maya, I spoke many things. How could I have bowed to this child of eleven years, the creator of the world, worshipped by Indra and other gods, who is the Lord of the world? I saw Him playing like a child, devoid of any formal conduct. Fie upon my knowledge, my greatness, and my position as the Creator! I shall create this world and remain in a hidden form. If this is the Supreme Soul, He shall create another. Having decided this in my mind, I withdrew my creation and myself. Meanwhile, the Lord, the elephant-faced one, disappeared. He did not see his friends, the boys, so he went to their homes. Seeing the homes empty, he became distressed and looked for the trees. He did not see the trees, creatures, animals, or birds. Then the Lord went to heaven and did not see the gods there. He saw no Gandharvas, Kinnaras, Yakshas, Pitris, Moon, or Sun. In the deep darkness, nothing could be perceived. Then, by meditation, realizing what Brahma had done, the all-encompassing Lord manifested His own power and Maya.

Lord Mayuresha created the universe through His own play. He created the world, the immortal and mortal beings, and the city of Mayuresha. The all-pervading Lord saw the entire three worlds as before. He saw Himself surrounded by the boys, the universe as it was, Shankara, Bhavani, the sages engaged in rituals, the Sun, the Moon, the stars, the heavens, the hosts of gods, the earth, all trees, the oceans, the rivers, and the netherworlds, just as before.

Brahma said: Then, regaining my memory, I saw the Lord, the embodiment of consciousness, the one who is the form of time—moments, hours, days, and fortnights. Seeing Him thus, I, of noble mind, bowed and sought forgiveness. I saw Him as the embodiment of months, seasons, years, and aeons, the form of all moving and non-moving things. He was adorned with countless universes in His pores, the form of divine sages, Yakshas, Gandharvas, rivers, and oceans. He was the form of humans, Kinnaras, creepers, trees, and serpents. Seeing Him thus, I bowed and sought forgiveness. Again and again, I saw Mayuresha wearing a crown, adorned with earrings, anklets, and armlets. He had the serpent as his navel and neck, was covered in divine garlands and garments, sat on a divine throne, and was praised by all the gods. He was accompanied by Siddhi and Buddhi, worshipped with divine opulence, the embodiment of all moving and non-moving things, and worthy of worship. I saw myself in every form of His. Then, bowing, I praised Him and prayed again and again. Forgive my offense, O Lord, for I was proud of my Maya, desiring to see Your power, being poor and seeking refuge. Salutations to You, who creates infinite universes in a moment. As I was saying this, He drew me into His stomach through the breath of His nose. There, I saw the entire world just as I had seen it outside. I saw the Lord in that form, with millions of universes in His pores. I saw the entire moving and non-moving world, just as it was on earth and in heaven. From one egg, I entered another universe. I saw everything in the same way, amidst various eggs. I saw the entire universe and myself as the Lord. Then, distressed, I said to the Lord, "I do not see Your end anywhere. Have mercy on me, O Lord, and remove this Maya."

Then, filled with compassion, He made everything disappear in an instant. Once again, I saw Mayuresha surrounded by the boys. Playing as before, I bowed and said, "I did not understand Your Maya. Please forgive my thousand offenses as a mother forgives her child." Mayuresha placed His hand on my head and said:

The Lord said: I have no anger, no divided intellect, and no illusion of 'mine' or 'yours'. There is no fear for Me from anyone, nor is there even a trace of fear from Me. Hearing these words of His, I saw the auspicious city of Mayuresha, Bhavani, and her son with the sage-boys. Having taken leave, I went to my own abode, filled with joy. Bhavani, with the boys, went to her own temple.

Chapter 105 : The Removal of Vishvadeva’s Duality

The sage said: Then, on the fourth day of the bright fortnight of Bhadrapada, all the young students of the hermitage came to collect clay and brought back pure, fine earth. With their own intelligence, they quickly fashioned various beautiful idols—some mounted on lions, some on peacocks, and others on mice. These idols, representing Ganesha, were adorned with various weapons and divine ornaments, had four arms, and were dressed in divine garments with fragrant scents. With supreme devotion, they installed them in a pavilion illuminated by flowers and lamps, worshipping each one individually. They offered beautiful garlands made of lotus flowers, and the city was filled with joy amidst the sound of musical instruments.

On that birth anniversary, Gauri bathed her twelve-year-old child and gave gifts to the married women. After worshipping with devotion according to the rites, using turmeric and vermilion, a demon named Vishvadeva arrived. He appeared in the form of the Lord, marked with the divine signs of Vishnu—his body radiant with the conch, discus, mace, lotus, and a garland of forest flowers. He was adorned with a string of Tulsi beads, divine clothing, and sandalwood paste, carrying a water-filled kamandalu and a staff in his hands.

Gauri, seeing the guest standing at the door, asked with devotion, "Where do you come from, O Brahmin? I am pleased by your radiance." The Brahmin replied, "I have heard of what happened here in the past, and seeing it with my own eyes has filled my mind with satisfaction. I am afflicted by hunger and have come to your house." Parvati said, "Please sit and eat comfortably." He sat on the seat provided. Then Mayuresha (Ganesha) washed his feet with the pure water of the holy site and sprinkled it all around. The Brahmin sat silently, lost in thought, having performed the ritual of sipping water.

Uma then asked the Brahmin to eat, saying, "If this food is not to your liking, I will provide whatever you desire. Tell me, and by the power of Shankara, I shall bring it immediately." The Brahmin replied, "I eat daily only after seeing the Lord who resides in the Ocean of Milk. I drink the water from there, but due to delusion, I forgot to do so today." Uma said, "O best of Brahmins, why did you not state your rule earlier? How can you leave the plate now, you who disrupt the sanctity of the offering?" Shankara became agitated, not knowing what to do.

Mayuresha said, "If your heart is firm in its devotion, you will have the vision of Padmanabha (Vishnu) right here. It is a great misfortune that you would leave behind cooked food and pudding out of foolishness. How can you abandon the food served to you, which is obtained only through the merits of many births? You have seen Shiva, the mother of all worlds, who is not easily accessible even to Brahma and others. She is the supreme power of all gods, and through her vision, the vision of Lord Vishnu is attained. Just as a peacock does not enjoy the food of a chakora bird, why do you not enjoy this nectar-like food? Or perhaps, through the vision of me, who is the embodiment of all, you shall see Him."

The Brahmin replied, "I am a servant of the Lord of Lakshmi; I do not bow to anyone else. If you are the embodiment of all and possess such power, then show me the Lord of the Universe, Narayana, who is free from all ailments." Seeing his firm resolve, the Lord of the Peacocks (Mayuresha) disappeared and manifested in the form of Narayana—wearing yellow garments, holding the conch, discus, mace, and lotus, adorned with the Kaustubha gem and a forest garland, resting on the serpent bed, with his lotus feet being massaged by Mahalakshmi.

Seeing this, Vishvadeva bowed and worshipped him. He became absorbed in Him with devotion, forgetting his own physical existence. The Brahmin, filled with the bliss of consciousness, said, "Today my birth is blessed, my life is well-lived. This city is blessed, and my parents are blessed. Through the instruction of Mayuresha and the vision of Bhavani, I have attained the vision of the Supreme Vishnu, the indweller of all, right here." Narayana embraced his devotee with his arms and said with a voice choked with emotion, "I came from the Ocean of Milk, leaving my food for your sake. Seeing your firm and unwavering devotion, I clear the path of the devotee, removing the thorns raised by the winds of life. I have taken the form of water to quench the thirst of my devotee, and I take many forms to destroy the suffering of those who love me. I show you this to demonstrate how dear a devotee is to me."

On that Bhadrapada Chaturthi, the great festival of Mayuresha took place. The boys and sages worshipped the earthen idols—adorned with various colors and ornaments—mounted on peacocks, mice, and lions. Some sang, some danced with devotion, some recited the Puranas, and others circumambulated. At that time, Parashara, the grandson of the great sage Vashistha, arrived. Being only four years old, he fashioned an idol with great devotion. He made a garland of dried leaves and offered it to the idol, and with great reverence, he smeared it with mud, thinking it was sandalwood. He offered sweets, laddus, fruits, and money, placing them before the idol with leaves and mud. He played and danced for a while, crying out, "Eat, eat!"

After he prayed to the idol in many ways, it gained consciousness. Whatever he offered with a specific intention, the idol accepted and ate—the laddus, the sweets, and the pudding. Vishvadeva, seeing this, perceived the illusory as real. He saw the earthen food, flowers, and fruits become real. He saw the idol become conscious, six-armed, and then again four-armed, in the form of Vishnu. He asked, "O Lord, what is this eating of mud?" The idol replied, "Whatever is offered to me with devotion becomes like nectar. Even nectar offered without devotion becomes poison to me."

Hearing this, Vishvadeva asked again, "Who is your devotee? Show him to me, O Lord of the Gods." Mayuresha took his hand and led him outside. He showed him the Lord being worshipped in every house. He saw Mayuresha mounted on a peacock everywhere. He saw Him accompanied by Vishvadeva in every place. He realized, "This is not just my Lord; I must abandon this sense of duality." In every house, Vishvadeva saw Narayana. Everywhere he looked, he saw the Lord of Qualities being worshipped. Sometimes he saw Mayuresha in the form of Vishnu, and again he saw Mayuresha.

He walked from house to house, seeing the worship of Mayuresha with music and dance. Sometimes he saw the form of Vishnu and bowed, only to see the form of Mayuresha mounted on a peacock again. Elsewhere, he saw Him mounted on Garuda or on the serpent. Whenever Vishvadeva went to bow with joy, he saw Him mounted on a lion again and again—eating, playing, and sleeping. Distressed, Vishvadeva went to the hermitage of Vashistha. There, Mayuresha said, "Behold my best devotee." He saw Parashara standing there, worshipping the Lord with earthen offerings, and the Lord eating the earthen laddus. He saw the same Lord in the sky, in the water, and on the earth.

In an instant, he saw Lord Narayana, and in the next, Mayuresha. Thus, he abandoned his sense of duality and delusion. Bowing to Him with his whole being and praising Him with a sense of non-duality, Vishvadeva, having received permission, returned to his ashram with joy.

Thus ends the one hundred and fifth chapter of the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Removal of Vishvadeva’s Duality."

Chapter 106 : Mayuresha's Delight

The narrator said: Then, in the thirteenth year, after bowing to the great Lord Shiva—who is adorned with ash, possesses five faces, ten arms, and is auspicious, wearing a garland of skulls, sleeping, and bearing the moon on his forehead—Mayuresha took the moon from his forehead. While playing, he went outside, surrounded by children, friends, and companions who were dancing and calling out to one another.

At that moment, a demon named Mangala appeared there. He had a terrifying face, was shaped like a boar, was a great conqueror, and was astonishing. He was a powerful boar-demon who shattered trees with his tusks and tore through the earth with his diamond-hard bristles. He was dark like a mountain of collyrium, and his eyes resembled pits of fire. Seeing him, the children fled, for his voice sounded like the rumbling of rain clouds. They said, "We have never seen such a boar anywhere." While the demon was killing the children, the divine Mayuresha caught the wicked boar-demon with his tusks. With his other hand, he grabbed the demon's lower jaw and suddenly tore him apart like a piece of bamboo. The demon, who had assumed a massive form ten yojanas wide, fell, crushing trees and rending the earth. Then the children said, "Blessed is this son of Parvati. In an instant, he destroyed this mighty demon with ease. We were terrified by the sight of him and fled in all ten directions. As he fell, he crushed the houses here."

The narrator said: Then, when Shiva did not see the moon on his forehead, his eyes turned red with anger, as if he were about to burn the universe. He asked his attendants in a rage, "How is this protection being carried out? Which demon has taken the pure moon from my forehead?"

The narrator said: Then all the attendants, trembling and terrified, gathered together. Some, gathering courage, said to Shiva, "O Lord of Uma, your son, known as Mayuresha, came out to play, and the moon was seen in his hand. We do not know when he took it, O Lord." Hearing their words, the great Lord Shiva said in anger, "How can you, who are only interested in eating, provide protection? If the moon or the one who took the moon is brought here, it is well; otherwise, I will turn you all into ashes. There is no doubt about this."

Then, with disturbed minds, they ran in haste and approached Mayuresha, speaking with angry hearts: "Go, you wicked one, and give back the moon to Lord Shiva, you thief." Hearing the words of the attendants, the Lord of the Ganas became angry. "I have no account for you or for him, O Ganas. I am the son of the mother of the three worlds, and I am powerful."

The narrator said: With his breath, he blew them all away like leaves in a storm. The miserable attendants fell before Shiva. In great rage, the great Lord Shiva said to the Pramathas and others, "Bring that wicked-minded son of mine here immediately." They went quickly to where the child was playing. They saw him playing, fearless. Vinayaka, having deluded those who came to surround him, disappeared. The Ganas looked for him in all four directions. They did not see Vinayaka in any house or forest. When they saw him somewhere, they said, "How are you able to go before us? We will take you to Shiva, who resides in Brahmaloka." Thus, he appeared and disappeared again and again.

Then, seeing the distressed attendants, the Supreme Lord, filled with compassion, stood before them. Seeing him, they were filled with joy. They bound the son of Girija and took him to Shankara. But the Ganas were unable to lift him, as he had become heavy like the weight of the earth. Astonished and having failed in their efforts, all the Ganas went to Shiva and said, "O Shankara, we are all unable to bring him." Shiva ordered Nandi, who was standing in front: "Go quickly and bring that thief Mayuresha."

Nandi said: "By your command, I would destroy even Shesha, the Sun, and the Moon. To me, this little one is of no account, O Maheshwara."

The narrator said: Having said this, he went with the speed of the wind, breaking trees and mountains. With eyes red with anger and sharp horns, he approached Mayuresha and said, "Go, you, to Shiva, or else I will take you today. I am not equal to the Ganas." As he spoke, Mayuresha, filled with anger, threw him into a cycle of breath, causing him to spin in confusion. Exhausted by the breath, he released him near Shiva. Nandi fell to the earth, vomiting blood from his mouth, and remained unconscious for two muhurtas.

Then Shiva saw Mayuresha standing by his knee, shining with his body and adorned with divine ornaments. Seeing the moon on his forehead as before, the Ganas said to Shiva, "The moon is on your forehead, O Shiva; we were sent in vain." Looking at the moon on his head, Shiva spoke to Mayuresha and the Ganas.

Shiva said: "Those Ganas are exhausted, and so are you, Nandi, by my command. Since the moon is on my forehead, the battle was in vain."

The Pramathas said: "From this day forward, O Lord of gods, let the Lord of the Peacock be our master."

The narrator said: Shiva said, "So be it," and he became the Lord of the Ganas. After bowing to Shiva, Ganesha, and the mother of Ganesha, and praising the Lord of gods and Mayuresha, the Ganas roared and went to their respective homes with joy.

Thus ends the one hundred and sixth chapter of the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, entitled "The Taking of the Moon from Shiva's Forehead."

Chapter 107 : The Destruction of Indra's Sacrifice

Ka said: Then, in the fourteenth year, the sages led by Gautama, accompanied by various divine beings, arrived at the house of the daughter of the mountain (Parvati), where Shiva, surrounded by his various attendants, resided. After bowing to the Mother, who sat upon various seats, and worshipping her according to the scriptures, they spoke their hearts' desire. They said, "Fearing obstacles, the excellent place of Trisandhya has been abandoned. Even here, many obstacles have arisen for this child of yours. Please grant us either a place free from obstacles or a way to perform our duties."

The sages said: "O Goddess, if an Indra-yajna (sacrifice to Indra) is performed, he will remove all obstacles."

Ka said: Parvati, filled with joy and having obtained permission from Shambhu, had a large pavilion constructed and arranged all the materials necessary to please Shakra (Indra). Under the guidance of the great sages, she performed the Indra-yajna according to the prescribed rites. The sages performed the meditation and invocation of Indra, and at the behest of Parvati, they worshipped Indra and his attendants with all the necessary offerings. After installing the fire in the sacrificial pit with mantras, the learned Brahmins offered sesame and milk-rice into the fire, using one-tenth of the number of their japa recitations. While the Brahmins chanted the peace mantras from the four Vedas, using mantras beginning with Om and ending with Namah and Svaha, the child (Ganesha) was playing with other children.

At that moment, two demons named Kalavinkala, who were approaching the sacrificial ground, assumed the forms of buffaloes. They were massive, with great horns and terrifying roars that shook the three worlds. Their manes were like dark clouds, and as they moved, they tore through mountains with their tails and crushed trees with the wind from their nostrils. Intending to kill the son of Uma, they rushed toward him, fighting like two maddened great elephants. Their bellows sounded like thunder in the sky, and they fought until they were covered in blood. The young sages, terrified like sheep before wolves, fled. The son of Parvati saw the two great demons and, thinking how he might destroy these wicked beings, he grabbed them in the sky while the children watched. Holding them by their tails, he spun them around and around with great strength, and within a moment, they fell to the earth, crushing trees as they landed. Their bodies were torn into hundreds of pieces, and they were devoured by wolves and other scavengers. The young sages then approached him with devotion and praised him, saying, "Even Brahma and the sages do not know your true nature; how could we, who are mere mortals, understand you, the indweller of all? Since childhood, you have destroyed countless demons. These two came to kill you in the form of buffaloes, yet you cast them aside like mice, holding them by their tails with ease—you, whose very breath moves mountains and trees."

Ka said: Mayuresha (Ganesha) then went to the sacrificial ground and, seeing the Indra-yajna, became angry and destroyed it instantly. Casting the image of Indra far away, he asked the sages, "O pure ones, what is this work you are doing, surrounded by the sons of sages? What can Indra, who is himself a beggar of all things, give you? Can one obtain the fruit of the Kamadhenu (wish-fulfilling cow) by praying to a goat?" He took away the sacrificial materials and extinguished the fire. The sages, witnessing his restlessness, were astonished. With their hearts fixed on Uma, they could not scold, teach, or speak to him in anger. They told everything to Uma and returned to their homes, leaving it to her to decide what should happen to the disturbed Indra.

When Indra learned of his insult, he became furious. With eyes reddened by rage, as if burning the three worlds, he summoned the gods and said, "My sacrifice was begun by the sages with great devotion, but it has been destroyed by Gunesha (Ganesha). I shall see his strength today. If I am angry, the entire three worlds, moving and stationary, will be destroyed." The gods replied, "If you command us, we shall bind Mayuresha and bring him here in an instant." Indra said, "May there be no place for fire in the city of Mayuresha, nor anywhere else. By my command, may you disappear even from the stomachs of all beings."

Ka said: Hearing the words of the enraged Indra, the fire disappeared from the city and from the stomachs of all. The sages, unable to find fire to perform their rituals, ate their food raw, which caused them great distress. They went to the compassionate Mayuresha and told him that Indra had summoned the fire away and that even the digestive fire had vanished because of the destruction of the Indra-yajna. Upon hearing this, Mayuresha restored the fire in everyone's stomach, and those who were hungry were immediately satisfied; the fire also blazed in the sacrificial pits as before.

Then, the powerful and angry Indra sent the wind, and the entire city became like a graveyard as the five vital breaths (pranas) departed. The Lord, being the embodiment of the five pranas, restored them to life. Indra then told the Sun to scorch the city. The Sun, appearing in the form of the twelve Adityas, scorched everything, drying up the water in ponds, wells, and rivers. The entire earth, including villages and forests, began to burn. Then, Mayuresha quickly became a cloud and rained, extinguishing the fire and reviving those who had perished. The people, filled with joy, praised him, saying, "Well done, well done!"

Ka said: Having seen that everything he did to oppose Ganesha was thwarted, Indra went to the Lord, bowed to him, and with folded hands, began to praise the Lord of all. Indra said, "O Lord, neither the Vedas, nor the sages, nor even Brahma can reach your essence. You are the indweller of all, the embodiment of Brahman, and the cause of the creators of the world, including Brahma. Forgive my offenses, for I have taken refuge in you." Saying this, he placed his head at Ganesha's feet and prayed with devotion.

Ka said: Recognizing that Indra had abandoned his pride, the Lord of qualities said, "Rise, rise, may you be well. Return to your own abode and dwell in peace." Then, having worshipped and bowed to the Lord and circumambulated him, Shachipati (Indra) returned to Amaravati.

Chapter 108 : The Removal of the Pride of the Son of the Sun (Yama)

K said: Then, in his fifteenth year, Mayuresha went with the boys to bathe in the holy river named Brahmakamandalu. After bathing and completing his daily rituals, he worshipped the Lord of the Ganas residing in his heart with sixteen separate mental offerings. Some worshipped Him as seated on a lotus, others as seated on a peacock, using divine scents, divine garments, and divine flowers. The sage Gargya meditated upon Him in the temple and worshipped Him with jewels. Meanwhile, the door of the temple suddenly closed. As soon as they tried to open it, it became firmly locked. He and the boys began to cry out.

The boys said: You are our mother and father; why are you inside? O Lord of Gods, open the door and forgive our offenses.

K said: At that moment, a demon of great strength and prowess, whose loud roar made the three worlds tremble, arrived. He had the form of a tiger with a mane that scattered the clouds, and he approached with his mouth wide open, as if to swallow the three worlds. Seeing him, the boys ran in all ten directions, crying. The demon broke through the door and approached Mayuresha. Mayuresha instantly assumed a form like a tiger and confronted him. The tiger suddenly attacked, but Mayuresha clung to his back. Wandering about, the demon went into the forest and hid in a thicket. The Lord climbed a tree and dropped into the middle of the thicket. With both hands, He firmly gripped the demon’s mouth, and with his other hands, He brought him outside and cut him with an axe. He severed his nose, ears, feet, and tail, and telling him never to show his face again, the Lord of Virtues released him. The demon assumed his own form, which was now disfigured, and said to the Lord, "I shall do the same to your body." The demon went to his own abode, and the Lord returned to His own.

Then, the sons of the sages wandered about, crying, "Where is the Lord of the Peacocks?" Exhausted, they fell asleep under the shade of a tree. Mayuresha followed their path, thinking, "I shall see their lotus feet." Seeing them lying with their feet towards the south, the son of the Sun (Yama) became angry. With eyes reddened by rage, he dared to swallow the universe. He bound them and took them to his realm. Mayuresha then arrived at the riverbank and, not seeing the boys, was filled with great astonishment. He became deeply anxious; without them, the Lord of the Peacocks found no happiness anywhere. Then the groups of sages arrived.

The sages said: O Lord, where have you taken our boys this morning? Without them, our lives will not continue now that you have come.

K said: The Lord said nothing, shedding tears repeatedly. Reflecting, He went to the city of the Sun (Yama), having been informed by messengers.

The messengers said: Someone has come, wishing to fight, a destroyer of the three worlds, with a fierce roar and great arms, who is being forcibly stopped, O Yama.

Yama said: The universe turns to dust at the strike of my staff. How will you, a mere boy, fight before me?

The Lord said: You describe yourself, but you appear to me like a beggar, O Yama. By the movement of my brow, the entire universe perishes.

Having said this, Mayuresha climbed onto Yama’s shoulders. He knocked him off his buffalo, and he fell instantly. Realizing His prowess, Yama folded his hands and praised Mayuresha, the Lord of Gods, with great devotion.

Yama said: The gods, Brahma and others, and the sages like Sanaka do not know your form. The Vedas describe you, the Supreme Lord, as "Not this, not this." You are the creator, the sustainer, and the destroyer of creation by your own will. You are the destroyer of all wicked demons and the embodiment of all. Please forgive the offense I have committed unknowingly.

K said: Having said this, he worshipped Him with garments, jewels, and fruits. Bringing the boys and placing them in His hands, he stood before Him. Everyone embraced with joy and spoke to one another.

The boys said: We were exhausted from trying to see you, and Yama took us away in bonds. By seeing you, we are freed from bondage and shall return to our own homes.

K said: Then they reached their hermitage, led by Mayuresha. They went to Mayureshapura, which was filled with various musical instruments, adorned with many banners and flags, and decorated with garlands of flowers. Yama also followed behind to witness this great marvel. The sages also came to meet Him; they worshipped and praised the Lord and embraced the boys. The Lord spoke to all the gods and sages who had been angry earlier: "The boys were brought back from the world of Yama in a single moment."

The sages said: You protect us everywhere; you are always all-powerful.

K said: Having said this and taking His leave, the sages went to their hermitages. Yama also bowed to Him and happily went to his own abode. The Lord also reached His home and brought joy to His mother.

Thus ends the one hundred and eighth chapter of the Krida Khanda in the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Removal of the Pride of the Son of the Sun."

Chapter 109 : The Slaying of the Demons

Once, while Lord Shiva was sitting comfortably, Parvati said to him, "Fifteen years have passed, O Mahadeva. Now, the marriage of Mayuresha should be considered. A bride should be sought who is virtuous, beautiful, fawn-eyed, graceful in her gait, sweet-voiced, slender, and well-formed, suitable for one who is as handsome as Manmatha."

Hearing these words, Shiva said, "Well said," and began to contemplate a suitable bride in his mind. As he was deeply thinking, Narada arrived. Shiva honored him, offered him a seat, and said, "O Sage, I am delighted by your arrival. You have come after a long time; please stay for a day. O sinless one, you who travel through the three worlds, please search for a bride for my son, who is of incomparable beauty." Parvati also requested him to find a bride quickly.

Narada replied, "O Lord, I cannot stay even for two moments due to my duties. I have been sent here by Brahma for this very purpose. The Creator, wishing to bestow Siddhi and Buddhi upon your son, has recognized his power, beauty, and age. Even Anasuya and Shachi were ashamed by the beauty of these two. Even Sanjna, Ahalya, and Vrinda, the wife of Jalandhara—whose beauty once captivated Madhava—all appeared as trees upon seeing his form. O Gauri, no other beauty or valor in the three worlds can match his. Your son would shine through their union, just as a jewel shines with gold or a pearl with coral."

Hearing these words of Narada, Shiva and Parvati were pleased. Shiva immediately mounted his bull and summoned the gods, led by Indra, and the sages, led by Gautama. Taking Parvati on his side, he set out with great joy. Mayuresha led the way, mounted on his peacock. Narada, through the power of his penance, traveled through the sky. Seven crores of Ganas followed, playing and roaring with joy, carrying various weapons. As the instruments played and the sun was obscured by the dust, eighty-eight thousand sages also followed with joy.

As they intended to go to Gandaki-nagara, they saw a thicket of demons on the way, guarded by seven crore Ganas. The demons, hearing the loud noise of the army, woke up from their sleep, armed themselves, and went out to fight. The demons asked, "Who are you? Where are you going? Why have you come? We will not let you pass through our city without knowing who your master is."

Mayuresha replied, "We are independent, the destroyers of demons, and the protectors of the virtuous. Give us the path, or you will meet your destruction." Meanwhile, a deformed demon named Hema arrived. He told the Lord that he had been sent earlier and now, along with the other demons, he would take his life. He told the demons, "Many demons have been killed by force, and I, Vikrita, have been struck by him, so I will kill him quickly."

Hearing this, Mayuresha became angry and commanded the sons of the sages, "Fight by my grace." They joyfully took up blades of grass and prepared to fight. Shiva said from behind, "Let the boys kill them with the grass." The enchanted grass blades severed many heads, and the demons' weapons became blunt by his grace. The boys struck the demons with the grass, and they fell in pieces, with severed arms, thighs, and knees. The demons were astonished, wondering how they could be killed by mere blades of grass. Thus, all the demons were killed by the boys. They roared and bowed to Mayuresha, worshipping him.

The boys said, "By your command and grace, all the demons have been killed. Give us further orders, O Lord of virtues." The sages who had arrived were filled with great wonder to see all the demons killed by the boys with bundles of grass. Parvati descended from the bull and embraced him. Shiva said, "O Lord, your prowess has been seen today. Those who were once killed by weapons have now been slain by boys with grass. O son of Girija, your power is inaccessible even to Brahma and others. I do not even know what you will do next."

Chapter 110 : The Sending of the Envoy

Ka said: Mayuresha, filled with joy, moved forward, followed by the boys, and then Shiva, who was mounted on his bull. Gautama and the other sages followed them to ensure the success of the marriage, their voices roaring and echoing in all directions. Upon reaching the banks of the Gandaki river, Mayuresha descended from his peacock and sat upon a magnificent, high-value throne. After seating Shiva and the leaders of the Ganas and sages, and while all musical instruments were playing, the Lord of the Peacocks spoke. He said that the gods, led by Indra, were imprisoned by Sindhu, and without their release, the marriage could not be successfully concluded. Therefore, a wise and great person must be sent to Sindhu to negotiate their release. If he brings the gods back, then through peaceful means, everyone’s welfare will be ensured; otherwise, we shall have to defeat the mighty Sindhu in battle. Only after freeing the gods will the marriage take place.

Ka said: Hearing these words, everyone addressed the Lord of Virtues, saying that Mayuresha had spoken well, like nectar, and that even in his childhood, his wisdom was as auspicious and great as that of Brihaspati. They suggested sending the highly intelligent Pushpadanta, who was well-versed in the scriptures of policy, to Sindhu to secure the release of the gods, as he was a powerful orator and one who could please the Lord with his hymns.

Ka said: Pushpadanta then spoke to Mayuresha, the Lord of the Gods, saying that his glory was beyond the reach of mind and speech, and that those deluded by Maya could not perceive his eternal vision. He noted that Mayuresha had incarnated in the abode of Shiva to relieve the burden of the earth, and requested not to be sent, as he felt unable to face the mighty Sindhu, who was accompanied by an infinite army. He expressed that he would rather fight with the strength of his arms, for if the task could be accomplished through peace, then war would be considered futile; however, he believed in the eternal policy that if conciliation, bribery, and sowing dissension fail, only then should force be used. Hearing this, the Lord of Parvati replied that Pushpadanta’s understanding of ancient policy was correct, but noted that a man of anger is only capable of peace if he is truly powerful. He reasoned that if Shanmukha were sent, he would be too fierce; if Virabhadra were sent, he would only express anger; if Shringi were sent, he would bring destruction; if Pramatha were sent, he did not know what he might do; if the Lord of Ghosts were sent, he would only speak to the assembly; and if Raktalochana were sent, he would be preoccupied with the enjoyment of women.

Mayuresha then spoke, suggesting Nandi, who was as deep as the ocean, as steady as a mountain in his patience, as wise as Brihaspati, as strong as Indra, and clever enough to understand the intentions of others. He should be sent for the sake of peace. Shiva agreed, acknowledging Mayuresha’s wisdom in discerning virtues and faults, and ordered that clothes and various jewels be given to Nandi. Mayuresha bestowed these upon him according to Shiva’s command and ordered him to go to the mighty Sindhu and employ such diplomacy that the gods would be released. Nandi bowed to Mayuresha, Shiva, and Shivaa, praised all the Ganas, and with great intelligence, spoke words appropriate to the occasion.

Nandi said: One becomes great in this world only by your grace. Since you have chosen me for this task, I shall surely accomplish it. By your favor, I could easily turn the earth upside down; I have no doubt that I could bring Shesha and the Sun to your presence.

Ka said: Having said this, the courageous Nandi departed to meet the demon Sindhu. He reached the city gate with the speed of the mind, meditating on Ganesha, Shambhu, and Shivaa, who fulfills all desires, and prayed in his heart to fulfill the vow he had taken.

Thus ends the one hundred and tenth chapter of the Krida Khanda of the Sri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Sending of the Envoy."

Chapter 111 : The Description of Deliberation

Ka said: Announced by the gatekeepers, Nandi entered the magnificent assembly hall. Seeing the demon Sindhu in the assembly, Nandi felt no joy. He was surrounded by great warriors holding various weapons, entertained by dancing courtesans, and fanned by men with delight. As all kinds of instruments were played, filling the directions with sound, some who saw Nandi thought the sun had arrived. Seeing his massive form and great prowess, some trembled, while others roared, trying to intimidate him with their strength. Nandi, filled with great courage and proud of his own strength, felt no fear. He took an excellent seat indicated by a gesture of the hand. Everyone became motionless, like figures in a painting. Understanding their hesitation, Nandi, who was like the lord of speech, spoke.

Nandi said: I have seen many assemblies before, but never such foolish people in them. You are all very powerful and adorned with great splendor. You are handsome, like the god of love, but devoid of wisdom, like wolves. When a virtuous or wicked person, weak or strong, enters an assembly, they should be asked and honored; this is the eternal policy. I have not seen this in this assembly, and therefore my mind is astonished. All the members of the assembly, the ministers, and the citizens are in vain. This is not just the duty of the king, but the duty of the members as well.

Ka said: Hearing these words, Sindhu said to Nandi, O storehouse of virtues, your intellect appears like that of the lotus-born Brahma. Who are you, where have you come from, and for what purpose, O lord of bulls? You are like fire in brilliance and like a lion in prowess.

Nandi said: Know me to be Nandi, the son of Surabhi, who can assume any form, and the vehicle of the Lord of Girija, who shatters the cosmic egg. I am the destroyer of the demon Sindhu, who has descended into the house of Shiva since childhood to remove the burden of the earth and destroy the wicked. Even the lotus-born Brahma would be silent in describing his glory. Just as gems emerged from the churning of the ocean, today, after churning the policy, consider the success of the task. Listen to the powerful command sent by Mayuresha. Whoever disobeys his command will be destroyed, for he creates, protects, and destroys the universe, both moving and stationary. You have forcibly imprisoned the gods. You have attained their positions; what more fortune could there be? Now, considering the release of enmity, you should act. Release all the gods immediately by the command of my master. Shiva instantly fulfilled the boon granted to Tripura. Hari descended into a pillar and killed Hiranyakashipu. Tarakasura defeated the gods and stood in their place himself, but was killed by Skanda in battle in an instant. Therefore, give the gods to me and live here in peace for a long time.

Ka said: Hearing these words of Nandi, the demon king Sindhu became angry. With red eyes, as if vomiting fire, he spoke.

Sindhu said: I have seen your cleverness in many ways, O son of the bull. You do not even know the context, which even the lord of speech would know. Due to ignorance, the words of the intended meaning become useless. Because of the words of a child like you, or even the words of your master, why should I release the gods as long as he himself is not defeated? What is the authority of your words, you who wander like grass? Where is your Girisha and his son? This fear is being displayed in vain. If you have not come as an envoy, I will yoke you to a plow today. If my brow furrows in anger, O bull, the three worlds would perish; what is the value of those two? What harm can a jackal do to a lion or a serpent?

Ka said: Pierced by these verbal arrows, Nandi spoke in anger.

Nandi said: I perceive your intellect to be perverse today, O lowest of the gods. You are raving like one suffering from a fever, facing death. I was told to use conciliation first. Policy is wasted on you, just as instruction is on a wicked person. If you have used insults against Mayuresha and Shiva, your lifespan is waning, and you will not achieve victory in battle. I would kill you now, you fool, but I do not have the command of my master. A leader should not kill an envoy, nor should an envoy kill a leader. Having said this, Nandi blew away the demons with the wind of his breath. Without asking him further, he departed in great joy. Upon reaching the presence of the Lord Mayuresha instantly, the Lord saw him from afar and said that Nandi had returned. Prostrating, he told the Lord the entire account from the beginning.

Nandi said: I instructed the demon in many ways, but he did not accept my words. He insulted me in many ways, and I did the same to him. Like a drop in an overturned pot, everything is in vain with him.

Ka said: Hearing these words, Mayuresha, eager to crush the demon, rejoiced. He ordered the Pramathas and other Ganas to go to war. Now, eager to fight in the city of Sindhu, let them roar with heroic sounds to release the gods from bondage. By killing Sindhu and his army, they would release all the gods and the sages who are in a hurry to return to their own hermitages.

Chapter 112 : The Commencement of Sindhu's Battle

Ka said: The next day, Mayuresha mounted his peacock, Shikhindi, and taking four weapons in his lotus-like hands, he roared like a thundercloud and set out, desiring battle. Shiva also roared suddenly and set out, mounted on his bull. Seven crores of Ganas, fully armed, followed with great vigor. Then Nandi said, "You should not go suddenly. While the leaders of the Ganas, Virabhadra and I, are present, or while Pramatha is there, capable of destroying the enemies of the gods, let them see the servants go first, and then you may proceed to the great battle."

Ka said: Hearing these words of Nandi, the Lord was very pleased and said, "Well said, well said. The valor of the valiant Sindhu must be witnessed. Therefore, Nandi, you go ahead, and you all shall go with us." Then the Lord of Ghosts and the valiant Pushpadanta went ahead, followed by crores of Ganas, all shining with the splendor of valor, heroic and intoxicated with pride. Invading the ends of the directions, roaring loudly like clouds, and causing the earth to bend under the weight of the heads of Shesha, they spewed the fire of anger and reached the city of Gandaki. They fell upon the demon battalion, which was accompanied by ten crore demons.

Then a tumultuous and hair-raising battle ensued there with weapons, missiles, showers of arrows, bhindipalas, and battle-axes. Some had their heads severed, others their feet, hands, and thighs; some had their knees, shins, and ankles broken, and some lost their lives. Falling and rising again, they fought with their own and others. Thus, countless demons fell and died. Some fled and reported everything to Sindhu, who was sitting in the assembly hall with his heroic troops. Some, bleeding from weapon wounds, looked like blossoming Kimshuka flowers. Some arrived there and died, while others told him with courage, "Ten crore demons at the city gate have been turned back after fighting among themselves with weapons. When the battle with the breaker of mountains (Ganesha) occurred, he was victorious. Why are you sitting silently, O Sindhu? Do what is in your own interest. Gardens have been burned by enemies, as well as houses and gateways. In the darkness caused by dust, they have reached us with the fire of their radiance. Some were eating, some were sleeping, and some fled the city, leaving their enjoyments and taking their children. Some, with clothes disheveled, were burned by fire while in meditation. Even there, they met their end by the blows of various weapons. Seeing the conqueror of wealth (Ganesha) in all directions, the citizens were terrified, thinking that the dissolution had arrived and the entire city was being consumed."

Ka said: Hearing this from the messenger, Sindhu yawned, like a lion emerging from the king of mountains, as if about to devour deer. He said, "The army is seen to be irresistible; what will happen?" He shone with the fire of anger, as if swallowing the three worlds. "What valor will a hare show before a lion? What can a moth do to move Mount Meru, or a jackal?
