What can this boy, the king of peacocks, do to me, the embodiment of Time?" Saying this, he made the directions and sub-directions resound with his roar. He stood for a moment, anxious and silent, as if observing a vow of silence. Then the heroes, fainting with rage, said to the demon Sindhu, "Give the command, O Lord of Mayura; we will conquer in a moment. Through your prowess, we will bring the three worlds under our control right now."

Sindhu said, "Well said, O great heroes. My mind is also eager to see the enemy, so let the army fight here." Then all the great heroes, countless in number, armed with weapons and accompanied by a four-fold army, went forward roaring. The demon Sindhu, mounted on a horse, filled with anger and joy, spewing the fire of anger from his mouth and eyes, invincible in battle, set out with his horse, which was as fast as the mind and of great value, in the lead.

Chapter 113 : The Slaying of Mitra and Kaustubha

Ka said: The infantry marched forward, causing the earth to tremble. They were heroic, with terrifying faces and heads reddened by vermilion. Some wore armor and had disheveled hair, appearing extremely fierce; others had their waists bound, and some were smeared with sandalwood paste. Armed with various weapons and skilled in diverse forms of combat, they advanced. The intoxicated elephants moved forward like mountains adorned with minerals. Their long tusks shone, decorated with various ornaments, and the sound of their bells instantly deafened everyone. Crushing mountains and trees with the strikes of their trunks, they moved along, their temples glistening, adorned with banners and colorful flags.

Then the horses, crushing the earth with their hooves, made the sky and the ten directions resound with their neighing. Adorned with gold, jewels, and pearls, they were ridden by great warriors who shone in their armor. Carrying various weapons, these heroes were destroyers of enemy armies. Then the charioteers advanced, slaying foes with their missiles and weapons. Accompanied by excellent charioteers skilled in the ways of war, they wore garlands of pearls and gems and were fragrant with perfumes. With bows on their shoulders, quivers in place, spears in hand, and swords drawn, they blinded the eyes of the people with the brilliance of their weapons.

Thus the army arrived, making the ten directions roar. This army was seen by the lord of ghosts, Pushpadanta. Both Mayuresha and he went forward, but then fled in fear. They said: "By your command, we went ahead, and we destroyed the army stationed at the city gate and burned much of the city. Hearing of our prowess, Sindhu himself has arrived, bringing a four-fold army carrying various weapons. Out of fear of him, we have come to you, O mighty one."

Ka said: While they were speaking, the ocean-like army of Sindhu was seen in the eastern direction, formidable with warriors armed with weapons. Then, filled with joy, knowing the state of the demons, Mayuresha mounted Nilakantha and went forward. He took his four weapons in his four hands. The crores of Ganas, armed with bows, arrows, and swords, stood surrounding the Lord Nilakantha, who was seated on his bull. The great sages praised him and gave him their blessings.

There was a great uproar from the sound of instruments from both armies. Seeing the strength of the other, both armies were astonished. Seeing the countless forces, the lord of the peacock was pleased; everything that Nandi had said before was seen to be true. Then Nandi, bowing to Mayuresha, went forward roaring repeatedly. Moving through the sky, he severed the heads of the heroes in the armies. With a great strike of his horns, he struck the horse of the demon firmly. Quickly flying up, he returned to Mayuresha unharmed. Everyone was amazed, and the Ganas praised him, saying "Well done!" Nandi also caused the umbrella to fall with a strike of his horns. When the horse and the umbrella fell, Sindhu moved between the horses and held up another very valuable umbrella.

The demon considered the act of Nandi to be a great wonder. He became angry and shouted, "Where has your valor gone, you great warriors?" Hearing those words, Kaustubha and Mitra, the ministers, bowed to him and said with their four-fold army: "Do not worry, Sindhu; we will bring about the destruction of the enemy. Otherwise, we will never show our faces to you, Lord." Having said this, they set out, the destroyers of the enemy army. The hosts of infantry marched, intent on shattering the universe. Desiring to overturn the earth, they were maddened by the battle. Kaustubha went to strike Mayuresha, and Mitra went to strike him.

When they approached him, covering him with showers of arrows, Virabhadra and the six-faced one (Kartikeya) came to fight. Accompanied by countless troops, they roared, making the ten directions tremble, and struck Sindhu with various weapons in their rage. Kaustubha and Mitra struck each other, shouting, "Endure this! Die!" They fought with many missiles and weapons, with volleys of arrows, bhindipalas, iron clubs, and maces. The soldiers of both armies struck each other, desiring mutual destruction. When all weapons and arrows were exhausted, they engaged in wrestling, killing each other alive. Some fell lifeless, while others vomited much blood from their mouths. They struck foot with foot, shoulder with shoulder, back with back, and hand with hand. They struck head with head and elbow with elbow. They fell, were thrown down, died, were broken, and were crushed. With necks severed, arms broken, and legs and thighs shattered, the army was destroyed, and Sindhu's army achieved victory.

Praised by bards with shouts of victory and the sound of instruments, the ministers Mitra and Kaustubha went to the demon lord Sindhu. Virabhadra and the six-faced one came to Mayuresha. Then the sun set, and the battle ceased. The next day, the demons were pressed by the divine army. They struck the fleeing army with volleys of arrows. Virabhadra and the six-faced one, possessed by rage again, roared, making the sky and the ten directions resound. Striking the enemy army, they destroyed them like Death himself. In the deep darkness, nothing could be distinguished. The mighty warriors struck their own and others while fighting. When Sindhu's army was broken, they raised shouts of victory.

The demon ministers went again, determined to die. Both urged their horses toward the enemy army. Scattering volleys of arrows, they struck with their swords. Then the six-faced one, seeing their great prowess, killed them with a blow of his fist and quickly returned to his army. Mitra fell to the ground, vomiting much blood from his mouth. Seeing him in that state, Kaustubha came to fight. He struck the six-faced one in anger with his sword. When he fell into a swoon, Virabhadra also fought. Kaustubha struck him in anger with many arrow-strikes. He also struck him with his fist and knocked him to the ground. When he was killed, the great warrior Virabhadra rejoiced. The demon army fled when Mitra and Kaustubha were killed.

Those wounded by weapons, bleeding profusely, reported the news to the demon king Sindhu, the enemy of the gods. With a clear voice, full of pain from the wounds of weapons, they spoke, for he always desired the victory of Mitra and Kaustubha.

Thus ends the one hundred and thirteenth chapter of the Krida Khanda in the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled The Killing of Mitra and Kaustubha.

Chapter 114 : The Description of the Battle

The heroes said: Those two, Mitra and Kaustubha, who wielded various weapons, have been killed. They had slaughtered countless soldiers both day and night. Then, two heroic figures appeared from the enemy's forces and, being very powerful, they struck down and crushed our own army. Those two, by whose blows the soldiers were sent to the abode of Yama, were seen, but no one could identify who those two warriors were.

Sindhu said: Even Death, who takes away all beings, fears them when he sees them. How then, O soldiers, do you say they were killed by arrows? Now, all soldiers shall witness my prowess. I will undoubtedly make the enemy's head dance on the battlefield. I shall fight with the discus, for I am the son of the Discus-bearer (Vishnu). Having said this, the wicked one mounted his horse and went toward Mayuresha.

Countless crores of mighty demons marched forward, eager to fight the Lord of the Gods and the son of Shiva. Then, the mighty Nandi and Pushpadanta went to fight, along with the King of Ghosts, Vikata, and ten lakh warriors. Chapala also set out with half a lakh soldiers, and Shad-anana (Kartikeya) and Virabhadra, accompanied by countless troops, went forth, both being powerful in battle and wielding various weapons. Seeing the seven formations, they arrayed the demon army. Gandhasura, Madana, Kanta, Vira, Dhvaja, Mahakaya, and the seventh, Dhurta, led these seven formations and fought against those who had arrived. Covering them with a rain of arrows, they struck them with the tips of their spears. Some fell to the ground, struck by bhindipala weapons.

Shad-anana fought the powerful Gandhasura with force, while Virabhadra fought Madana-Kanta in the battle. Nandi and Viraraja fought each other. Dhvajasura and Pushpadanta fought with weapons and volleys of arrows, and the mighty-bodied King of Ghosts fought against them. Dhurta and Vikata engaged in a duel. Chapala, in his rage, struck Shardula, who fell to the ground. Regaining consciousness, Shardula struck him with a sword, causing Chapala to faint in the battle.

As the duel progressed, both armies fought, striking each other with volleys of weapons and arrows. They shouted, "Endure, strike, do not fear; if you are killed, you will go to heaven." When weapons and arrows were exhausted, they fought with kicks and fists. All the soldiers destroyed one another. Some grabbed their enemies by the feet and dashed them down. Some leaped and fell, crushing their opponents. Some severed heads, others arms in the battle. Some cut off feet, knees, and ankles. Others unleashed rains of arrows upon each other.

Bleeding from weapon wounds, they fought until death. Truncated bodies (kabandhas) fought there, striking both their own and the enemy. Others fell suddenly with torn chests. Some died in the arena, struck by mutual blows. They fought a heavenly battle for the sake of attaining a single nymph. Rivers of blood flowed there, with hair serving as moss. The sword was the fish, the shield the great turtle, making it difficult to cross. The corpses were the logs, and the yak-tail fans were the grass. The armor served as the decoration, the great battalions as the frogs, bringing joy to the heroes and increasing the terror of the cowards. The fat was the water, the flesh the mud.

When the sun set, nothing could be discerned. "We should not kill the gods moving in the demon army," and "We should not kill the demons moving in the god's army"—amidst such clamor, they fought each other. The wild beasts, ghosts, and demons gave their blessings there. Jackals, birds, and hawks were satisfied. Thus, a great battle raged day and night for three days.

Announcing victory at night with the sound of musical instruments, Gandhasura fought the commander extensively. Intoxicated by drinking, he abandoned his weapons and suddenly struck Shad-anana with his fists. He fell to the ground like a mountain struck by a thunderbolt. But Shad-anana, like a winged mountain, quickly regained his senses and struck the great demon with his twelve hands, releasing many volleys of arrows from his six bows. Gandhasura, with great speed, warded off the arrows and covered Shad-anana with his own. Shad-anana warded those off, caught him by the feet, whirled him around suddenly, and threw him to the ground. His body shattered into a hundred pieces, and he died from the heavy impact. When he was killed, his army fled in all ten directions.

Shad-anana pursued them, rebuking the army. Sena, Krodhana, and Shataghna came to fight, their roar like that of thundering clouds. They said to Shad-anana, "Wicked one, you have killed many of our army. Now go home, for the son of the Sun has been killed." Then they simultaneously covered Shad-anana, just as many powerful dogs attack a lion. Shad-anana struck them down with arrows released from his six bows. They fell unconscious but rose again after a moment. The three threw a noose around Skanda's neck and dragged him like an animal.

Seeing the six-faced one being led away, the three—Hiranyagarbha, the powerful, dark-complexioned one with red eyes—rushed forward. Struck by three fist blows, they fell to the ground. Then Madana-Kanta struck Virabhadra with a weapon, and he fell into a deep faint. Recovering, Virabhadra struck him down with a staff. Seeing Madana-Kanta dead, the powerful Viraraja went to kill those three powerful gods. Then Nandikeshwara, in his rage, struck them with his horns, and blood flowed from their mouths as they fell to the ground in a hundred pieces.

Seeing them killed, the four great demons—Shardula, Dhvajasura, Mahakaya, and Dhurta—came to kill Nandi, having defeated all gods in battle. They struck the powerful Nandi with various weapons. When Nandi fell, the four—Pushpadanta, the King of Ghosts, Vikata, and Chapala—who were maddened by the battle, picked up mountains, weighed them, and threw them. The heads of all were shattered, and some were crushed to dust. All of them fell to the ground in the battle, struck by arrows. Some, tormented by weapon wounds, fled to the sea.

They proclaimed the defeat of their own army and the victory of the gods. The divine army, having attained victory, played many instruments. "Mayuresha, Mayuresha, be victorious, always be victorious!" they cried out in joy and danced with delight.

Thus ends the one hundred and fourteenth chapter of the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, describing the victory of the army of Shri Ganesha.

Chapter 115 : The Defacement of Sindhu

Sage Ka said: Upon hearing of the defeat of his army, the demon Sindhu was filled with immense anxiety. With a deeply saddened face, he sank into an ocean of sorrow. His mind burning with distress, the demon king pondered.

Sindhu said: How did this reversal occur? I cannot understand it. Can an ant swallow the entire universe, filled with all its worlds? Before me, even gods like Indra were mere mosquitoes. How then has his army been defeated today by this child of Shiva?

Sage Ka said: Saying this, he mounted his horse, armed with a bow, arrows, sword, and shield. He declared that he would not show his face again until he had slain the Lord of the Peacock (Ganesha). With this resolve, he rushed to the battlefield like a moth to a flame. He strung his bow, making the sky and the directions resound, and released a volley of sharp, fire-tipped arrows. The earth trembled, and all living beings fainted. He showered the divine army with countless missiles. The gods were struck, torn apart, and fell, suffering from the impact of his weapons. Those who fled were struck in their backs by his arrows.

Soon, the entire army of the Lord of the Peacock fell into chaos. Some had their legs or heads severed. A fierce, inevitable, and terrifying battle ensued, with arrows raining from Sindhu’s hand. In the great, horrific din and the thick darkness caused by the dust, the soldiers could not distinguish between friend and foe, and many were struck down by their own side.

Then, Sindhu dismounted his horse and began to fight on foot. He suddenly struck Virabhadra with great force. He hit the powerful Nandin on the head, and with his sword, he swiftly killed the lord of the ghosts. He tore open the belly of Pushpadanta and, grabbing Hiranyagarbha by his hair, dragged him with great strength. He split the forehead of Shyamala with an arrow, and in his rage, he struck the jaw of Chapala. He pierced the throat of Lambakarna with a barrage of arrows and, grabbing Raktalochana by his feet, threw him to the ground. Sumukha barely escaped his hand, and in his shame, he struck Soma with a kick, felling him on the battlefield. He tore open the belly of Bhringi with a sword strike and split the head of Davanala with arrows. He struck the back of Panchanana in the battle. Thus, all the great heroes were struck down on the battlefield.

Others fled quickly, and Sindhu marched forward, roaring like a thundercloud, making the three worlds tremble. He pierced the Lord of the Peacock with many volleys of arrows. Seeing his terrifying and formidable form, Virupaksha and all the others fled. Ganesha, accompanied by the sages, fought in the battle. At the sight of the demon, the young elephant-god trembled like a lion cub. Then, in his rage, Sindhu spoke to the Lord of Virtues.

Sindhu said: O child of Shiva, I have heard much of your prowess from afar, but now I see you in person, as weak as a jackal. How can you, a fool, withstand the blow of my fist that can shatter mountains? You should be drinking your mother’s milk and dancing in the courtyard of your home. What is your worth, you who have defeated Indras and others? My mercy, which is eager to kill you, is the only thing holding me back. How can I shoot sharp arrows at your delicate limbs?

The God (Ganesha) said: Why do you boast, you base and wretched fool? If I did not know that you possess a small body, I would not have taken this incarnation. I shall destroy your petty body in an instant. Because of the boon given by the Sun, you have become addicted to evil deeds. Now, that time has passed, and the hour of your death has arrived. What kind of battle will you fight today, you who are skilled only in boasting? I have taken this form to kill you and liberate the gods. When the end is near, all human effort is in vain. By being killed by me, you will attain my supreme and rare abode.

Sindhu said: As long as I have not severed your delicate and excellent body, you may boast. Whoever is a devotee of someone, he goes to that person when he dies. Your talk of your own praise is in vain.

Sage Ka said: Having said this, the great demon took an arrow that could pierce anything—a weapon never before revealed, extremely sharp, and victorious. Remembering the Sun, he took his bow, strung it, and set the arrow. The twang of the bowstring made the three worlds resound. The wicked demon forcefully released the arrow at the Lord of the Peacock. It set all the directions and the sky ablaze. Seeing this, the Lord of the Peacock released his own axe. It was harder than a thunderbolt, the destroyer of all enemy pride, and it burned like the fire of universal dissolution. The axe shattered the arrow in the sky and, falling to the earth, severed the demon’s hand. The heavy bow fell from his hand while still in the air. Then, in anger, the demon threw his discus, which roared like thunder and made the directions echo. Seeing this, the Lord of the Peacock released his fierce trident. It burned the world and fell upon the demon’s head, shattering his crown and earrings before returning. 

The demon, his ear severed, spoke in anger: I have shown my prowess today, and now I shall show yours. With this arrow strike, I will cut off your nose. Saying this, sword in hand, he rushed toward the Lord of Virtues. Ganesha then manifested various forms all around, holding four weapons in his four hands. Sindhu, filled with wonder, looked in all ten directions and saw the God adorned with four weapons. Ashamed, he decided to go to his own home. Yet, wherever he looked, he saw the Lord of the Peacock in that same form. He covered his eyes and fled, but he still saw Him in his heart. Opening his eyes, he saw Him standing before him in the city. Then, by the grace of the Sun, the demon realized the truth. He fled from the battlefield to his city, covering his lotus-like face, surrounded by the few remaining heroes. He fell onto his bed, his mind overwhelmed with anxiety. The others who had fled also reached their homes, their hearts troubled by worry.

Chapter 116 : The Gods Take Refuge Under Mayuresha's Lordship

Lord K said: When Mayuresha achieved victory, the great sages went there. Shiva, accompanied by Parvati, also went to see him. Out of affection, Parvati embraced him and spoke.

Parvati said: O son, you are extremely exhausted from the battle with the formidable demon. How did your delicate limbs endure the weapons? The demon was like the God of Death, illusory and very powerful. How did you, with your delicate limbs, defeat him in battle? The Goddess wept pitifully while holding her child to her body. Then, the groups of sages and Shambhu (Shiva) restrained her.

Shiva said: You do not know Mayuresha, who is the cause of all causes. He is the beginningless and endless Lord, beyond the reach of Vedanta, the all-pervading one. He is praised by the thirty-three crore gods and is the destroyer of the enemies of the gods. He is the cause of creation, sustenance, and dissolution, the auspicious one. He assumes various forms to lift the burden of the earth. His hair pores contain the entire universe; he is both one and many. He is endowed with the power of penance and is the essence of Yoga. He is the destroyer of illusion, the master of all illusionists. Do you not know that even in his childhood, he is the slayer of demons?

Lord K said: Hearing this praise, Parvati was delighted. Then, a joyful Mayuresha spoke to the sages, Shiva, and Parvati.

Mayuresha said: I have no fear of Indra (the wielder of the thunderbolt) or anyone else; how could I? Mother, I have the blessings of all the sages and the obeisance of all. By the grace of Shiva, I have defeated the Sindhudaitya. Those who were defeated by Skanda and others are not able to fight against me. Countless gods have been killed by the demon army, and rivers of blood have flowed, bringing joy to the heroes. The great sages are searching for the gods who fell in battle.

Lord K said: Hearing these words of Mayuresha, the righteous sage Vashistha said, "Let us all go." Then, the Lord of the Peacock (Mayuresha), along with the sages, went to see the battlefield. The smell of blood and fat was unbearable. Seeing the bodies, torn and broken, looking like blooming Palash flowers, the minds of those who hated the demons were filled with disgust. They saw the heaps of heroes lying on the battlefield—some alive, some unconscious from weapon wounds, and some with limbs scattered. The gods were pulling the bodies of other gods, and in the confusion, they could not distinguish between gods and demons. Some said, "We came to die for your sake; having seen you at the end, we now depart," and saying this, they gave up their lives.

Lord K said: Hearing these words, the Lord of the Peacock, with a tearful throat, wandered about and saw the six-faced Skanda unconscious. Tears falling, Mayuresha said with affection, "Arise, arise, may you be well! Why do you lie like an ordinary person? You are exhausted from killing the demon; accept my embrace now." He wiped him with his hand, and Skanda stood up. He saw the lotus feet of the Lord nearby, which dispel all fear, and said to Mayuresha, "By your sight, all my sorrow and fatigue have vanished, and I am now rested." He embraced Mayuresha with his twelve arms.

Both went to see the battlefield, holding each other. They saw Virabhadra lying unconscious, wounded by arrows. Nandi was severely pained by arrow wounds, and the Lord of Ghosts (Bhutaraja) had his head severed. Vikata was crying, and Pushpadanta was deeply distressed. Hiranyagarbha was pierced deeply in the forehead. Some were near death, some were restless, and some were dead. Lambakarna, in his confusion, could not recognize anyone in the battle. Sumukha was overcome by the upward-moving breath (Udana Vayu). Soma was in great distress with bloodshot eyes. Bhringi lay unconscious, and Panchasya was near death. Shankha had attained the state of a ghost, causing fear to all warriors. Seeing all the heroes either dead or near death, Mayuresha, filled with great concern, asked the six-faced Skanda.

Mayuresha said: The demons and our own great heroes have been killed in battle. They have all gone to their own abodes; what is the fate of those who are alive here?

Skanda said: You are the Lord of the infinite universes and the master of all qualities. You are the repository of all fourteen branches of knowledge. Why do you ask me about these arts? I do not know the mantra; you know it, not the gods. You increase your glory by destroying various demons. I speak to you according to my understanding and by the power of memory. Previously, during the destruction of Tripura, Shiva fought a wonderful battle. He brought the gods who had died back to life instantly by applying the juice of the herbs from the Drona mountain to their wounds.

Lord K said: Hearing this, the Lord spoke to the six-faced Skanda. "Now, crores of powerful demons will come for battle. Who will fight them, O six-faced one? Who will go to bring the excellent herb juice from the mountain?" Saying this, Mayuresha manifested his illusion. He brought them back to life by the touch of the air from his own body. Then, filled with joy, they bowed to the Lord of Qualities. They embraced him and expressed their desire to fight again. "By the fall of your gaze, our strength has doubled."

The Lord of Qualities said: Brahma and the groups of gods praise your valor. The demons led by Tarakasura have been killed.

Lord K said: Then, accompanied by those gods, Mayuresha went to Shiva. After bowing to Shiva and Gauri, he prepared to go to battle. Gauri joyfully embraced him, and Shiva embraced the Lord of Qualities. Then, Skanda and the others joyfully embraced Shiva. "O Hara, we fell in battle and were brought back to life by the air from Mayuresha's body, which performs wonders. We will go to fight again with him now. By your grace, we will defeat all the demons in battle."

Thus ends the 116th chapter named "The Investigation of the Battlefield" in the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana.

Chapter 117 : Durga's Instruction to Sindhu

Ka said: At that time, Sindhu, lying on his bed, was filled with great anxiety. His heart was scorched by fire, and he could find no peace in his thoughts. Devoid of joy, his face withered, lacking luster, and feeling incomplete, he lay there. Then, Durga, the daughter of Ugra, a wave of beauty and auspiciousness, hearing of him lying on his bed, consumed by the poison of anxiety, approached him. Her hair was adorned with countless jewels, and a musk tilak shone beautifully upon her forehead. She wore a necklace around her neck and a jeweled girdle upon her waist. Observing her, adorned with all ornaments and beautiful in every limb, arriving eagerly near his bed, the servants departed. Then, she spoke to her husband.

Durga said: Why are you worried, my lord? Whatever is destined to happen will happen. The world is under the control of God and is never independent. Why do you distress the minds of everyone, including mine? Tell me, O Lord, what is the cause, and I will suggest a solution to you.

Ka said: Hearing these words from his beloved, the lord of Gandaki became attentive and told her the whole story.

Sindhu said: What shall I say, my beloved? It is a matter of great sorrow. In the battle, my ears were severed by Mayuresha. My army of seven crores was destroyed by his prowess. Great heroes like Skanda fell under the rain of arrows. Suddenly, my ears were taken away by the enemy, who sent a trident. I have come to my palace covering my face with a cloth. Tell me the means by which I can kill my enemy.

Durga said: You have performed the duty of a warrior, and the soldiers have been killed. But, my lord, one does not attain glory by being an enemy of cows, Brahmanas, and gods. No one attains well-being through such enmity. Through service, worship, meditation, remembrance, and adoration, the gods, including Indra, attained their stable positions. He who is equal to all beings and grants the fruits of good and evil deeds—through His service, one attains the fulfillment of desires, just as with the Kamadhenu. A sprout grows according to the seed that is sown. Sorrow comes from evil deeds, and happiness from good deeds. Therefore, the wise always perform good deeds with devotion. One should do what is beneficial for all creatures in body, mind, and speech. You have tormented the divine sages for the sake of your own selfish desires. A true goal is that which fulfills the four aims of life. A mind that is not greedy for wealth and never desires another’s wife—that is the true goal. He who does not criticize the blameless, is firm in protecting those who seek refuge, is devoted to righteousness, and is equal to all beings—that is known as the true goal. My lord, listen to my supreme advice, which is beneficial to you. Be of pure speech, truthful, humble about your own virtues, devoted to helping others, and free from backbiting. Do this, O fortunate one, if you desire my love. Release all the gods, including Indra, whom you have imprisoned. Mayuresha, the protector of the entire world, will then depart, and we shall live happily, my lord. Otherwise, there will be no happiness.

Ka said: This nectar-like speech of hers became like poison to him, just as medicine is to one who is dying or suffering from a chronic disease. With eyes reddened by anger, Sindhu spoke to Durga. He did not hold her beneficial advice in his heart.

Sindhu said: You have spoken well, O lady, but your words bring shame to the world. I have understood your cleverness in distinguishing between what should and should not be done. No one embraces an enemy, and an enemy is one who is proud. One should not start an unjust act, but if it is started, one should not abandon it. Whether there is sorrow or happiness, infamy or fame, gain or loss, life or death—it is all the same. Previously, O lady, I did not make peace with him through conciliation. Now, whatever is to happen will happen in every way. Whatever is written for a creature, whether good or bad, no one has the power to change it through personal effort. Dying in battle leads to heaven and fame in the three worlds, while seeking refuge in the enemy leads to infamy and hell. There is no doubt that one meets the same fate as his ancestors. I know that Mayuresha is the God of gods and the teacher of the world. He has appeared for my destruction, just as Rama appeared for Ravana. I am determined to cut off his head. Heroes abandon their lives, but never their pride. I do not care for death at all, O beautiful one. What then is the count of others? I have been shamed by the loss of my ears.

Ka said: Having said this, he donned his ornaments and garments. He wore armlets, a jeweled crown, a necklace, and earrings. He took his quivers, sword, shield, bow, and weapons. Covering his ears with a golden cloth and a turban, he sat upon his excellent throne.

Chapter 118 : The Killing of Kala and Vikala

The King said: The demon king Sindhu, overwhelmed by the fervor of war, sat upon his great throne and addressed the foremost warriors who were seated before him. Sindhu said: My two chief warriors, who were capable of attracting the three worlds, are gone. There is no such hero here who could forcibly slay my enemy. There are no others as wise or as eager for victory in battle as they were. The warriors then said to Sindhu: Do not worry at all. As long as we are alive and ready, why do you, O King of demons, fall into such anxiety? Rest comfortably on your couch, O fortunate one. Why should there be any fear from those who have already struck down the God of Death?

Having said this, the two mighty brothers-in-law of the demon king, Kala and Vikala, who were intoxicated by the pride of battle, set out. They were skilled in various forms of combat, carried many types of weapons, were adorned with diverse ornaments and garments, and were accompanied by numerous armies. They filled the sky with their various roars and shouts. They were slayers of millions of gods and were followed by countless troops. The heroes marched forward with heads reddened like vermilion, carrying fire-weapons, with loose hair, and anointed with sandalwood paste. All of them carried shining weapons, ready as if to challenge Yama himself. Then came the elephant divisions, decorated with various minerals, led by great mahouts, adorned with many ornaments, having sharp tusks, painted with vermilion, and resounding with the ringing of bells. Then came the cavalry, shining in their armor, carrying bows and arrows, and decorated with glistening umbrellas. After that came chariots equipped for various battles, carrying many heroes and driven by skilled charioteers.

Thus, the two brothers-in-law, filled with rage, went to the battlefield. They saw the Lord of Mayura (Ganesha) seated upon his vehicle, the bull, surrounded by heroes like Shanmukha (Kartikeya) and others. Seeing their terrifying four-fold army and the countless, diverse forces that had arrived, the gods sent a messenger skilled in diplomacy. Having learned the situation, he returned and spoke to the gods. The messenger said: Kala and Vikala, accompanied by various armies and skilled in many forms of combat, have come to defeat the gods.

Pushpadanta and the Bull arrived to fight them. Having readied their bows, they pierced the demons with sixty arrows. The demons, warding off the arrows with a rain of their own, covered the divine soldiers and began to slaughter them. The great Bull split Kala’s sword with his own sword. Taking his army, he quickly cut the sword and made it fall. Pushpadanta severed Vikala’s bow with his arrows. Then the two fought each other with wrestling. The two armies fought there with various weapons and projectiles. Using diverse illusions, each desiring to defeat the other, the demon army was severely struck by weapons. Some gods followed from behind, killing those demons. The soldiers of the demon army were struck down by kicks. A river of blood flowed there, terrifying to the timid, a great and horrific scene that delighted the ghosts and birds.

Then, Kala and Vikala flew up with great speed and, intoxicated by the battle, unleashed a fierce rain of weapons. The divine army, struck by this, bled and fell. Then the Bull struck their army repeatedly with his horns. He struck them quickly with his legs and knocked them down with his snorts. With a blow of his tail, he crushed some of the demon leaders. Thus, the army filled with various demons was struck by him. There was a great cry of distress among the demons as they perished. Then the two mighty heroes, Kala and Vikala, roared in anger, making the ten directions tremble. They mocked the bull, saying, "Why do you fight before us? You were Indra among the blades of grass, but now you are like a blade of grass yourself." Having said this, the two mighty ones grabbed his horns and tried to drag him toward their four-fold army. Pushpadanta rushed forward with speed to free him. Then Vikala knocked him to the ground with kicks and roared with joy, accompanied by the sound of musical instruments. After a moment, he regained consciousness, unhurt.

Then Virabhadra and Shanmukha arrived to fight. Kala fell to the ground, shattered into a hundred pieces by a mountain-blow. Virabhadra knocked Vikala down with a blow of his palm. Seeing Kala the demon slain by Skanda with a great mountain, the victorious Ganas returned to their camp. Some who were struck by weapons but still alive fled to the Gandaki River. They reported to the demon king that Kala and Vikala had been killed in anger by Virabhadra and the other Ganas. The victorious divine Ganas then returned to their camp.

Chapter 119 : The Slaying of Sindhu's Sons

Ka said: Having heard that Kala and Vikala, his brothers-in-law, were slain by Virabhadra and the mountain-like great demon was killed by the six-faced Lord (Kartikeya) with a palm blow, Sindhu, seated on his throne and surrounded by various warriors, fell into great anxiety. Remembering the son of Shiva, he thought, "What will that God do? In this way, the enemies of the gods are being destroyed." Overcome by such thoughts, he fell into a deep swoon. At that moment, his two mighty sons, Dharma and Adharma, arrived and spoke to their father on the throne: "Father, why do you fear that small child of Shiva? Do not show weakness, O Lord of Daityas; we shall kill him in an instant. Our uncles, who stood before us, have gone to heaven, having achieved great fame in the service of their master after killing many armies protected by various warriors. We too shall fight; we shall go and kill the enemy by force, or if we are killed by him in battle, we shall attain liberation. As long as we are alive, do not worry. The gods like Indra are currently in your prison. When you conquered them before, who were their protectors? We shall surely bring you the head of that enemy; otherwise, we shall never show our faces in the world again."

Ka said: Having heard their words, Sindhu was filled with joy. He said to them, "Engage in supreme battle and attain great glory. Destroy the terrible enemy and return to me quickly." Hearing this, they set out, eager for battle. After bowing to their father's feet and receiving blessings from their mother, they each took a massive army of ten million soldiers, equipped with chariots, horses, elephants, infantry, and various weapons. The foot soldiers, with their heads reddened by vermilion, marched joyfully. The elephants, resembling various mountains, were adorned with banners of different metals. The horses were mounted by riders holding swords and various weapons, roaring like clouds and causing the ten directions to resound.

The two brothers shone in the midst of the army, adorned with various ornaments and wielding diverse weapons. They were resplendent with gold and jewel-studded earrings and brilliant crowns, possessing great strength. Wearing necklaces of pearls and gems, and intoxicated with the pride of battle, they reached the battlefield to the sound of various musical instruments. Virabhadra and others saw them and their army. Seeing them nearby, urged by Mayuresha (Kartikeya), Virabhadra and the six-faced Lord advanced. Hiranyagarbha (Brahma), the Lord of beings, became enraged, and a battle ensued with countless soldiers. The warriors fought fiercely, severing heads, legs, arms, thighs, and bellies. As the sun was covered by darkness, the slain were being killed. Some threw chakras, others bhindipalas, some rained arrows, and others used various weapons. A wrestling match also took place between the soldiers.

In this way, the army protected by Mayuresha destroyed the enemy forces. Those who remained fled in the ten directions in confusion. Then, the six-faced Lord, enraged and adorned with twelve weapons, struck the demon's army. Some had their arms severed, some were cut in two, and some had their heads split on the battlefield. Thus, the army filled with elephants, horses, and chariots was destroyed. Some went to heaven, while others attained the form of the six-faced Lord. Those who looked at Mayuresha and were struck down attained their own divine abode by his grace. By his mere glance, all their sins born of birth were destroyed.

Seeing his army destroyed by the arrows of Vishakha (Kartikeya), the two heroic sons, Dharma and Adharma, fought in many ways with weapons, missiles, and showers of arrows. They immediately engaged in a wrestling match with the great commander. Seeing their strength, the mighty Kartikeya, with his six faces and six arms, forcibly caught them by their hair. He whirled them around many times and dashed them to the ground. Upon falling to the earth, their bodies shattered into a hundred pieces. When the son of Shiva achieved victory, musical instruments played everywhere. All the soldiers chanted, "Victory to you, O Mayuresha!" The four of them went to their Lord, Mayuresha, and embraced each other with joy. They rejoiced, feeling as if they had been reborn. They reported the entire account to Shiva and Shivaa (Parvati), telling them of the destruction of Sindhu's army and his two mighty sons.

Chapter 120 : The Dialogue between the Father and Sindhu

K said: The few remaining heroes, bleeding and deeply distressed, approached Sindhu in the assembly and spoke in great fear.

The heroes said: O archer, listen to these words that bring great sorrow. Your sons, Dharma and Adharma, after fighting a great battle, sent the divine army to the abode of Yama by killing them repeatedly. Then, a great hero, radiant with six faces, appeared. He too was felled to the ground by them in that fierce battle. But then, that hero caught both of them by their hair, whirled them around many times, and threw them to the ground. Their bodies shattered into hundreds of pieces, and they departed to the highest heaven.

K said: Hearing these words, the lord of the Daityas fell down. He was plunged into an ocean of grief, like a mountain struck by a thunderbolt. His friends rushed to him and forcibly lifted him up. After a moment, he regained his senses and lamented with great sorrow.

Sindhu said: How could those two mighty heroes be killed by the six-faced one, when they had previously defeated even Indra and the guardians of the world in battle? Leaving me behind in the mortal world, how did both of them go to heaven? They were the slayers of the divine army and the destroyers of Death himself. If I do not bring the head of the enemy, I shall not show my face. When they were leaving, they promised me victory; why did you not fulfill that?

At that moment, the companions of Durga, weeping in great distress, told the news in the inner chambers in detail.

The companion said: O fair-browed one, your husband is weeping because Dharma and Adharma are dead. Upon hearing this, she fell from her couch onto the ground, like a delicate red-leafed creeper struck by the wind. Wounded in her heart by the cruel words of her friend as if by arrows, her hair and ornaments disheveled, she wept bitterly. Her clothes were disarrayed, her beautiful breasts were heaving, her cheeks were darkened by tears, and her radiance was destroyed by the fire of grief. She struck her face with her hands, her face stained with blood, and she became disoriented and shameless. She rolled on the floor of the assembly hall like a tantrum-throwing child. All the noble members of the assembly wept, overcome by grief. With tears flowing from her eyes, the Queen spoke to the assembly.

The Queen said: While all the heroes were alive, why were my two tender sons sent to the battlefield without asking me? If I had given them my blessing, they would not have died. The Creator does not wish to make my words false. How could they, who defeated the hosts of gods, meet their end? Where shall I see them, who conquered the God of Love with their beauty? How could they, who were the very essence of wisdom, leave me and go? Burned by the fire of grief for them, I shall die this very day. Saying this, she fell to the ground, striking her heart repeatedly.

Then the companions and the citizens comforted her, saying: O Mother, no one who has died ever returns. In the mortal world, no one has been seen or heard of who stays forever, except for Hanuman, Kripa, Bali, Vyasa, Parashurama, Vibhishana, and Ashwatthama; no one else is eternal, nor will there be. Even Brahma and others must face death, so what is the count of a mere soul? If death were not to happen, then one should weep. When the debt of karma is exhausted, a wife, son, or cattle do not remain; therefore, grief is in vain. Just as wood floating in a river is separated by the current, so a living being is forced into union and separation. Just as flocks of birds gather on one tree at night and depart in ten directions in the morning, what is there to lament?

K said: Thus comforted, Durga, Sindhu, and the people restrained their grief and sat down, fearing that their enemies would mock them if they wept. The people forcibly took Durga to the inner chambers. Sighing, she fell onto her bed, overcome by sorrow.

Sindhu, filled with anger, took up his weapons. To avenge his sons, he mounted his horse and went to the battlefield. As he departed, the great four-fold army, eager for battle, followed. The infantry went first, running and flying. They were of various colors, uniform in appearance, and armed with various weapons. Then came the herds of elephants, adorned with various minerals, decorated with bells and ornaments, dripping with rut, and terrifying with their roars. Then came the horses, swift as the wind, striking sparks from the ground with their hooves. The cavalry looked splendid, holding shields, spears, and swords, their bodies smeared with sandalwood and camphor, adorned with garlands, wearing armor, and their heads shining with helmets. They seemed to swallow the sky, making the ten directions resound. Then came the chariots, occupied by various great heroes, filled with weapons, bows, and quivers.

In the midst of them, Sindhu shone, wearing a great crown and earrings, holding various weapons and bows, adorned with a quiver, wearing heroic bracelets and armlets, his eyes red with anger, desiring to consume the three worlds. While all instruments were playing and the bards were singing his praises, a messenger said to him as he moved with great speed: "O Lord of the Daityas, your father is coming; wait for him, O virtuous one."

As he turned back to look, he saw his son approaching on horseback. He bowed to him, and the father embraced his son, having gathered his troops. He gave him advice for his happiness in this world and the next.

Chakrapani (Sindhu's father) said: O son, you act with pride and are intoxicated by wealth; you do not understand. One who desires prosperity should ask the wise elders. One should not perform an inauspicious act when desiring auspiciousness. Even a small amount of sin can destroy all merit, just as a small spark can burn everything. O son, I do not see any gain in these battles. A true son is one who always obeys his mother and father. By disobeying them, one's merit is destroyed, and a web of faults and delusions is created, leading to various hells. Therefore, listen to my beneficial words and release the gods immediately. If they are released, Mayuresha, who grants all desires, will become your friend. No one on earth has ever attained happiness through enmity. Hiranyaksha and other demons were destroyed by the gods.

K said: Hearing these words from his father, the son became extremely angry. Disrespecting his father, he said: "I was mistaken before. You are my father, yet you speak like a fool, preaching wisdom. Go away with a darkened face! How can I perform such a disgraceful act of peace when my army, numbering in the billions, has been slaughtered? Wearing a rosary of Kusha grass is not desired in the duty of a king. Therefore, a king should not show mercy to a host of enemies. Ruthlessness towards an enemy is considered policy, just as medicine is for a disease." Saying this, he bowed to his father, turned back, and went to fight, eager for battle.

Chapter 121 : Sindhu's Defeat

Sage said:
The gods, terrified and breathless, rushed to the Lord of the Ganas, who was seated comfortably with heroes like Virabhadra. They reported the situation, and Sindhu himself arrived. The gods felt that the time for their destruction had come. Then, filled with joy, Mayuresha mounted his peacock, seized his four weapons, and roared. After bowing to Shiva, he departed with speed, illuminating the directions. Accompanied by his heroic companions and his army, he proceeded to slay Sindhu. As he moved forward, Shanmukha (Kartikeya) said, "O Lord of Obstacles, I am going to battle. While so many heroes are present, why should you go yourself? Observe the prowess of all, and if that fails, then you engage in the fight." Having said this and bowing, Shanmukha went to battle, taking his fourfold army, and struck Sindhu’s forces.

Sindhu also struck that army with various weapons and showers of arrows, shouting, "Endure and strike! I am killing you, come forward!" A great clamor arose as the heroes fought. Warriors fell, struck by weapons, their lives extinguished by the net of arrows. Limbs were severed, feet, arms, and heads were cut off by swords, spears, maces, and clubs. The heroes, firm and maddened by the battle, killed their enemies. A great noise arose from their shouts and the neighing of horses. With the echoes of musical instruments, the trumpeting of elephants, and the sound of chariot wheels, the battle between the two armies became chaotic.

As promised, the heroes struck heads, faces, eyes, arms, bellies, navels, feet, ankles, and knees. Some destroyed enemies with their hands and feet, others with swords in the thick darkness. As the sun set, they struck after asking and identifying each other. Knowing them to be gods, the demons killed them, and vice versa. A river of blood flowed in that fierce battle, carrying away the bodies of the dead. With roars, they drove the enemies out. Thus, the foot soldiers of Sindhu were killed by the millions by the gods.

Then, the countless elephant division, mounted by heroes skilled in various forms of combat, advanced. Virabhadra and others struck the formidable elephant army. Some had their heads severed, others their arms. Some were felled along with their riders by those wielding fire-weapons. Virabhadra, with great strength, struck an elephant with another elephant; both elephants were torn into eight pieces and fell. Then Shanmukha shone as he struck the elephant division. With his spear and countless arrows released from his bow, he destroyed the maddened, infinite elephant herds along with their heroes. Hiranyagarbha also felled those accompanied by many heroes. The Lord of Ghosts (Bhuta-raja) also felled those various types of elephants in the battle with his weapons. He felled those carrying heroes, their lives ended by arrows. Pushpadanta, becoming a lion, tore those elephants apart. Nandi, in the form of an elephant, broke many of those elephants. All of them were felled by other showers of arrows. Some were grabbed by the tail, whirled around, and thrown by the enemy; both perished, crushed by the heavy impact.

When the elephant division was checked, the cavalry fought. They killed countless gods in the battle with various weapons. The gods fell, fainted, and then regained consciousness. Then, in anger, they struck the heroes on horseback and the horses themselves. The great demons mounted on horses struck the gods in anger. They brought the gods to their end with showers of weapons and arrows. Hearing that the gods had been killed in battle, the six heroes returned. They again struck the demon horsemen. The four of them fought powerfully in the four directions, protecting their own army and the host of gods. They killed the horses and the heroes mounted on them. They fought on foot, killing many enemies. Millions fell there, tormented by arrows. Seeing the horses and riders fallen, Nandi and Bhringi quickly felled them with kicks. Virabhadra also felled countless horse-warriors. With the main heroes killed by the four, it was as if the end of the age had come for the demons' war efforts.

Then some sought refuge with the gods. A great cry of distress arose among the demon army's horsemen. Having thus killed the entire army, the six heroes rejoiced. As all instruments played, they roared and praised the Lord. "Victory is attained through the grace of Mayuresha, by remembering and bowing to him." Then Sindhu, hearing from the words of those who sought refuge about the destruction of his army, spoke to his ministers and all his heroes, eager for battle.

Sindhu said: "I hear that whichever warriors go are killed by the enemies. Now I myself will go to slay this Lord of Gunas by force."

Sage said: Having said this, with a roar, he made the sky, the ten directions, and the three worlds tremble, swallowing the earth and the heavens. He fixed an arrow and released it with speed, pulling the string to his ear. Observing all the heroes in the army protected by Virabhadra and others, the demon king placed an arrow on his bow. Suddenly, the fire-born (Agni) was produced from that arrow, which had been chanted with a mantra a hundred times. It burned the divine army, the earth, the forests, and the mountains. Then the soldiers saw a man born of fire, matted-haired, of brilliant radiance, with a tongue of lightning and a terrible face, devouring the divine soldiers. Shanmukha and others, terrified, fled in the ten directions. Whomever he devoured in the battle, that one attained his own abode, remembering Mayuresha with joy. Wherever the army went, the fire, burning like the fire of dissolution, followed, emerging from his mouth.

Thus, the army of Mayuresha was burned by the fire; in the great darkness of smoke, nothing could be discerned. Then all took refuge behind Mayuresha, crying "Save us, save us!" as they were burned by the fire. Seeing that irresistible great weapon, the Lord Mayuresha, feeling diminished in the world, became fearful and thought: "If Shiva shows grace, only then can there be victory here." Having said this, he took his axe, chanted a mantra, and threw it with force. With a radiance that conquered the sun, it roared through the sky and directions, burning the enemy army like the fire of the world's end. From that, too, a great man was born, in whose mouth the entire globe, with the sky, seemed to fit. The man fought with the man, and the weapon with the weapon. The divine sages went to watch. The demon's weapon was quickly devoured by that weapon of death (Kritanta-astra). It went to burn the army like a garland of flames. Seeing that, the demon king released a shower of arrows. From one chanted arrow, infinite arrows emerged. The divine soldiers were submerged in them, full of speed. Mayuresha, in anger, released various weapons. With those weapons, he repelled all the demon king's weapons, and the man of time (Kala-purusha) again devoured the demon army. Wherever the demons fled, he went there. The demon, filled with anxiety, did not know what to do. He wondered what to do, where to go, and where not to stay. As the sun set, he returned to his own abode. Stumbling and falling on the ground, his earrings and ornaments lost, he entered the city like another Shambhu. He lived in hiding somewhere, among women and servants. The Lord, surrounded by his Ganas, roared, having quickly learned of that event. By that sound, the three worlds were suddenly filled. He withdrew the weapon of death, like a snake charmer with a serpent. Mayuresha, surrounded by his Ganas, quickly returned to his own place.

Chapter 122 : The Victory of Ganesha’s Army

Ganesha said: When the Lord of the Devas, Mayuresha, was seated on his throne surrounded by his ganas, the sages led by Gautama praised the Lord of Virtues. The sages said: O Lord of the Devas, you have defeated the one who conquered Indra in battle and subdued Shesha on earth; what account is there for others? Even he has been defeated by you in battle. O Lord of the Devas, many have been protected from the wicked Sindhu; otherwise, how could the ganas and all the worlds survive?

Ganesha said: While they were speaking of his glory, Parvati arrived. Embracing her son, she said, "You are tired from your desire for battle." Then, Shiva arrived quickly and embraced the Lord, saying, "You have accomplished what was impossible for Indra and the other gods. Even Brahma and the other gods do not know the greatness and the true form of you, who are the Supreme Brahman, the Guru of the moving and non-moving, the omniscient one, and the one engaged in lifting the burden of the earth. What power do we, or the sages led by Gautama, have to comprehend it?"

Ganesha said: As Shiva, the Lord of the Devas, was speaking, the best of sages, Narada, said to Parvati, "Mother, listen to my words. Many days have passed here, O sinless Shiva; when will this wicked demon Sindhu attain liberation? When will the marriage of Mayuresha take place, O virtuous one? How will he, who has defeated Indra and others, meet his end? Give us permission to leave, and we shall return quickly. When will the gods be freed from the bonds of the demon Sindhu? His death seems impossible to me."

Ganesha said: Hearing the words of the sage, the heroes led by Shad-anana (Kartikeya) spoke to Narada, who was inciting the Lord Mayuresha. They said, "O Narada, it is a wonder that even you, who are omniscient, are unaware of this. The Lord has come to this earth from his own abode for this very purpose. Mayuresha, who fulfills all desires, has already achieved his goal. Do you not know the nature of the Lord of Virtues, who is beyond qualities yet creates the agitation of qualities, and whose form is not described even by Brahma? Do you not know the power of Mayuresha, who seeks to lift the burden of the earth, who destroys the demons that Indra and others cannot kill, who is the Lord of infinite millions of universes, the soul of all, and the creator, sustainer, and destroyer of the worlds?"

Ganesha said: Hearing this, Narada replied, "When I see that demon killed and attained to liberation, then I will consider all these words to be true and not otherwise." Hearing these words, the King of Peacocks (Mayuresha) became angry. Shining with rage, he roared, shaking the three worlds. As if burning the three worlds and shattering the earth, he spoke to the sage with a voice as deep as a cloud.

Mayuresha said: "O best of sages, you are worthy of respect because you are the son of Brahma and because you are omniscient. Therefore, I speak to you today while upholding my dignity. By your grace, I could destroy the universe in an instant. I could turn the earth upside down and dry up the oceans. I could move Mount Meru with the breath of my nostrils. Listen, Narada, to my true vow today: I will kill the demon Sindhu; there is no doubt about it."

Ganesha said: Having said this, Vinayaka mounted his peacock and went to fight. Nandi and Bhringi both said to Vinayaka, "We will fight him; watch our skill in battle." Saying this, they went to the city of Gandaki with the speed of the wind. Virabhadra, the king of ghosts, also went upon hearing of their departure. The earth trembled, and Shesha became distressed. The four of them saw the fortress, which was considered impregnable by the gods. Spies reported to Sindhu, who was seated comfortably on his couch, "Four mountain-like heroes are roaring and have reached your city. Why are you sitting still, great demon?" Hearing this, Sindhu was instantly plunged into an ocean of anxiety. Durga was also filled with worry, and her face turned pale. With heads bowed, both fell into deep sorrow. Durga said, "O King, I told you, but you did not listen. This is the fruit of that; what is the use of worrying now?"

While she was saying this, those best of heroes arrived at his assembly hall, which was filled with many wonders and made of various jewels and gold. Bhringi flew up suddenly and stood in the hall, angry. With his great strength, he immediately broke the hall. Its fragments fell all around in the courtyard. The other three heroes joined Bhringi. With faces reddened by the fervor of battle, eager to devour the demon and his army, they saw the army of Sindhu coming forward. Armed with swords, shields, bows, arrows, spears, and clubs, the demon soldiers shouted, "Kill them! Kill the four swift and heroic ones!" The demon soldiers came to kill the four, and a fierce battle ensued. There was a great uproar of "I will kill you" and "Kill me." In the darkness of dust and the flashing of weapons, the four heroes killed a hundred crore demons in that battle. They struck down countless great warriors with force, grabbing them by their feet, whirling them around, and throwing them into the sky, where they fell to the earth in a hundred pieces. By arrows, weapons, feet, and blows of their hands, the entire demon army was destroyed on the battlefield.

Then they went to his house and dragged Sindhu, who was sitting on his couch, by his hair to the battlefield. Sindhu fought them with great weapons. He released a serpent weapon, but the three were surrounded by snakes. Bhringi immediately released the Garuda weapon. When Sindhu released the fire weapon, they released the rain weapon. When he released the wind weapon, they released the mountain weapon. Then they fought him with wrestling. Nandi knocked the crown off the demon's head. Bhringi struck the demon on the back with great rage. Virabhadra grabbed his wife Durga by her hair in front of everyone. The king of ghosts kicked him with enmity. Durga closed her eyes, unable to see him in that state. Cursing his actions, she went to her own palace. In a fit of rage, the demon Sindhu grabbed Virabhadra by the foot, killed Nandi with a fist, grabbed Bhringi by his topknot, and threw him to the ground. The king of ghosts fainted. Placing his crown on his head and a pearl necklace around his neck, mounting his best horse, and calling the remaining soldiers, he said, "I will kill Mayuresha today," and fought again. The demon roared suddenly, making the directions and sub-directions resound.

When those four heroes had gone, the Ganesha-nayaka (Mayuresha) said, "The demon army has been destroyed, and the demon Sindhu has been brought to the battlefield. O Lord of the Ganas, free him from the ocean of existence with the help of a few soldiers. We ourselves will kill him; you do not need to worry, O Lord of Obstacles."

Chapter 123 : The Slaying of Sindhu

K said: Hearing that Sindhu had arrived, the Lord of the Peacock was delighted. Mounting his peacock, he set out quickly for battle. With four weapons, he illuminated all the directions, making the sky resound with a roar like the clouds at the end of an age. He saw Sindhu standing before him, determined for battle. Seeing him in the fray, Sindhu looked at him again and again, just as a king of serpents looks at Garuda, as the wind-eater (snake) looks at the wind, as Vishnu looks at Madhu and Kaitabha, as Shiva looks at Tripura, or as the Great Goddess looks at Shumbha and Nishumbha. Then they began to fight each other with various weapons. Their bodies shone like hibiscus flowers, and the fire generated by the clash of their weapons scorched the earth, mountains, and all. The entire earth, with its oceans, islands, and mountains, trembled. Taking an arrow and consecrating it with the fire-weapon, Sindhu released it to burn the Lord of the Peacock on the battlefield. It spread in all ten directions, burning the divine army. Then, the Lord of the Peacock, having invoked the cloud-weapon, cast his noose upon the demon army, causing the three worlds to tremble. The fire was extinguished by the downpour of water, and then darkness covered all ten directions. Mountains were shattered and trees fell; the demon king lamented, thinking the dissolution had arrived. Sindhu then checked those clouds with the wind-weapon, and that wind shook the sky and all ten directions. Then the Lord invoked a lotus in his hand and suddenly released the mountain-weapon at the great demon. Uprooting trees and illuminating the sky and all directions, it went toward the demon army, showering many mountains. The surface of the earth was covered with countless great mountains; there was no room to stand or move. Seeing the wind obstructed and the mountains everywhere, he unleashed the thunderbolt-weapon, and thunderbolts emerged. With countless thunderbolts, the mountains were pulverized. Then the Lord of the Peacock invoked the goad with the thunderbolt-weapon. He released it, and a battle of thunderbolts ensued. By that sound, the earth, the netherworlds, the directions, and the sky trembled. The fire fell with the strike of the thunderbolts and burned the worlds. The thunderbolts, striking each other, then vanished.

Then the great demon said to his ministers in great rage, "What is the use of this? I shall turn him to ash in a moment—this child of Shiva, who is skilled in drinking Shiva’s milk and is worthy of Shiva." Saying this, the demon rushed to kill the Lord of Virtues. Seeing the great demon approaching, the Lord of the Peacock, in his cosmic form, suddenly split the sky. His feet trod upon the netherworld, and his ears filled the horizons. Seeing him suddenly with a thousand feet, a thousand eyes, and a thousand heads, standing and pervading the sky and the earth, Sindhu fainted and fell to the ground. Covering the entire sky with his hand, the Lord of Gods, with a composed mind, remembered the boon of the Sun-god. Sindhu said, "I was told before by the radiant Sun that if a being covers the sky with one hand, he will lead you to the path of liberation after a long time. Therefore, enough of this battle; whatever is to happen will happen." Looking again, he saw him in a small form, the six-armed Lord of the Peacock, and was filled with great wonder. Then the Lord of Gods, the Lord of the Peacock, descended from his peacock, touched pure water, and chanted the supreme mantra. He invoked his axe, which filled the horizons, and aiming at the enemy’s navel, combined with the nectar-mantra, the son of Girija released it, his eyes reddened with anger, as if shattering the universe, surrounded by a garland of flames. As it was released, it made the sky and all directions resound, illuminating the earth with its brilliance, including its mountains, forests, and oceans. Seeing the axe approaching like Death, the demon quickly prepared his bow and arrow. Before he could aim, it pierced his navel, which was infused with nectar. As the nectar escaped, the demon fell to the ground like a tree struck by the wind, like a mountain struck by a thunderbolt. Opening his mouth and vomiting blood, he gave up his life. By the prowess of the Lord of the Peacock, he attained the most difficult liberation, and to all the worlds watching, it was like a miracle.

The celestial chariots descended and stood to watch the battle. A rain of flowers fell, and the clouds thundered gently. The dust of the earth settled, and a pleasant breeze blew. All directions became clear, and the Gandharvas sang sweetly. The Apsaras danced, and the sages and gods praised him. The heroes, led by the Six-Faced One, were filled with joy. They all said: "We bow to the primordial Lord of the Peacock, who is the form of Supreme Brahman, the form of consciousness and bliss, the eternal bliss, the Lord of Gods, the Supreme Lord, the ocean of virtues, the Lord of virtues, the one beyond virtues. We bow to the primordial Lord of the Peacock, who is the one to be worshipped by the world, the one Omkara, the supreme cause of virtues, the one without doubt, the protector of the world, the destroyer, the savior. We bow to the primordial Lord of the Peacock, the son of Mahadeva, the destroyer of the great demon, the great being, the eternal destroyer of obstacles, the eternal sustainer of devotees, the supreme treasury of knowledge. We bow to the primordial Lord of the Peacock, the beginningless, the source of virtues, the source of gods, the giver of great satisfaction to Shiva, the one always worshipped by all, the destroyer of the enemies of the gods, the giver of worldly enjoyment and liberation. We bow to the primordial Lord of the Peacock, the supreme master of illusion, inaccessible even to those who possess illusion, the one meditated upon by sages, the one like the sky, the Lord of people, the one with countless incarnations, the destroyer of his own ignorance. We bow to the primordial Lord of the Peacock, the cause of many actions, inaccessible to the Vedas, the seed of many actions taught by the three Vedas, the cause of the success of actions, the one served by the king of gods and others. We bow to the primordial Lord of the Peacock, the form of Great Time, the form of moments and others, the form of arts and ages, the one always inaccessible, the cause of human knowledge, the giver of success to men. We bow to the primordial Lord of the Peacock, whose feet are always served by Maheshwara and other gods, the eternal protector, the consciousness of yogis, the one who takes the form of desire, the ocean of compassion. O Lord, you are the giver of supreme bliss to your devotees, as you quickly show great compassion to the worlds. O best of gods, destroy the waves of the six urges, for liberation is praiseworthy from your worship, which gives infinite happiness. O Elephant-faced one, what praise can we, who are incomparable, offer to you? You are a treasure of virtues and the love of the worlds. We do not have the power to describe your virtues; your process of creation is like an ocean."

K said: Having praised him thus, they prayed to him with reverence, and the Lord of the Peacock fulfilled their supreme request. The demon, who was invincible to all the hosts of gods, was killed. Then Parvati went there and, delighted, embraced him. Shiva also went there, and after embracing him, said, "You have done well, my son; the three worlds are filled with joy. The killing of Sindhu was impossible for all the gods, and you did it without effort. You are full of prowess, devoted to the protection of all worlds, the knower of the four Vedas, and a treasury of all knowledge." Having said this, they all went to their respective places with respect. Having bowed to the Lord of the Peacock, the gods said to him, "He who reads this hymn will obtain all his desires. By reciting it a thousand times, one is freed from prison. By reciting it ten thousand times, a mortal achieves the impossible in a moment. He attains victory everywhere and obtains supreme, rare wealth. He becomes blessed with sons and wealth and brings everything under his control." K said: Having said this and being permitted, the gods said "So be it" and departed. The Lord of the Peacock, accompanied by his attendants, returned to his own abode. Thus ends the one hundred and twenty-third chapter of the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Killing of Sindhu."

Chapter 124 : Ganesha's Entry into Gandaka City

K said: When Sindhu the demon fell and the remaining soldiers stood on the battlefield, they reached the Gandaki river, filled with anxiety. Ugra, Durga, and Chakrapani were extremely distressed. At that moment, various ominous signs appeared for them. Some lay down with their heads towards the north, which is considered inauspicious. One stood silently, as if trembling, resting her head on her knees. Another stood without joy, resting her chin on her palm. Meanwhile, messengers arrived, looking like the messengers of Yama. Their faces were pale and miserable; they were silent and could not speak. When asked by others, they narrated the events that had occurred on the battlefield.

The messengers said: After killing countless great warriors, Sindhu has gone to heaven. Abandoning us like children, we are weeping, bereft of him. Seeing the gods slaughtered, the son of Girija (Ganesha) became angry. After neutralizing their weapons with his own, he released a sharp axe. By that, Sindhu was killed and fell to the earth. All his citizens and followers also fainted there.

Hearing this news from the messengers, everyone wept. Then Durga lamented and struck her head violently. With disheveled hair, beating her face, and stripped of her ornaments, she cried out. Ugra, his mother, and Chakrapani, his father, along with the citizens, wept there, striking their faces with their hands. They threw themselves on the ground, wailing with terrible sounds. Some, in their grief, threw dust on their faces and heads. Others bit the backs of their hands and struck the earth with them. The women cursed the son of Girija, breaking their fingers in despair.

Ugra said: Through various efforts, penance, pleasing the Sun, prayers, praises, fasting, and charity, you were protected until the morning. How were you, a hero and a man of honor, taken away? By whom have you been led away? I was praised in the three worlds as the mother of the lord of the three worlds. Now, people will curse me, calling me unfortunate. You had previously conquered Death; how did you meet him? How did you, my son, get caught, and why was he not defeated again? O son, now that you have gone to heaven, why did you leave without asking me, who desired your happiness? How will you find satisfaction when I am in such sorrow? The clouds do not thunder when you, my son, are thundering. How have you fallen silent on the battlefield, my son?

Durga said: The Creator, seeing the couple, made our bodies one. Why did the foolish-minded Brahma not make our souls one? I do not count Sachi, Savitri, or any others who are proud of their auspiciousness; how did I become a widow? When you used to apply musk and sandalwood to my limbs with your own hands, they felt cool. Now, they are burning with grief; extinguish them. You never used to eat even a bit of poison without me; how will you enjoy such heavenly bliss without me? Even servants are not ignored without cause; you are virtuous and of similar nature, so why do you ignore me? We shared a love without conditions, indescribable in the past; why have you left me and gone away, O slayer of gods?

K said: The elders, advanced in penance and wisdom, spoke harsh words. Those who know the scriptures do not praise weeping. Tears shed by friends fall upon the deceased; therefore, the compassionate should do what is beneficial for the departed. Considering this, the merciful, attributeless, blissful, and Brahman-realized Lord granted him liberation. By Mayuresha, who bears the burden of the world and who, out of compassion, took birth to destroy the wicked in battle, he was struck face-to-face. There is no death or birth for the Self, as It is beginningless, attributeless, and eternal, as confirmed by the Vedas. A creature weeps for its own sake, not for the benefit of the deceased. The earth cannot bear the weight of the countless. The tortoise, the boar, and the mountain-bearer (Shesha) support the dead. Having said this, they abandoned their grief, following the words of the wise.

Ugra, Chakrapani, Durga, and their companions, along with the citizens, went to the battlefield, grieving. They saw Sindhu, being fanned by servants on the battlefield, fallen with his lotus-like face open and his white eyes staring. He was bleeding, emitting a foul odor, and surrounded by birds and beasts. They sat around him, grieving and deeply distressed. Durga, with a loud voice, lamented while holding his head in her lap. She said, "My lord, I used to massage your feet with my hands; that is not possible in the midst of the people. Rise, lord of my life! How can you sleep while your enemy is still alive?" She placed her face against his and wailed. The wise elders restrained her, and then Ugra spoke.

Chakrapani, his father, also lamented while washing his face. "Rise, child! Why do you sleep while the enemy forces are still present? You, who fell to the earth like the wielder of the thunderbolt struck by a thunderbolt, how have you, a child, fallen in battle? You conquered Death; how did you come under his control? Say something, O lord, that brings joy to the heart. O sinless one, you whose voice once made the three worlds tremble, why do you not speak today, O one of diminished prowess? We have not offended you, so why have you resorted to silence? You do not speak out of anger, surrounded by the remnants of your army. How have you become so distressed, you who conquered desire with your radiance?"

Then the wise and the elders, showing various examples, comforted them, saying that mourning for the dead is not right. "Did Rama, the son of Dasharatha, not go to the other world? Did the best of the Raghus not go after defeating his enemies with his valor? Hundreds of other kings have died for your sake; having achieved various kinds of fame, they enjoy happiness in heaven."

K said: Then they performed the funeral rites with bilva and sandalwood logs. Durga, endowed with the quality of devotion to her husband, accompanied him. Then Chakrapani, along with the citizens, went to Mayuresha. Bowing down, with folded hands, he praised him slowly.

The King said: You are the attributeless Supreme Soul, the mover of the moving and the non-moving, O Lord. You are attainable through the three Vedas, the master of qualities, pure, and the Lord of the universe. Deceived by your own Maya, the gods do not know you. I am blessed, and the citizens are blessed by your vision, O Lord.

K said: Praised thus by him, the Lord, the ocean of compassion, the King of Mayuras, spoke, being extremely pleased and knowing the essence of all scriptures.

The Lord said: Ask for a boon from me, O Chakrapani, father of the deceased.

The King said: If you are pleased, O Lord of gods, and if a boon is to be granted to me, then come to my house and purify this city.

K said: Hearing those words, he mounted his peacock. Then, accompanied by Tryambaka (Shiva) and Girija (Parvati), he went to the city. In front were all the Ganas and the sages, filled with joy. With all musical instruments playing, they went to the city of Gandaki, which was adorned with various flags and banners and had its paths sprinkled with water.

Thus ends the one hundred and twenty-fourth chapter of the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Liberation of Sindhu and the Blessing of the Gods."

Chapter 125 : The Marriage of Mayuresha

Kala said: Chakrapani (Vishnu) had the assembly hall prepared, adorning it with precious fabrics, banners, flags, and whisks. The ground was inlaid with various shining jewels and pillars, reflecting the images of the people present as if they were infinite. The Lord (Vishnu) beheld the city, which was resplendent with numerous flags, while the wives of the Asuras watched from the tops of the palaces.

Then, the gods—Madhav (Vishnu), Shakra (Indra), Agni, Kubera, Marut, Ravi (the Sun), Chandra (the Moon), and others—arrived quickly to meet Chakrapani. They descended from their respective vehicles amidst the sound of music and bowed to him. The gods and the citizens embraced Mayuresha with great respect, and Madhav, seeing Mayuresha, was filled with joy. All the gods, led by Hari (Vishnu), praised him for the destruction of Sindhu.

The gods said: "He by whom the universe was conquered in a mere moment, he who has granted us our positions—never have we seen or heard of such a one. The Vaishnavas, Shaivas, Shaktas, and Sauras, upon seeing his form, worshipped him with supreme joy. Hearing the words 'Victory to Mayuresha,' the children, the innocent, the mature, the women, the elderly, and the youth were all astonished and worshipped the Lord in their hearts."

Mayuresha sat upon a beautiful throne in the house of Chakrapani, surrounded by the gods, sages, and all the hosts of Ganas, numbering seven crores. The bards sang praises, the gods and kings felt blessed, the Apsaras danced, and Narada and others sang. Chakrapani, having worshipped everyone according to the rites, said in the assembly, "My birth and my deeds are blessed today, for Indra and the other guardians of the world are present in my assembly. Through the merits earned over a hundred lifetimes, I have beheld Mayuresha."

Kala said: Indra, seeing Mayuresha being worshipped first, became angry and, deluded by the Maya of the Lord of the World, spoke suddenly.
