Shakra said: "O Chakrapani, even the four-faced Brahma and the Lord of Lakshmi (Vishnu), the creators of the world, are deluded by your worshipping this child. You have ignored us and worshipped a boy out of delusion, abandoning the Creator of all worlds, the Destroyer, the Mother of the three worlds who creates and sustains, and the Sun who leads the three worlds and governs the duties of the Vedas. This is not right."

Kala said: As the King of the Gods spoke thus, Chakrapani replied, "His prowess was seen in the destruction of the demon Sindhu and in the liberation of all the gods from prison. He is the Lord of Qualities, the Supreme Soul, the infinite power who has descended to remove the burden of the earth and destroy various demons."

Kala said: As he spoke, a great sound was heard by the gods. Some fainted and fell, fearing the explosion of the universe. The earth trembled, and nothing could be discerned. The world was suddenly covered by the light of a crore of suns. Then the gods saw the Lord, extremely beautiful, adorned with various ornaments, ten-armed, and elephant-faced. Seeing this form, the gods were astonished, and then they saw the same Lord in five forms: Gajanana (Ganesha) in the center on a lotus seat, Shiva in the northeast, the Sun in the southeast, Parvati in the northwest, and Narayana in the northeast. The gods were bewildered. Then they heard a voice from the sky that dispelled their confusion: "This one, who is to be worshipped by all, has become five. He is the beginningless and endless Lord, the all-pervading Gajanana. He alone is always to be worshipped as the destroyer of all obstacles by gods, humans, Yakshas, Nagas, and Rakshasas. In worshipping him, all five are worshipped. Do not let your intellect be divided, for that leads to hell."

Kala said: Hearing this, the gods, led by the wielder of the thunderbolt (Indra), saw Mayuresha, adorned with his trunk, as the form of the primordial Om. Then the gods, awakened and having abandoned their delusion and pride, worshipped him with shouts of victory as the indwelling soul of all. Then Chakrapani worshipped the Lord of Qualities with joy, using the five nectars, pure water, divine garments, ornaments, flowers, incense, lamps, various offerings, fruits, betel leaves, and many gifts. He performed the waving of lights, offered flowers with mantras, and bowed with praises. He also worshipped all the gods with devotion. The King said to them, "I am blessed by this worship, for such a gathering has never occurred anywhere else."

Then Narada, happy, said to the four-faced Brahma, "O Creator, as per your command, I have consulted with Shiva and Shiva (Parvati) regarding the marriage of Siddhi and Buddhi. It has been decided that they should be given to the multi-talented Mayuresha." Then all the gods, filled with desire, requested Brahma, "Give them to me, give them to me," with respect. Then Brahma placed garlands in their hands and said: "The one who shines in the hearts of the thirty-three crore gods, choose him with these garlands before me." Leaving all the gods, both Siddhi and Buddhi joyfully placed the garlands around the neck of Mayuresha. The gods, dejected, sighed. Mayuresha looked radiant with them sitting on his knees. Brahma then performed their marriage according to the rites.

He said in the assembly, "What was in my heart has been attained." He presented the Lord of Gods, Mayuresha, according to the scriptures. "Until today, I have protected them with great effort; now I give them to you, protect them with great effort." Then Indra and the other gods said to Mayuresha, "By your grace, O Lord of Gods, we have been freed from prison. Sindhu has attained liberation and returned to his abode by your mercy. We shall now depart; grant us permission, along with sages like Gautama."

Kala said: Mayuresha gladly gave his permission to those wishing to depart. Parvati, with Shiva, placed both daughters-in-law on her lap with respect and was filled with supreme joy. She gave many gifts, garments, and ornaments. Any mortal who hears this auspicious marriage of Ganesha and the victory over Sindhu shall attain all their desires.

Chapter 126 : The Story of Gajanana in the Dvapara Yuga

Kshama said: After the marriage was concluded, Mayuresha mounted his peacock and desired to depart for his city. The gods, mounted on their respective vehicles, followed him instantly, and the sages walked behind. All the citizens, including women and children, followed the Lord of the Discus (Chakrapani) out of the city with great joy. As all musical instruments played, the celestial nymphs danced. When Mayuresha, surrounded by everyone, reached the battlefield, Skanda and other heroes prayed to the Lord. The heroes said: "The army was destroyed by the demons in the heat of battle. Now, all of them have risen, sprinkled by the nectar of your sidelong glance." Seeing them alive, all the gods and citizens were filled with great wonder and praised the Lord.

Then, having granted a path for a yojana, the Lord stood still. Placing his auspicious hand on the head of Chakrapani and embracing him with his ten arms, which were fearsome to the enemies, the Lord said: "Go quickly to Gandaki with the citizens, according to my word." Hearing these words, Chakrapani shed tears immediately. The citizens, bowing down, spoke with sorrow: "Where you go, you should not leave us behind. If you had not come earlier, we would not have felt this pain." Saying this and bowing, they departed by his command. Chakrapani also bowed and went ahead, describing his many wonderful qualities.

Reaching the city in a moment, the citizens, encouraged by Chakrapani, returned to their homes, and Chakrapani to his own. He built temples for the five gods and installed the Panchayatana deities within them. He worshipped them daily with devotion. Mayuresha also reached Mayuresha-puri at that moment. All the Ganas were filled with great wonder upon seeing it, where the pillars were made of jewels and the walls inlaid with gold. One who is counted among the people is not seen there. Entering the beautiful assembly hall and seating Brahma and others, he entered the center and looked around himself.

In the eastern part, he saw Manmatha (Kamadeva) with Rati. He saw the Goddess known as Marjari, famous in the world for granting happiness. In the southern direction, he saw Parvati and Shiva. Before them was the Goddess Viraja, the fulfiller of devotees' desires. In the western direction, he saw Varaha with the Earth, and the auspicious Goddess Ashraya, the remover of all obstacles. In the northern direction, he saw Sri Hari and the famous Goddess Mukta, the bestower of all liberation. Thirty-three crore gods stood there, desiring his vision.

On the first day of the bright fortnight of Bhadrapada, one should bathe, worship Gajanana, go to the eastern direction, and worship Marjari. After giving donations to Brahmins and meditating on Gajanana, one should observe a vow of silence and not listen to the sound of footsteps. One should not harm worms, insects, or flies, remaining pure and restrained. Again, one should bathe and worship the Lord of Lords, Gajanana. In the same way, on the second day, one should worship Viraja at the southern gate; on the third day, Ashraya at the western gate; and on the fourth day, one should worship the Goddess Mukta and Mayuresha at the northern gate. One should perform a festival of worship and keep a vigil at night. Whoever performs this with devotion and fasting at the gates attains all desires through the grace of Mayuresha.

Whoever is able to perform the worship at the four gates as described, from the initial bath to the vision of the deity, Gajanana, being satisfied, grants the fruit immediately. Therefore, starting from the first day of the bright fortnight, one should remain celibate. On the fourth day, at dawn, one should bathe according to the rules, perform daily rites, and worship Mayuresha. One should perform twenty-one circumambulations and salutations. Bowing at the eastern gate, one should worship Marjari with haste. In this way, one should bow to Shiva, Ashraya, and the supreme Goddess Mukta. At the end of the day, one should begin the worship of Mayuresha. One should worship him with sixteen offerings with a focused mind. One should keep a vigil at night with songs and instrumental music. In the clear morning, one should bathe and worship the Lord again. One should perform the breaking of the fast with Brahmins according to one's capacity. One should give them gold, clothes, grain, and cows. The mortal who does this achieves even the difficult.

Endowed with sons and grandsons, after enjoying pleasures, he goes to heaven at the end. The guardians of the directions, like Indra, will worship such a person. The eight Nayikas serve his lotus feet. The blind gain sight, the mute gain speech, the deaf gain hearing, and the lame gain the ability to walk. The seeker of a wife gains a wife, and the student gains wisdom. This is the fruit declared for fasting at the gates.

Then, the Lord of Gods, Mayuresha, spoke to all the gods, Hari, Brahma, Shiva, and others, with a gentle voice: "O Gods, the task for which I descended has been accomplished. Many demons have been killed, and the burden of the earth has been lightened. All the gods have been freed from the prison of Sindhu. Svaha, Svadha, and Vashatkara will continue as before. By your command, O Gods, I shall now depart to my own abode."

Hearing these words, all the gods were filled with sorrow. Shedding tears, they looked at each other's faces and said: "Mayuresha, leaving us, where do you intend to go? Why have you abandoned affection and become so harsh?" Hearing this, Parvati was suddenly overcome with grief. She fell unconscious to the ground and, after a moment, said: "O Lord of the poor, Ocean of mercy, destroyer of the demon Sindhu, where are you going, leaving me, your mother, O Lord of the world? My life will not remain if you depart, O Lord of the gods."

The Lord said: "In the Dvapara Yuga, I shall again become your son, O auspicious one. My word shall not be false; you should not worry. I also feel incomparable sorrow due to your separation. O Mother, friends cannot always live in one place. The demon named Sindhu will also become very formidable. He will be invincible to all the gods; at that time, I shall be your child. Upon being remembered, I shall stand before you, O son of Shiva."

Then, Shad-anana (Kartikeya) said: "Take me where you are going. Indifference is not appropriate for a child who is helpless and poor." The Lord said: "Do not worry, brother; I shall come here again. There is no separation between me and you, for you are the indweller of all." Mayuresha gave that peacock to his brother and immediately named him Mayuradhvaja. Skanda mounted that peacock by his brother's command. At that very moment, the Lord disappeared.

When he vanished, the gods always saw him in their hearts. Then, Brahma made an image of Gajanana with sand and installed it in a beautiful temple according to the rules. All the people worshipped him with offerings according to the scriptures. Sages like Vashistha bathed in Brahma's kamandalu, performed all daily rites, and went to the beautiful image. Some fasted and closed the gates to attain the abode. Others, having bathed and bowed to Mayuresha, ran. The gods and sages said to each other with joy: "There is no such holy place where the Lord himself resides. Mayuresha, the remover of obstacles, is the fulfiller of devotees' desires." Having worshipped and said this, they went to their respective hermitages. Brahma and the other gods returned to their respective abodes. Shankara, with his retinue, went to Kailasa with joy. Indra and others rejoiced with Svadha, Svaha, and Vashatkara.

Kshama said: Thus, O Sage, I have told you everything that happened in the Treta Yuga. The story of Mayuresha, when heard, grants all desires. It is blessed, fame-giving, life-extending, and removes all sins of men. It grants sons, wealth, and knowledge, removes sorrow, and destroys physical and mental ailments, leprosy, and other wicked diseases. For those who read or hear it, it grants enjoyment and liberation. It is auspicious and grants victory and prosperity to men, Kshatriyas, Vaishyas, and even Shudras. It increases growth and prosperity. Whatever you asked me, O Sage, I have told you. You will hear the story of Gajanana again from my mouth.

Thus ends the one hundred and twenty-sixth chapter of the Krida Khanda in the Sri Ganesha Purana, describing the disappearance of Mayuresha.

Chapter 127 : The Description of the Origin of Sindura

Vyasa said: O four-faced Lord of gods, the auspicious deeds of the Lord of Gunas (Ganesha) have been narrated by you in detail, yet I am not satisfied by hearing them. A person never gets bored of hearing stories, just as one does not get bored of nectar. I have heard the entire story of the Treta Yuga regarding the Lord of Gunas. Why did he get the name Gajānana (Elephant-faced) in the Dvapara Yuga? And how did he come to have a mouse as his vehicle, O great Lord? Please dispel this doubt of mine, O lotus-seated one.

Brahma said: You have asked well, my son; this was also in my mind. O sage, a listener, a speaker, and an inquirer all attain success. I shall tell you the story of what Vinayaka did. Listen now to the deeds he performed in the Dvapara Yuga, how the God became Gajānana, red-complexioned, four-armed, and a rider of a mouse. I shall tell you all of that.

Once, Shiva went to the abode of Brahma by chance. He then awakened the sleeping lotus-seated Brahma. Brahma rose and, filled with anger, let out a great yawn. From that yawn, a very terrifying person emerged. He made a loud, terrifying sound that frightened everyone. The earth, along with its oceans, islands, and mountains, trembled because of him. The guardians of the directions were terrified, and Shesha, disturbed, emitted poison. All the mountains shattered, and all living beings became distressed. It was as if the end of the Kalpa had arrived for the people living in the three worlds. He stood there as if he were about to shatter the universe with his head. A beautiful fragrance emanated from his body and spread throughout the three worlds. The directions were reddened by the radiance of his body, which resembled a China rose (Japa flower). Was this a second Cupid born from Brahma? Even Cupid felt ashamed upon seeing his form. Seeing him standing before him, the lotus-seated Brahma was astonished. He asked, "Who are you, where have you come from, and what do you wish to do? Tell me."

The person said: O Lord of gods, you create many universes. Why do you ask me as if you are confused, even though you are omniscient? I have emerged from your yawn; how do you not recognize me? Bless your son, give me a name according to your wisdom, and grant me a place, O Lord, and tell me what I should do.

Hearing his words, the four-faced one said: Since your body is red, you shall be known as Sindura. You shall have great power, capable of attracting the three worlds. Whomever you embrace in anger shall be shattered into a hundred pieces. You shall never have any fear from the five elements, nor from gods, demons, Yakshas, or humans. You shall have no fear from the guardians of the directions like Indra, nor from Time itself. You shall have no fear from serpents, demons, or from day and night. O Sindura, you shall have no fear from the living or the non-living. Dwell wherever you wish in the three worlds.

Then, the son of the four-faced one was pleased with the boon. He roared, shaking the three worlds, both moving and stationary, with his sounds. All the oceans were agitated, and the guardians of the directions fled. He then bowed to his grandfather and said: O Lord of the universe, I am pleased by the nectar of your words. You alone create, protect, and destroy the world through the three Gunas. When you sleep, the world sleeps, and darkness arises. It is my great fortune, O Lord, that I have attained this without penance, charity, or vows. You are pleased out of affection for your son; otherwise, you are not easily pleased. You become pleased only after crores of Kalpas.

Brahma said: Having said this and having bowed and circumambulated him, he left. In his mind, he pondered on the way: "I have done no penance, meditation, chanting, or study. How then were these boons granted to me? Are they true or false?" Saying, "I will go and see my father," he went to the grandfather. He roared terrifyingly, flexing his arms. He wanted to embrace him, and then the grandfather said: "I gave you boons that are rare for others out of affection for my son. But you have come here to harm me due to your wicked nature. Milk given to a snake only turns into poison. Since you have turned to wickedness, you shall become a demon. The Supreme Soul will soon incarnate and kill you, Gajānana. Knowing your end, he will crush you with his own limbs. He will have a red body like Sindura and will be fond of Sindura. That God will also be the killer of Sindura."

Having said this, the lotus-seated Brahma fled in fear, his breath heavy with the great speed of his mind. The demon, hearing the curse, followed him, filled with rage, his body moist with sweat, looking ahead as he went. The demon ran, thinking, "I will catch him at the next step." Brahma, trembling and in a hurry, went to Vaikuntha.

Thus ends the one hundred and twenty-seventh chapter of the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Description of the Origin of Sindura."

Chapter 128 : Sindura's Defeat

K said: He saw the Lord Narayana sitting peacefully, resting on a lotus seat that shone with gems and gold. Seeing him pale-faced and the great demon standing behind him, whose head touched the sky, the compassionate Lord of Lakshmi rose quickly. He took his hand, embraced him, and seated him on his own seat. After worshipping him, he asked, "What task has brought you here? Why is your face pale, your breath heavy, and your radiance lost?" The Grandsire replied, "Seeing you, O Lord, my sorrow has arisen."

K said: Hearing this, the Lotus-seated Brahma spoke: "I was awakened by Shiva while sleeping in my abode. Then, from my yawning mouth, a great being emerged. His head touched the sky, and the stars fell to the earth at his movement. The gods were amazed by his fragrance. His beauty shamed even the God of Love, and his cry made the three worlds tremble. He bowed to me and stood humbly before me. Out of fatherly affection, O Madhava, I granted him boons: 'Whomever you embrace shall meet death, and even Time shall not be able to stand against you in battle.' I gave him a place to stay as he wished. He bowed and went far away, but returned due to some doubt. He came to embrace me, and fearing this, I fled on my swan and came to you in haste. Seeing him behind me, I trembled and gasped. To whom else can I go for refuge, O Lord of all Lords?"

Then the great Vishnu said to the refuge-seeking Brahma: "Having granted boons, why worry now? What is to happen will happen. He is intent on causing suffering in the three worlds."

K said: While Brahma and Hari were thus lost in thought, the great demon stood before them. Seeing him, the wicked one roared, shaking the universe and Brahma himself. Brahma cried out to Vishnu, the protector of the three worlds, to save him. Then Vishnu spoke to the demon in sweet words: "O giver of boons, I am not fit to fight you, as I am filled with the quality of goodness and am always engaged in protection. This is Brahma, the Brahmin; you should not fight him. The God known as the Destroyer of Madana is the one who will give you battle. Fight him, and your fame will spread through the three worlds."

K said: Hearing this, the demon rejoiced. He flew off, shaking the three worlds, crushing mountains and trees. The elephants of the directions were displaced. He reached the plateau of the great mountain Kailash. There, he saw Girisha (Shiva) in meditation, surrounded by Nandi and the Bhringi hosts, served by Parvati. He was adorned with a tiger skin, the crescent moon, and ashes. Seeing the Lord, the demon mocked him: "Why should I fight this ascetic? I will take his beautiful wife and go." Deciding this, he approached Gauri. At that moment, the earth trembled as if at the end of a cosmic cycle. Parvati, terrified, fainted and closed her eyes. The wicked demon seized her by her hair and flew away. Gauri lamented, "How has the Lord, who knows all, become indifferent to me? Why does he remain in meditation while his own body is being stolen? Who will save me? Who will show me Shankara again?"

K said: Seeing her distressed, the Ganas said, "We have no power to fight this destroyer of the three worlds. Shiva is in meditation. Our Mother, the creator and destroyer of the universe, the enchantress of all gods, the best of women, has been taken. When Shankara awakens from meditation, the fire from his eyes will destroy everything." As they wept, Maheshwara released his meditation out of anger. Burning the ten directions with the fire of his wrath, he asked the Ganas, "What is the crisis? I will reduce to ashes whoever has caused you harm."

Hearing this, the Ganas told him how the demon, resembling the God of Death, had come and taken the Mother. Shiva, hearing this, blazed with supreme rage. Mounting his bull and wielding his trident, he flew through the sky, roaring. He reached the place where the demon Sindura was. He struck him from behind, and the demon turned to face him. "Release my wife, you wicked one! Where will you go?" Shiva shouted. The demon, filled with rage, continued toward Shiva, boasting, "I do not fear the words of a mosquito, O Lord of the world. I do not count you. If you have the strength, fight me. Otherwise, marry another and find happiness. Why should I, the Lord of the world, fight a mosquito like you?"

As he prepared to fight, Durga Devi remembered Mayuresha in her heart. Suddenly, Mayuresha appeared in the form of a Brahmin, shining like a million suns. He stood between them and stopped the demon with his axe. He spoke to Sindura: "Place the Mother of the three worlds, Gauri, near me. Fight Shankara until one of you wins. Whoever wins shall take her."

K said: Hearing the Brahmin's words, Sindura was pleased and set Gauri down. As they watched, the demon and Maheshwara fought. Both were skilled in battle, their eyes red with rage. As the demon tried to embrace Shiva, the axe struck his chest. Then, Shiva struck the weakened demon with his trident. The Brahmin then said, "You should not fight the Lord of the three worlds. Leave Gauri and go to your home, or Shiva will turn you to ashes."

Told this, the demon abandoned his desire and left. Then Parvati asked the Brahmin, "Who are you, O great sage, who saved me from the wicked one? Show me your true form." The sage replied, "O Mother, I did nothing; Shiva defeated the demon and saved you." Saying this, he revealed his form as Vinayaka, adorned with jewels and ornaments. Seeing the Supreme Lord, Gauri rejoiced and bowed at his feet. He lifted her up and said, "In the Treta Yuga, I will give you my vision again. In the Dvapara Yuga, I will be born in your house as Gajanana and will destroy the demon Sindura with my power."

K said: The Lord in the form of a sage disappeared. Gauri lamented and fainted. Then Vishwanatha said, "Be at peace, my beloved. See that Lord Vinayaka in your heart. What he said is not false; he will do it." Saying this, the great Lord, with her, returned to Kailash, the king of mountains.

Chapter 129 : The Description of Gauri’s Longings

Kavi said: Then the demon, having gone to the mortal world, roared with pride. All the mountains trembled, and the trees fell to the ground. Birds, lions, and wild animals wandered in the forest at that time. Then the demon conquered all the kings and warriors. How could the kings fight against one whom even Brahma and the other gods had been defeated by? Some were cut in two by him, while others wandered in the sky. Some kings, having fought him face-to-face, went to heaven, while others sought refuge and became his servants. Some, having lost their positions, went to the forest out of pride. Having thus defeated all the kings, his wicked mind turned toward the sages. He suddenly bound them. At that time, some groups of sages abandoned their bodies and went to heaven. Some lived in the caves of Mount Meru, free from distress. Some were killed by him, and others were severely beaten. All their palaces were destroyed by him, and even the gods were oppressed. Thus, when the dissolution occurred, Vedic rituals were abandoned. The offerings of Svaha, Svadha, and Vashatkar ceased, and cries of distress arose.

The gods, sages, Yakshas, and Kinnaras, who were residing in mountain caves, consulted one another for the sake of their mission. There, Brihaspati said: "There is no fear for those gods who are still present. Therefore, O sages, pray to Lord Vinayaka. He will manifest in Shiva's abode. By the name Gajanana, he will act now. He will forcibly kill the demon Sindura; there is no doubt about this. The entire world will become free from obstacles, O gods." Having been told this by Jiva (Brihaspati), all the gods and others, filled with supreme devotion, praised Vinayaka.

The gods said: "I bow to that Vinayaka, who is the cause of the world, who is born from the sun and stars, from whom all the hosts of Siddhas and Sadhakas arise, and from whom the oceans, Gandharvas, Kinnaras, Yakshas, humans, serpents, and demons originate, and from whom the entire moving and non-moving universe exists. I bow to that Vinayaka from whom Brahma and other gods, sages, and great seers have come, and from whom the three Gunas were born. I worship that Ganapa, who has many incarnations, who resides in the hearts of all, and whom even Shesha is unable to praise. By whom was Sindura, the destroyer of the world, created? Because of him, the world is in distress while you, our Lord, are awake. To whom else shall we go for refuge? Who else will protect us all? Descend into Shiva's abode and destroy this wicked-minded one."

Having said this, they engaged in various rituals. Some were without food, some practiced controlled eating, and others were devoted to breath control (pranayama). Some were engaged in smoke-drinking, some observed silence, some stood on one leg, and others stood in water. Some held their arms upward, some practiced yoga, and some offered their bodies or heads as sacrifices. Seeing their penance, the Lord of the Ganas manifested, resembling millions of suns and the fire of dissolution. Seeing that effulgent form, the gods rejoiced. "This is the very one we contemplated, the Lord of all who has manifested. He will remove our sorrow; there is no doubt about this."

Then the Lord spoke to the gods who were filled with anxiety: "I will kill Sindura, O gods; you should not worry. This hymn, which removes sorrow, will become famous as yours. Because of this, the destruction of sorrow will occur through my grace. Whoever recites it once, twice, or three times a day will never experience even the slightest trace of the three types of sorrow. I, at this time, O gods, having descended into Shiva's abode, will become famous by the name Gajanana, the fulfiller of all desires. I will kill the great demon Sindura and others. I will serve Shiva while displaying divine pastimes."

Kavi said: Having said this to the gods, he disappeared right there. Then, suddenly, through the grace of Shiva, the daughter of Himavan (Parvati) conceived. That womb grew day by day, just like the moon. Then, scorched by the heat of that fetus and longing for various things (dohada), Gauri, distressed by the heat of the womb, spoke to Shankara: "Take me, O Shankara, to a place that is very cool." Then, mounting his bull and seating her behind him, illuminating the directions with a great mass of radiance, and with all musical instruments playing, he went to the earth. Accompanied by various Ganas, he wandered through the forests. While wandering, he saw a great and beautiful forest. There, with Parvati's consent, he rested. It was filled with various flowers, trees, and fruits, adorned with lakes and ponds, and had thick shade. It was charming, where even the sun could not enter, resembling the peak of Kailasa. It was more beautiful than Nandana or Chaitraratha. Seeing that, Girija said to Shiva: "O Lord, we have reached a forest according to my desire. Let us play here for a long time." The Ganas also felt great joy there. Then the Ganas, desiring to please them, built a pavilion there, equipped with various altars, houses, and amenities. Then Shiva said to the Goddess: "Dwell here with the Ganas. Whatever you desire, they will grant you through my grace." Then, he himself went away and became absorbed in meditation in the Himalayas. Parvati played there with her companions as she pleased. Millions of Ganas guarded her, surrounding her on all sides. All of them, obedient to her commands, lived on roots, tubers, and fruits.

Thus ends the 129th chapter of the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Description of Gauri’s Longings."

Chapter 130 : The Manifestation of Gajanana

The King said: Then, in the ninth month, she gave birth to a child. He had a beautiful moon-like face, eyes as lovely as lotus petals, and a divine form. He wore a crown and armlets, and his radiance was equal to ten million suns. He was adorned with a red-lipped beauty and possessed four arms. He wore a necklace of pearls and was decorated with beautiful bracelets. In his hands, he held an axe, a lotus, a garland, and modakas. His lotus feet were marked with the signs of a banner, a goad, and a lotus. His ankles were adorned with tinkling bells, and he was truly auspicious. His forehead shone like the moon, his brilliance was like fire, and seeing this form, she trembled with joy.

She asked, "What is this great light that has appeared and dazzled my eyes? Who are you? Tell me, gladden my heart, and show me your grace." Having said this, she bowed to him, remembering her previous promise. Then the great form spoke, "Do not be distressed, Mother. I am Vinayaka, the knower of all qualities, the lord of countless universes. My incarnations are infinite and unknown even to the gods. By my own will, I perform creation, preservation, and destruction. I take on three forms for the sake of the world, O daughter of Shiva. In the Treta Yuga, I descended into your home in play and performed many deeds, including the destruction of the demon Sindhu. I was known as Mayureshwara, six-armed and of golden complexion. At that time, I gave you my word that in the Dvapara Yuga, I would be your son again. I also said I would be your servant, having liberated you from Sindhu, appearing suddenly in the form of a Brahmin. Remember everything, O fair-smiling one. I promised then that I would be your son, and that promise has now been fulfilled by me, O Parvati. I will remove the burden of the earth and destroy the mighty Sindhu. I will serve you, Mother, as Gajanana. I will become famous as the fulfiller of the desires of my devotees."

The King said: Hearing these words, Gauri remembered everything from the past. She bowed and praised the Lord of the Universe, the destroyer of obstacles. Gauri said, "I bow to you, the form of Brahman, who is pure consciousness and bliss, devoid of duality. You are dear to your devotees, formless, yet taking form according to the qualities. I bow to you, who are greater than the greatest and smaller than the smallest. You are the unmanifest become manifest, possessing the qualities of Rajas, Sattva, and Tamas. You are the master of Maya, the knower of all illusions. You are the indweller of all, the eternal, the foundation of all, and beyond the beyond. You are beyond the reach of the minds of even the four gods. It is my great fortune, O Lord, that you have become my son. I have been waiting for your direct vision. O Lord, ensure that I am never separated from you."

As she was speaking, the Lord assumed another form—four-armed, with a moon on his forehead, and adorned with a trunk. He wore many ornaments, the Chintamani gem on his chest, divine garments, and divine fragrance, accompanied by Siddhi and Buddhi. Then, he began to cry like an ordinary child. Seeing this, Girija was distressed and lamented, "Who has taken my treasure? No child with a trunk has ever been seen in the three worlds. Brahma and others will laugh if they see such a child." Hearing her lamentations, Shankara came home. Taking the child with his attendants, he said to her, "Listen to me, my dear. This is the beginningless and endless Lord, adorned with a trunk. He is the one whom the four Vedas, scriptures, sages, and gods contemplate. He is the one by whom this universe is woven, the indweller of all, and the creator of countless universes. He has four forms in the four Yugas. In the Krita Yuga, he is known as Vinayaka with ten arms. In the Treta Yuga, he is the six-armed Mayureshwara of white complexion, who descended to his own abode after killing Sindhu. This same one, of red complexion and four arms, has descended into our home to destroy Sindhu. Riding a mouse, the Lord will remove the burden of the earth. He will be famous in the three worlds by the name Gajanana. In the Kali Yuga, O Goddess, he will be known as Dhumraketu, four-armed and beautiful-eyed."

Hearing the words of Shiva, the child spoke clearly. The child said, "I have understood my true nature, and you have spoken well, O Shiva. I have descended into your home to serve you, to kill Sindhu, the destroyer of gods and kings. I will destroy those who are proud of their strength and conquerors of the three worlds. By killing him, I will bring joy to the world, O Shankara. I will be the fulfiller of the desires of my devotees, the promoter of Vedic duties, and the bestower of knowledge to the worthy. Whoever has devotion to me, is intent on my meditation, is a worshiper of gods, Brahmins, and guests, and performs the five sacrifices—who is fond of listening to the Puranas, is righteous, truthful, and pure—his wife, Pushpika, is also devoted to duty. She is a chaste woman, devoted to her husband and his words. They performed severe penance for twelve years. I gave them a boon that I would surely be their son. But now, the child born to Pushpika has been taken by demons and will lose his life. Therefore, take me there." Hearing these words, Shiva, filled with joy, worshiped him with devotion using various flowers of speech.

Chapter 131 : The Defeat of the Gandharvas

K said: Then he became anxious about how to take the child there. Knowing this, Nandi said to him, "O Lord, by your grace, I can dry up the oceans and crush the mountains. Command me, therefore, to perform the task that lies in your heart." K said: Hearing his words, Hara was pleased and said to him. Hara said: "Well said, well said! You have expressed my intention. O Nandikeshwara, I have known your prowess a hundred times over. Now, do what I tell you. In the great city of Mahishmati, there lives a king of great strength named Varenya, who is famous and devoted to various virtues. His wife, a noble lady named Pushpika, has just given birth and is asleep. A demoness has taken the child; you must quickly take the child from her and place him before her before the virtuous lady wakes up."

K said: Having heard the command spoken by Shankara, he quickly took the child and hurried through the sky. He placed the child before her, and she, not being asleep, recognized him. Nandikesha quickly flew off and went to Maheshwara. He told him the account of what he had done on the way. "O Lord, suddenly a terrifying demoness appeared from the sky, eating the flesh of children, and stood before me. By your grace, I wrapped the demoness with my tail, whirled her around, and threw her onto the peak of a great mountain. She was shattered into a hundred pieces by the mere utterance of your name, O Lord. Then I saw a great army of wicked Gandharvas. How was I to fight them and protect the child? Being anxious, I remembered you in my heart, O Shankara. Then, with my tail, horns, angry breaths, kicks, and roars, I repelled those powerful ones. Some died, some were struck down, and some had their heads and limbs broken. They fell to the ground in hundreds, and then a shower of flowers fell. O God, I have gone and performed the task you commanded. Who in the three worlds can kill one who is the object of your will? Therefore, by your name, O God, that child has attained victory."

Then Shiva, filled with joy, embraced Nandikeshwara and, with a pleased heart, said, "I have known your firm prowess. There is no one in the three worlds equal to you, O Nandikeshwara." K said: Then, bowing to the Lord of the Universe, he went to Parvati. Bowing to her and joining his hands, he spoke in a sweet voice, "Mother, by Shiva's command, the child was taken to the city of Mahishmati and placed before Varenya's wife, Pushpika, because a boon was given to him in the past that 'I will become your son, the revealer of Brahma-jnana'." Hearing his words, the all-knowing Parvati rejoiced, knowing the infinite power of that child. She said with great devotion, "O son, your prowess is known. You have killed the terrifying demoness with the dreadful sound, destroyed the wicked Gandharvas, and protected the child. You have performed this great task by placing him there unnoticed." Having said this, she dismissed him, and he then took his rest.

Thus ends the one hundred and thirty-first chapter of the Krida Khanda in the Shri Ganesha Purana, entitled "The Defeat of the Gandharvas."

Chapter 132 : The Journey to Kailasa

K said: Once, the demon king Sindura, blinded by his own pride, sat upon his throne and lamented that his prowess was being wasted. He boasted that Indra and the other gods, including Brahma and Vishnu, dared not fight him, and the kings of the mortal world were not even worth counting. He likened his wasted strength to the youth of a noble woman without a husband. Just then, he heard a miraculous voice from the sky.

The celestial voice said: O fool, why do you boast? Your destroyer is already born. A child is growing in the womb of Parvati, currently residing in the house of the excellent one (Shiva), growing like the moon in the bright fortnight, possessing infinite divine play.

K said: Hearing this voice, Sindura fainted. Upon regaining consciousness, he wondered who had spoken and what it meant. He thought that if he could see this child, he would devour him entirely, for how could his death be possible, just as the sun rises after the night? Thinking this, he rose, roaring so loudly that the directions trembled, and flew toward Kailasa, the abode of Parvati. As he flew, his very presence caused mountains to crumble, trees to fall, and the earth to shake, disturbing even the cosmic tortoise and the serpent Shesha.

He did not find Shiva there, so he searched the entire earth in a frenzy. Filled with rage, he arrived at a great forest called Paryalya, where he saw Shankara accompanied by Gauri, along with their pavilion, attendants, and lotus-filled ponds. He rushed toward the place where Gauri was resting. Seeing no child there, he burned with anger, thinking the celestial voice could not have been false. He reasoned that if the son had not yet been born, he would surely be born to her, so he decided to kill her now to destroy the root of the problem.

Having decided this, he drew his weapon. Just as he was about to strike Uma, he saw a child before him—a beautiful, four-armed boy adorned with a crown and armlets, holding an axe, a lotus, and a garland, appearing truly wondrous. Seeing the child, he stopped his attack, thinking the child was asleep. He picked up the boy, intending to throw him into the great ocean. However, as he held him, the child suddenly began to grow, becoming as massive as a mountain. Sindura, burdened by the weight and overwhelmed by his own pride, began to tremble. Gasping for breath and unable to move, he dropped the child in a state of confusion.

When the child fell to the ground, he let out a roar so great that the mountains shook and the earth trembled. Birds flew in the sky crying out, the seven oceans were agitated, and the very universe seemed to crack. The child fell into the waters of the Reva (Narmada) river near a sage, creating a sacred place known as Ganesha-kunda. The mere remembrance of this place destroys the sins of a lifetime; seeing it removes the sins of ten lifetimes, and bathing in it removes the sins of a hundred lifetimes. The blood from the child’s body turned the stones there red, and these stones, known as Narmada-Ganeshas, are famous for destroying sins and granting all desires to the faithful.

The demon, thinking his enemy was destroyed, rejoiced. But then, a terrifying, massive being emerged from the pond, covered in matted hair and vines. He had fearsome tusks, a tongue like a serpent, long limbs, and eyes wild with breath. Seeing this, Sindura, his eyes burning with rage, declared that this being was of no consequence to him. He rushed to strike him with his sword, but as he swung, the being vanished into the sky.

The being spoke from the air: O demon, you foolishly let the child go on the earth. Your destroyer has indeed been nurtured elsewhere. He will surely kill you, for he is the protector of the righteous. Having said this, the terrifying being disappeared. Sindura, in a great rage, ordered his servants to seize the one who had spoken, but when he could not find him anywhere, he returned to his own place, thinking he would defeat his enemy when he saw him again.

Parvati, unaware of these events due to the influence of divine illusion, felt a great sense of confusion. She humbly said to Girisha (Shiva) that she felt a disturbance caused by demons and wished to go to Kailasa, asking him to take her if he so desired. Hearing this, Shankara rejoiced. He mounted Nandi with Parvati, and accompanied by his seven-crore attendants, he reached Kailasa in an instant. Upon entering their abode, Gauri was filled with supreme joy.

Chapter 133 : The Vision of Parashara

Vyasa said: O Lotus-born, tell me in detail what happened after the child was abandoned near the flowers in the house of Varenya.

Ka said: You have asked a heart-delighting question, O son. I shall narrate to you that sin-destroying story in the proper manner. When that night passed, Pushpika saw her son; he had four arms, a reddish complexion, an elephant face, and was adorned with ornaments. He was decorated with a musk tilak and a garland of pearls, wearing yellow garments and anointed with beautiful sandalwood paste. He was shining with his body and adorned with various jewels. Seeing the child in such a state, Pushpika became astonished, distressed, and terrified. She ran outside, beating her chest with her hands, overcome with grief. Hearing her cries, the maids gathered, and upon seeing the child in that extraordinary form, they were also amazed.

The King, having heard the news, arrived at the house with his attendants. Seeing the child in that form, they too were struck with fear. Some became restless, some fainted, and others said to the King, "Such a child has never been born, nor will one ever be. Such a child has never been seen or heard of by men. He should not be kept here, as he will destroy the lineage." Hearing the words of all, the King agreed and told his messengers, "Abandon this child in the dense forest."

The messengers took the child and went into the deep forest, a place untouched even by the wind. They left him on the bank of a lake, covered him with a pile of leaves, and quickly returned to King Varenya. Seeing the King in the assembly, they bowed and said, "O King, by your command, we have abandoned the child in the forest infested with lions and tigers; he must have been devoured by the wild beasts."

Ka said: Just as the jackals were approaching to devour him, the great sage Parashara, an ocean of compassion, saw the four-armed, elephant-faced child who shone like ten million suns. He was adorned with various jewels, divine garments, a serpent coiled around his navel, and the Chintamani gem. The sage, though a repository of all knowledge, was deluded by Maya. He lamented, "What is this obstacle created for my destruction? Has Indra, desiring to destroy my penance for his own selfish ends, done this? I, who fear sin, have done nothing wrong. O Lord of the helpless, O Lord with the moon on your crest, protect me from this great fear."

Seeing him lamenting, the elephant-faced Lord, full of compassion, removed the veil of delusion. The sage then perceived Him before him as the Supreme Soul, the form of the Supreme Brahman, who had assumed this guise to protect His devotees. "Blessed is my birth today, and blessed are my parents and my great penance. My cycle of birth and death is ended, and I have attained the highest goal. What unfortunate person abandoned this child in the forest?"

Ka said: Having said this, the sage took the child to his hermitage. His affectionate wife, seeing the child brought by her husband, was filled with joy and love. Holding him to her heart, she said to her husband, "My lord, the great penance performed for a long time has borne fruit. We have today seen Him whose form is not known even to Brahma, Hara, the Lord of Lakshmi, or the sages. He who is the creator, protector, and destroyer, the Lord of the entire universe, has descended to remove the burden of the earth by assuming various incarnations. It is our great fortune that the Sustainer of the Universe, who is beyond speech, has come within the range of our vision."

Ka said: Upon the child’s touch, the lady’s breasts became filled with milk, and the child drank it, bringing her great joy. Then, King Varenya heard that the child was being cared for by the sage Parashara, who possessed divine vision. With great joy, the King ordered the beating of drums and the distribution of sugar in every house. He satisfied the Brahmins and his friends with clothes, gold, and heaps of jewels. Cows began to yield abundant milk, dried ponds became filled with water, and in the sage’s hermitage, withered trees became laden with fruit. Any mortal who hears this story shall become blessed with children and wealth.

Chapter 134 : The Story of the Mouse as a Vehicle

Brahma said: The child grew there day by day like the moon, bringing joy to his mother and father through his own actions.

Vyasa said: O Brahmin, tell me the story of the Lord of Gunas (Ganesha) as it occurred in the Dvapara Yuga. Having heard the previous stories, I am not yet satisfied, O Lord. Why is he called Gajanana (Elephant-faced), and how did the mouse become his vehicle? Tell me all this in detail, O Four-Faced One.

Brahma said: In this regard, they cite an ancient story, a dialogue between Prahlada and the sage Bhargava (Shukracharya). Just as you have asked me, the great sage was once asked by Prahlada, and the Lord Bhargava told him the story. Listen to it now. Listen, Prahlada, I shall tell you how the mouse became his vehicle, the hearing of which grants all desires.

Long ago, in the assembly of Indra, a Gandharva named Krauncha, in his haste, accidentally stepped on the sage Vamadeva. The sage cursed the Gandharva, saying, "You shall become a mouse. The Lord of Gunas will conquer you and make you his vehicle. Being full of compassion, the Creator of the world will eventually lead you to liberation." The Gandharva then fell into the ashram of Parashara in the form of a mouse. He was terrifying, mountain-like in size, with huge hair, a loud cry, great strength, massive tusks, and a thunderous voice. He caused great havoc in the ashram, eating the grain, breaking earthen pots, and devouring books, clothes, and bark garments. He destroyed everything of value. With the strike of his tail, he knocked down trees, and with his squeaking, he made the world tremble.

Seeing him, the great sage thought, "Because of the violence of this wicked creature, I must surely leave this place. But where should I go now? Where would I find peace? The scriptures say that abandoning one's life is a great sin. How has such misery come to my peaceful ashram due to past karma? Whom should I remember, and who will deliver me from this suffering? Who can destroy this creature, and who is capable of killing him? To whom should I go for refuge today, and who will be my savior?"

Hearing his father's words, the child (Ganesha) of infinite prowess said in a sweet voice, "Do not worry at all. While I, the destroyer of the wicked and the great Lord, am here, I have become your son and will do what is dear to you. O Sage, at whose cry this earth would shatter and mountains would turn to dust by the strike of my feet, watch my wonder, Father. I shall make him my vehicle."

Having said this, the child released a noose that shone like a billion suns. It shone in the sky like a cluster of lightning among the clouds. The gods immediately abandoned their abodes in fear. The noose went forth, fiery-faced, roaming the ten directions. It bound the mouse in the netherworld by the neck and dragged him out. The mouse, tormented by the power of the noose, fainted. He lamented in great anger, his breath stifled, "How has death come by the decree of fate? What is to happen will happen; human effort is useless. I, who have shattered many mountains with just the tip of my tusks, and who never feared gods, demons, or men—by whose noose has my neck been seized now that my life is ending?"

While the mouse was speaking thus, Gajanana pulled him with his mind. He came along with the mouse, just as a master of the Garuda-mantra instantly pulls a serpent. The mouse, seeing the Lord Gajanana with the noose around his neck, regained his senses. He bowed to the Lord, the auspicious Gajanana, and praised him with great devotion: "You are the Lord of the worlds, the Creator, the Destroyer, and the Protector. You are devoid of the three Gunas and yet the supporter of them. You are beyond Maya, yet the master of Maya, and the one who deludes even the deluders. You are inaccessible to Brahma and others, yet you reside in the lotus of the sages' hearts. You are the cause, the instrument, the doer, and the cause of all causes. Today, I have seen you with my own eyes; my life is blessed. My parents, my eyes, my penance, my knowledge, my vows, and my chants are all blessed."

Hearing these words, the elephant-faced Lord was pleased. Knowing his firm devotion, the Lord said to the mouse, "O sinless one, it is because of your own merit that I, who am Nirguna (without attributes), have taken on attributes to destroy the wicked and protect the righteous, as you have sought refuge in me. Therefore, I grant you fearlessness; ask for the boon you desire."

The mouse said, "O Elephant-faced One, I do not ask for anything; you yourself ask for what you desire."

Brahma said: When the proud mouse said this, Gajanana replied, "If your words are true, then become my vehicle." Upon being told this, the mouse immediately climbed up. Crushing the Lord with his weight, he again asked, "I have become your vehicle, O Lord; please become light." At the request of the one who was begging, the Lord became light in weight. Seeing this great wonder, the sage bowed and said, "I have never seen such prowess in a child in the three worlds. At your word, mountains shattered and the guardians of the directions were shaken from their places. Yet, you have made him your vehicle in a moment by your power."

Then, his mother, full of affection, took the child and, with joy, let him drink from her breasts. She said, "I do not know your true form or your prowess. By the merit of past lives, you have come to our home." Then, having bound the mouse, he played as if he were an ordinary child. Thus, I have told you how he came to have the mouse as his vehicle.

Chapter 135 : Krauncha's Curse on the Gandharva

Vyasa said: O Lotus-born (Brahma), what virtuous or sinful deeds did he perform in the past, due to which he attained the birth of a mouse and became the vehicle of the Lord?

Brahma said: O son, you have asked well, and it is dear to my heart as well. Listen with a focused mind as I tell you the whole story. On the beautiful peak of Mount Sumeru, there was a great hermitage belonging to Saubhari, filled with trees and various flocks of birds. Sages like Vashistha and gods led by Indra used to come daily to see Saubhari, who was radiant with the growth of great penance, possessed the brilliance of fire and the sun, was famous in all worlds, and was devoted to meditation on the Lord. His wife, the highly fortunate Manomayi, was renowned among all chaste women and was very dear to her husband. Her beauty surpassed all, and even Shachi and other goddesses could not match her.

Once, Saubhari rose early in the morning in his sacrificial hall, performed the fire ritual, and went to the forest to collect sacrificial wood. Manomayi, of good character, remained at home engaged in household duties. At that time, a wicked Gandharva named Krauncha arrived. Seeing the excellent hermitage, filled with various halls and offering cool shade, he felt rested. He thought, "The lord of such a beautiful hermitage must have earned great merit through his chants and penance; this place is blissful." Saying this, he entered the house and saw the moon-like face of Manomayi, which was so beautiful that even Hara (Shiva) might have become infatuated upon seeing her. Struck by the arrows of Cupid upon seeing her sidelong glances, he became overwhelmed by desire and suddenly grabbed her hand. Upon his touch, she trembled and fainted.

Manomayi, intent on remembering her husband, did not curse him. She became pale, her throat dried up, she sweated, and tears flowed from her eyes. She was extremely distressed, bewildered, and sorrowful, thinking, "Whom shall I take refuge in? Who will save me from this wicked one? I do not remember having committed any sin in this life; this happiness and sorrow must be due to the sins of a past life." Realizing his wicked intent, she consoled him and said, "I am like a daughter to you, and you are like a father to me. You are wise; do not act in sin, O shameless one. Sinners go to hell for countless millions of years. Therefore, release this helpless woman who is like your daughter. If you do not, I will surely give up my life. The sin of killing a woman will be a great burden upon you. My husband is a great soul and will return from the forest now. His fire of anger will reduce you to ashes in an instant. I have no need to do anything small, but if I were to, I could reduce you and even the creation of Brahma to ashes."

As she was saying this, Saubhari arrived. Seeing him in his courtyard, radiant like the midday sun, the Gandharva was struck by his brilliance and released her hand. He looked down, trembled, withered, and became afraid. The sage, burning like the fire of dissolution, suddenly uttered a curse upon the Gandharva.

The Sage said: Since you have molested my wife in my absence, O fool, you shall become a mouse. You will tear up the earth like a thief and fill your belly.

Krauncha said: I did not molest this Manomayi intentionally. It happened by chance when I saw her beautiful form. I only held her hand, and you saw it at that moment. O sinless sage, I released her because I was afraid of your brilliance. Therefore, O ocean of mercy, you should forgive my offense. Show me grace, O lover of those who seek refuge. I take refuge in you; have mercy on me. I have not seen anyone in the three worlds endowed with such virtues of chastity.

The Sage said: The ocean may overflow, Mount Meru may float, or the sun may rise in the west, but my words shall not be in vain; fire may become cold, but my curse remains. Yet, I will tell you this, O wicked one: listen with respect. When the Goddess incarnates in the house of Parashara in the Dvapara Yuga, you shall become the vehicle of the one known as Gajanana (Ganesha). Then, Brahma and other gods will honor you with respect. Once you have reached heaven in his hands, you will return quickly.

Hearing these words, filled with both sorrow and joy, he fell to the earth in the form of a great mouse. When the Dvapara Yuga arrived at Parashara's hermitage, he became a mouse of great strength and valor, resembling a great mountain. By the power of the sage's blessing, he became a mighty mouse near Gajanana. I have told you everything you asked, O sinless one, regarding how the mouse became the vehicle of Gajanana.

The Sage (Vyasa) said: O Brahma, tell me in detail how the Lord Ganesha killed Sindura. Hearing your words, I am not satisfied, just as one is never satisfied with drinking nectar. I listen with devotion; you are all-knowing, please tell me.

Chapter 136 : The Battle with Sindura (Shrimad Ganesha Gita)

Krtayuga said: One day, the Lord, being distressed by the suffering of others, spoke to the best of sages, the highly fortunate Parashara.

Gajanana said: The entire world is being tormented by the wicked Sindura. The world is devoid of Svaha, Svadha, Vashatkara, and the chanting of the Vedas. The groups of gods have been displaced from their positions, and the groups of sages have also been ruined. In the form of Gajanana, I shall destroy the wicked. I will protect the virtuous, remove the burden of the earth, establish all the gods, and bring joy to the world. O father, place your auspicious, fear-dispelling hand upon my head. By the grace of your blessing, I shall slay the wicked demons.

The Sage said: This is a great wonder, O child, that you speak in such a way despite your childhood. A child, filled with curiosity, might ask for the moon, but you wish to perform a task that is impossible for all the gods combined. He, by whose breath mountains fall to the earth by the hundreds, and by whose footfall the three worlds tremble—how will you, with a body as delicate as a lotus fiber, fight him? You have only completed nine years of age. Only by my grace will you become capable.

Then, the sage placed his fear-dispelling hand upon his head.

Krtayuga said: Then, the Lord, filled with joy, bowed to the sage, and also to his mother Durga, and to Sri Hari and Hara. Gajanana mounted his mouse and went to fight. Bearing a goad, an axe, a noose, and a lotus in his four hands, he roared, causing the three worlds to tremble. Shining with his own radiance like the fire of dissolution, Gajanana reached the city of Sindura in an instant. Near the presence of Ghrusneshvara was the fortress of the wicked Sindura. Living there, Sindura ruled over the three worlds. The Lord stood to the north of that place and roared, causing the directions to echo. The seven oceans were agitated, and even the mountains were shattered. Hearing that roar, all the demons trembled. The cowardly ones fainted, and some even met their death. Sindura also fainted but regained his senses in a moment. He asked all his servants, "Who is roaring? Go and see. Why did I faint? By whose sound did this happen? Who has the power to stand before me?"

Then the servants went near the child. Seeing his form, like a lion before an elephant, they were terrified and trembled. Some, out of courage, asked him, "Who are you? Where have you come from? For what purpose, and what is your name?" Sindura, who delights in the destruction of the world, was filled with anger. Hearing the cries of his servants and seeing them trembling, he approached. "You appear to be a powerful child, though only four or five years old. You seem capable of causing the destruction of all in an instant."

Hearing those words, the Lord replied, "I am known as the son of Shiva, residing in the house of Parashara. I delight in destroying the wicked and am intent on protecting the devotees. I am skilled in various incarnations and am beyond the reach of Brahma and others. My name is Gajanana, and I have come to fight. Go to your master and tell him everything. When that invincible one is slain, my mind will find satisfaction."

Krtayuga said: Then they left quickly and spoke to Sindura. The messengers said, "We went quickly to the person as per your command. Seeing him, who resembles the God of Death, we all trembled. We told him of your prowess, and he told us everything about himself. He says his name is Gajanana and he is the son of Shiva, come to destroy wicked demons and protect the virtuous. He has come to fight you. Though he is a four-year-old child, he boasts greatly. Now, by your own nature, destroy this small enemy. With just your breath, this child should be blown far away. If even Shiva and others tremble at the sight of you, what is the count of this small-minded child?"

Krtayuga said: Hearing the words of the messengers, Sindura became anxious. His face withered, and his eyes turned red with anger. Vomiting fire from his eyes like the fire of dissolution, he spoke to the messengers with a mix of anger and joy. He said, "Your words are a great wonder to me, O messengers. How can a mosquito come to fight a lion? Must I fight with a four-year-old child? I do not know why you are so afraid. When I am angry, the world perishes; what is the count of this one?" He roared, shaking the sky and all directions. Taking up his weapons, he went out, eager for battle. Then his ministers, bowing to the lord of the demons, said, "When your powerful ministers are present, why do you go to the battlefield yourself? We will kill him by your prowess, O demon lord. We maintain a great army for such times; he has come to us. We will all give up our lives for your sake, O great lord. Grant us permission; we will go to kill this child, your enemy."

Sindura said, "I will go; let all my soldiers see my prowess." Saying this, he went quickly, eager to kill the child. In an instant, he reached the place where the child was standing.

Thus ends the one hundred and thirty-sixth chapter of the Krida Khanda of the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Exit of Sindura."

Chapter 137 : The Instruction to Varenya

Kritayuga said: Seeing the boy, the demon Sindura, puffed up with pride, said, "I feel ashamed, O fool, to fight with a mere child. Go home quickly, child, and drink your mother’s milk with joy. By uselessly crossing my path, you will meet your death immediately. Even the gods in heaven flee upon seeing me. If you die, your parents will die out of grief for you. Therefore, go away and do not show your face to me, O youth, for with a single blow of my palm, I can shatter the entire universe."

The Lord said: "You have spoken truly, O wicked one, but you have not reflected in your heart. You have not understood my power or my diverse forms. By my angry glance alone, the lords of the gods are shaken. By my own will, I create, and with three aspects, I destroy. I am the one who assumes various forms in every age to protect the world of the moving and the stationary, and I remove the heavy burden of the earth by destroying the wicked. A small thing does not become insignificant if it possesses great power; just as a tiny spark of fire can burn down a vast city. If you wish to live, bow to me and go to your palace. The noble never kill those who bow or seek refuge. Otherwise, by my weapon, you shall go to the heavenly realm. Once you are slain, the world, which has been tormented by you, will find peace. Do not be proud because of the boon granted by Brahma, O king of demons. Everything can turn against you, just as it did in the past. The Lord descended in a pillar and slew Hiranyakashipu; Sugriva, by taking refuge in Rama, killed his brother in due time. Your intellect has also become perverse due to the passage of time; you appear very small to me now. Gather your courage and your shame, and fight with me."

Kritayuga said: Having said this, the Elephant-faced Lord immediately assumed his cosmic form. His head reached beyond the universe, and his feet pierced the seven netherworlds, while his ears spanned the directions. When the demon saw that boy in his universal form—the thousand-headed Lord, the thousand-eyed one, the one who pervaded the earth and the heavens, the one with a thousand feet standing across the ten directions, adorned with divine garments, divine fragrances, and divine ornaments, the Lord who resembled countless suns and possessed countless forms—the demon’s heart trembled. Gathering his courage, the demon went near the Lord and roared, shaking the sky and all the directions. Suddenly raising his sword, he sought to kill the Lord, rushing toward him in anger like a moth into a flame.

The Lord said: "This fool does not understand my rare form. I will release him myself." Saying this, he caught him by the throat. He crushed the powerful Sindura with his hands, and his limbs were smeared with the demon's red blood. From that time on, he became known in the world as Sinduravadana (the one with the red face) and Sindurapriya (the one fond of red), the fulfiller of his devotees' desires.

When Sindura was slain, the gods showered flowers with joy, played auspicious instruments, and the groups of celestial nymphs danced. The sages led by Vashistha and the gods led by Brahma and Indra arrived there with offerings in their hands. Filling the ten directions with shouts of victory and salutations, all the kings also arrived there, filled with joy. They worshipped the Supreme Lord, the four-armed Elephant-faced one, adorned with all ornaments, wearing divine garments, smelling of divine fragrance, and riding a mouse. Indra and the others prayed, "We are unable to praise you, for even the Vedas, Brahma, and the great sages are bewildered by you. You are the creator, the cause, the action, the protector, and the nourisher. You are the destroyer, the deluder of this world, and the giver of knowledge. The rivers, oceans, trees, mountains, and all animals, the wind, space, earth, fire, and water—you are all these. Brahma, Vishnu, Shiva, Indra, the winds, the sages, the Gandharvas, Charanas, Siddhas, Yakshas, Rakshasas, and serpents, the nymphs and Kinnaras—O Lord, you are all this, moving and stationary. We are blessed to have seen you directly, the means to liberation. With Sindura slain, the best of gods have found peace. Kings, sages, and the people are happy in their respective duties. The rituals of Svaha and Vashat will now be performed. Through various incarnations, you protect us, destroy the wicked, and fulfill the desires of your devotees."

Kritayuga said: Having said this, the gods established the Elephant-faced Lord in a temple with a beautiful spire. By the mere sight of him, a person is freed from sins, and by remembering him, a human saves seven generations of his ancestors. Having worshipped and bowed to him, the gods were satisfied. Then all the sages worshipped him with great devotion. They named him Sindura-heti (the destroyer of Sindura) and, with very satisfied hearts, bowed and went to their ashrams. Then all the great sages bowed to the Lord with devotion and worshipped the Supreme Soul with various offerings. This place became famous as the 'Royal Palace' (Rajasadana). Varenya, seeing him, recognized him as his own son. "Because you, O Lord, have destroyed the terrible demon Sindura, the thorn of the world, and have given the kingdom to the kings, you shall be known as Daitya-mardana (the crusher of demons)." He worshipped his son with affection, having seen his prowess. He shed tears of love and was unable to speak; his throat choked with emotion, and he wept, deeply moved.

Varenya said: "I abandoned you out of foolishness, fearing obstacles, O sinner that I am. You, whom Brahma, Indra, and others went to worship, I did not know you to be the Lord of countless millions of universes. Just as a fool might abandon the wish-fulfilling cow, a treasure, or the wish-fulfilling tree, I, deluded by your Maya, abandoned you from my home."

Kritayuga said: Hearing his grief, the Supreme Lord, out of compassion, embraced Varenya with his four arms and spoke to the king with great devotion.

The Lord said: "In a previous age, in a great forest, you both performed severe penance, eating only the fallen leaves of a banyan tree for a thousand divine years. I was pleased with you then. You asked for a son, but not for liberation, due to your foolishness. I have become your son to remove the burden of the earth, to slay Sindura, and to protect the virtuous. Otherwise, I, the formless witness, would have no body. My word has been kept, and the three worlds are satisfied. I shall now go to my own abode; do not let your mind dwell in sorrow."

Varenya said: "I have seen many unbearable sorrows in this world. Now, O destroyer of sorrow, please show me the path to liberation. How can there be bondage for me after seeing you, O Elephant-faced one? Tell me the instruction by which I may attain liberation, the yoga by which I may abandon lust, anger, and the fear of death."

Kritayuga said: Hearing those words, the Elephant-faced Lord, out of compassion, made him sit on a seat and placed his hand on his head. He began to instruct him in the Ganesha Gita, dispelling all his doubts and showing him his universal form. Having understood the essence of the Gita through the instruction of Ganesha, Varenya entrusted his kingdom to his ministers and went to the forest for penance. Filled with intense detachment, he meditated on the Elephant-faced Lord, constantly reciting the Gita, his mind fixed on nothing else. Just as water poured into water becomes water itself, so too, through that meditation, he became one with Him.

The Sage said: "O four-faced Lord, O Lord of gods, please tell me with great compassion that Gita of Ganesha, which destroys all ignorance."

Thus ends the one hundred and thirty-seventh chapter of the Krida Khanda in the Shri Ganesha Purana, titled "The Instruction to Varenya."

Chapter 138 : The Essence of Sankhya Yoga in the Shrimad Ganesha Gita

Suta said: Just as I was asked by the great-souled Shaunaka in the past, I shall narrate the Gita as heard from the mouth of Vyasa. I have already tasted the nectar described in the eighteen Puranas, but now I desire to drink that supreme nectar which makes a man immortal and leads him to the nectar of Brahman. O noble one, please tell me of this nectar of Yoga out of your compassion.

Vyasa said: I shall now reveal the Gita, which illuminates the path of Yoga, as requested by the King (Varenya) to the Elephant-faced Lord (Ganesha).

Varenya said: O Lord of Obstacles, O mighty-armed one, expert in all knowledge and knower of the essence of all scriptures, you are worthy to explain Yoga to me.

Gajanana said: O King, your intellect is well-disposed due to my grace. Listen, O King, as I reveal the Gita, which is filled with the nectar of Yoga. People speak of many things as Yoga, but true Yoga is not wealth, nor is it attachment to sense objects, nor the fulfillment of worldly desires. It is not the bond of parents, children, or relatives, nor the acquisition of the eight supernatural powers. It is not the union with a woman of wondrous beauty, nor the attainment of a kingdom, nor the possession of elephants and horses. It is not the status of Indra, nor the position of the Creator in Satyaloka. It is not the path of the Shaivas or the Vaishnavas, nor the attainment of the sun, moon, or Kubera. It is not the nature of air, fire, immortality, or time; it is not the status of the lords of the quarters, nor universal sovereignty.

Wise men practice various forms of Yoga, O King. They become free from desire, conquer their hunger, and control their senses. They uplift all worlds, having mastered the three realms, and with hearts full of compassion, they enlighten others. They are liberated while living, immersed in the heart of supreme bliss, closing their eyes to behold the Supreme Brahman residing within. Meditating on the Supreme Brahman, they see all beings as equal to their own self. Whether struck, pulled, or sheltered by anyone, they wander the earth with hearts full of compassion for the sake of all beings, having conquered anger and their senses. O King, such great souls, who see gold, stone, and clay as equal, are rare to behold.

Now I shall tell you that supreme Yoga, by hearing which a creature is freed from sins and the ocean of existence. O King, to have the non-dualistic vision that Shiva, Vishnu, Shakti, the Sun, and I are one—this is what I consider true Yoga. Because I alone create, sustain, and destroy the world in various forms through my own play. I am the Great Vishnu, I am the Eternal Shiva, I am the Great Shakti, and I am Aryama. I am the one Lord of men, who appeared in five forms in the past; due to ignorance, people do not know me as the cause of all causes. From me come fire, water, earth, space, and air; Brahma, Vishnu, Rudra, the guardians of the directions, the Vasus, the Manus, the sages, the animals, the rivers, the oceans, the Yakshas, the trees, the birds, the twenty-one heavens, the Nagas, the seven forests, humans, mountains, and the Siddhas—all come from me.

I am the witness, the eye of the world, untouched by all actions. I am changeless, immeasurable, unmanifest, all-pervading, and eternal. I am the Supreme Brahman, the embodiment of eternal bliss. Maya deludes all these people, who are dear to me, and constantly engages them in the six transformations of the body. By abandoning the web of ignorance over many lifetimes, a creature gradually becomes detached and realizes Brahman through clear understanding. This self cannot be cut by weapons, burned by fire, wetted by water, or dried by air; it is indestructible even when the body is being destroyed.

The foolish, attached to the flowery words of the Vedas, believe there is nothing beyond the fruits of heaven and birth. They constantly perform actions that lead to birth and death, their intellects destroyed by the desire for enjoyment. They forge their own bonds and keep the wheel of samsara turning. Whatever action is prescribed for one, that should be performed as an offering to me. By doing so, the seeds of karma are destroyed. Purity of heart leads to the realization of science, and through that science, the Supreme Brahman is known by the sages. Therefore, O King, perform your duties with a balanced mind. No one should be inactive or abandon their own duty. If one abandons duty, one does not attain perfection. Initially, one is not qualified for knowledge; one must engage in action. Through action, the heart is purified, and one attains the non-dual vision. This is the Yoga described for attaining immortality.

I shall tell you of another supreme Yoga. Whether it be a beast, a son, a friend, an enemy, or a relative, one should view them with a balanced mind, both externally and internally. One should be equal in pleasure and pain, in resentment and joy, in fear, in sickness, in enjoyment, in victory and defeat, in the presence or absence of wealth, and in gain or loss. He who sees me residing within and without all objects—in the sun, moon, water, fire, Shiva, Shakti, air, the Brahmin, the heart, the great river, the holy place, Vishnu, all gods, Yakshas, serpents, Gandharvas, humans, and animals—is called a knower of Yoga.

To withdraw the senses from their objects through discrimination and maintain an equal vision everywhere—that, O King, is my view of Yoga. When the intellect, through the grace of God and self-discrimination, remains attached to one's own duty, that is called Yoga. By this, one abandons both merit and demerit. Therefore, practice Yoga, for Yoga is skill in action. The wise man, having abandoned the fruits of merit and demerit and having conquered his senses, is freed from the bonds of birth and attains the state of eternal peace.

When the cloud of ignorance is removed from the intellect, one gradually attains detachment. When the intellect becomes steady in the Supreme Self, one attains Yoga. When a wise man abandons all mental desires and is satisfied in the Self by the Self, he is called a person of steady wisdom. He who is free from desire for all pleasures, not agitated by sorrow, and devoid of fear and anger, is called a person of steady wisdom. Just as a tortoise draws its limbs in from all sides, the one intent on Yoga withdraws his senses from objects. For one who abstains from food, the objects of sense fall away, but the taste for them remains; even that taste vanishes upon seeing the Supreme Brahman.

The senses are powerful and carry away the mind even of a wise man who is striving. Therefore, one should control them and always be intent on me. He who has restrained his senses is considered a person of steady wisdom. By constantly thinking of objects, attachment arises; from attachment, desire is born; from desire, anger increases. From anger comes delusion, from delusion the loss of memory, from the loss of memory the destruction of intellect, and from the destruction of intellect, the person perishes. But he who moves among objects without hatred or attachment, with a heart under his own control, attains peace. In that peace, all threefold sorrows are destroyed, and the person with a steady intellect becomes joyful. Without peace, there is no intellect; without intellect, there is no meditation; without meditation, there is no tranquility; and without tranquility, how can there be happiness?

The mind that follows the wandering senses destroys the intellect, just as the wind destroys a boat on the water. That which is night for all beings, the sage is awake in; and that in which they are awake, is night for the sage. As the rivers enter the ocean, which is filled but remains steady, so do all desires enter the sage, who attains peace. Therefore, one should restrain the senses from all sides, and the intellect will become steady. By abandoning the sense of "I" and "mine" and all desires, one becomes constantly devoted to knowledge, and through knowledge, one attains liberation. He who understands this knowledge of Brahman, O King, attains the fourth state and achieves liberation.

Chapter 139 : Karma Yoga in the Shrimad Ganesha Gita

Varenya said: O Lord, you have spoken of two paths: the path of knowledge and the path of action. Please tell me definitively which one is more conducive to the ultimate good.

Gajanan said: O beloved, I have previously described these two paths in this world: the path of knowledge for the Sannyasis and the path of action for those following the Vedic injunctions. A person cannot attain the state of inaction simply by abstaining from work, nor can they reach perfection merely by renouncing actions. No one can remain inactive even for a moment, for everyone is helplessly driven to action by the qualities born of nature. One who restrains the organs of action but continues to dwell on the objects of the senses in their mind is a hypocrite and is called a pretender. But one who controls the senses with the mind and engages in the yoga of action without attachment is superior, O King.

Perform your prescribed duties, for action is better than inaction; even the maintenance of your body would not be possible without action. People are bound by actions unless they are performed as a sacrifice to Me. Therefore, perform your duties constantly without attachment, dedicating them to Me. Actions performed for My sake do not bind one, whereas actions performed with personal desire bind the embodied soul.
